The Howling Ice

The Howling Ice

Riarhin XI

Part I — The people at the quay

Chapter 1 — A berth already promised

The boom was up.

Eyvind saw it first, from the masthead, and shouted it down, and the whole ship heard him. Riarhin heard it in the hold, where she was sitting on the sacks with her back against a frame and her bad leg out straight, mending the eye of a sheep-hide bucket because it was the only thing aboard that Asta had let her mend. She did not know the word he used. She knew the tone. It was the tone of a boy who had expected one thing and seen another.

Feet went across the deck above her. Sigrun’s voice, short. Orm’s, longer. The ship came up into the wind and shook, and the sail rattled, and somebody laughed in a way that was not amusement.

She put the bucket down and went up the ladder.

It took her some time. She went up it the way she had learned to on the voyage, left hand on the rung above, good leg first, the stitched thigh after, the right arm held against her body where Asta had bound it with a fresh band of linen that morning. At the top she rested her forehead on the coaming for a breath. Then she came out onto the deck into the cold.

It was a hard, clean cold, with the light very low. They were in a fjord. Riarhin had seen the coast close in the day before, black rock and white snow and long grey slopes going up into cloud, and in the night the ship had crept in under it with a lantern at the bow. Now the slopes stood up on both sides of them, sheer, with snow lying in every crease, and ahead, at the head of the water, there was a town.

Hvalgard. She had heard the name every day for forty days and had begun to think of it as a word rather than a place. It was a place. It was a curved shore of black shingle with long houses drawn up above it, their turf roofs white, their gable-ends dark, smoke going up from them straight in the still air. She counted the large ones to eight, and there was another beyond them, and a great many small ones crowded between and behind. Boats were drawn up on the shingle under the houses. There was a quay of heavy timber built out into the water on the near side, and a second quay further along, and across the mouth of the inner harbour, between two posts, there was a chain.

The posts were whale bone. She could see that from here, even at this distance: two great ribs set upright in stone footings on either side of the gap, grey-white, taller than the ship’s mast, curving in toward each other at the top so that they made a kind of gate. Iron rings were set into them. Between the rings ran a chain on floats of black wood, lying on the water, and behind the chain lay the inner harbour, still, with boats in it.

It was up. It lay across the gap and nothing could get past it.

“Well,” said Asta, beside her. Riarhin had not heard her come up. “There’s a welcome.”

Sigrun was at the steering oar with Orm. She was a lean, hard woman in her fifties, grey braid down her back, and she had said perhaps forty words to Riarhin in forty days, most of them about where Riarhin could and could not sit. She was looking at the chain with no expression at all.

“They know us,” said Orm. “They saw the lantern last night.”

“They know us,” said Sigrun.

“Then why—”

“Look at the berth.”

Riarhin looked. Inside the chain, along the second quay, there was a place where a ship might lie: a long stretch of heavy timber with iron rings along its edge and a ramp running up from it onto the shingle. A ship was lying there now. It was smaller than the Staðfast, broad and low, with a stubby mast and no sail bent on, and it was crowded. People were sitting on its deck. Even from here Riarhin could see that they were sitting in the way of people who have nowhere else to sit, close together, wrapped in blankets, with bundles at their feet.

“That’s our berth,” said Eyvind, who had come down from the mast. He was outraged. “That’s ours. Gudleif promised it. In the spring. He promised.”

“He promised it in the spring,” said Sigrun. “It isn’t spring.”

The boat that came out to them was rowed by four men, and the man in its stern was old, bald, and wrapped in a coat of white bear-fur that had gone yellow. He stood up in the boat as it came alongside and put a hand on the Staðfast’s rail and looked up at Sigrun, and he did not look happy.

“Sigrun,” he said.

“Gudleif.”

They spoke fast. Riarhin had spent forty days learning the northern speech in the hold, from Eyvind, who was a patient teacher and a bad speller, and from Asta, who corrected her grammar without looking up from her knitting, and from Orm, who talked. She could follow most of what was said to her if it was said slowly. She could follow very little of what passed between two people who had known each other thirty years and were about to quarrel.

She caught Ottarshall. She caught it several times. She caught a word that Eyvind had taught her meant burned, and another she did not know, and a number. She watched the old man’s hand on the rail. He kept moving his thumb along the wood as if he were rubbing out a mark.

Sigrun said something short. Gudleif shook his head. Sigrun said it again, and Riarhin understood that one: I have freight. Gudleif lifted both hands and let them fall and pointed at the crowded boat on the quay.

“What is he saying?” Riarhin asked Asta, low.

Asta was watching too. “That the boat came in four days ago from up the coast. People from Ottarshall. Their hall burned. They’ve nowhere to go, the halls here are full, so they’re sleeping on the boat. If he moves them off the berth they’re on the shingle. In this.” She tipped her head at the white roofs. “He says he’ll lower the boom when they’ve somewhere else to be.”

“How long?”

“He doesn’t know. Neither do they.”

Sigrun looked at the sky, which was clear and very pale, with a long bank of grey low down in the north-west. Then at the water. Then back at the boom.

“We unload tomorrow,” she said, in the northern speech, slowly and clearly, so that everybody on the deck could hear. “Or the weather does it for us. You know what that bank is.”

Gudleif knew. He looked at it and his thumb moved on the rail.

Riarhin found that she had stepped forward.

She did not quite know why. She had been in the hold for forty days, sitting where Sigrun said, eating Asta’s apples, being lifted and carried and stitched and fed, and she knew how to do one thing on a ship well, which was to keep out of the way. She was standing at the rail beside Sigrun with her bad arm against her ribs.

“I have a place below,” she said. Her northern was slow and she knew it was wrong in several places. “Dry. Near the brazier. If there are people on the boat who are hurt, or old. They could have it.” She thought. “I could go ashore.”

Gudleif looked at her for the first time. He took in her face, her ears, the sword hilt over her shoulder. He did not seem to find anything in particular to say about any of it.

Sigrun did not look at her at all.

“You could go ashore,” Sigrun said. “How?”

Riarhin opened her mouth.

“You can’t climb down the side,” said Sigrun. “Not with that leg. Not with that arm. You’d go in the sling. Somebody would have to lower you into a boat, and somebody would have to row the boat, and somebody would have to lift you out of it onto the shingle, and carry you up to one of those halls that are full, and find you a corner, and come and look at your leg every day because Asta won’t trust anybody else to do it.” She still had not looked round. “That’s four people. For your dry place. Which would hold two of theirs.”

Riarhin shut her mouth.

“It was kindly meant,” said Asta.

“I know it was.” Sigrun looked round then. Her eyes were very pale, like the sky. “Sit down.”

Riarhin sat down on the hatch cover. She sat down hard, because the thigh caught, and felt her face go hot, and looked at the boom.

Beside the ship, a second boat had come out. It was smaller, rowed by one man, and in the bow there was a woman standing up. She was tall and thin, with grey hair cut short as a man’s and a long coat of undyed wool, and she had a harp case slung on her back in sealskin. She did not speak to Gudleif or to Sigrun. She looked up at the ship’s rail, and along it, at the faces, and her eyes stopped on Orm.

“You’re Orm Ketilsson,” she said. Her voice was low and cracked, as if she had shouted too much in her life.

Orm looked down. “I am.”

“I hear you went into the forest shore and took a woman out of the trees.”

Riarhin went still.

“I hear,” said the tall woman, “that you said it on the stones in front of six men so you couldn’t take it back. I hear Hallbjorn said no.” She looked further along the rail, at Hallbjorn, who looked at the water. “I want it from you. Not from Gudleif’s nephew who had it from a man on the Kráka who had it from your cook.”

“Kolli,” said Sigrun, under her breath, as a curse.

Everybody was listening. The men in the boats, the crew along the rail, Gudleif in his yellow furs. Orm looked round at them. Then he leaned his forearms on the rail and smiled down at the tall woman, and Riarhin, watching from the hatch cover, saw what he did with his face. She had watched it for forty days. It was the face he made when the cook asked who had taken the last of the apples.

“Not much to tell, Ranveig,” he said. “We put in for water at the Fox Point stream. Same as every year. There’s a woman on the shingle with a coat on a stick. I go and fetch her. That’s all.”

“That’s all,” said the woman called Ranveig.

“That’s all. Hallbjorn said she might be a deer.” Somebody laughed along the rail. “Eyvind said she wasn’t. I walked down the beach. She was sitting on the stones. I said, are you of the forest, and she said, half.” Orm shrugged. “So I took the half that wasn’t.”

The laugh went all along the rail this time, and down into the boats. Gudleif smiled despite himself. Ranveig did not smile. She looked at Orm with her head a little on one side, the way a bird looks at something on the ground.

“You weren’t afraid,” she said.

“Of a woman sitting down?” said Orm.

It was a good line. It got its laugh. Riarhin sat on the hatch cover and felt it go through her like cold water.

She had been there. She remembered every part of it. She remembered him standing two hundred paces off with the others, arguing, and the big man walking away, and the long moment when nobody moved. She remembered him walking toward her along the shingle, and she remembered his face when he got close, before he spoke. He had been grey. His hands had been shaking. He had kept them away from his knife, and they had shaken all the way, and when he had taken her round the waist to lift her his arm had been trembling so hard she had felt it through her coat.

“You were afraid,” she said.

She said it in the northern speech. She said it not loudly, from the hatch cover. She had not decided to say it. It came out the way her offer about the dry place had come out, before she had looked at it.

The laugh stopped.

Orm turned his head and looked at her. Ranveig looked at her. Gudleif looked at her. All along the rail, the crew of the Staðfast looked at her, and then, one by one, carefully, they looked somewhere else.

“Your hands were shaking,” said Riarhin. She could hear how it sounded and could not stop. “On the beach. All the way. I felt them.”

Orm looked at her for a long moment. His face did not change very much. The smile went out of it, that was all, and nothing came in to replace it.

“I expect they were cold,” he said.

He turned back to Ranveig.

“She was on the stones,” he said. “I went and got her. You can make what you like of it.”

Ranveig looked at him, and then at Riarhin on the hatch cover, and then at him again. She did not write anything down. She did not seem to be the kind of person who needed to.

“I will,” she said. “Thank you, Orm Ketilsson.”

She sat down in her boat, and the boatman pulled away.

Riarhin sat on the hatch cover and looked at the deck between her boots. Nobody spoke to her. The crew went back to their work. Orm went back to the steering oar and stood by it with his arms folded and did not look round.

Below, in the hold, he had told it differently. He had told it to her on the second night after her fever broke, when she had asked him why, and he had said: I was frightened out of my wits. I said it fast so I couldn’t take it back. That’s what you do. He had said it sitting on the sacks with his rope across his knees, and she had believed every word of it, and been glad of it, and it had been the beginning of her liking him.

She had taken it out of the hold and said it on the deck in front of Ranveig and Gudleif and two boats full of strangers.

She sat on the hatch cover and understood that, and could not take it back either.

Gudleif and Sigrun went on talking. It went on for a long time. At the end of it, Sigrun turned to the crew and gave orders, and the crew went to the oars, and the Staðfast crept in toward the boom under oars alone, very slowly, until her bow was a length from the floating chain.

Then Sigrun went to the rail and called across the water to the crowded boat lying in her berth.

Riarhin could not follow it all. She caught Hvalgard and Sigrun’s house and my sister. She caught a phrase she knew from the voyage, which meant on my name, and was used when a person wanted it understood they would answer for what they said. On the crowded boat, people stood up. A man called back. Sigrun answered. There was a pause, and then a woman on the boat began to laugh, and then to cry, and somebody put an arm round her.

Gudleif, in his boat, lifted his arm.

On the far whale-bone post a man began to turn a windlass, and the chain sank. It went down with a long rattle, the floats dipping one after another, and lay on the bottom, and the way into the inner harbour was open.

“What did she say?” said Riarhin, very low, to Asta.

“That her sister has a hall at the top of the town with a byre that’s half empty since the cattle went to the autumn slaughter,” said Asta. “That they can have the byre, and the loft over it, and her sister’s fire, until the spring. On her name. Her sister will be furious.” She considered. “Her sister will also do it.”

The crowded boat began, slowly, to move off the berth.

It did not move far. It was pulled by hand along the quay to a lesser mooring, and its people began to come ashore up the ramp with their bundles, and the Staðfast came in through the gap between the whale-bone posts under oars and laid herself along the timbers where they had been. Lines went ashore. Men on the quay took them. Riarhin watched from the hatch cover. She could do nothing else.

There were a great many people on the quay. More than there needed to be for a ship coming in. She looked at them. Some were plainly from the town, in good coats, come down to see. Some were from the other boat, standing with their bundles and staring at the Staðfast as if they had not decided yet whether to be grateful. Some were working. A line came ashore from the bow and three men caught it and ran it round a post and made it fast, and one of the three was a young man in a coat with a sleeve that had been burned.

She saw it because it was the wrong colour. The rest of the coat was brown. The right sleeve, from the elbow down, was black, and stiff, and split along its length in a way that cloth splits when it has been in fire, and the hand at the end of it was wrapped in a rag. He worked the line one-handed and well, as if he had been doing it like that for some days.

When the ship was fast he did not go away. He stood on the quay at the foot of the ramp, with the burned arm held against him exactly the way Riarhin was holding hers, and looked up at the crew coming to the rail. He looked at each face in turn.

“Is anybody aboard from Ottarshall?” he said.

Nobody answered. On the deck, the crew looked at each other.

The young man waited. Then he said it again, louder, as if he had been saying it for some days to every boat that came in and had got used to the silence after it.

“Did anybody come from Ottarshall?”

Chapter 2 — The wood below the paint

The rumour reached the ship before breakfast on the third day, and it came, as Asta said, by the usual road: from the man who wanted it believed, by way of three people who did not know they were carrying it.

Riarhin heard it from Eyvind. He came down the ladder into the hold with the porridge pot and a face like thunder and set the pot on the brazier stone so hard the lid jumped.

“They’re saying we’re rotten,” he said.

Riarhin was sitting where she had sat for forty days, on the sacks, though the sacks were fewer now. Most of the freight had gone ashore the day before in a long chain of men and sledges up the shingle, and the hold was emptier and colder and smelled more of tar. She had sat and watched it go. She had counted the bales of wool to eight, and then started again at one.

“Who is?” she said.

“Everybody. The whole quay. They’re saying Sigrun’s keel is soft. That she came up the coast with rot in her and she knows it and she’s hiding it.” Eyvind ladled porridge into a bowl and gave it to her without looking at what he was doing, and some of it went on her knee. “They’re saying you can smell it. Sweet. Like old apples.”

“Can you?”

“No!” He was furious. “It’s tar. It smells of tar. It’s always smelled of tar.”

Asta came down the ladder behind him, slowly, with her hand on the rail. She took the ladle from Eyvind before he could do anything else with it.

“Thorvald Hrafnsson,” she said, “has been trying to take the winter run up the bays off Sigrun for six years. Seal oil and wool to the halls, and meal back down. It’s good work. It keeps a ship through the dark months.” She filled a bowl for herself. “He has a boat in the harbour called the Kráka that leaks like a basket and he has three sons who need feeding. If Sigrun’s keel is soft, the halls give the run to him. That’s all it is.”

“So it isn’t true,” said Riarhin.

Asta ate a spoonful of porridge.

“Hallbjorn’s going to look,” she said. “At low water. He’s going to look at every strake below the line and push a knife into every one of them in front of whoever wants to come and watch. And then we’ll know.”

Low water was at mid-morning. The Staðfast lay against the quay with the tide going out under her, and as it went she settled, a little at a time, onto the hard black shingle of the berth, and leaned, very gently, against the timbers, and stayed there, high and dry, with her keel on the stones and her belly showing.

People came to look. Riarhin had expected that. She had not expected so many.

She was on the quay. She had come up out of the hold in the sling, sitting on its board with her good hand on the rope, and been swung over the side and set down on the timbers by Hallbjorn, who had done it without a word and gone away. Then Eyvind had brought her a bench from somewhere, and a heap of the ship’s lashings, and told her Sigrun wanted them sorted: good ones in one pile, the ones that wanted splicing in another, the rotten ones in a third. It was the first work she had been given on land. She sat on the bench with the lashings in her lap and her back against a post, out of the wind, and sorted rope, and watched Hvalgard come down to look at Sigrun’s keel.

They came along the quay and stood on it and looked down. They came along the shingle and stood under the ship’s side. They were mostly men, in heavy coats, with their hands under their arms, and they talked to each other in low voices and looked at the planking and did not come close. Among them, standing a little apart with two young men who had his long jaw, was a heavy man with a fur hat who looked at the Staðfast the way a man looks at a horse he has been told is lame and would like to buy.

“That’s him,” said Eyvind, at her elbow. “Thorvald.”

“I thought so.”

“How did you know?”

“He’s the only one who looks pleased.”

Eyvind looked at her with respect and went away to help Hallbjorn.

The fog came in while they were working. It came in off the fjord without any warning, a long low wall of white rolling up the water from the mouth, and it came over the quay and the ship and the town, and in a few moments Riarhin could not see the far side of the harbour, or the whale-bone posts, or the top of the Staðfast’s mast. Sounds came strangely in it. A gull cried somewhere and seemed to be beside her. A man laughed on the shingle and seemed to be very far away.

“Can’t move her now,” said Orm, somewhere near. He was speaking to Sigrun; Riarhin could not see either of them. “If we wanted to take her round to the Long Strand to look at her in better light, we couldn’t. Not in this. You couldn’t find the channel.”

“I don’t want to take her anywhere,” said Sigrun’s voice. “I want him to look at her here, where they can all see.”

Hallbjorn looked.

He did it as Asta had said he would. He went down onto the shingle under the ship’s side with a short knife and a lantern, because the fog had taken the light, and he began at the stern and went forward strake by strake, along the lowest planks first, and at every strake he set the point of the knife against the wood and pushed. He pushed hard. Riarhin could see his shoulders move. Every time, he drew the knife out and held it up to the lantern and looked at the point, and then he called out a number, and Eyvind, beside him with a slate, wrote it down.

The men from the town came closer in the fog to watch. They came close enough to see the knife. Thorvald Hrafnsson came with them.

Riarhin sorted rope. Good, splice, rotten. Most of it was good. The lashings that had held the freight for forty days were salt-stiff and hard as wood and she had to work each one loose with her good hand and her teeth before she could see whether there was anything wrong with it. She found the work steadying. It used her attention without asking anything of her that she did not have.

At the twentieth strake from the stern, Hallbjorn stopped.

He pushed the knife in, and it went in further than it had gone before. Riarhin saw it from the bench. She saw his shoulders not move, because there had been nothing to push against. He drew it out and held it to the lantern and looked at it for some time.

The men from the town were very quiet.

Hallbjorn did not call out a number. He set the lantern on the shingle and crouched and put his face close to the planking, and then he put his nose to it, and then he took the knife and began, very carefully, to dig.

It was a seam. When he had finished digging, and called Sigrun, and Sigrun had come and looked, and then Orm, and then, with a curious stiff reluctance, Thorvald Hrafnsson and his two sons, everybody on the shingle could see what it was: a place where two strakes met under the turn of the bilge, where the caulking had been driven in years before and had worked loose in forty days of heavy sea, and the water had got in behind it and softened the edge of the lower plank for the length of a man’s forearm. The wood was dark and wet and gave under a thumb.

“There,” said Thorvald.

Hallbjorn looked at him.

“Smell it,” said Hallbjorn.

Thorvald hesitated. Then he bent and smelled it, and straightened, and his face did not change.

“Salt,” said Hallbjorn. “Salt and tar and wet oak. That’s water got in a seam. That’s a day’s work with a caulking iron and a strake end let in, and I’ll do it this week.” He stood up. He was a head taller than Thorvald and broader than both his sons together. “Rot smells sweet. You all know that. Go down the shingle and smell the Kráka if you want to know what it smells like.”

Somebody laughed. Then several people did. Thorvald Hrafnsson stood in the fog with his sons and looked at Hallbjorn, and then at Sigrun, and said something short that Riarhin did not catch, and walked away up the shingle.

“That won’t be the end of it,” said Asta. She had come and sat down on the end of Riarhin’s bench without Riarhin noticing. “The halls will hear there was a soft plank. They won’t hear what kind.”

“It’s a seam,” said Riarhin.

“It’s a seam that wants mending before she can go up the bays in the dark months.” Asta looked at the fog. “And she can’t move till the fog lifts, and she can’t go up the bays till she’s mended, and the halls won’t sign for the winter till they’ve seen her mended.” She looked down at Riarhin. “You’ll be here a while. So will we.”

Riarhin looked at the heap of rope at her feet.

There was a long coil there, one of the heavy lines from the bow, that had come off the ship wet and been thrown down on the quay. She had been working round it. It was too heavy for her to lift with one hand; she had been sorting the light lashings off the top of it and leaving it. Now she looked at it and thought that if she could drag it nearer the bench she could get at the end and see whether it wanted splicing, and then it would be done, and Sigrun would have one less thing.

She got her good hand under the top of the coil and her good foot braced against the bench leg and pulled.

The coil moved. It was much heavier than she had thought. It was full of water. It came toward her a little way across the timbers and she leaned back to bring it further, and as she leaned she felt the stitched thigh take her weight, and pull, and something in it go tight and hot, a single line under the skin.

“No,” said Asta.

She had not raised her voice. She had put her hand flat on Riarhin’s good forearm, that was all. Riarhin let go of the rope.

“Don’t,” said Asta. “Not with that leg. If you tear it I’ll have to do it again, and it won’t close so well the second time, and you’ll limp till you’re old.” She looked at the coil. “Leave it.”

“It’s only rope.”

“It’s a bow line full of seawater. It weighs more than you do.” Asta took her hand away. “Leave it.”

Riarhin sat back against the post. The line in her thigh went on being hot for a moment and then faded to an ache. She looked at the coil, a yard from her feet, and at her own hand, which had rope-tar on it and was shaking a little, and she was angry and could not think at whom.

Asta got up and went to see what Hallbjorn wanted.

The fog thickened. It came down closer over the harbour, and the light went yellow and dim, and the men from the town drifted away up the shingle in twos and threes, talking. Riarhin sorted the last of the light lashings. Good. Good. Splice. She laid them out in their three heaps on the timbers beside her and looked at them and was not satisfied, because the big coil was still there, unsorted, and she could not do anything about it.

Some time later she looked up and Hallbjorn was standing over her.

He did not say anything. He looked at the coil. Then he bent and took it up in both arms, the whole wet weight of it, as if it were a cat, and carried it away along the quay into the fog.

She watched him go. She did not thank him. He had not given her the chance, and she did not think he wanted it.

She sat on the bench with her hands in her lap.

There were voices on the shingle below the ship, in the fog. Several people, talking in low voices, the way the men who had come to look at the keel had talked. Riarhin could not see them. She could hear them, close, distorted, the fog carrying their words up the side of the quay to her and changing them as it came.

“—say they came up out of the water,” somebody said. “Out of the sea. Like seals.”

“They came by sled. Over the ice behind the hall.”

“That’s what Bersi says.”

“Bersi was there.”

A pause.

“Dogmen,” said a third voice. It was a young voice, hoarse. “It was Dogmen.”

Somebody laughed.

It was the fond laugh of a man who has been told a story by a child, about a thing under the bed, and is fond of the child and sorry for him. Riarhin heard it in the fog and knew the word from Eyvind, who had used it once on the voyage when he wanted to frighten her and she had made him explain it. The dog-faced ones. From the north. From the old songs. They used to come, in my great-great-grandfather’s time, his grandfather’s time. Teeth like this. He had shown her with his fingers. They eat people. And Asta, not looking up from her knitting: Eyvind, nobody you or I or your great-great-grandfather ever met has seen one of them. Go to sleep.

“Dogmen,” said the man who had laughed. “All right. Did you see their faces, Bersi? Did you see their snouts?”

“I saw them,” said the hoarse voice.

“In the dark. With the hall burning.”

“I saw them.”

“Bersi—”

“They took four,” said the hoarse voice.

The laughter did not come again. Nobody said anything.

“They didn’t take the grain,” said the hoarse voice. “They didn’t take the oil. They didn’t take the silver out of Ingrith’s brother’s chest. They went past the store with the door open. I saw them go past it. They went to four houses and took four people and went away.” A pause. “Gunnvor that weaves. Kári, Aud’s boy, he’s nine. Old Ulf that kept the count-stones. And Svala.” The voice cracked on the last name and came back. “Ingrith’s brother’s girl. Svala.”

The fog moved on the water.

“Gunnvor,” said the hoarse voice, “Kári, Ulf, Svala.” As if it had been said many times, and would be said many more, and must be said exactly. “Those four. And nineteen in the hall.”

Nobody laughed.

After a while the voices moved off along the shingle. Riarhin heard their feet on the stones, and then nothing.

She sat on her bench in the fog and looked at her three heaps of rope.

When she looked up again there was a coil lying beside her bench. It had not been there before. It was the bow line, the wet heavy one. It had been flaked down neatly on the timbers by somebody who knew how to do it, so that it would run clear, and at its end, where it had been frayed when she found it, there was a short neat splice, new, the strands tucked under and tapered and rolled smooth.

She looked along the quay. There was nobody there. Only the fog, and the ship leaning against the timbers with her keel on the stones, and somewhere behind her, unseen, the sound of a caulking iron, and a mallet, and a man working.

Chapter 3 — Four places at the table

The squall came out of a clear sky an hour past noon, while the boats were strung out along the coast under the cliffs, and it took them apart in the time it would have taken to say what it was.

Riarhin was in the sheltered boat, because Asta would not hear of anything else. It was a broad heavy thing with a deck over the forward half and a low hood of tarred hide over the deck, and under the hood, out of the wind, there was room for three people to sit with their knees drawn up. Asta sat there with her bag of salves and needles. Riarhin sat beside her. The third place was taken by sacks of meal, which was why Riarhin had been allowed to come at all: she could keep them dry.

“They want me at Ottarshall,” Asta had said, the evening before, packing. “There are people there with burns, and the woman who was tending them has gone to her daughter’s. Four days, perhaps five. I won’t leave that leg to anybody else for five days. You come or I don’t go.” She had looked at Riarhin. “I’m not asking.”

So Riarhin had come, in the sling, into the sheltered boat, with Remorse wrapped in sailcloth under the thwart where Hallbjorn insisted it lived, and the two knives at her belt where Hallbjorn had decided he could tolerate them.

The trackers had gone the day before, on foot and by sled, along the shore. Ingrith’s men, Asta said. Six of them, with dogs, going up behind Ottarshall to find where the raiders had gone. Orm had looked at them going with an expression Riarhin had not been able to read. He had not spoken to her since the quay. He had not been unkind; he had been somewhere else whenever she was near, and when he could not be he had found a rope to look at.

The boats had gone that morning. Five of them, out through the whale-bone gate with the boom down, carrying food and blankets and timber to Ottarshall a day up the coast. They went in a line under the cliffs, under sail, with the wind behind them, and for half a day it was as easy a passage as Riarhin had made.

Then the light went.

She saw it from under the hood: the sky over the cliffs going from pale blue to a strange dark yellow and then to black in the space of a few breaths, and the sea under it going flat and grey, and the men in the boat ahead turning to look. Then the wind came off the cliffs. It came down on them from above, straight down, the way water comes off a roof, and it hit the sea and the sea went white, and the boat heeled over so far that Riarhin saw green water at the edge of the hood and the meal sacks slid into her and Asta’s bag went over and needles went everywhere.

She did not see much after that. She held on to the frame of the hood with her good hand and the sacks with her knees. The boat came up and went over the other way. Somebody was shouting. The sail came down with a noise like a great wing beating and somebody screamed and then went on shouting. There was hail. It came in under the hood in a white rattle and stung her face and the backs of her hands, and she could not see the cliffs or the other boats or anything but white and grey.

It lasted perhaps a quarter of an hour. Then it went on up the coast and left them.

When the light came back, they were alone.

The sail was down in the bottom of the boat in a wet heap. One of the men was holding his wrist and swearing. The steersman was standing up at the oar looking round at the empty sea, and the cliffs, which were closer than they had been, and a long way up the coast, very small, a sail.

“Selvík,” said the steersman, after a while. “That’s the Grá. She’s running for Selvík. And the meal boat with her.” He looked the other way, south, where there was nothing. “And Rorg knows where the rest are.”

“And us?” said Asta.

The steersman looked at the cliffs. “Ottarshall’s round the next point,” he said. “We’re nearer than anybody.”

Ottarshall had been a hall.

Riarhin saw it first as a shape against the snow, at the head of a small bay, where a stream came down out of a cleft in the hills: a long low black shape with the snow round it trodden grey. It was only when the boat came closer that she understood what she was looking at. The hall had been very large. It had had turf walls as high as a man and a roof of timber and turf over them, and the roof had burned and fallen in, and the timbers of it were lying inside the walls now, black, and the snow had come down on them and melted and frozen and come down again. The walls still stood. They stood round nothing, like the rim of a pot.

There were people. That was the second thing she saw. There were people moving round the black walls, and smoke going up from a smaller building further up the slope, and a boat-shed on the shore with its doors open and a fire in front of it, and dogs.

They came in to the shingle. Men came down to meet them. One of them waded out and caught the bow and looked up at the boat, at the sacks under the hood, and at the steersman, and at the man holding his wrist, and at Asta.

“Where’s the rest?” he said.

“The squall,” said the steersman. “The meal’s gone to Selvík.”

The man looked at the sacks under the hood. There were six.

He did not say anything. He took hold of the bow and began to help them bring the boat in.

They got Riarhin ashore on a man’s back. She had not wanted that. There was no sling here and no Hallbjorn, and the shingle shelved steeply and the water was cold enough to stop the breath, and there was nothing else. The man who carried her was young and silent and smelled of smoke. He set her down on the stones above the tide-line and went back for the sacks.

She stood on the shingle and looked up at the burned hall.

It had been a long time since she had seen a place after a raid. She had seen a great many, once, with the Black Banners. You learned not to look at them, or you learned to look at them in a particular way, the way you looked at a field after a battle, for the things that could still be used. She looked at Ottarshall that way without deciding to. The hall: gone. The byre behind it: standing, its roof half-burned. A row of small houses along the stream, five or six: two burned, the rest whole. The boat-shed on the shore: whole, doors open, a fire in front of it, people round the fire. A store-house of stone and turf, built into the slope above the hall, with a heavy door.

The door was open.

She looked at it for a moment and then looked away. There was a woman coming down the slope toward the boat.

She was in her middle years, broad, with a strong heavy face reddened by cold and a coat of fine blue wool that had been scorched along the hem. Her hair was fair and going grey and she wore it in two braids that came down over her breast to her belt, and the ends of the braids were tied with black thread. She walked down the shingle to the boat and looked at the six sacks of meal being carried up the beach, and at Asta, and at the steersman, and her face did not change at all.

“The meal,” she said.

“Selvík,” said the steersman. “The squall. I’m sorry, Ingrith.”

The woman nodded slowly, as though she had expected it, as though she expected everything now.

“Nineteen,” she said, to nobody in particular. Then, to Asta: “You’re Asta of the Staðfast. Good. The burns are in the boat-shed. Five of them. One’s bad.” Her eyes came to Riarhin and stopped. “Who’s this?”

“She came off the ship with me,” said Asta. “She’s mine to look after. She’ll be no trouble.”

Ingrith looked at Riarhin a moment longer, at her face and her ears and the knives at her belt, and seemed to put her somewhere to be dealt with later.

“Then she can help,” she said, and went back up the slope.

There was a great deal to do, and very little of it was the kind of thing Riarhin could do sitting down.

She found what she could. She sat on an upturned keg in the lee of the boat-shed with a heap of torn sailcloth and a bone needle, and cut and sewed coverings for the burned roof of the byre, where the cattle that were left were standing in the snow. A woman showed her how to do it, roughly, in a few words, and went away. The sailcloth was stiff with frost. Riarhin sewed it with her good hand and held it with her knee and her teeth, badly, and kept going.

Asta was in the boat-shed with the burned. Riarhin could hear her, now and then, through the open doors, saying short things in a level voice, and once a man crying out, and then quiet.

People came and went. She learned some of their names without being told them, the way she learned everything, from hearing them called. There were perhaps forty people left at Ottarshall. They were living in the boat-shed and the byre and the four houses that had not burned, and they moved about the place in the snow with the stunned, careful look of people who had not yet decided what to do and were doing whatever was in front of them in the meantime.

There was an argument about a child.

It happened by the boat-shed doors, in the afternoon, between two women. Riarhin did not understand all of it. She understood that there was a girl of about six whose mother had died in the hall and whose father had not been at Ottarshall that night, and was somewhere up the coast with a fishing crew and did not know yet. She understood that one woman wanted to take the girl to her own sister at Selvík, where there was a whole house and food, and the other woman said the girl must stay here, where her father would come, and not be sent away among strangers before he could get here. They argued it back and forth in low fierce voices with the child standing between them in an adult’s coat, looking at the snow.

Riarhin sewed and did not look up.

In the end Ingrith came. She listened to both of them for perhaps the space of ten breaths and then she said something short, and the woman who wanted to go to Selvík turned away, and the other took the child by the hand and led her into the byre. Ingrith stood alone by the doors for a moment afterward. She stood with her arms at her sides and her face quite still and looked at the black walls of the hall.

“Nineteen,” she said, under her breath.

Then she went back to work.

Late in the afternoon, when the light was going, a woman came and sat down on the keg beside Riarhin’s.

She was old, small, with a face so lined that her eyes were almost lost in it, and she had both her hands wrapped in rags. She sat for a while and watched Riarhin sew.

“You’ve a good eye,” she said at last.

Riarhin looked up.

“Asta says,” said the old woman. “She says you see things. She says you came out of the trees on the forest shore and you saw the ship before the ship saw you.” She held up her bandaged hands. “I can’t dig. Not with these. I’ve asked them all and they’re all busy, and they’re right to be busy. I’ve a thing in the ash.”

“What thing?”

“A key.” The old woman lowered her hands into her lap. “My mother’s chest. It had her things in. Her brooch, and her wedding linen. It was by my bed in the hall, and the chest is gone, it burned, I saw it. But the key was on a cord on the post by my bed, and it’s iron. It won’t have burned.” She looked at the black walls. “It’s in there somewhere. I want it. I don’t know why.”

Riarhin looked at the black walls too, and at the snow lying on the fallen timbers.

“Where was your bed?” she said.

The old woman’s name was Thora, and her bed had been in the north-east corner of the hall, the third bed from the end, with a post at its foot carved with a ship.

Riarhin went in with a lantern and a rake. She went in slowly, over the threshold, where the great door had been and was now a gap with a charred stump on either side, and she stood in the hall.

It was very quiet. The snow had come in over everything and lay soft on the black timbers. Under it the floor of the hall was a confusion of fallen beams and ash and things she did not look at closely. The smell was still there, under the cold: smoke, and wet ash, and something else. She knew the something else. She breathed through her mouth.

Somebody had already been through it. That was plain. There were paths trodden through the snow, between the beams, to the places where the beds had been, and at each place the ash had been raked and turned over. They had come for the dead first. Then for what could be saved.

She found the north-east corner. She found the place where the beds had been along the wall, and the stumps of the posts between them, and she counted along. One, two, three. The third post was burned to a stump a foot high, but there was enough left of it to see the curve of something carved: a prow, a line of shields.

She knelt. It hurt. She put the lantern down on a beam and began to rake the ash at the foot of the post, slowly, with her good hand.

It took a long time. She found nails. She found the twisted remains of something that might have been a lamp. She found a bone comb, unburned, which she set aside. She raked and turned and sifted the ash through her fingers, and her hand went black to the wrist, and the cold came up through her knees.

She found the key.

It was in the ash a little to one side of the post, where the cord had burned and dropped it: a heavy iron key as long as her hand, black with soot, the bow of it shaped like a knot. She picked it up and wiped it on her coat and held it to the lantern and looked at it, and was absurdly pleased.

Then, still kneeling, she looked up through the gap in the hall’s north wall, where a door had been, at the store-house on the slope above.

She had seen its door was open when she came ashore. She had looked away. She looked at it now, from inside the hall, where she could see straight up the slope to it, and she saw it as the raiders must have seen it, coming in from the hills behind on that night: the first building they would have reached. The door was heavy, oak, banded with iron. It stood wide. Inside, in the lantern-light from the boat-shed below, she could see the shapes of things stacked to the roof: sacks, kegs, the pale bundled shapes of dried fish hung from the rafters.

The trodden paths in the snow outside the store went in and came out. They were recent. Ottarshall had been eating out of it for seventeen days.

She knelt in the ash with the key in her hand.

“They opened it,” said Thora.

She had the key in her rag-wrapped hands, in her lap, and she was looking at it, and she spoke without looking up.

“They opened the store. They broke the bar. I saw it in the morning. And they looked in, and they left it.” She turned the key over. “There’s two hundred dried cod in there, and the seal oil, and the barley that came down from Selvík in the autumn, and Ingrith’s brother’s chest with the coin in it. Nobody touched any of it.” She looked up. “Why would you break a door and not take anything?”

Riarhin did not answer.

There was a younger woman sitting across the fire from them, in the lee of the boat-shed, with a baby asleep inside her coat. She had been listening. Her name was Ragna; she had been a servant in the hall.

“They came for the four,” said Ragna. “That’s all they came for. Bersi says.”

“Bersi says a lot,” said a man on the far side of the fire. He was elderly, with a white beard and a burned hand, and Riarhin had heard him called Thorkel.

“Bersi was at the door. He saw them.” Ragna held the baby closer. “He saw them come round the end of the hall from the store and go straight along the row. Not to every house. They went past Halldor’s door. They went past mine. I was behind it with her—” she looked down at the baby “—and they went past. I heard them. They went to Gunnvor’s house and they called her out.”

“Called her?” said Riarhin.

They looked at her. She had said it in the northern speech, carefully, and she saw them notice how carefully.

“Called her name,” said Ragna. “Through the door. Not Gunnvor. They called her Selhús-Gunnvor. Seal-house Gunnvor.” She shook her head. “Nobody’s called her that since she was a little girl. It was her great-grandmother’s house, the Seal-house, down by the point, it fell down before I was born. Her mother used to call her that when she was naughty. Seal-house Gunnvor, come in out of the rain. That’s what they shouted through the door.”

“They said Sel-hús,” said Thorkel. “Not Selhús. Bersi said they said it wrong. Like foreigners.”

“They said it right.”

“They said it the old way. My grandmother said it like that.”

“Then they said it right.”

They looked at each other across the fire. The baby stirred inside Ragna’s coat and she rocked it. Nobody said anything for a while.

“Kári,” said Thora, after a time, very low. “They called Kári too. Aud’s Kári. His mother was screaming. And they went in for Ulf and he couldn’t walk, he never could walk since his hip went, and they carried him. Carried him. And Svala.” She stopped. “They broke the door of Ingrith’s brother’s house, and they went in, and they brought Svala out.”

“And nineteen in the hall,” said Thorkel.

“Nineteen in the hall,” said Ragna.

Riarhin sat by the fire. She looked down the slope at the black walls of the hall, and up the slope at the open door of the store, and along the row of houses by the stream, the burned ones and the whole ones. She did not say anything. She did not think there was anything to say yet that would not be a guess, and she did not want to make a guess in front of these people and hear it come back to her in somebody else’s mouth.

When it was dark Ingrith came down from the byre to the fire and stood by it and looked at the six sacks of meal stacked against the boat-shed wall.

“We’ll open the store tomorrow,” she said. “Properly. Count what’s in it. We’ll need it, now.” She looked round at the faces by the fire. “Tell the others.”

Then she went into the boat-shed.

Riarhin, following Asta in later to sleep, saw her there. There was a table at the back of the shed, made of planks on trestles, where the people of Ottarshall ate. It had been cleared after supper. At its head Ingrith sat alone, in the lamp-light, with her two braids tied in black. In front of her the table had been laid. Not for the forty living who were lying wrapped in blankets on the floor all round her. Four places: four bowls, four spoons, four cups, set out in a row along the clean boards, with a little bread in front of each, and nobody sitting at them.

Ingrith sat at the head of the table and looked at them.

She did not look up when Riarhin passed.

Chapter 4 — No horse with him

He came down out of the hills on the evening of the day they got back from Ottarshall, on foot, in the dusk, and the dogs found him before anybody else did.

Riarhin heard them from Jorunn’s hall. Jorunn was Sigrun’s sister, and she had turned out to be every bit as furious about the byre as Asta had promised, and every bit as willing; the people off the Ottarshall boat were in it now, eleven of them, with Jorunn’s fire and Jorunn’s second-best blankets, and Jorunn went in to them twice a day with porridge and came out complaining about the smell. The crew of the Staðfast were in the hall itself, on the benches along the walls, while Hallbjorn finished the seam and the halls argued about the winter freight. Riarhin had a corner at the end of the women’s bench, near the hearth, with a hanging of old sailcloth that Asta had rigged so that she could change the binding on her leg without the whole hall watching.

The dogs began at the top of the town, a long way off, and then more of them, and then all the dogs in Hvalgard, in a great rolling outcry that came down the slope from house to house. Men went out. Riarhin heard them in the dark outside, calling to each other. Then the dogs stopped, one by one, the way they do when they have been shouted at, and there were voices instead, a lot of them, and coming nearer.

They brought him to Jorunn’s because Jorunn’s was nearest the top of the town. Riarhin saw him come in through the door with four men round him and the cold coming in round all of them, and she knew what he was before he had taken three steps into the light.

He was Ykrul. He was young, perhaps twenty-five, not tall, broad through the shoulders, in a long coat of felt and sheepskin that had been made for riding and was white now with frost all down one side, as if he had lain in the snow. His face was burned dark by wind. His hair was braided, two long braids behind the ears in the way the riders wore it, with a third small one at the left temple threaded with red; and at his throat, on a thong, hung a wolf’s tooth. He had a short bow in a case on his back, unstrung, and a quiver, and a long knife at his belt, and the men round him were looking at all three.

He had no horse.

That was what was wrong. Riarhin saw it the way she would have seen a man come in without his hands. The coat was a rider’s coat. The boots were riders’ boots, soft-soled, laced to the knee. He stood the way a rider stands on the ground, a little wide, as if the floor might move. And he had come out of the hills on foot, in the dusk, in winter, from inland, where Eyvind said there was nothing at all this time of year but snow and wind and the old roads nobody used.

“He came down the Hallsdal track,” said one of the men to Jorunn. “From the top. Walking. Asking for somebody.”

“Asking for who?” said Jorunn.

“That’s what we can’t make out.”

They sat him by the fire, because it was Jorunn’s hall and she would not have it otherwise, and they gave him soup, because she would not have that otherwise either. He took the bowl in both hands and held it under his chin and drank from it, and did not use the spoon.

Riarhin watched him from her corner.

She watched the hall too. It had filled up. Word had gone round the town, the way it did, and men had come in from the other halls to look, and stood along the walls in their coats with the snow melting off them, not sitting down. The crew of the Staðfast were on their benches. Orm was by the hearth, where he usually sat in the evenings, splicing. He had his rope across his knees and was not splicing it.

The Ykrul drank his soup. When he had finished he set the bowl down on the hearthstone beside him and took something from inside his coat. It was a flat piece of bone the length of his hand, old, yellowed, polished by handling, and there was a mark cut in it. He held it up so that they could see.

“This,” he said, in the northern speech. He said it badly. He said it slowly, as though each word were a stone he had to lift. “This. You see? On stone. On wood. On door.” He touched the mark. “You see this?”

They looked at it. Riarhin, from her corner, looked at it too. It was a simple thing: two short strokes side by side, joined at the bottom by a loop. She did not know it.

Nobody answered. The men along the walls looked at each other.

“On door,” said the Ykrul again. “Old. Old mark. Very old. In north. You see?”

“He wants to know if we’ve seen it in the north,” said Jorunn.

“We can hear what he wants,” said a man by the wall. He was one of the ones who had brought him in, a thick-set man with a fox-fur hood. “I want to know what he wants it for.”

There was a murmur.

“He comes down out of the north,” said the fox-fur man, “on foot, in the dark, three weeks after Ottarshall, asking about marks on doors in the north. What marks? Whose doors?” He looked round the hall. “They went to four doors at Ottarshall. Bersi says they went to four doors like they knew which. Maybe they had marks to go by.”

The murmur came again, louder.

The Ykrul looked round at them. He did not seem to have followed most of it. He had followed the tone, and the faces, and Riarhin saw him set his weight back very slightly where he sat, the way a man does who has decided not to get up.

“Nobody here has seen your mark,” said Jorunn, slowly and clearly, to him. “Where have you come from?”

He frowned. He looked at her mouth while she spoke, and then for a moment at nothing, and then answered. It came a beat late, and Riarhin saw the hall notice that, too. “Inland,” he said. “Four days. Five.”

“From where inland?”

“A fire.”

“Whose fire?”

He opened his mouth and closed it. He did not have the word, or he did not want to give it. The fox-fur man laughed shortly.

Riarhin had not meant to say anything. She had said things at the quay that she had not meant to say and had been sorry for six days. She sat in her corner with her mouth shut and watched the young man by the fire hold his empty bowl and look at the floor, and she watched him touch the wolf’s tooth at his throat, twice, with two fingers, and let it go.

She knew that. She knew it from the camps. She knew it from a man at another fire a long time ago who had touched the tooth at his throat in exactly that way, twice, when somebody at the fire had said something he did not intend to answer, and had then said something courteous about the weather.

“He’s not refusing you,” she said.

She said it in the northern speech, from her corner. The hall turned.

“That,” she said. She touched her own throat, twice. “It means he’s been asked something that isn’t his to tell. It’s somebody else’s. He’ll tell you if they give him leave. It isn’t rudeness. Among the riders it’s—” She did not have the northern for it. “It’s a courtesy.”

The fox-fur man looked at her. So did the rest of them. She saw them see her ears, and her face, and the corner she was sitting in.

Then the Ykrul spoke.

He did not speak in the northern speech. He spoke in his own, the speech of the riders, which Riarhin had not heard aloud since a road in the south more than a year ago, and it went through her like warm water.

“Who taught you that?” he said.

She looked at him. He was looking straight at her, across the fire, past all of them. His face was perfectly still.

“A man I rode with,” she said, in the same speech. Her own Ykrul was rusty and came out stiff, but it came. “Long ago.”

“Then he taught you wrong.” He did not raise his voice. “That is not what it means. Where I come from it means: I have heard you and I am thinking whether you are worth an answer. I am still thinking.” He looked at the fox-fur man and back at her. “You have just told them I am keeping a secret for somebody. Now they will want to know who.”

Riarhin felt her face go hot.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

“Don’t answer for me,” said the Ykrul. “I do not know you. I do not know what you have told them already. If they ask me something, let me answer it. If I answer it badly, that is my business.” He paused. “Please.”

The please was the worst of it.

She sat back in her corner. The hall was looking from her to him, not understanding a word, and she could feel the fox-fur man deciding that whatever had just passed between the two foreigners was not to be trusted either.

“What did he say?” said Jorunn.

“He says I was wrong,” said Riarhin, in the northern speech. “He says he’ll answer for himself.”

He did answer for himself, after that, slowly and badly and with long pauses, and Riarhin sat in her corner with her hands in her lap and did not help.

She found she was watching his hands.

They were a rider’s hands, broad and short-fingered, the skin of the palms thick and pale with old callus where the reins went, and the knuckles of the right one were scarred. He held the bone with the mark on it in the right one and turned it as he talked, over and over, the way a man turns a thing he has carried a long way. When Jorunn gave him more soup he took the bowl in both hands again, cupped, the thumbs hooked over the rim, and lifted it to drink.

She had seen that. She had seen it a thousand times. On a ship’s deck with a pot of beans. At a fire on the south road with the mules hobbled behind. In a ship’s forepeak with a sailor laughing too hard to swear, and his slow deep voice telling a story about a horse that had eaten a saddle. Both hands round the bowl. The thumbs over the rim. The bowl lifted, and the head bent to it, and the eyes looking over it at whoever was talking, politely, as if the talk were the more important thing.

She did not see the hall for a moment. She saw a granary yard in the dark, and a cart going out of a gate, and a man on the ground by the gatepost who had stopped breathing. She had gone back to him. She had knelt in the mud and put her hand on his neck and there had been nothing there, and she had left him, because the cart had Berent in it and Mara and could not wait, and she had got up and gone after it.

“Why are you looking at my hands?”

He had said it in Ykrul. Riarhin came back to the hall. He was looking at her over the bowl.

The men along the walls were watching too, not understanding, waiting.

She could have said I wasn’t. She could have said no reason. He would not have believed it, and she did not want to lie to him; she had already got enough wrong.

“You hold it like somebody I knew,” she said.

He looked at her over the rim. His face did not change.

“The man who taught you wrong,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Is he dead?”

“Yes.”

He lowered the bowl. He did not say anything for a moment.

“My grandmother’s brothers all held a bowl like this,” he said. “And my mother. And every person at every fire I have ever sat at. It is how you hold a bowl.” He set it down on the hearthstone. “I am sorry for your friend. I am not him.”

“I know.”

“Good.” He looked away, at the fire. “I don’t cook either,” he said, as if it followed.

Riarhin stared at him.

She did not know where he had got it, or whether he had got it from anywhere. It was the kind of thing that could have been said to anybody. She did not think it had been said to anybody. She thought he had looked at her face and seen something in it about a cook, and had decided, coldly and fairly, to take it away from her before she could put it on him.

She found she liked him for it. She did not want to.

She did what she ought to have done at the beginning.

“What’s your name?” she said, in Ykrul.

He looked back at her. Something in his face eased, very slightly.

“Temür,” he said.

“I’m Riarhin.”

“I know. The skipper’s sister told the man with the fox hood. They were talking about you in the snow when they brought me down. A woman from the forest shore with a sword who killed a queen.” He paused. “Did you?”

“Yes.”

“Hm.” He did not seem impressed. “Why have you come north?”

“I didn’t come. I was carried.” She looked at the bone in his hand. “Why have you?”

He turned it over once. He was quiet so long she thought he would not answer. Then he touched the wolf’s tooth at his throat, twice, and she saw him see her see it, and he very nearly smiled.

“An old woman sent me,” he said. “Borte. My grandmother’s sister. She sings bones. She dreamed something in the autumn, and she woke up and looked at me across the fire in a way she had never looked at me before, and the next morning she told me to go north and find where this mark is cut.” He held up the bone. “She would not tell me what she dreamed. She would not tell me why. She said, go and look, and come back and tell me what you saw, and then I will tell you. So I went.” He looked at the fire. “My horse died on the ice four days ago. I came the rest on foot.”

“She sent you without telling you why.”

“Yes.”

“And you went.”

“She is Borte,” said Temür, as if that were an answer.

It was late when the hall settled. The men from the other halls went home through the snow, most of them, still talking. The fox-fur man stayed till the last, by the door, watching Temür, and then he went too.

Temür stayed where he was, on the hearthstone by the fire, with his back against the stones and his bow-case beside him. Nobody had offered him a bench. Nobody had offered him anything since the soup. The crew of the Staðfast were lying down along the walls, and Jorunn’s people, and the lamps were going out one by one, and he sat by the fire alone, in his frosted coat, with his hands round his knees.

Orm got up.

Riarhin, in her corner behind the sailcloth, watched him do it. He got up from his place on the near side of the hearth, with his rope, and went round the fire, and sat down again on the far side, on the end of the bench nearest the hearthstone, so that there was room on the bench beside him where he had been. He did not say anything. He jerked his head at the empty place.

Temür looked at it. Then at Orm.

“There’s a blanket on it,” said Orm, in the northern speech, slowly. “Sit on the bench. The stone’s cold.”

Temür looked at him a moment longer. Then he got up and came round and sat down on the bench beside Orm, and pulled the blanket over his knees.

“That’s Jorunn’s bench,” said a voice from along the wall, out of the dark. It was Hallbjorn. “She’ll want to know who said he could.”

“I said,” said Orm.

“You don’t know who he is.”

“No.”

“You don’t know who he came for.”

“No,” said Orm. “I know he walked five days. Go to sleep.”

“Orm—”

“If he cuts my throat in the night, Hallbjorn, you can tell Jorunn you said so.” Orm took up his rope again, and began, at last, to splice it. “Go to sleep.”

There was a silence along the wall. Then Hallbjorn said something under his breath that Riarhin did not catch, and turned over, and was quiet.

Orm spliced. Beside him, Temür sat with the blanket over his knees and looked at the fire.

After a while, without looking up from the rope, Orm spoke again, not to Hallbjorn, not to Temür. To the corner.

“What did he say?” he said. “At the start. When he spoke to you in his own talk. Before he got angry.”

Riarhin, behind the sailcloth, sat very still. It was the first thing he had said to her in six days.

“He asked who taught me,” she said.

Orm spliced.

“And who did?” he said.

She told him. She told him in the northern speech, slowly, in the dark, across the hall: a man she had ridden with, a long time ago, who had died. She did not tell him more than that. Orm did not ask for more. He nodded once, over his rope, as though he had heard it, and went on splicing, and after a while Temür, beside him, leaned his head back against the wall and closed his eyes, and the fire burned down.

Chapter 5 — The woman in the net

They brought her down the strand at noon two days later, in a herring net, on a sledge, with half the town walking behind.

Riarhin was on the quay when they came, on her bench, with Asta’s permission and a heap of net floats to sort. She heard them before she saw them. It was lower than the noise the dogs had made for Temür, and it moved, and there were voices in it that she did not like the sound of. Then the crowd came round the end of the boat-sheds onto the shingle, and in the middle of it six men were dragging a sledge, and on the sledge there was something wrapped in brown netting, tied and tied again with line, that moved.

“Dogman,” somebody shouted, from the top of the shingle, and the word went along the crowd.

They had found her in the dunes north of the town, Eyvind said, coming down the quay at a run to tell her. Two of the Ottarshall men and three from Hvalgard had been out after hares and found tracks, and followed the tracks, and found her lying in a hollow in the marram grass with snow over her, half dead, and she had got up and tried to run, and they had thrown a herring net over her the way you would over a seal.

“She bit Hauk,” said Eyvind. His eyes were very wide. “Through his glove.”

Riarhin got up.

She did not get up fast. She could not. She got her good hand on the post behind her and her good leg under her and stood, and found her balance, and went along the quay toward the strand with her left hand on the rail of the ramp and the stitched leg stiff and slow. By the time she reached the foot of the ramp the sledge had stopped on the shingle below Gudleif’s hall and the crowd was all round it.

She could not see over them. She could hear. Somebody was shouting. Somebody else was shouting back. In the middle of it all there was a sound she knew, which was the sound of a boot going into something soft, and a grunt.

She went down onto the shingle.

People got out of her way. Some of them because they saw her coming and knew who she was, the forest woman off the Staðfast, the one Orm had carried. Some of them because she had Remorse on her back, the hilt over her shoulder, and she had not wrapped it, and she did not move as if she would mind using her elbows. She came through to the front of the crowd and stood at the edge of the sledge and looked down.

The net had been pulled half off. Under it a woman was lying on her side on the boards of the sledge with her knees drawn up and her arms tied behind her with line, and she was looking up at the faces round her.

Riarhin looked at her.

The woman lay slack against the boards with her knees drawn up. Her eyes were dark and swollen. Between the strands of net Riarhin could see her hands: cracked knuckles, broken nails, two fingers on the left blackened at the tips with cold. She was wearing furs, a great many layers of them, filthy and torn and stiff with old frost. She was very thin. Under the furs Riarhin could see the shape of her collarbone like a bar.

Then she saw the face.

“Look at it,” said somebody beside her, in a low voice. “Look at the mouth on it. Like the carving. Like the one over the door of the Old Hall.”

Riarhin had seen that carving. Eyvind had shown it to her on her first day ashore, over the great door of the oldest hall in Hvalgard, black with age and smoke: a head, snarling, with a long muzzle full of teeth and pricked ears and a tongue lolling. That’s them, Eyvind had said, that’s what they look like, and Asta, behind him, had said, That’s what a man with a chisel who’d never seen one thought they looked like, three hundred years ago.

The woman on the sledge did not look like the carving.

Her face was long and broad at once, with a heavy jaw that came forward under the mouth further than a human jaw would have come, so that the lower lip and the line of the teeth stood out a little beyond the upper. That was all. That was the muzzle. Her nose was broad and flat and her cheekbones were high and wide and her skin was the grey-brown of wet slate. Her ears lay flat against her head, long, ragged at the edges with old frost-damage, under hair that had been black and was matted grey with dirt. From the corner of each ear down along the jawline to the point of the chin there were lines tattooed, blue-black, close together, like the grain in wood. Her lips were cracked and bleeding. Between them, when she drew breath, Riarhin saw her teeth, and the teeth had been filed. Every one of them, to a point.

Her eyes were yellow-brown. They were looking up at the crowd, at each face in turn, with no expression at all. It was the look Riarhin had seen on prisoners in the camps who had decided that nothing they said would make any difference and were waiting to find out how much it would hurt.

Somebody kicked the sledge.

The woman flinched. She did not make a sound.

“Get her up,” said a man at the front. He had his hand wrapped in a rag that was bloody through. Hauk, Riarhin thought. The one she had bitten. “Get her up. Make her talk.”

“She can’t talk,” said somebody else. “They can’t. They bark.”

“She talked in the dunes. I heard her.”

“Make her say where they took them.”

“Make her say where Svala is.”

The crowd pushed in. The sledge rocked. Hauk bent down and took hold of the woman by the hair and hauled her head up off the boards.

Her eyes went to Riarhin.

They did not ask for anything. They looked at Riarhin, at her face and her ears, the way the eyes of the crowd had looked at her every day since the Staðfast came in, as if she were the one thing on the shingle that did not belong to it. Then they moved on.

“Hauk,” said Riarhin.

She did not say it loudly. Hauk looked up.

“If you hurt her, she’ll tell you whatever you want,” said Riarhin. Her northern was slow and she made it slower. “She’ll tell you anything. And you won’t know which of it is true.” She looked down at the woman. “And then she’ll die, from the look of her. Today, perhaps. And you’ll know nothing.”

Hauk looked at her.

“Who are you to say?” he said.

“Nobody.” She looked at him. “I’ve seen it done.”

He did not let go of the hair. But he did not pull either.

There was a push in the crowd behind her, and Temür came through it.

He came the way she had come, without hurrying, and people got out of his way faster than they had got out of hers. He did not look at Hauk or at the crowd. He came to the side of the sledge and stood and looked down at the woman in the net, and something happened in his face, briefly, that Riarhin could not read.

Then he crouched, and spoke.

Riarhin did not know it; it was neither Ykrul nor the northern speech. It was low and rough and full of sounds made at the back of the throat, and it was slow, and he said it carefully, as a man speaks a language he has only ever heard old people speak.

The woman on the sledge went very still.

Her eyes came to Temür’s face. She looked at him a long time, the braids, the wolf’s tooth, the rider’s coat. Hauk’s hand was still in her hair and she seemed to have forgotten it.

She lifted her face toward Temür, as far as the hand allowed, and her tongue moved behind her filed teeth, and she spoke.

It hurt her. Riarhin saw that at once. Every sound came out thick and broken, as if her mouth were full of something, and some of the sounds did not come out at all; she would stop, and touch her tongue to her teeth, and try again, and the second try would be worse. Her face twisted with it. She said perhaps a dozen words, and they took a long time, and when she had finished she let her head drop back and shut her eyes.

The crowd had gone quiet.

“What did she say?” said Hauk.

Temür looked at him, and then, briefly, at Riarhin. Then he said to her in Ykrul, very low: “I have perhaps half of it. It is the old speech. My grandmother’s grandmother’s. Some of the words are the same.”

“Tell me the half.”

“She says she is behind,” said Temür. “Something is behind her. Following. Or she is behind something. Late.” He frowned. “She said a word I think is ice. And a word I know: four.”

Riarhin told the crowd. She told it exactly as he had said it, with the half missing.

“Something following,” said Hauk. “More of them.”

“Bródd,” said a woman at the back of the crowd. “Get Bródd. The cooper’s Bródd. His grandmother had that talk.”

They sent for Bródd. It took some time. He was at the cooper’s at the other end of the town, and when he came he came slowly, as if he did not want to, and the crowd parted for him in a different way from the way it had parted for Riarhin and for Temür. People looked at him as he came through and then looked at the woman on the sledge and then looked at him again.

He was a big quiet man of thirty or so, in a leather apron, with a cooper’s adze still in his belt. His hair was black and his skin was a little darker than the people round him, and his jaw was heavier. Not much. Enough that Riarhin, who had just been looking at the woman on the sledge, saw it at once, and saw the crowd see it.

He stood at the edge of the sledge and did not crouch.

“Ask her again,” said Hauk.

Bródd spoke. It was the same rough throat-speech Temür had used, but different: smoother, quicker, the sounds worn down, like a stone that has been carried in a pocket. The woman on the sledge opened her eyes and looked at him. Riarhin saw her look at his jaw.

She answered. It was the same dozen words. It hurt her as much the second time.

Bródd listened with his head bent. When she had done he was quiet for a moment. Then he straightened and looked round at the crowd.

“She says they are late,” he said. “She says—the ice is waiting. She says, they are late, and the ice is waiting. She said a number. Four.” He paused. “I think she says she is sorry.”

The crowd murmured.

“That isn’t what he said,” said Hauk, pointing at Temür. “He said something was behind her.”

“It’s the old talk,” said Bródd. “It’s hard. It can mean either.” He looked at Temür, and Temür looked back at him, and something went between them that Riarhin could not follow. “I think it is late. And I think she is sorry.”

Temür said, in Ykrul, very quietly, not to the crowd: “She did not say she was sorry.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“There was no word like sorry,” said Temür. “There was a word like owed. I am almost sure.”

She did not tell the crowd. She looked at the woman on the sledge, whose eyes were closed again, and at Bródd, who was looking at the ground.

“She’ll freeze,” said a voice behind them. “Or Hauk’ll kill her, or the Ottarshall men will. Somebody has to keep her till the Thing.”

It was Gudleif. He had come down from his hall in his yellow bear-fur and was standing at the edge of the crowd. Behind him was Jorunn.

“The Thing’s not called,” said Hauk.

“It will be. Ingrith’s sent to Kettlebeach. Ranveig’s gone to the stone.” Gudleif looked at the sledge. “Ingrith will want to hear what she has to say. Ingrith will want her alive to say it. Whose house is she going in?”

Nobody answered. Nobody wanted her in their house.

Riarhin heard herself say, “Mine.”

They turned. She stood at the edge of the sledge with her hand on Remorse’s strap and felt them all look at her.

“You haven’t got a house,” said Jorunn. “You’ve got the end of my bench.”

“Then the end of your bench.” Riarhin looked at Gudleif. “I’ll keep her. I’ll keep her alive. I’ll bring her to the Thing when it’s called. If she gets away I’ll answer for it at the stone.” She thought of the phrase Sigrun had used across the water. “On my name.”

There was a silence.

“That’s a thing to say,” said Gudleif. “Do you know what it means?”

“I think so.”

“It means if she runs, and kills somebody on the way, it’s yours.”

“Yes.”

Gudleif looked at her a long time. Then he looked round the crowd.

“You heard her,” he said. “Jorunn. Hauk. All of you. She said it, on her name. Gudleif heard it.”

“Jorunn heard it,” said Jorunn, very dry.

Hauk let go of the woman’s hair.

It took four of them to carry her up the slope to Jorunn’s, in the net, because she would not walk and they would not untie her. Jorunn put her in the back of the hall, by the wall, on a heap of old sailcloth behind the hanging that had been Riarhin’s, and stood with her arms folded and looked at her.

“You’re a fool,” she said to Riarhin.

“Yes.”

“She stinks.”

“Yes.”

“Fine,” said Jorunn, and went and got a bucket of warm water.

Riarhin knelt by the woman on the sailcloth. It hurt the leg; she did it anyway. She drew Toothpick from her belt, and the woman on the sailcloth saw it and went rigid, and Riarhin held it up so that she could see it, and then cut the line round her wrists, slowly, and put the knife away.

The woman did not move. She lay with her freed hands on the sailcloth in front of her and looked at them.

Asta brought a cup of warm water with honey in it. Riarhin took it and held it out.

The woman looked at the cup. She looked at Riarhin. She did not take it.

Riarhin drank a mouthful from it herself, and held it out again.

The woman took it in both her cracked black-tipped hands and drank, slowly, holding the water in her mouth before she swallowed, and the water ran down her chin over the blue lines of the tattooing. When she had finished she held the cup against her chest.

Then she lifted one hand and touched her jaw. She touched it just under the ear, lightly, with two fingertips, and ran them along to the chin, the way a person feels for a bruise. Then she spoke.

It came out thick and slow, in the northern speech, which Riarhin had not known she had. Two words. She said them very carefully, the way Riarhin herself said things in the northern speech, as if each had to be got exactly right.

“Sifa,” she said. She touched her breast. “Sifa.”

“Riarhin,” said Riarhin.

The woman looked at her. Then, after a moment, quite clearly, despite everything wrong with her mouth, she said something else in the northern speech.

“Your water,” she said, “tastes of fish.”

Asta laughed out loud.

There was a man by the door.

Riarhin did not see him come in. She saw him when she turned round, with the empty cup, to go back to the hearth: a young man standing just inside the door with the cold coming in round him, in a brown coat with one black sleeve, his burned hand wrapped in a rag. Bersi. He was looking past her at the woman on the sailcloth.

He had been looking, she thought, for some time.

Riarhin went to the hearth and filled the cup again from the pot and turned and held it out to him. He looked cold. He looked as if he had walked up from the strand behind the men carrying the net and had not stopped walking until he was here.

He looked at the cup.

He looked at it a long time. Then he looked at her face, and at the cup again. It was the same cup. The woman had held it to her breast.

He did not take it.

He did not say anything either. He looked once more at the back of the hall, at the woman on the sailcloth with her black-tipped hands round her knees, and then he turned and went out of the door into the snow, and Jorunn, after a moment, got up and went and shut it after him.

Chapter 6 — At the stone

The thing-stone at Kettlebeach stood on a holm, a low flat island of turf and rock a stone’s throw off the shore, and you reached it by a causeway of flat stones that the tide covered twice a day. When the tide was out the causeway lay bare and black and slippery with weed, and the people of the coast walked out along it in a long line, and stood on the holm in a ring round the stone, and spoke.
