The stone had not heard anybody speak for two winters. Riarhin learned that on the boat going over. Eyvind told her, and then Asta corrected him, and then Orm, at the steering oar, said it was nearer three, and Eyvind and Asta both told him to be quiet. Nobody had called a Thing at Kettlebeach since the year the eel-weirs failed, Eyvind said, because there had been nothing that all the halls agreed was worth agreeing about, and because calling it was a thing you did not do lightly. Whoever called a Thing at Kettlebeach first in a bad year set the shape of everything after.

“Ingrith called it,” said Riarhin.

“Ingrith and Ranveig,” said Asta. “Between them. They don’t agree about anything else.”

It was a grey still day with snow in the air that did not fall. They walked out to the holm at low water. Riarhin walked it. She had told Asta she would, and Asta had looked at her and at the causeway and said slowly, and she had gone slowly, with a staff in her good hand and Temür on one side of her and Sifa on the other.

Sifa was not tied. That had been argued for most of a day in Jorunn’s hall, and Riarhin had won it by being the one who would answer at the stone, and by pointing out that a woman with two frost-blacked fingers and no strength in her legs who had not tried to leave Jorunn’s hall in six days was not likely to try to swim the sound in winter. Sifa walked in a borrowed coat with her hands in its sleeves and her head down, and did not look at the people on the causeway ahead and behind. They looked at her.

There were perhaps two hundred people on the holm. They stood in a rough ring round the stone, in their winter coats, with their breath going up. The stone was not large. It was a grey standing slab as high as a man’s chest, leaning a little, with a flat top worn smooth, and round its foot the turf had been trodden to mud and frozen hard. There were marks cut into the face of it, very old, worn almost away.

Ranveig stood beside it. She had her harp out of its sealskin case and held it against her hip but she did not play. Ingrith stood on the other side, in her blue coat with the scorched hem, with her braids tied in black. Around them, in the front of the ring, were the people Riarhin had begun to learn: Gudleif in his yellow furs, the jarls and hall-holders of the other bays, whom she did not know, the men from Ottarshall.

Ranveig spoke first.

Riarhin did not understand all of it. It was the old form, Asta said, and the old form used old words. She understood that Ranveig named the stone, and the holm, and the bays that sent to it, one after another, in a long list, and at each name somebody in the ring answered, here, or a name, or once, from a hall that had not sent anybody, nothing. She understood that Ranveig named the thing that had happened at Ottarshall. She said nineteen, and Ingrith, across the stone, said it after her. She said four, and said their names. Gunnvor. Kári. Ulf. Svala. Then she was silent, and the ring was silent with her, a long time, and the snow that did not fall hung in the air.

Then Ingrith spoke.

She did not use the old form. She spoke plainly, and Riarhin understood almost all of it, because Ingrith said everything twice.

Ottarshall had burned. Nineteen dead, four taken. A hall that let that stand was no hall; a coast that let a hall burn was no coast. Her trackers had followed the Dogmen north across the high ground until snow covered their trail at the first moor. She wanted ships and men from every bay, sleds and dogs, before the dark of the year. Burn their dens. Bring back the four. If the four were dead, bring back what was owed for them.

There was a noise from the ring. It was deeper than a cheer, and it went round the stone like wind through a stand of trees.

Riarhin looked at the people in it. She looked at their faces. Some were angry. A good many were frightened. They were the same faces, often, and the fright did not make them less angry; it made them louder. She remembered what Eyvind had said about the ninth horn. You blow it fast. Before you can think.

Then a man stepped out of the ring.

It was Hauk. He had his bitten hand still wrapped in a rag. He walked to the stone and stood in front of it and took something out of his coat and laid it on the flat worn top.

It was a ring. A bronze ring, broad, thick, green with age, the size of a woman’s bracelet. It lay on the stone and the ring of people looked at it, and the noise went out of them.

“My brother died in the hall,” said Hauk. “Eyolf. He was in the hall that night. He was nineteen of the nineteen.” He looked round. “I want the Dogman woman given to Ottarshall. Now. Here. For Eyolf.”

People turned and looked at Sifa. Riarhin felt Sifa go still beside her.

But not all of them. Riarhin saw it, and did not at first understand what she saw. Across the ring, on the far side of the stone, among the people of Kettlebeach, a group of men and women had turned to look not at Sifa but at the ring on the stone. An old man among them had gone white.

“That’s Sæmund’s ring,” somebody said.

“It’s Grim’s ring,” said Hauk. “It was my grandfather’s. Grim Hauksson’s. Sæmund of Kettlebeach paid it to him for the Seal’s Daughter that went down off the skerries with Grim’s two sons in her, because Sæmund’s man was at the steering oar. Paid it, and said it was paid in full.” He did not look at the old man. “It wasn’t. My grandfather said it wasn’t. My father said it wasn’t. Now I say it.”

The old man across the stone said something sharp. A woman beside him took his arm.

“What has the Seal’s Daughter to do with this?” said Ingrith.

“Nothing,” said Hauk. “Everything. You want ships, Ingrith. You want men from every bay. You want Kettlebeach’s men in the boats beside Ottarshall’s.” He put his hand flat beside the ring. “I won’t row beside Sæmund’s sons while this is on the stone. Nor will my cousins. Settle it first. Give me the Dogman woman for Eyolf, and give me what’s owed on the ring, and then I’ll go north with anybody.”

The ring of people broke into noise.

It went on for a long time. Riarhin followed perhaps a third of it. She followed enough to understand that the Seal’s Daughter had gone down forty years ago, and that half the people on the holm thought Grim Hauksson had been paid fairly and the other half did not, and that there were three other matters of the same kind between three other pairs of halls that came out one after another as soon as the first was on the stone, like eels out of a pot when the lid comes off. She understood that Ingrith had wanted a war-band and was getting a quarrel. She watched Ingrith stand by the stone with her hands at her sides and her face like a closed door and wait.

Ranveig spoke in the end. She did not speak loudly. She struck the harp once, a single low note, and the ring went quiet to hear what came after, and she spoke into the quiet.

“The ring is on the stone,” she said. “It stays on the stone. Ranveig has seen it. When the ice is off the sound in the spring, Sæmund’s house and Grim’s house will come here at low water and say it again, and the stone will hear it.” She looked at Hauk. “Not now.”

“And the Dogman woman?” said Hauk.

Ranveig looked across the ring at Riarhin.

“She’s in somebody’s keeping,” Ranveig said. “On a name. Let the name speak.”

Riarhin walked out to the stone.

She went slowly, with the staff, because there was no other way to go. Sifa came with her. Riarhin had not asked her to. She had walked out of the ring, and Sifa had walked beside her, as she had walked beside her on the causeway, with her hands in her sleeves.

They stood by the stone. The bronze ring lay on top of it between them and Hauk.

“I said I’d keep her,” said Riarhin, in the northern speech, slowly. “And bring her to the Thing. I’ve kept her and brought her.”

“And now give her to Ottarshall,” said Hauk.

“That isn’t mine to say.” She looked at Ingrith. “It’s yours. All of you. I said I’d bring her. Not what you do with her.”

“Then say what she did,” said Hauk. “You’ve had her six days. You talk to her. Did she kill Eyolf?”

Riarhin looked at him.

She could have lied. She did not know what Sifa had done. She knew Sifa had come down out of the north, behind the raid, starving, and lain down in the dunes three miles from Hvalgard, and that she had said four, and something like owed, and that she had cut-down fingers and filed teeth and had not once in six days asked for anything except, on the third evening, a second blanket. She did not know where Sifa had been on the night Ottarshall burned.

“I don’t know,” she said.

There was a murmur.

“You don’t know,” said Hauk.

“I can’t promise you she killed nobody. I wasn’t there.” Riarhin looked at the ring of faces. “I don’t know what she did. Neither do you. That’s why I wanted her alive.”

Hauk turned to Sifa.

“Did you?” he said.

Bródd was in the ring. Riarhin saw him come forward, slowly, and stand a little behind Hauk where Sifa could see him, and say the question again in the old speech. Temür had come forward too. He stood on the other side.

Sifa looked at Hauk. She lifted one hand from her sleeve and touched her jaw, under the ear, and along. Then she spoke. It was short, and it hurt her, and Riarhin saw Temür’s face while she said it and Bródd’s face, and they were not the same.

“She says,” said Bródd, and stopped. He looked at the ground. “She says she was on the hill. Behind. With the sleds. She says she did not go down to the hall.” He paused. “She says she is sorry for your brother.”

Temür said nothing. Riarhin looked at him. He shook his head, very slightly, once.

“Ask her the rest,” said Ingrith, across the stone. “Ask her if they’ll come again.”

Bródd asked it. Sifa answered. This time it was longer.

“She says she doesn’t know,” said Bródd. “She says—it depends. If they have paid. If the ice is—” He searched. “Satisfied. She says she can’t promise Ottarshall anything. She says nobody can.”

“Then what good is she?” said Hauk.

Nobody answered him.

Ingrith looked at Sifa for a long time across the stone. Her face did not change.

“She stays in the keeping she’s in,” said Ingrith at last. “On the name she’s on. Ottarshall keeps its claim against her. I don’t give it up, Hauk. I don’t give it up for Eyolf and I don’t give it up for any of the nineteen. When we know what she did, we’ll know what’s owed.” She looked at Riarhin. “You keep her. If she runs, you answer.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

What the Thing agreed, in the end, after the light had begun to go and the tide had begun to turn and the people of the coast had begun to look over their shoulders at the causeway, was not what Ingrith had asked for.

There would be no war-band yet. There could not be, with Grim’s ring on the stone and three other quarrels beside it. What there would be instead was a search. A small one, Ranveig said, speaking for the stone: enough people to go north into the hills along the trackers’ line and look for the four, and find out who had taken them, and why, and whether more would come. A handful of people, with sleds and food from the halls, under the stone’s word. They would go at the turn of winter, as soon as the people going could walk. They would come back before the thaw, and say what they had found, at this stone, before the halls decided whether to muster.

“And if they don’t come back?” said somebody.

“Then the halls will have learned something,” said Ranveig.

Nobody laughed.

“Who goes?” said Ingrith.

The ring was silent.

It went on being silent. Riarhin stood by the stone with Sifa beside her and looked round at the faces, and saw them looking at each other, and at the ground, and at the causeway. Nobody wanted to be the first. She did not blame them. Ingrith had asked for ships and men from every bay and been given a handful to send into the north in the dark after something that had killed nineteen people in a night, and the handful did not yet have a name.

She looked across the ring for Orm.

She did not know why she looked for him. She found him at the edge of the crowd with Sigrun and Hallbjorn and Eyvind, near the causeway end of the holm, and he was looking at the stone.

She saw his hands.

He had them at his sides. He had taken his gloves off, she did not know when, and he was holding them in his left hand, and his right hand was hanging open by his thigh, and it was shaking. She could see it from where she stood. It was shaking the way it had on the shingle on the forest shore, a steady fine tremor from the wrist, and he was not looking at it. He was looking at the stone.

Then he put his gloves in his belt, and walked out of the ring, and came to the stone, and put his shaking hand flat on top of it beside Grim’s ring, so that it was still.

“Orm Ketilsson,” he said. “Of the Staðfast. I’ll go.”

Behind him, at the edge of the ring, Sigrun closed her eyes.

“I said it,” said Orm. He did not look round. “On the stone. You all heard. I’m going north for the four, and I’ll come back and say what I found.”

The ring made a sound. Riarhin did not watch them. She watched Orm. He had his hand flat on the stone and his face was grey under the red beard, and he was breathing in short shallow breaths through his nose, and he did not take his hand away.

He looked at her, across the top of the stone.

He did not say anything. She did not either. She thought of the deck, and Ranveig in her boat, and you were afraid, and she kept her mouth shut, and he saw her keep it shut, and something in his face changed a very little.

“I’ll go,” said Riarhin. “When Asta says I can walk.”

“You’re keeping her,” said Hauk, and pointed at Sifa.

“Then she comes too.”

There was a silence.

“She knows where they went,” said Riarhin. “Doesn’t she?”

They looked at Sifa. Sifa looked at the stone. She did not touch her jaw. She did not say anything at all.

Temür stepped forward then. He did not put his hand on the stone. He looked at it, and at the ring of Linn faces round it, and said in the northern speech, slowly, one word at a time, “I go. I look for the mark. Same road.”

Nobody objected. Nobody said anything. Riarhin thought that the ring had decided, without discussing it, that if an Ykrul with no horse wanted to walk into the north after the Dogmen, it was his own business.

“And who talks to them?” said Ingrith. “When you find them. Who talks?”

The ring turned, the whole of it, slowly, and looked at Bródd.

He was standing where he had stood behind Hauk, in his leather apron, with his adze in his belt. He looked back at them. Riarhin, by the stone, watched him look, and saw that nobody in the ring had any doubt at all about what he would say, and that he could see that, and that it made no difference.

“I’ll go,” said Bródd. “I’ll talk to them.”

Nobody asked him why. Nobody asked him what he wanted. Ranveig, by the stone, struck her harp once, a low single note, and said the names into the dusk: Orm Ketilsson. The forest woman. The Ykrul. The cooper’s Bródd. The woman in keeping. Then she put the harp back in its sealskin case, and Ingrith came round the stone to Orm and put something folded into his hands, heavy blue wool, and went back to her place without a word, and the people of the coast went back along the causeway in a long line, in the half-dark, with the tide coming in round the stones behind them.

Chapter 7 — The cargo called grain

The Temple ship came in on the morning the wreck was being argued over, and for half a day nobody could decide which of the two things was more important.

The wreck had come ashore on the skerries north of the harbour mouth four days after the Thing. It was one of the relief boats, the Hjalti, that had been scattered by the squall on the way to Ottarshall and never heard of again. Her people had not come ashore with her. What had come ashore was her bottom, stove in, and a good part of her starboard side, and a great tangle of her gear and her cargo, and the sea had spread all of it along the stones for a quarter of a mile.

Thorvald Hrafnsson’s eldest son had found it. He had gone out to the skerries at first light in the Kráka’s boat, and he had put a mark on every piece of it he could reach, and come back to Hvalgard and said so on the quay in front of witnesses. That was salvage. That was the old law. A thing the sea had thrown away belonged to the one who found it, if he named it and marked it before anybody else.

The Hjalti’s owner had been a man called Bárd. His widow was a woman called Halla, and she had four children and a mother-in-law and no boat now, and she stood on the quay with the youngest on her hip and said that the timber on the skerries was her husband’s boat and the sacks in the weed were her husband’s blankets and the meal he had been carrying north for Ottarshall, and that no son of Thorvald Hrafnsson’s was going to put his mark on her husband’s bones.

It had been going on since dawn when the Temple ship came round the point.

Riarhin was on the quay. She was on the quay most days now. Asta let her walk the length of it twice in the morning and twice in the afternoon, with the staff, and in between she sat on her bench and did whatever Sigrun found for her, which was mostly rope. She saw the ship before most people did, because she was looking at the harbour mouth while everybody else was looking at Halla.

It was not a Linn ship. She saw that at once. It was broad and deep and heavy, built high at the stern with a little castle of painted timber, and it had two masts, which no Linn ship had, and it was coming in under oars with its sails furled. On the forward mast there was a long white banner, and on the banner, embroidered in gold, a candle.

“Temple,” said Eyvind, beside her. He sounded uncertain how he ought to feel about it. “That’s the Temple ship. The broadshore. Brother Halle’s. Gudleif said it might come before the ice.”

“What’s it carrying?”

“Barley,” said Eyvind. “They said barley.”

Brother Halle was a small, round, tired man in a grey robe and a sheepskin coat over it, and he came up the ramp from the broadshore’s boat onto the quay with his hands held out a little from his sides, as if he expected to be pushed back down it.

He was not pushed. He was looked at. He stood on the quay and looked back, at the crowd, at Gudleif in his bear-fur, at Halla with her child on her hip, at the long grey slope of the town going up behind them with its white roofs, and he said in the northern speech, which he spoke carefully and with a southern lilt to it, “I am so sorry about the weather.”

Riarhin, on her bench, looked up.

“The cold,” said Brother Halle. “It must be very hard, after what has happened. I am sorry.” He seemed to hear himself, and stopped, and coloured, and began again. “I have forty tons of barley in the ship. From Ecktoria. It is good barley, I saw it loaded myself. It is for you. For the coast. For Ottarshall.” He looked round. “I would like to unload it before the ice.”

There was a silence. Then Gudleif stepped forward and began to talk to him, and the crowd shifted, and the noise came back.

Riarhin watched. She watched Brother Halle, mostly, because she had not seen anybody quite like him before. She had seen the Temple’s people in the south, in the towns, priests and clerks, men who looked at you as if they were reckoning what you would cost. Brother Halle did not do that. He looked at people as if he were afraid of them and sorry for them at the same time and could not decide which feeling was more correct.

There was another man behind him. He had come up the ramp second and he was taller and thinner and younger and had a satchel on his shoulder and a wax tablet in his hand, and he did look at people as if he were reckoning what they would cost. Riarhin heard him called Brother Anselm.

It was Brother Anselm who understood about the wreck.

He understood it very quickly. He listened to Gudleif for a short while, and then he looked along the quay, at the berth where the broadshore would have to lie to unload, and at the berth next to it, where the Kráka lay, and at Halla and her children, and at Thorvald’s son, and Riarhin watched him put it together. Then he leaned to Brother Halle’s ear and said something.

Brother Halle looked troubled.

“Brother Anselm says,” he said, to Gudleif, “that if the Temple were given first place at the quay, for the unloading, we could perhaps help with the—” He gestured toward the skerries. “The wreck. The disagreement. The Temple could buy the salvage from the young man at a fair price, and give the meal and the blankets to the widow, and the timber, and then nobody would have to quarrel.” He looked at Halla. “It would be quick. Brother Anselm says.”

Gudleif looked at him. Then at Brother Anselm. Then at Thorvald’s son, who was suddenly very interested.

“And the Temple would have first place at the quay,” said Gudleif.

“For the unloading,” said Brother Anselm smoothly. “And afterwards. A ship of the Temple will be coming and going. It would be convenient.”

Halla said something short and hard.

Riarhin did not catch it all. She caught my husband, and buy, and something about the Temple and the sea that made several people in the crowd look away. Halla stood with her child on her hip and her face white and spoke to Brother Anselm without looking at him, and Brother Anselm smiled politely and wrote something on his tablet.

Brother Halle was looking at her.

They went out to the skerries at the next low water, a great many of them, in boats, because the question could not be settled on the quay. Riarhin went in Gudleif’s boat because Gudleif asked her.

“You were in the flotilla,” he said. “Asta says you saw the boats go out. Asta says you look at things.” He looked at her with his old pale eyes. “Look at this.”

The skerries were a long low spine of black rock running out from the shore, with weed on them and white water breaking along their seaward edge, and on the landward side, in a strip of shingle and rock pools, the wreck lay where the sea had put it.

It was a ruin. The Hjalti’s bottom lay on its side on the stones with its ribs showing through the burst planks. Around it, scattered along the shingle, were timbers and spars and a broken oar and coils of line and torn sacks spilling grey meal into the weed. Every piece of it that could be reached had a mark on it, a notch cut with a knife and smeared with red ochre: Thorvald’s son’s marks.

Halla walked among them. She walked from piece to piece with her child given to someone else to hold and her hands at her sides, and at each piece she stopped and looked and said, that’s his, and walked on.

“Is it?” said Gudleif, quietly, to Riarhin.

Riarhin was on the stones. She had got out of the boat with Orm’s hand under her good arm; it was the first time he had touched her since the deck. She stood with the staff and looked at the wreck.

She had seen the boats go out. She had seen all five of them, from under the hood of the sheltered boat, strung out along the coast in a line, for half a day, before the squall. She had not been trying to remember them. She remembered them anyway. She remembered everything; it was the way she was made, and she had stopped being surprised by it a long time ago.

She remembered the Hjalti. She had not known its name. She had known it as the fourth boat in the line, the one with the steersman in a red hood, the one whose sail had a patch in the lower corner shaped like a spoon, the one with an eye painted on its bow in white and black, on the starboard side only, because the paint on the port side had worn away.

She walked to the bow.

It was there. The bow had broken off from the rest and lay a little way up the shingle, on its side, with the stem-post sticking up. On the starboard planking, under the weed and the sand, there was a painted eye. White and black. She knelt, slowly, with the staff, and brushed the weed off it with her good hand, and looked at it.

“That’s from the fourth boat,” she said.

Gudleif came and looked.

“The Hjalti,” he said.

“I don’t know its name,” said Riarhin. “I know it was the fourth boat in the line when we went out. I know it had this eye on the bow, this side only. I know it had a patch in the sail like a spoon.” She looked along the shingle at a long heap of sodden brown cloth in the weed. “That might be the sail. If the patch is in it, it’s the same boat.”

They looked. The patch was in it.

Halla had come to stand behind them. She looked at the painted eye and her face did something and then stopped doing it.

“That’s Bárd’s,” she said. “That’s Bárd’s eye. He painted it. He painted it every spring.” She turned to Gudleif. “You heard her. She saw it. It’s his.”

Gudleif looked at Riarhin.

Riarhin looked at the eye. She looked at it for a long time. Then she looked at Halla.

“I saw the boat,” she said slowly. “I saw it go out with that eye on it. I don’t know whose it was.”

Halla stared at her.

“I didn’t see anybody paint it,” said Riarhin. “I didn’t see who owned it. I never heard its name. If you tell me it was your husband’s, I believe you.” She looked at Gudleif. “But I didn’t see it. You asked me what I saw.”

“Everybody knows it’s Bárd’s,” said Halla.

“Then everybody can say so,” said Riarhin. “Not me. I wasn’t here.”

Halla looked at her a moment longer. Then she said something very low and very quickly that Riarhin did not catch, and turned and walked back up the shingle toward the boats.

Thorvald’s son was smiling.

Gudleif was not. He looked at Riarhin for a long moment with his old pale eyes.

“That was hard on her,” he said.

“Yes.”

“It was true, though.” He looked down at the painted eye. “Halla’s husband’s brother will say it. And Bárd’s steersman’s mother, if she’s willing. And the man who sold him the paint, likely. They saw it. That takes a day. Two.” He sighed. “She needed it today.”

“I know,” said Riarhin.

She stood on the shingle with the staff and looked at the wreck, and she did know, and it did not help her at all.

In the end it was Brother Halle who settled the first part of it.

They came back from the skerries in the dusk, and the broadshore was lying out in the harbour at anchor with its forty tons of barley still in it, and Brother Anselm was waiting on the quay with his wax tablet. He had found the clerk of the quay and the two of them had been going over the berths. There was room, Brother Anselm said, for the broadshore to lie at the second quay and unload, if the Kráka moved along and the Ottarshall boat moved along and the berth at the end, the small one, where Halla’s family kept their other boat, the little one, was given over for the Temple’s lighters to come and go.

Halla’s family needed that berth. Riarhin knew that. Everybody knew it. Without the Hjalti they had one boat left, a small one, and they would need to bring the salvage in with it, piece by piece, if they ever got it, and they would need to bring it in to their own berth.

Brother Halle stood on the quay and listened to Brother Anselm read out the arrangement of the berths from his tablet. He listened all the way to the end. Then he looked at Halla, who was standing at the top of the ramp with her children round her and her face like stone.

“No,” said Brother Halle.

Brother Anselm stopped.

“Not that one,” said Brother Halle. He pointed, with a hand that was not quite steady, at the small berth at the end. “We will not use that one. That one is hers.” He looked at Gudleif. “We will unload at the far end. By the shingle. With the boats. It will take longer.”

“Brother,” said Anselm, “it will take three days longer. Perhaps four. The ice—”

“Then it will take four days longer,” said Brother Halle. He had gone red to the ears and he looked as if he would very much like to sit down. “I am sorry. I am sorry about the ice. Gudleif, would that be possible? With the boats?”

Gudleif looked at him.

“It’s possible,” he said slowly. “It’s cold work.”

“Then I will do some of it,” said Brother Halle, “if somebody will lend me a pair of gloves.”

Nobody laughed. Riarhin, on her bench, did not either. She watched Halla, at the top of the ramp. Halla was looking at the little round man in the grey robe with an expression Riarhin could not read, and after a while she turned and went up into the town with her children, and did not say anything to anybody.

They unloaded the barley for four days, from the broadshore’s boats onto the shingle, by hand, in the cold. Riarhin watched it from the quay. She watched Brother Halle carry sacks. He was not good at it. He fell down twice on the first day and once on the second and on the third day somebody gave him a pair of proper mittens and a sheepskin hat and after that he fell down less. The barley was good. Riarhin saw a sack split on the shingle on the second day and the grain that ran out of it was gold and dry and smelled of summer, and three children came and scooped it up off the stones in their hands before anybody could stop them, and nobody tried.

On the third day, in the afternoon, Brother Halle came and sat down on the end of her bench.

He did not ask. He sat down because he had been carrying sacks for six hours and there was a bench and somebody on the end of it who did not look as if she would mind, and he sat with his hands in their borrowed mittens on his knees and breathed.

“You are the woman from the forest shore,” he said, after a while.

“Yes.”

“They say you are going north. With the others. To look for the people who were taken.”

“When I can walk.”

He looked at the slope of the town above them. “I am afraid of them,” he said. He said it plainly, as he said everything. “The Dogmen. I have read about them. In the Temple’s library, at Thepyrgos, there is a book of the northern peoples with drawings. They are terrible. They worship a thing in the ice that eats children. I do not think I could go north. I think I would die of fear before I died of anything else.”

Riarhin looked at him. She thought of Sifa, in the back of Jorunn’s hall, with her black-tipped fingers round a cup, saying your water tastes of fish.

“Have you ever seen one?” she said.

Brother Halle looked surprised. “No,” he said. “Of course not. Have you?”

She did not answer that. He did not seem to notice.

“I would like to build a chapel here,” he said. “By the spring. A small one. Nothing grand. A place where people could come in out of the cold and be quiet, and a bell to tell the hours, and somebody to teach the children letters. The Bishop would like it too.” He looked along the shore. “There is a flat place, by the whale-bone gate. Beside the water. Brother Anselm has looked at it. It would be just right for the footing. Level, and dry, and near the boats.”

Riarhin looked where he was looking.

She knew the place. She had walked past it every day, on her way along the quay. It was a long level shelf of turf between the foot of the whale-bone post and the shingle, and it was not empty. There were posts set in it, rows of them, and between the posts on fine days the nets of Hvalgard hung to dry, and on bad days the women of the town sat in the lee of the gate-post among the folded nets with their needles and mended them. She had seen them there that morning. An old woman with a belt of knotted cords had been sitting there in the snow with a net across her knees, singing under her breath.

“That’s where they dry the nets,” she said.

Brother Halle looked.

“Is it?” he said. “Oh.” He looked a little longer. “I am sure they could dry them somewhere else.”

Then he got up, and thanked her for the bench, and went back down the shingle to carry sacks.

Chapter 8 — What she may carry

The herring stairs went up the cliff at the north end of the town in a long zigzag of wooden steps and landings, and on every landing there were racks, and on every rack, all winter, there were fish.

Riarhin climbed them for the first time eleven days after the Temple ship came in. It was Asta’s idea. Asta had looked at her thigh that morning, and pressed round the scar with her thumbs, and watched her walk the length of Jorunn’s hall and back without the staff, and said that the leg would bear stairs now if it was taught to, and that there was no better teacher than the herring stairs because there was no way down from them except the way you had come up.

“Old Hild wants hands,” Asta said. “Sitting hands. She asked me if I had any. I said I had one and a half.”

So Riarhin climbed. It took her the better part of an hour. She went up with the staff in her good hand, a step at a time, and rested on every landing among the racks, and the wind came off the sea and through the hanging fish and rattled them, a dry sound like bones. The fish were cod mostly, split and gutted and hung in pairs over the poles by their tails, dried hard and grey in the frost. On the lower landings they were old, from the autumn, and stiff as boards. Higher up they were new.

At the top there was a flat shelf of rock under an overhang, and a fire, and a great many women.

They were working. They sat on stools and upturned kegs round the fire with baskets of fresh fish at their feet and knives in their hands, and the knives went very fast. Riarhin stood at the top of the stair, out of breath, and watched them. A fish came out of a basket, and a knife went into it below the gills and along the belly and round, and the guts went into one bucket and the head into another and the split fish went onto a pile, and the next fish came out of the basket. The knives flickered in the firelight. Nobody looked at their hands.

And they were singing.

It was a counting song. Riarhin understood that at once, from the shape of it, though she did not understand the words. Each verse was short and ended on a number, and the whole circle of women came down on the number together, hard, like a mallet on a peg, and the knives came down with it. Then the next verse began.

An old woman by the fire looked up.

She was very old and very small and wrapped in so many shawls that she was nearly round, and round her waist, over the shawls, she wore a belt of plaited cord with knots tied in it at intervals, a great many knots. She had a net across her knees, not fish, and a netting needle in her hand. Riarhin had seen her before, in the snow by the whale-bone gate. She looked Riarhin up and down, from the staff to the sword hilt to the face, and pointed with the netting needle at an empty keg beside her.

“Sit,” she said.

Riarhin sat.

The old woman put a net in her lap. It was torn in a long ragged hole, and she put a netting needle in Riarhin’s good hand already threaded and showed her, once, how the knot went: under, round, through, and pull. Then she went back to her own net and did not watch.

Riarhin mended. She did it badly. She had tied a great many knots in her life, in rope and leather and linen and other things, but she had never mended a net and her right hand would not hold the mesh open properly and her left hand was not used to working the needle alone. She went slowly, and pulled each knot tight, and moved on.

“No,” said the old woman, without looking up.

Riarhin stopped.

“Too tight.” The old woman reached over and put a finger on the last knot. “You pull them like that, the mesh shrinks. You get a hole that lets the little fish through and a hole that won’t let the big fish in, side by side, and the net tears between them first time it’s full.” She took her finger away. “Even. All the same. You’re not tying up a thief.”

“Sorry.”

“Don’t be sorry. Be even.”

Riarhin went back and did it again, and the old woman watched her this time, for three knots, and grunted, and looked away.

The song went round. Riarhin counted the verses under her breath without meaning to, as she counted most things. One. Two. The women came down on each number with their knives. Three. Four. She found she was waiting for the number before it came, and her needle went under and round and through on the beat. Five. Six. Seven.

Eight.

The song stopped.

It did not stop the way a song ends. It stopped the way a cart stops at a gate, on the beat, with everybody still holding their knives up. There was a moment of silence round the fire. Then a woman on the far side laughed at something and reached into her basket for another fish, and the woman next to her began the first verse again, one, and the circle came in with her, and it went round once more.

Riarhin mended. She did not ask.

She felt the old woman beside her look at her, briefly, sidelong, from under the shawls. Then look back at her own net.

“You’re the one from the forest shore,” said the old woman, when the song had gone round a third time.

“Yes.”

“Hild,” said the old woman, and touched her own chest with the netting needle. “I tie the knots.” She touched the belt round her waist. “One for every bad weather I’ve come through. Twenty-nine. Thirty, after the squall.” She looked at Riarhin. “What was it like? Down there? The weather.”

Riarhin thought.

“Rain,” she said. “Off the sea. Sideways. All afternoon. I was on the shingle with nowhere to go.”

“How long?”

“A few hours.”

“Hm.” Hild looked at her a while. “Cold?”

“I don’t remember being cold. I remember not being able to stop shaking.”

“That’s cold.” Hild put the needle through a mesh. “That’s the cold that kills. When you stop noticing it.” She drew the knot even. “Did you tie a knot for it?”

“No.”

“You should.” Hild did not look up. “Otherwise how do you know you came through it?”

Riarhin looked at the net in her lap.

“I know,” she said.

“You know now,” said Hild. “You’ll forget.”

Riarhin did not think she would. She did not forget things. She did not say so. She thought of the rain on the shingle, the grey wall coming in off the sea, and the coat on its pole streaming, and she thought that perhaps there were people who did forget, and perhaps they tied knots so that they would not.

She mended the net. She mended it until the light went, and Hild looked at it, and pulled at two of the knots and said they would do, and pulled at a third and said it would not, and cut it out with a knife and made Riarhin do it again.

She went up the herring stairs every day after that.

The days were very short now. The sun came up late over the southern hills and went along the top of them for a few hours, low and red, and went down again, and the rest was dark and lamplight and snow. The fjord did not freeze; Gudleif said it never did, the sea came in too strong. But the edges of it froze, along the shingle and in the still places behind the quays, a skin of grey ice that the boats broke every morning going out.

Riarhin climbed the stairs in the short daylight and mended nets with Hild, and when Hild ran out of nets she split fish, slowly, with Toothpick because it was the knife she trusted, while the women sang and came down hard on their numbers and stopped at eight. She learned the song. She did not sing it. She learned to tell the verses apart and to know which of the women had sung it longest by the way they came down on the numbers, and she learned that the youngest girls sometimes came down a beat early, out of eagerness, and were laughed at.

Her leg healed. Her arm healed more slowly. Asta took the binding off it in the third week and looked at the long red line from shoulder to elbow and made her lift a full bucket with that hand, and Riarhin lifted it as high as her waist, shaking, and put it down.

“Again,” said Asta.

Riarhin did it again.

“You won’t swing a sword with that for a while,” said Asta.

“I don’t swing a sword with that.”

“I know which hand you use,” said Asta. “I’m telling you about the other one. You’ll want it.”

The letter came in the fifth week, on the last trader before the ice shut the outer sound.

It had come a long way. She could see that from the outside of it before she opened it: a square of good paper folded small and sealed with green wax that had no device, only a thumbprint, and then wrapped in oilcloth, and the oilcloth tied with string, and on the string a wooden tag with writing burned into it. The writing on the tag was in Vesper Hawthorn’s hand. Forwarded from Thornholt. Found waiting. Riarhin, care of the ship Staðfast, Hvalgard. Under it, in another hand, the name of the harbour where she had sent her own letters south, and a mark that meant it had been paid for.

The paper inside was in Coryd’s square letters.

She read it at the top of the herring stairs, by the fire, with the women singing round her. She read it once quickly, and then again slowly.

Coryd had written in late summer, before the Gate. The letter had waited all autumn on a shelf at Thornholt, where Riarhin had said she would be passing. Vesper had found it and sent it north.

It was a short letter. Coryd’s letters were always short.

There was news of Sivelle, and the house, and the stair that did not creak. There was a line about Harissa that made Riarhin smile and then stop smiling. And then there was the other thing.

Riarhin had known it was coming. Coryd had mentioned it in the spring, in two lines, at the end of a letter, as gossip: that Sivelle had cousins on the Mistlands coast, and that the cousins had written of strange things in a village up the coast from them, and that she would tell Riarhin about it when she came. She had not come. Now Coryd told her anyway.

There is a young woman in one of the villages north of Sivelle’s cousins, not a girl now, very nearly grown, who has been doing something with the dead. I don’t know what. The cousins don’t say it plainly, they write round it. They say people who were buried have been seen walking on the shore road at dusk, and that they walk toward her house. They say she has a book, old, that she found as a child in a ruin in the hills, and has been reading it for years, and that it is Lethai, very old, older than anything. I don’t know if that is true. People say things about books. They say the village priest has written to the bishop and the bishop has not answered.

They asked me whether my sister would come. I said I didn’t know where my sister was. I am asking you now. I don’t know what you could do. I don’t know what anybody could do. But you are the only person I know who goes towards things like this instead of away, and they are frightened, Riarhin, the cousins are frightened, I can hear it in how carefully they write.

Come when you can. I am still expecting you on the first.

And at the bottom, under her name, two lines from a song Bea used to sing, that only the three of them knew.

Riarhin sat by the fire with the letter on her knee.

She thought about the Mistlands, which she had never seen. Fog, and a cold sea, and a coast that the songs said was full of drowned things. A young woman who had found a book as a child and had been reading it ever since. People walking on the shore road at dusk toward a house. She did not know if any of it was true. Coryd did not know. The cousins did not know. Nobody who had written a word of it had seen it with their own eyes, and Riarhin had spent the last five weeks learning, on this coast, what happened to a thing when it had passed through enough mouths.

She looked down at the harbour, far below, grey under the short red light. The ice along the edges of it. The whale-bone gate. The Staðfast lying at her berth with her seam mended and her winter freight still unsigned.

She went down the stairs and found Sigrun.

“No,” said Sigrun.

She was in Jorunn’s hall, at the table, with a lamp and a slate and the freight lists. She did not look up.

“I didn’t ask yet,” said Riarhin.

“You’re going to ask if there’s a ship south. There isn’t. Not till the ice goes out of the sound. Three months. Four.” Sigrun made a mark on the slate. “The trader that brought your letter is the last one, and he’s gone already, he went this morning before the sound closes behind him. If you want to go south, you can walk. It’s about four hundred miles and there’s nobody living on most of them.”

Riarhin sat down across the table from her.

“When the ice goes,” she said.

Sigrun looked up then.

“When the ice goes,” she said, “I take the Staðfast down the coast with the spring freight. Every year. I go as far as the first harbour, where Asta told you about the letter-man, and some years further. There are ships from there going south and west. If you’re aboard, you’re aboard.” She looked at Riarhin with her pale eyes. “I don’t promise more than that. I don’t promise the ice goes out on time. I don’t promise you’re alive to be aboard. You’re going north with Orm.”

“Yes.”

“Then come back,” said Sigrun, “and ask me again.”

Riarhin wrote to Coryd that night, at the end of the women’s bench, with a pen she had borrowed from Brother Anselm and ink that Brother Halle had given her without being asked. She wrote that she was alive. She wrote that she was on the Hvalgard coast, among the Linn, and that she had been hurt and was mending. She did not write how. She wrote that she would come when the sea opened, and that it would not be before the spring. She asked Coryd to write to the cousins and ask them to write down what they had seen themselves, not what they had heard: who had seen the dead walking, and when, and where, and what the young woman’s house looked like and who lived near it, and whether there was somebody in the village who could be trusted to meet a stranger off a boat and not tell the whole village she had come. She wrote: Tell them I am coming. Tell them not to do anything until I get there. She did not know whether that was the right thing to write. She left it on the page.

She put the stupid lines at the bottom.

There was no trader to take it. Gudleif said he would keep it, with the other letters for the south, in a box in his hall, and give it to the first ship out in the spring.

Asta let her go a week later.

She did it in the morning, in front of Sigrun, in a few words, the way she did everything. She had looked at the leg and the arm and the rib and listened to Riarhin breathe and made her walk the length of the hall and back carrying a keg of water on her left shoulder, and when Riarhin put the keg down Asta looked at her and said, “You can go. Not far. Not fast. No climbing anything you could go round. If the leg opens, you come back.”

“If the leg opens,” said Sigrun, “she won’t be able to come back. She’ll be in the hills.”

“Then she’d better not open it,” said Asta.

Riarhin packed that evening. She packed on the end of the women’s bench, by lamplight, with her things laid out round her: the blankets Jorunn had given her, the felt-lined boots from Hild’s son, the mittens, the hood, the oilskin, the sleeping skin. The food that was hers to carry, in bags. The flint and steel. Farieth’s chisel, wrapped in a rag. The water skin. A spare shirt. Two spare pairs of stockings. A needle and thread. A small pot. Rope. A coil of line. Asta’s salve for the leg in a horn box. A second horn box of something Asta said was for the cough she was going to get.

She packed it all into the frame Hallbjorn had made her, and put the frame on her back, and stood up, and her leg told her at once that it was too much.

She took it off and unpacked it and laid it out again.

The second pair of stockings went. The small pot went; Orm had a pot. The spare shirt went. The coil of line went; there would be line on the sleds. She looked at Farieth’s chisel for a long time in its rag and then put it back in, at the bottom, where it weighed least and she would not see it.

Remorse she would carry on her back, as she always did. Toothpick and the Last Blade she would carry at her belt, as she always did. She did not take them out and lay them on the bench with the rest. She did not think of it.

She packed the frame again, and stood up with it on, and the leg did not object, quite.

Sigrun came along the hall in the dusk and stood and looked at her.

“Orm goes,” said Sigrun. “Orm said it on the stone and Orm goes. Hallbjorn stays with the ship, because if Hallbjorn goes north there’s nobody to stop Thorvald Hrafnsson putting his hand on my winter freight while I’m looking the other way. Asta stays, because she’s too old to walk in the hills and she knows it and so does everybody, and because Ingrith’s burned need her more than you do.” She paused. “Eyvind stays.”

From the end of the hall, by the hearth, there was a sound.

Riarhin turned. Eyvind was sitting on the hearthstone with a sled-harness across his knees that he had been oiling, and his face had gone red and then white.

“I’m going,” he said.

“You’re not,” said Sigrun.

“Orm’s going. She’s going. I saw her first. I saw her on the slope, I told Hallbjorn, I—”

“You saw her first,” said Sigrun. “That’s yours. Nobody will ever take it from you. You’re still not going.” She did not raise her voice. “I need somebody on the quay who knows the ship. Who can go up the mast and say what’s coming into the sound. Who can run to Gudleif’s when Thorvald’s boys are where they shouldn’t be. Hallbjorn can’t climb the mast any more and won’t admit it. I need you here.” She looked at him. “That isn’t a kindness. It’s work.”

Eyvind looked at her. His jaw was shaking.

He got up, very fast, and threw the sled-harness down on the hearthstone, and went out of the door into the dark.

Sigrun looked after him for a moment. Then she went back to her slate.

He came back an hour later. Riarhin was sitting on the end of the bench with the packed frame on the floor beside her, rubbing salve into her thigh. He came in with snow on his shoulders and walked straight down the hall to her and did not look at Sigrun.

He crouched down by the frame.

“That’s wrong,” he said.

She looked at him.

“That.” He put his hand on the strap that went round the bottom of the frame, where she had tied it off. He was still angry; his hands were shaking with it. “You’ve tied it so it comes loose if you pull it from the top. You’ll be on a slope, and you’ll go to take the frame off, and you’ll pull the top strap, and the whole bottom’ll open and everything will go down the hill.” He undid her knot with two quick jerks. “Like this. Over, and back under, and through the loop. Then when you pull from the top it holds and when you pull this end it lets go.” He did it. He did it fast and hard and pulled it tight with a jerk that shook the whole frame. “There.”

He stood up.

“That’s how,” he said. “Don’t do it the other way.”

“I won’t.”

He looked at her a moment. His face was red and set and his eyes were wet, and he was fourteen, and furious, and he was not going north.

“You’d better not,” he said, and went to bed.

Part II — The grain and the road

Chapter 9 — The ruling that travels

They left Hvalgard on the shortest day of the year, in the dark, in Leif Torfason’s boat, with the whole quay come down to watch them go.

Riarhin had not expected that. She had expected Sigrun, and Asta, and Jorunn, perhaps, to see that Sifa was gone from the back of her hall. She had not expected the lanterns. There were thirty or forty of them, all along the quay and down the ramp onto the shingle, held up by people in heavy coats who stood in the snow and did not say much, and watched the five of them climb down into the boat with their frames and their bundles. Ranveig was there. Gudleif was there. Brother Halle was there, in his borrowed mittens, holding a lantern of his own. At the very end of the quay, apart from everybody, with his hands in his armpits and his face set, Eyvind was there.

“That’s for Orm,” said Asta, at Riarhin’s elbow, low. “He said it on the stone. They’ve come to see him go. So that it’s seen.”

“And if he doesn’t come back?”

“Then it was seen,” said Asta, and gave her the horn box of cough salve she had forgotten, and went back up the ramp.

Ranveig came down to the boat last. She had something in her hands, wrapped in sealskin, and she gave it to Orm over the gunwale and he unwrapped it in the lantern-light and looked at it. It was a piece of bone as long as his forearm, curved, flat on one side, and the flat side was cut all over with small close marks.

“A whale’s rib,” said Ranveig. “From the stone. It says what the stone said. The search, and who’s in it, and the woman in keeping, and on whose name.” She looked at Riarhin. “Show it at any hall on this coast and they’ll know the stone has spoken. They don’t have to like it. They have to know.”

“Thank you,” said Orm.

“It’s good for one telling,” said Ranveig. “The same matter, the same hall. Don’t waste it.”

Then she went back up the ramp, and the boat pushed off.

The landing at Brimness was a day and a half up the coast, at the mouth of a long valley that ran inland toward the hills, and it was the last place a boat could put people ashore on the road north. There was a household there, Leif said, and a stone jetty, and a strand behind the jetty where you could beach a boat if you had to. After Brimness there was the valley, and the old road up it to Skarth, and after Skarth the hills.

They reached it in the afternoon of the second day, in a grey half-light under low cloud, with no wind.

It looked easy. Riarhin, sitting in the waist of the boat on a heap of bedding with her frame between her knees, looked at it as they came round the point and saw a little bay with steep sides and a stone jetty running out from the near shore and a long house of turf above it with smoke going up. The sea in the bay was smooth. It lay like grey oil, heaving gently, long and slow, the whole surface of it lifting and settling as if something under it were breathing.

Leif stood at the steering oar and looked at it with satisfaction. He was a tall slow man with a long fair beard and a habit of humming. Grani, his boatman, a thick young man with very red hands, stood in the bow with the line coiled ready.

“Flat as a plate,” said Leif. “We’ll be ashore before dark.”

They came in toward the jetty under oars.

Riarhin was watching the house. She saw people come out of it and stand on the slope and look down at them. She saw a man lift his arm. She was not watching the sea, and she did not see what Sifa saw.

Sifa was in the stern, at Leif’s feet, where she had sat for two days without speaking, with her hands in her sleeves. Riarhin felt her move. It was a small movement, the movement of somebody who has been sitting slack for a long time and suddenly is not, and Riarhin turned her head and saw Sifa half up on her knees, staring past the bow at the jetty.

At the foot of the jetty, where the stone met the water, the sea was doing something strange. As each long slow swell came in under the boat and passed on toward the shore, the water at the jetty’s foot drew back. It drew back a long way, much further than the swell had seemed to warrant, and showed the weed on the stones, and black rock, and then it came in again, all at once, and broke against the jetty’s side with a sound like a door slamming, and sheeted up white over the top of it, and fell back.

Then it was smooth again. Then the next swell passed under the boat.

Sifa said something. She said it to Bródd, who was sitting beside her, in the throat-speech, quick and thick and broken. Riarhin caught none of it.

Bródd listened. He looked at the jetty. He seemed to hesitate.

“She says she would rather we didn’t go in to the jetty just yet,” he said, to Leif. “She says she doesn’t like the look of it.”

Leif glanced down at Sifa, briefly, and then at the jetty, and then at the smooth grey water all round them, and smiled into his beard.

“She doesn’t like the look of it,” he said. “I’ve brought boats into Brimness for twenty years.” He leaned on the oar. “Pull,” he said, to the rowers.

They pulled. The boat went on toward the jetty.

Sifa came up off her knees.

She did not say anything else. She stood up in the stern, in the narrow space beside Leif, and she took his arm. She took it above the elbow, where he held the steering oar, with both her cracked black-tipped hands, and she held on, and Leif looked down at her in astonishment and tried to shake her off and could not. She was stronger than she looked. She held his arm and turned her face up to him and spoke. She spoke in the throat-speech, and it hurt her, and she did not stop; she said the same thing again, and again, louder each time, the same short broken string of sounds, her filed teeth showing, her eyes on his face.

“Get her off me,” said Leif.

“What’s she saying?” said Orm, from the oars.

Bródd opened his mouth.

Temür, in the bow beside Grani, had turned round. He was looking at Sifa. Riarhin saw his face change. Then he shouted. He shouted in the northern speech, three words, at the top of his voice, so that it rang off the sides of the bay.

“Turn! Now! Rocks!”

Leif turned.

He did it without thinking, the way a man does who has been shouted at in a boat. He threw his weight on the oar and the boat came round, hard, heeling, the rowers on one side catching crabs and swearing, and the bow swung away from the jetty toward the open strand.

The swell came in under them.

Riarhin felt it lift the boat. She felt it lift and lift, far higher than any of the others had, and she looked over the side and saw the water at the jetty’s foot draw back, all the way back, and show not weed and stone but a long black reef of rock running out under where the jetty ended, under where they had been going to lay the boat alongside, a reef that was invisible at every other moment under the oily smooth swell. Then the water came back. It came back as a wall. It came in on the reef and burst over it and struck the side of the jetty with a noise like the sky splitting, and the white water came up over the top of the jetty in a sheet as high as a house, and fell, and came rolling across the bay toward them.

It caught the boat side-on.

She did not know how long it lasted. She knew the boat went over, far over, and the sea came in over the gunwale, green and white and freezing, and filled the bottom of the boat in one great cold rush up to her waist, and she grabbed for something with her good hand and found the frame between her knees and held it. She knew somebody went over the side and came back. She knew there was a sound under her, deep in the boat, a crack and then a long grinding scrape, as if the whole bottom were being dragged across a road.

Then the boat came up. It came up slowly, heavily, wallowing, full to the thwarts with sea, and lay in the middle of the bay with the swell lifting it, and everybody in it was alive.

“Bail,” said Orm.

He said it quite calmly. He was standing up in the waist with water to his thighs and his red beard streaming, and he looked round the boat once, at all of them, and said it again.

“Bail. Everybody who’s got hands. Grani, Leif, the oars. Temür, bail. Bródd. Riarhin.” He looked at her. “Can you?”

She could. There was a wooden bailer floating at her knee and she took it in her good hand and began to throw water over the side. Beside her Temür had found a cooking pot. Bródd was using his cupped hands. Sifa was using her hat.

The boat was leaking. Riarhin could feel it. Every time she threw out a bailer of water, the water in the bottom did not go down as far as it should have. There was something wrong under the floor-boards, aft of the mast. She could hear it, a thin steady trickling, under the slop and the curses.

Orm went forward over the thwarts to the bow, where Grani was.

Grani was sitting in the bottom of the bow with his back against the stem. He was holding his left arm with his right and his face was the colour of the sea.

“The line,” said Orm. “Where’s the line?”

Grani looked at him. “It went,” he said. “It went over when I went over. I lost it.” He looked down at his arm. “I think I’ve broken something.”

Orm looked at him for a moment. Then he looked at the shore, at the long gravel strand beyond the end of the jetty, two hundred yards off across the heaving grey water, where the people of Brimness were running down from the house.

He began to take off his coat.

“Orm,” said Leif.

“The strand’s steep-to. If we come in on it without a line she’ll broach on the first swell and roll.” Orm threw his coat down in the bottom of the boat. “Somebody has to take a line ashore. Get me the other line. The stern line.”

“You’ll freeze,” said Leif.

“I won’t be in it long,” said Orm, and Riarhin, bailing, looked up and saw his face and saw that he was afraid.

He went over the side with the stern line round his waist and the boat as close in to the strand as Leif dared bring it, wallowing, half full, and he went in up to his chest and waded, and the swell lifted him off his feet twice and put him down again, and the people of Brimness came down to the edge of the water and stood in it to their knees and reached for him, and on the third swell they got him.

The line went taut.

It did not hold the first time. They tried to bring the boat in on it, the men on the strand hauling, and the swell came under and lifted the boat and swung it round broadside and the line came out of their hands and they had to let it go or be pulled into the sea. Leif swore and brought the boat round again with the oars. They tried again. A man on the shore took a turn round a rock this time, and the line held, and the boat came in on the next swell to the edge of the strand and grounded, hard, on the gravel, and the swell went back and left it, and then came in again and slewed it round.

Riarhin was in the stern with the bailer.

The load had come loose. She saw it as the boat slewed: the sleds they had brought for the valley road, lashed upright to the mast, two of them, heavy, their runners shod with iron, and the lashing had parted in the wave and they were swinging. They swung out on the slew and came back. The runner of the lower one went past Grani’s head, where he sat with his broken arm in the bow, by a hand’s breadth.

She dropped the bailer.

She went forward over the thwarts. She did not go fast; she could not. She went with her good hand on the gunwale and her stitched leg dragging, through water to her knees, and as the boat slewed back the other way and the sleds swung back toward the bow, she reached Grani and took him by the collar of his coat with her good hand and pulled him down flat in the bottom of the boat, under the thwart, into the water, and lay half across him.

The sleds swung over them. The runner struck the stem where his head had been.

“Stay down,” she said into his ear.

He stayed down.

They brought the boat up the strand on the next swell, and the one after that, the people of Brimness hauling on the line and on the gunwales and Leif and Temür and Bródd in the water to their waists pushing, and on the fourth try she grounded and stayed grounded, and the swell went back from her and did not come far enough up the strand to lift her again.

Riarhin lay in the bottom of the boat in the water across Grani with her face against his wet coat and breathed.

The people of Brimness were not pleased to see Sifa.

Riarhin had thought they might not be. She stood on the strand in the last of the light, soaked to the skin and shaking, with Grani on one side of her being helped up the slope by a woman of the house and the boat lying on its side behind her with water pouring out of a long split in its planking, and she watched the man who must be the master of Brimness look at Sifa.

He was a big man with a grizzled beard and a wolfskin over his shoulders. He looked at Sifa, standing on the strand in her borrowed coat with her hands in her sleeves and the sea running off her, and then at the rest of them, and his face went hard.

“No,” he said. “Not in my house.”

Orm was beside Riarhin. He was blue with cold and shaking so hard his teeth rattled, and somebody had put a blanket round him. He fumbled inside his wet clothes, and brought out the sealskin bundle, and unwrapped it, and held out Ranveig’s whale-rib.

The master of Brimness looked at it.

He took it. He held it up to the light from the house door and turned it, and read the marks on it, slowly, with his lips moving. Riarhin watched his face. She watched it go from hard to something else, something unwilling and tired, the face of a man who has been handed a thing he cannot argue with and would very much like to.

“Kettlebeach,” he said.

“The stone,” said Orm, through his teeth.

The master of Brimness looked at Sifa again for a long moment. Then he gave the rib back to Orm.

“She sleeps in the byre,” he said. “With the woman whose name she’s on. Not in the hall.”

“That’s fair,” said Riarhin.

He looked at her, and grunted, and turned and went up toward the house, and the rest of the household followed him, carrying Grani and the wet bedding and the frames.

Riarhin went to help. She bent to pick up a bundle of blankets from the gravel and her leg told her, very clearly, that it had had enough for one day, and she stopped, and straightened, and stood.

On the strand beside her, Bródd had held out his hand to Sifa.

He had done it without thinking, Riarhin saw, the way you hold out a hand to somebody on a slippery slope. Sifa looked at it. She looked at it for a long moment, his broad brown cooper’s hand held out to her in the dusk, with the sea running off his sleeve.

Then she put her own hands back in her sleeves, and walked up the strand without it.

Chapter 10 — The sentence again

In the morning Bródd said it properly.

He said it in the byre, where they had slept, among the Brimness cattle, with the grey light coming in through the cracks round the door and the wet bedding hung up on every rafter and steaming. Riarhin had woken stiff in every joint, with the stitched leg aching from hip to knee and her bad arm aching worse, and had lain on the straw for a while looking at the rafters and thinking about the jetty. Sifa had been awake already, sitting with her back to the wall and her knees drawn up, watching the door.

Bródd came in from the house with porridge in a pot. He set it down in the straw. He did not serve it. He stood over it with his big cooper’s hands hanging, and looked at Sifa, and then at the others, Orm and Temür and Riarhin, waking one by one on their heaps of straw.

“Yesterday,” he said. “In the boat. What she said.”

Nobody spoke.

“She said,” said Bródd. He stopped and drew breath, and said it again, and this time he said it as if he were reading it off a stone. “The water goes back. There is rock under the end. Turn the boat. Turn it now or we are all dead.”

He looked at the porridge pot.

“That’s what she said,” he said. “The first time. Before she took Leif’s arm. That’s what she said, and I said she would rather we didn’t go in yet.”

The byre was very quiet. One of the cattle shifted its weight and blew.

Orm sat up slowly on his straw. He looked at Bródd for a long time. He had been in the water up to his chest the evening before, carrying a line, because of what Bródd had said, and his lips were still cracked from it, and his hands, when Riarhin had looked at them by the fire, had been swollen and white at the fingertips.

He did not say anything.

“Why?” said Riarhin.

Bródd looked at her. He looked, she thought, as if he had been waiting all night for somebody to ask, and had hoped nobody would.

“I don’t know,” he said. “I don’t—” He stopped. “It came out like that. I heard what she said, and I knew what it meant, and what came out was—” He made a small helpless movement with one hand. “Softer.”

“Softer,” said Temür.

He had been lying on his back with his arm over his eyes. He took the arm away now and sat up and looked at Bródd across the straw. He spoke in the northern speech, which was getting better every day, Riarhin had noticed; he had been listening to Orm.

“It is not softer,” said Temür. “It is different. There is a word she said. Twice. Turn. It is the same in my grandmother’s speech. It is a word you say to a horse, when it is going to the edge.” He held Bródd’s eyes. “You did not say turn. You said rather. There is no rather in what she said. There is no rather in any of it.”

Bródd looked back at him.

“No,” he said. “There isn’t.”

Sifa had not moved. She sat against the wall with her knees up and watched Bródd. She had watched him the whole time he spoke, with no expression at all. Now she lifted one hand from her sleeve and touched her jaw, under the ear, and along, and she spoke.

She spoke in the northern speech. It came out thick, and slow, and some of the sounds did not come out at all, and she had to stop twice. But Riarhin understood every word, because Sifa had made sure that every word could be understood, the way Riarhin herself made sure, and it had cost her.

“What else,” said Sifa, “did you make better?”

Bródd did not answer.

“At the stone,” said Sifa. “When the man asked did I kill his brother. What did you say I said?”

Bródd looked at her. Then at the floor.

“I said you said you were on the hill,” he said. “With the sleds. That you didn’t go down. That you were sorry for his brother.”

“What did I say?”

“You said you were on the hill with the sleds. That you didn’t go down.” He paused. “And that you didn’t know his brother and you weren’t going to pretend to.”

Riarhin heard Orm let out a long breath.

“And the first day,” said Sifa. “In the net. What did I say?”

“That you were late,” said Bródd. “That the ice was waiting. That—” He stopped. “That something was owed. I said you were sorry. You didn’t say you were sorry.”

“I didn’t say I was sorry,” said Sifa. “I have not said I am sorry. Not once. Not to anybody.” She lowered her hand from her jaw. “When I am sorry I will say it. Myself. Don’t say it for me.”

They ate the porridge. Nobody said much. It was the first time since they had left Hvalgard that Riarhin had been glad of porridge because it gave everybody something to do with their mouths.

Afterward, when Bródd had taken the pot back to the house, Orm went outside, and Riarhin followed him.

He was standing on the slope above the strand, in the cold, looking down at Leif’s boat. It lay on its side on the gravel where they had dragged it the night before, above the tide-line, and in the morning light the damage was plain. The split ran along three strakes on the starboard side, below the waterline, where the boat had struck the reef: a long jagged opening with the planks sprung outward and the caulking hanging out of the seams like pale hair. Leif was walking round it slowly with his hands behind his back. He was not humming.

“He can’t take her round the Ness like that,” said Orm.

The plan had been to go round by sea. Leif would have taken them up the coast past the Ness, a day and a half, to the sea-landing below Skarth, and they would have gone up to Skarth from there with the sleds, and saved four days’ walking. Now there was no boat to take them.

“The valley road,” said Riarhin.

“The valley road,” said Orm. “Four days to Skarth if the snow’s kind. Six if it isn’t.” He looked at the boat. “Leif’ll stay. He’ll mend her here, him and the Brimness men, and Grani with his arm. They’ll go home when she floats.” He was quiet a moment. “I went into the water,” he said, “for a man who couldn’t say turn.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“I don’t blame him,” said Orm. “I don’t know if I blame him. I was frightened out of my wits. I said I’d take the line, and I said it fast so I couldn’t take it back, and I took it.” He rubbed his swollen hands together. “That’s what you do.”

She heard the same words he had said to her in the hold, on the second night after her fever, the words she had taken up onto the deck in front of Ranveig.

She did not say anything about it. She stood beside him and looked at the boat.

“He didn’t know he was doing it,” she said after a while.

“I know he didn’t.” Orm looked at her sidelong. “That’s worse.”

They got a guide from Brimness.

When the master of Brimness heard that they would have to go up the valley road on foot, he looked at the five of them in his hall at noon, at Riarhin with her staff and Sifa with her black fingers and Bródd with his face set and Temür, whom he had not looked at once since they came ashore, and he shook his head and said nobody went up the valley road in midwinter who didn’t know it, and nobody who knew it would go.

Orm took a small packet out of his pack. It was wrapped in waxed cloth, and inside the waxed cloth, when he unwrapped it on the table, there was a strip of something dry and grey and scaled, as long as a hand and folded in three.

The master of Brimness looked at it.

“That’s an eel skin,” said Riarhin.

“It’s a pilot’s token,” said Orm. “My father’s. He took a boat through the Grísey reeds for a woman called Arnkatla, forty years ago, when nobody else would, and she gave him that, and said whoever brought it back to her house would be guided where they wanted to go.” He looked at the master. “Arnkatla’s your mother.”

The master of Brimness was quiet for a long time.

“She’s dead,” he said. “Six years.”

“Her house isn’t.”

The master looked at the eel skin on his table. He did not touch it. He looked at it the way the man at the strand had looked at the whale-rib, unwilling and tired, and then he looked up at the rafters and called out a name.

A woman came in from the back of the hall. She was perhaps forty, broad and weathered, with her hair cut short and a frost-scar across one cheek, and she wiped her hands on her apron and looked at the eel skin and then at her brother.

“Arnkatla’s token,” he said. “They want the valley road to Skarth.”

The woman looked at the five of them.

“Is it dry?” she said.

“Bone dry,” said Orm.

“Then I’ll take you,” she said. “Tomorrow, first light. If it’s wet by Skarth, I’ll leave you where you stand.” She looked at Sifa, a long hard look, and then back at Orm. “I don’t take that one past Skarth.”

“Nobody’s asking you to,” said Orm.

They repacked in the byre that afternoon. Everything they had brought had been soaked in the boat and dried overnight on the rafters, more or less, and all of it had to be sorted and packed again for walking instead of sitting: the two sleds lightened and rigged for hauling, the frames repacked, the wet things separated from the dry.

Riarhin did what she could. She sat on a keg in the byre and sorted the food, which had come through in its sealed bags better than anything else, into the loads for the sleds and the loads for the frames. She could do that sitting down. She counted it out. She counted it twice.

She did not watch Bródd. She knew where he was without watching. He was at the other end of the byre, taking the wet bedding down off the rafters and folding it, one blanket at a time, with great care, as if each one had done something to him.

When she had finished counting she got up and went down the byre to him.

He looked round at her and then back at the blanket in his hands.

“I want something,” said Riarhin.

He waited.

“When she speaks,” said Riarhin, “and you tell us what she said, and Temür can follow it, I want Temür to say whether he heard the same. Every time. Out loud. In front of her.” She looked at him. “And when Temür can’t follow it, I want you to say exactly what she said. Word by word. Even if it sounds wrong. Even if it sounds rude. Especially then.”

Bródd folded the blanket in half, and in half again.

“You want me to stop talking,” he said.

“No.”

He looked up.

“I want you to talk,” said Riarhin. “Temür has half the old speech. Less. I have none. Orm has none. When we find the people who took the four, if we find them, they won’t speak to us in the northern speech. They’ll speak in hers.” She tipped her head down the byre toward Sifa, who was sitting by the door with Temür, saying nothing, watching the snow. “And you’re the only one who has it. If you stop talking, we’re deaf. I can’t go north deaf.”

Bródd was quiet a long time.

“My grandmother had it,” he said at last. “Properly. She had it from her grandmother. There were songs. And stories. About before. About the north, and the people we came from, and how we came south, and what it was like.” He looked at the blanket. “She used to tell them at the feasts in Hvalgard. When I was small. People liked them. They liked the strange words and the old names and the—” He made a shape with his hand. “The sadness. They liked to be sad for a while and then go home.”

“And you told them after her.”

“Eight years. Since she died.” He set the folded blanket on the pile. “Every midwinter. Somebody says, Bródd, tell the one about the crossing, and I tell it. And some of it is—” He stopped. “Some of it isn’t a story that you’d want at a feast. Some of it’s about things my people did. Before. In the north. And things that were done to them. And if you tell it as she told it, people stop eating.”

“So you changed it.”

“I made it so they could eat,” said Bródd. “Eight years. A little at a time. A word here. I made the bad parts sorry instead of bad. I made the warnings sound like advice.” He took another blanket down from the rafter. “Nobody ever asked me to. Nobody ever noticed. I didn’t notice. Not till Temür said rather.”

He began to fold.

Riarhin stood and watched him.

She did not tell him it was all right. She did not think it was all right. She thought of Orm in the water to his chest, and Grani in the bottom of the boat with the sled runner going past his head, and the long split in Leif’s boat.

She took the other end of the blanket.

They folded it between them, without speaking, the way two people fold a sheet: lengthwise, and then lengthwise again, walking toward each other until their hands met, and then she gave him her end and he folded it once more and laid it on the pile. Then the next one. It was slow, and they did not look at each other, and neither of them said anything for a long time.

“She asked me what else,” said Bródd, at the fourth blanket.

“Yes.”

“I don’t know what else.” He did not look up. “That’s the thing. I’ve been trying all morning to remember every time I put words into her mouth, and I can’t. I don’t know which ones. I don’t know how many.”

Riarhin took the next blanket down from the rafter. It was still damp at one corner. She gave him one end.

“Then you’ll have to say it right from now,” she said. “And when you don’t, Temør will catch you, and you’ll say it again.”

“Temür,” said Bródd.

“What?”

“You said it wrong. His name.” He took the blanket. “Temür. Not Temør.”

Riarhin looked at him. Then she found that she was very nearly smiling, and that he was not, and that he had not meant it as a joke at all; he had heard it wrong and said so.

“Temür,” she said.

“Yes,” said Bródd, and they folded the blanket.

Chapter 11 — Timber for a roof

The runner broke on the fourth day, a mile below Skarth, on a stone under the snow that the guide had warned them about and that nobody, in the end, could see.

It was the second sled, the heavy one, with the food. Temür was hauling it. He felt it go and stopped, and they all heard the crack, a flat dry sound like a branch in frost, and when they dug the snow away the forward end of the left runner had split back along the grain for the length of a forearm and was hanging by its iron.

The guide from Brimness looked at it and said a word Riarhin had heard sailors use. Then she said, “Bjarni’s.”

“Who’s Bjarni?” said Orm.

“Shipwright at Skarth. He’ll have ash.” The guide looked up the valley, where the smoke of the settlement was rising grey into a grey sky. “He’ll have ash, and he’ll have an opinion about it.”
