Skarth stood where the valley forked, on a shelf of level ground above a frozen river, and it was larger than Riarhin had expected: a dozen long houses and a great many lesser ones, and a shipyard. She had not expected a shipyard. They were four days inland. But the river, the guide said, ran down from here to the sea-landing below the Ness, and in the spring when the ice went out of it, Skarth built boats from the hill timber and floated them down, and the halls on the coast paid well for them.

The shipyard was a long open shed with a turf roof, and inside it, on a cradle of trestles, there was a boat half built: the keel laid and the stem and stern-posts raised and the first four strakes on each side, pale new wood shining in the lamplight. Beside it, stacked under the eaves on bearers, out of the snow, there was timber. Planks and baulks and curved knees and long straight pieces of ash, sorted and stacked and every piece of it marked with something cut into its end.

And there was a quarrel.

It was being conducted round a table at the back of the shed, where a brazier burned and a pot of broth sat on it, and it had plainly been going on for some time, because the people round the table had bowls in front of them that had gone cold. There were six or seven of them. Riarhin, coming in with the broken runner carried between Orm and Temür, picked out three. A huge old man with arms like hawsers and a leather apron black with pitch. A thin elderly man at the head of the table with a horn cup in front of him in which something was dripping. And in a grey robe and a sheepskin coat and a pair of borrowed mittens, holding his bowl in both hands and looking as if he would very much like to be somewhere else, Brother Halle.

He saw them come in. His face went through surprise into something like relief.

“Oh,” he said. “Oh, it’s you. From the quay. I’m so sorry. You must be frozen.”

The quarrel was about the timber.

Riarhin learned it in pieces, sitting at the end of the table with a bowl of broth that somebody had put in front of her, while Orm and the old man in the apron looked at the broken runner. She learned it from Brother Halle, mostly, who told it with a great many apologies, and from the thin elderly man at the head of the table, whose name was Steinar and who was the law-speaker for the upper valley and had been called in to hear it.

The old man in the apron was Bjarni. He had built boats at Skarth for fifty years. The timber under the eaves was his: he had felled it himself from the woodlot up the eastern fork, over three winters, and seasoned it, and it was meant for the boat on the trestles and for the two after it.

But the woodlot was not his. It had been his brother’s. His brother had died in the autumn, and his brother’s widow, who lived down at the coast now, had sent word in the first snow that she was giving the woodlot to the Temple for the good of her husband’s soul, and everything cut from it, for the chapel at Hvalgard.

And Brother Halle had accepted. He had written a letter thanking her and come up to Skarth with two sledges and four men from Hvalgard to fetch it.

“I didn’t know,” he said. He said it to Riarhin, very low, across the table, as if it were important that she in particular should understand. “I didn’t know it was cut. I didn’t know whose it was. She wrote that it was hers to give. I believed her. I thought—” He looked at the timber under the eaves. “I thought it was trees.”

“It’s not trees,” said Bjarni.

He had come back to the table. He had the broken runner in his hands and he set it down on the boards between the bowls, and the table shook.

“It’s not trees,” he said again. He looked at Brother Halle, and then at Steinar, and then he turned and pointed at the stack under the eaves. “That’s Geira.”

Nobody spoke.

“That long piece. The one with the ring cut on the end.” Bjarni’s finger did not move. “That’s my aunt Geira. That’s for the keel of the next one. She was the best sailor in the valley and she drowned off the Ness when I was eight. The two knees beside her, that’s my father and his brother. They’re for the stern. The ash, at the top, that’s Hrolf, that’s my brother, that’s the one who died.” He lowered his hand. “I name them. Every piece I cut. I name them for my dead. I always have. My father did. You put your dead in the boat and they keep her up.”

He looked at Brother Halle.

“You want to take Geira down the valley and saw her into planks for a southern roof,” he said. “With no name on her. You want to take Hrolf and put him in your wall.”

Brother Halle had gone white.

“I didn’t know,” he said.

“I know you didn’t,” said Bjarni. “That’s why I haven’t thrown you in the river.”

At the head of the table, Steinar lifted his horn cup and looked into it. There was water in the bottom, and over the cup, on a little iron stand, there was a lump of ice, and the ice was melting, and dripping, slowly, one drop and then a long time and then another, into the cup. He set the cup down again.

“The widow has a claim to the woodlot,” he said. He spoke dryly and precisely, like a man reading. “Hrolf held it. It came to her. She can give it where she likes. Bjarni has a claim to what he cut, because he cut it, with his labour, on Hrolf’s leave, before Hrolf died. The Temple has the widow’s gift and a letter saying so.” He looked at the cup. “I can hear it. I can’t settle it at one sitting. There are things I need to know. Whether Hrolf gave leave in front of anybody. Whether the widow knew the timber was cut when she wrote.” A drop fell. “It may take till the spring.”

“I need it now,” said Brother Halle.

“You don’t,” said Bjarni. “You need it in the spring. You can’t build in this.”

“The Bishop—”

“I don’t care about the Bishop.”

“And I need a runner,” said Orm. Everybody looked at him. He had been sitting very quietly at the end of the table beside Riarhin with his swollen hands round his bowl. “I’m sorry. I don’t want to be in this. But I’ve a sled that won’t move without a new runner, and the only seasoned ash in Skarth is up there in that stack, and I can’t take it if nobody knows whose it is.”

Steinar looked at him.

“Then you’ll have to wait,” he said, “with the rest of us.”

They waited three days.

Riarhin had not thought she would mind. She had thought, at first, that three days in a shipyard with a brazier and broth would be good for her leg, which had carried her four days up the valley road and was angry about it. It was good for her leg. It was not good for anything else.

Orm minded worst. Riarhin watched him. He had said it on the stone, I’m going north for the four, and every day they sat at Skarth was a day the four were wherever they were, and he knew it and could not do anything about it, and he went out to the edge of the shelf above the river every morning and stood looking north up the eastern fork, where the valley went on up into the hills under its snow, and came back with his face set.

Sifa minded too, in her own way. She did not stand on the edge of the shelf. She sat in the back of the shipyard shed, where Bjarni had let them sleep among the shavings, and watched the door, and every time somebody came in from the north she looked at them.

Temür did not seem to mind. He helped Bjarni. He did it without being asked, on the first morning, by picking up the other end of a plank Bjarni was struggling with alone, and Bjarni looked at him and grunted and let him, and after that Temür was in the shipyard every day, holding things and fetching things and watching, with his rider’s hands that had never worked wood, and Bjarni shouted at him in a way that seemed to please them both.

Riarhin found her own work.

She found it because Steinar, on the second morning, came and sat beside her at the table while she was cleaning Toothpick, and asked her what she had seen at Ottarshall.

She told him. She told him about the store with the door open, and the key in the ash, and the four houses along the row. She told him what Ragna had said about the name shouted through the door. She told it the way she had told Gudleif about the painted eye, what she had seen and what she had been told and which was which, and Steinar listened with his dry face and his melting cup and said nothing until she had finished.

“You keep them apart,” he said.

“What?”

“What you saw. What you heard. You keep them apart.” He looked at the cup. “Most people don’t. They tell you what they heard as if they saw it. By the third telling they believe they did.” He looked at her. “Did Hrolf give Bjarni leave to cut, do you think?”

“I don’t know. I wasn’t here.”

“No.” He very nearly smiled. “Would you know it, if you saw it? The leave. If there was any.”

Riarhin thought about it.

“I’d know whether the trees were felled before he died or after,” she said slowly. “If somebody showed me the stumps. And whether anybody could have seen it being done. From where.” She looked at him. “That isn’t leave. It’s what the leave would have needed.”

“Yes,” said Steinar. “That’s what I want.”

So on the second afternoon she went up the eastern fork with Steinar and Bjarni and Brother Halle, by sled, slowly, on the good runner, to the woodlot.

It was a steep hanging wood of ash and birch above the river, under deep snow. The stumps were there, where Bjarni had felled, in three rows, three winters’ worth, and Riarhin went among them with the staff and looked at each one. She looked at the cut faces, where the snow had blown off them. She looked at the weathering of the wood, grey on the oldest and paler on the newest, and the way the bark had begun to lift at the edges. She looked at where the stumps were, and where the river path ran below them, and where the nearest house was, at the foot of the slope, with its smoke going up.

“These were cut three years ago,” she said, at the first row. “These two. These last winter.” She looked down the slope at the house. “Anybody on that path would have seen them being felled. Anybody in that house would have heard. You can’t fell ash quietly.”

“That’s Hrolf’s house,” said Bjarni.

Steinar looked at it for a long time.

“Then he heard,” he said.

“Or he was away,” said Riarhin. “I don’t know. I’m telling you what you can see from here.” She looked at the stumps. “That’s all. I can’t tell you whose they are.”

“No,” said Steinar. “That’s for me.”

Brother Halle had said nothing all the way up. He stood among the stumps in his borrowed mittens and looked at them, the grey cut faces in the snow, three rows of them, and Riarhin saw his face.

“I’m sorry,” he said. Not to Steinar. To Bjarni. “I wrote to her and thanked her. I should have asked her. I should have come up and asked you. I’m sorry.”

Bjarni looked at him a long time.

“There’s birch,” he said at last, gruffly. “Down the western fork. Green, but it’ll do for a chapel roof by the spring if you stack it right. Nobody’s named it. You could have that.” He paused. “If you fell it.”

“I will,” said Brother Halle.

“You can’t fell a tree,” said Bjarni.

“Then I’ll learn,” said Brother Halle, and went red to the ears.

He did learn, after a fashion. He went down the western fork the next morning with his four men from Hvalgard and two of Bjarni’s, and came back at dusk with blistered hands and a cut on his forehead and two sledges of green birch, and sat down at the table in the shipyard and could not lift his bowl.

Steinar gave Orm his ash that evening. Not the ash from the stack. A short straight piece that Bjarni had kept back in the corner of the shed, unmarked, because it had a twist in the grain near one end that made it no good for a boat. Bjarni brought it out himself, and laid it on the table, and said it was nobody’s, and would do for a sled if Orm wasn’t particular, and that if Orm was particular he could go and cut his own.

Orm was not particular.

So it was done, and it had taken three days, and the runner would take one more. Riarhin sat at the table in the lamplight with her leg up on a bench and watched Temür hold the piece of ash on the trestle while Bjarni shaped it with an adze, and listened to Bjarni shout, and thought about the four houses in the row at Ottarshall, and the time.

The door opened.

A man came in from the north with snow on his shoulders. He was a Skarth man, one of the ones who had been out on the eastern road with a sled. He stood in the doorway and looked round the shed and found Steinar, and went to him, and spoke low.

Steinar listened. Then he looked down the table at Brother Halle.

“The Bishop’s column is at Hornvik,” he said. “Came in yesterday. Three hundred, he says.” He paused. “The Justiciar went in ahead of them. To clear the ground. He’s burned the shrine.”

Brother Halle put down his bowl.

At the end of the table, Orm had gone still.

“Hornvik,” said Orm. “That’s on our road.”

“It’s on your road,” said Steinar.

“The man we were going to. For the ice-road. The one Ingrith sent word to. Ásgeir.” Orm looked at the man from the north. “Ásgeir lives at Hornvik. By the shrine.”

The man in the doorway looked back at him.

“He keeps it,” he said. “Kept it. Him and his wife.” He looked at the snow on his boots. “He was there when they burned it.”

Chapter 12 — Ash under the bell

Hornvik was a fishing place at the head of a long lake, a day and a half north of Skarth, and they smelled it before they saw it.

It was not the smell of Ottarshall. Riarhin knew that at once and was ashamed of how quickly she knew it. It was wood smoke and wet ash and tar, and under it nothing worse. Whatever had burned at Hornvik had been a building and not the people in it.

The settlement stood on a low point running out into the frozen lake: a cluster of turf houses and boat-sheds, with the lake ice white and flat beyond them to the far shore and the hills going up behind into cloud. On the highest part of the point, above the houses, there was a ring of standing stones, nine of them, man-high, and inside the ring a black heap.

Around the point, on the flat ground at the edge of the lake, there was a camp.

She had seen camps. She had lived in them for years. She looked at this one and saw the shape of it before she saw anything else: the rows of tents laid out straight, square to each other, with lanes between; the horse lines along the windward side; the cook-fires in a row at the end of each lane; the sentries. It was a soldiers’ camp, a southern one, and it had been laid out by somebody who had learned how in a hard school. Over the middle of it, on a pole, a long white banner hung limp in the still air, and on the banner a golden candle.

“Three hundred,” said Orm, beside her. He was counting the tents. “He said three hundred. I make it more.”

“Not all fighters,” said Riarhin. She was looking at the horse lines, at the wagons drawn up behind them. “Look at the wagons. That’s a household. Cooks, clerks. Servants.” She looked at the white banner. “A bishop travels with a great many people who aren’t soldiers.”

“And some who are,” said Temür.

There were soldiers at Hornvik. Not in the camp; in the settlement. Riarhin saw them as they came down the last of the slope above the point: men in grey cloaks over mail, with spears, standing at the doors of the turf houses. Two at each of four doors. Two more at the gap in the ring of stones on the high ground, with their backs to the black heap, looking down at the lake.

“They’ve taken the houses,” said Orm.

Yrsa met them at the edge of the point.

She was a short square woman of fifty with grey hair and a face reddened by wind and something else, and she came down the track toward them alone, quickly, with her skirts held up out of the snow, as though she had been watching for them from the slope since morning. She looked at Orm. She looked at the sleds, and at the others, and at Sifa, longest, and her face did not change.

“You’re Ingrith’s people,” she said. “For the ice-road. For Ásgeir.”

“We are,” said Orm.

“Ásgeir can’t take you.” She said it flatly. “Not now. He’s in the house with his head broken. He went up to the stones when they came to burn them and stood in the gap, and they knocked him down.” She looked over her shoulder at the soldiers at the doors. “He’ll mend. He won’t leave. Not while they’re here. He says if he leaves they’ll burn the house as well.”

Nobody answered that.

“You’ll want a roof,” said Yrsa. She looked at the soldiers again. “They’ve taken four houses for their officers. The Justiciar’s in the long house. They haven’t taken mine. Not yet.” She turned back to Orm. “You can have mine. You’re guests in it. On my name and Ásgeir’s.”

“They’ll see us go in,” said Riarhin.

Yrsa looked at her.

“Yes,” she said. “They will.”

Sifa would not go in.

They came to Yrsa’s door, which was the last house on the point, nearest the stones, and there were no soldiers at it. But there were soldiers at the door of the next house, ten paces off, two of them, in their grey cloaks, and they turned their heads and watched the five of them come down the track, and when Sifa came level with them they stopped watching the others and watched only her.

Sifa stopped.

She stood in the snow ten paces from the door with her hands in her sleeves. She did not look at the soldiers. She looked at the door of Yrsa’s house, which Yrsa was holding open, with the firelight coming out of it, and she did not move.

“Sifa,” said Riarhin.

Sifa did not answer. One of the soldiers said something to the other and laughed, low.

Riarhin went back to her. She stood beside her in the snow. She did not take her arm.

“They’re looking,” said Sifa. It came thick, very low. “I go in under that, they come in after. Tonight. For me.” Her jaw moved. “Or they burn the house. Because I went in.”

“You don’t know that.”

Sifa turned her head and looked at Riarhin. It was the look from the sledge on the strand at Hvalgard, flat and yellow-brown and waiting to find out how much it would hurt.

“I know soldiers,” she said.

Riarhin looked at the two men by the next door. They looked back at her. They were young, both of them, and cold, and bored, and one of them had a raw red nose, and they were looking at Sifa the way a dog looks at a cat on a wall.

She thought of the camps, and of what she had seen soldiers do on cold nights in a strange country to people nobody would ask after.

“The byre,” said Yrsa, behind them. She had come back from the door. “There’s a byre at the back. Out of their sight. With the goats.” She looked at Sifa. “It’s warm. The goats don’t care who you are.”

Sifa looked at her for a long moment. Then she went round the side of the house to the byre, and did not look back at the soldiers once.

Orm went in at the front door. Riarhin saw his face as he passed the two soldiers, and saw his shoulders, and saw him look at them, and saw him decide not to. It cost him. He went in with his hands opening and closing at his sides.

The bell was under Yrsa’s bed.

She showed it to them that night, when the others were asleep and Ásgeir was lying by the fire with his head in a bandage, breathing loudly. She knelt and drew out from under the box bed a bundle wrapped in sheepskin, and unwrapped it on the floor between her knees.

It was small. Riarhin had expected something larger. It was an iron bell no bigger than two cupped hands, black, old, cast rough, with a loop at the top for a cord and a crack running up one side from the lip almost to the shoulder. The clapper had been taken out and wrapped separately in a rag so that it could not sound.

“It hung in the stones,” said Yrsa. “In the middle. On a post. Ásgeir’s grandmother’s grandmother brought it from the coast. It’s rung for every boat that went out on the lake and every boat that came back, as long as anybody remembers. It’s rung for the dead.” She did not touch it. “I took it down the night before they came. When we heard. The rest of it they burned. The post. The cloths. The bones in the little house under the post. The old head off the top of the post, that my grandmother’s mother put there. The Justiciar read out a paper first, all of it, standing in front of the stones, and then they burned it.” She looked at the bell. “They didn’t know about this. They’re asking. They’ve asked twice where the bell is. The officer in the long house wants it. He says it’s an idol and it has to be melted.”

“Then he’ll search the house,” said Riarhin.

“Tomorrow,” said Yrsa. “He said tomorrow.” She looked up. “I want it gone. Out of the house. Up the lake, to my sister’s at Dalsendi, where they won’t look. Before morning.” She looked at Orm. “You’re going north. Up the lake is north.”

Orm looked at the bell.

“If they catch us with it,” he said.

“Then they catch you.”

Orm was quiet. Riarhin watched him. She saw him look at the bell, and at Yrsa, and at Ásgeir asleep by the fire with his broken head, and then at the front door. Riarhin remembered him passing the soldiers there.

“We go round the back,” said Riarhin. “Before light. Through the byre. Along the shore under the bank, where the camp can’t see. Nobody carries it but me. If anybody stops us, it’s mine.”

Orm looked at her.

“Why you?” he said.

“Because I’m the one they can’t do anything to that matters,” said Riarhin. “I’m not from here. I’ve no hall. If I’m caught with it, the Justiciar can be angry at a foreigner. He can’t burn anybody’s house for it.” She looked at Yrsa. “If you want it carried. Your bell, your word.”

Yrsa looked at the bell a long time.

“My word,” she said.

It did not work.

It very nearly did. They went out through the byre an hour before the late midwinter dawn, Riarhin with the bell wrapped in its sheepskin at the bottom of her frame under the food, and Yrsa with her to show the way, and Temür behind. They went along the shore of the lake under the low bank, on the ice at its edge, in the dark, without a light. The camp lay above them on the flat, quiet, with its fires banked. They got past it. They got three hundred paces past the end of the point, where the shore curved away north, and Yrsa stopped and pointed up the lake into the grey dark and said Dalsendi, and Riarhin was turning to say something when a lantern came round the curve of the shore ahead of them.

It was a patrol. Four men. They had been out along the lake to the north and were coming back to the camp, and they walked straight into them.

There was a moment when nobody did anything.

Then the man with the lantern lifted it and looked at Yrsa, and knew her. Riarhin saw him know her. He looked at Yrsa and at the two strangers with her, out on the lake shore before dawn with a laden frame, going north, and his face changed.

“Search it,” he said.

Two of them came toward Riarhin.

She did not run. She could not have run if she wanted to. She stood on the ice at the edge of the lake with the frame on her back and her staff in her good hand, and she said, in the northern speech, as clearly as she could, “It’s mine. What’s in it is mine. She asked me to carry it and I said I would.”

One of the soldiers did not understand the northern speech, or did not care. He took hold of Yrsa by the arm, roughly, and pulled her out of the way.

Yrsa cried out.

Temür moved. Riarhin saw him from the side of her eye, quick and low, and she said “No,” in Ykrul, sharply, and he stopped. He stopped with his hand an inch from the soldier’s wrist.

Riarhin did not stop.

She put her left hand on the soldier’s arm, the one holding Yrsa, just above the wrist, and she closed her fingers, and turned. It was a very small movement. It was the first thing Ghere had ever taught her, in a yard in the south with a wooden stick, a long time ago: not the arm, the wrist, and not hard, but exactly. The soldier’s fingers opened. He let go of Yrsa with a grunt and stepped back, holding his wrist, and looked at Riarhin in astonishment.

The other three had their spears down.

“Stand,” said the man with the lantern. “Stand where you are.”

Riarhin stood. She stood beside Yrsa, close, with her shoulder against Yrsa’s shoulder, and did not move again.

The Justiciar Rhone was a tall spare man of forty with a close-cropped grey head and a face like a clean blade, and when they brought the three of them into the long house at Hornvik in the grey of the morning, with the bell unwrapped on a table in front of him, he did not raise his voice once.

He looked at the bell. He looked at Yrsa, and at Temür, and longest at Riarhin. Then he took a folded paper from inside his coat, and unfolded it, and read it aloud.

He read all of it. It took some time. It was in the southern tongue and then again in the northern, in a careful clerk’s rendering, and it said that by the authority of the Bishop Serapion and the synod of the Temple at Thepyrgos, the Justiciar Rhone was charged to clear the ground before the Bishop’s progress of all idols, false altars, and places of offering to demons, and to see that the objects of such worship were destroyed or rendered harmless, and that any person who hindered him in this was to be named in his report to the Bishop. He read it standing, in a level voice, to the end. Then he folded it again and put it away.

“That is the warrant,” he said. “I read it before I act on it. Always. So that nobody can say afterward they did not know what I was doing or why.” He looked at the bell. “This is an object of worship from the stone circle on the point. The keeper concealed it. You carried it.” He looked at Riarhin. “Who are you?”

“Riarhin.”

“Of where?”

She thought of Eyvind with his slate.

“Of nowhere,” she said. “I came north on a Linn ship. I’m going north with Ingrith’s search, under the stone at Kettlebeach. The keeper asked me to carry it. I said I would.”

Rhone looked at her a while.

“I know about the stone at Kettlebeach,” he said. “I know about the search. The Bishop has been told.” He turned to the officer beside him. “Write her name. And the keeper’s. And the Ykrul’s.” He looked at Temür. “What is your name?”

Temür told him. Rhone listened, and spelled it to the officer, correctly, the first time.

“You are guests of the keeper,” said Rhone. “I’m told. Under her roof.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“Then I will note that too.” He looked at Yrsa. “The keeper’s store, the stone house behind her dwelling. It will be sealed. Today. Nothing goes in or out of it until the Bishop has heard this matter.” He did not look away from her. “That is not a punishment. It is to make sure nothing else is concealed.”

Yrsa went white.

“That’s our winter,” she said. “That’s the fish. That’s the meal. All of it. That’s what we eat.”

“Then the Bishop will hear it quickly,” said Rhone. “He is very fair.” He turned back to the bell. “This stays with me.”

They let them go. There was nothing else to hold them on, Rhone said, until the Bishop had heard it. They walked back along the point to Yrsa’s house in the full grey light of morning, the three of them, between two soldiers, past the doors of the other houses, past the ring of stones with the black heap in it, and the people of Hornvik came out of their doors and watched them go by.

At Yrsa’s house two soldiers were already at the store behind it. Riarhin watched them. They had a bar of iron and a length of chain and a seal, a lump of red wax with a candle pressed into it, and they barred the low stone door and chained the bar and pressed the wax over the chain, and stood back.

Yrsa stood in the snow in front of her house and watched them do it.

Riarhin stood beside her.

She did not say she was sorry. She did not think it would help, and she did not think it was hers to say. She had offered to carry the bell. She had said if anybody stops us, it’s mine. She stood beside Yrsa and watched the soldiers seal the store with Yrsa’s winter in it.

Orm came out of the house. He looked at the sealed door, and at Yrsa, and at Riarhin. His face was very white under the beard.

“We’ll see it put right,” he said. He said it to Yrsa, aloud, in front of the soldiers. “Your store. Whatever the Bishop says. Ingrith’s search, on the stone. We’ll see it put right before we’re done.”

Yrsa looked at him.

“You’re going north,” she said.

“We’re coming back.”

Yrsa looked at the sealed door for a long time. Then she turned and went into her house, and left the door open behind her, so that they could follow if they wanted to.

Chapter 13 — The bishop’s own portion

The Bishop heard it at supper, in the long house, with the people of Hornvik at his table.

Riarhin had not expected that either. She had expected a hall of judgement, a chair, clerks; she had seen the Temple sit in judgement in the south and it had looked like that. Instead, in the evening, a young priest came to Yrsa’s door and said that the Bishop asked the keeper and her guests to eat with him, and when they came along the point to the long house in the dark it was full of people from Hornvik, the families who had been put out of their houses by the officers, sitting on the benches along the walls with bowls in their laps, and the officers were not there.

The Bishop was at the head of the table. He was eating porridge.

He was an old man, tall and thin and stooped, with a long face and a great deal of white hair that stood up from his head in the warmth of the fire like frost on a hedge. He wore a plain grey robe like Brother Halle’s and over it a sheepskin coat like Brother Halle’s, and the only sign of what he was hung at his breast on a cord: a small golden candle, no longer than a finger. He looked up when they came in, and his eyes were pale and very clear, and tired.

“Sit,” he said, in the northern speech, which he spoke well. “Please. There is fish. It is good. The people of Hornvik cooked it, and I am told I am to say so loudly, because it is their fish.”

Somebody on the benches laughed.

They sat. Yrsa sat stiffly at the end of the bench with her hands in her lap. Orm sat beside her. Temür sat by the door. Sifa was not there; Sifa had not been asked, or had not come, and Riarhin had not argued.

The Bishop ate while he listened. Riarhin watched him do it. A woman from Hornvik was speaking to him, one of the ones whose house the officers had taken, about her children and the cold in the boat-shed where they were sleeping now, and the Bishop listened to her with his eyes on her face and his spoon in his hand, and while he listened he took the piece of fish from his own bowl, the large piece, and set it in the bowl of the child sitting at his elbow, without looking down. The child stared at it. The Bishop went on listening.

He did it twice more while Riarhin watched. Once with his bread. Once with the cup of something hot that a priest set at his place, which he moved along the table to an old man coughing at the end of it, still without looking, still listening to the woman.

“You will have your houses back the day after tomorrow,” he said, when the woman had finished. “The officers will be moved into the camp. And for every night they were in them, you will have a measure of barley from the Temple’s stores at Hvalgard in the spring. Brother Anselm will write it down for each house.” He looked along the table. “Is that fair?”

The woman looked at him for a long moment.

“It’s fair,” she said.

“Then it is done.” The Bishop turned to Yrsa. “Now. The keeper.”

He heard it all. Riarhin, trying to be fair about it, gave him that: he heard every word.

He heard Yrsa. He heard her tell about the stones, and the bell, and how long it had hung on its post, and the cloths and the bones in the little house under the post and the paper read aloud and the burning. He heard her tell about the store, and the meal and the fish in it that were her winter and Ásgeir’s. He heard Riarhin tell about carrying the bell, and why, and about the patrol on the lake shore. He did not interrupt either of them. When they had finished he sat for a while with his hands folded on the table in front of him.

“The store,” he said at last. “That was wrong.”

He said it plainly, to the whole room.

“The Justiciar sealed it so that nothing could be hidden in it. That is sensible. But it is your food, in the middle of the winter, and he has no warrant to take a family’s food, only its idols.” He looked at Yrsa. “It will be unsealed tonight. Brother Anselm will go with you and count back to you everything in it.” He paused. “I am told that some of the meal from Hornvik was taken into the column’s stores yesterday, by an officer who believed he was being efficient. If any of it was yours, it will be repaid. Not in meal. In the southern barley at Hvalgard in the spring, measure for measure, and a measure over.”

Yrsa did not say anything. She looked at him as if she were waiting for the other half of it.

The Bishop gave it to her.

“The bell,” he said, “I will not give back.”

He said it gently.

“I am sorry. I can see what it is to you. I can see what it has been to Hornvik. But it hung in a ring of stones over a house of bones, and it rang to call something that I believe is not a god and that I believe has eaten your children, and I cannot give it back to be hung there again.” He looked at Yrsa with his tired clear eyes. “The Justiciar wishes it melted. I will not have that either. It will be kept, with the Temple, safely. It will not be harmed. If ever there is a church at Hornvik, it can hang in it, and ring for the boats.”

“It rang for the boats,” said Yrsa. “It rang for the boats already.”

“I know,” said the Bishop.

“The stones,” said Yrsa. “The house under the post. Ásgeir wants to put the roof back on it. In the spring. Not the—” She stopped. “Just the roof. For the bones.”

The Bishop was quiet a moment.

“No,” he said. “I’m sorry. Not for that.”

He explained it. Riarhin listened to him explain it, and found it hard to hear because he did it so well.

He said that the Temple did not come north to take the north’s customs away. He said that the Temple had read the north’s laws, the Thing and the stone and the oath and the guest-right, and that most of them were good and some of them were better than anything the south had, and that he would keep them. A Thing at Kettlebeach could judge a quarrel and he would let it stand. A guest under a roof was a guest and he would see that none of his officers forgot it. A thing sworn on a name was sworn, and he would hold the Temple’s own people to their word as the Linn held theirs.

But a house of bones in a ring of stones, with a bell to call something out of the ice, was not a custom. It was an altar. And he would not let an altar stand that he believed fed the thing the Dogmen served.

“There are limits,” he said. “There must be. You would not let a man keep a wolf in his byre because his grandfather did.”

There was a murmur along the benches. It was not quite agreement.

The Bishop turned then and looked down the table at Riarhin.

“You were at Ottarshall,” he said. “I am told. With Asta of the Staðfast. Will you tell me what you saw?”

So she told him.

She told it as she had told Steinar at Skarth. The store with its door broken and wide, and the two hundred dried cod in it, and the seal oil, and the barley, and the chest with the coin, all untouched. The four houses in the row. The name shouted through the door, Selhús-Gunnvor, the old house-name, the old way of saying it. The houses they had passed without going in. She told what she had seen, and then she told what she had been told, and said which was which, and she watched the Bishop listen.

He listened closely. He did not eat while she spoke. When she had done he sat and looked at the table for some time.

“They left the food,” he said.

“Yes.”

“All of it. With the door open.”

“Yes.”

“And took four people. By name.” He looked up. “What do you think it means?”

“I don’t know,” said Riarhin. “I think they weren’t there for the food.”

“No,” said the Bishop. “I think you are right.” He folded his hands. “I think that is exactly what it means. They were not there for the food, because what they serve does not want food.” He said it quite gently, as if he were explaining something to a child he was fond of. “A demon need not want grain. Why should it? It wants souls. It wants the children. It has always wanted the children, in every account we have of it, back to the oldest. They were there for what it wanted.” He looked at her with his pale tired eyes. “You have seen the proof of it, I think, better than anybody. I am grateful.”

Riarhin looked at him.

She wanted to say that it was not what she had meant. She had not meant anything. She had told him what she had seen, carefully, keeping it apart from what she had heard, and he had taken it and put it into a shape she had not given it, and the shape fitted so well that she could see half the room agreeing with it already.

“I didn’t see a demon,” she said.

“No,” said the Bishop kindly. “Nobody does.”

There was a woman at the far end of the table who had not spoken all evening.

Riarhin had noticed her early. She sat among the priests, but she did not look like a priest: a small spare woman of perhaps fifty in a dark robe with ink on the cuffs of it, with a thin careful face and short grey hair, and she had listened to everything without eating very much. While Riarhin had been telling about Ottarshall she had been watching Riarhin very closely, and once, when Riarhin said that’s what Ragna told me, I didn’t see it, she had lifted her head.

When the Bishop rose from the table, and the people of Hornvik began to go, the woman came down the room.

“You’re the one who was at Ottarshall,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Ilva.” She did not offer a hand. “I keep the Temple’s papers for the Bishop. The old ones.” She looked at Riarhin. “You said four times tonight I didn’t see that. That’s what I was told. I don’t know whose it was.” She paused. “Do you always do that?”

“I try to.”

“Hm.” Ilva looked at her a while. “Most people stop trying when it would be convenient to.”

Riarhin did not know what to say to that.

Ilva glanced over her shoulder at the head of the table, where the Bishop was speaking to Rhone, who had come in at the end of the meal and stood at his shoulder. Then she looked back.

“The woman at Ottarshall,” she said. “Ragna. She said the old man who was taken kept the count-stones.”

“Ulf. Yes.”

“What did he count?”

Riarhin looked at her.

“I don’t know,” she said. “Nobody told me. The stones are—” She tried to remember what Thora had said. “In a pouch. By the hall door. He kept them for the hall.”

“For how long?”

“I don’t know.”

“Who kept them before him?”

“I don’t know.”

Ilva nodded slowly, as if those were the answers she had expected.

“Would you find out?” she said. “If you can. When you go north. Or when you come back.” She hesitated, and Riarhin saw her hands move, under the edge of her sleeve, the fingers of the left touching the thumb one after another, quickly, as if she were counting something under the table that was not there. “It’s a small thing. I keep an old count. A long one. And there’s a place in it that—” She stopped. “It’s a small thing. I would like to know what the old man counted.”

Then she was gone back up the room to the priests, and Riarhin stood by the door and watched her go.

They got their safe passage in the morning.

It came from the Bishop, by way of Rhone, in the long house, in front of Orm. Rhone read it out to them from a paper, all of it, as he had read the warrant. The search sent from the stone at Kettlebeach might pass the Bishop’s column and go north on the lake road, unhindered by any officer of the Temple. It might have an escort of eight of the Bishop’s guard, under a captain named Doukas, as far as the column’s writ ran, which was to the boundary stone where the river road came out onto the high moor. It might draw food from the column’s stores for ten days.

In return, it would bring back what it found. Whatever it learned in the north about the Dogmen and what they served, it would bring first to the Bishop, before it took it anywhere else.

“First,” said Rhone. He looked at Orm over the paper. “Before the stone at Kettlebeach. Before the jarls. To the Bishop.”

Orm looked at him a long time.

“The stone sent us,” he said. “We go back to the stone.”

“You go back to the stone,” said Rhone. “By way of the Bishop. That is the condition. The Bishop is very generous. He is giving you food he could sell and men he needs.” He folded the paper. “He asks only to know.”

Riarhin, standing behind Orm, looked at the folded paper in Rhone’s hand and thought about the Bishop at supper, listening to her with his whole attention.

Orm looked at her over his shoulder. She did not know what he saw in her face. He turned back to Rhone.

“By way of the Bishop,” he said.

Rhone made a mark on the paper, and wrote Orm’s name beside it, and gave it to him.

Chapter 14 — What she sent him into

Skeggi kept the road at the head of the lake, and he would not take a step on it until he knew what they were carrying.

He lived alone in a turf hut on a knoll above the last of the ice, where the lake narrowed to a river and the river went up into the hills, and he had kept the road from there to the hills for forty years, Yrsa said, marking it with cairns in the autumn and clearing it in the spring and taking people over it in the winter when they had to go. He was very old, and very upright, with a white beard cut short and a sheepskin hood, and his left ear, when he pushed the hood back to look at them, was a ruin: a lump of scar where the ear should have been, puckered and shiny and white.

He looked at the five of them standing in the snow outside his door with the eight soldiers of the Bishop’s guard drawn up behind, and the sleds, and he said, “What’s on the sleds?”

“Food,” said Orm. “Blankets. Rope. Fuel.”

“What else?”

Orm hesitated.

Skeggi looked at him. Then he turned to go back inside.

“Wait,” said Riarhin.

He stopped in the doorway.

“There’s a woman with us who’s a prisoner,” said Riarhin. “From the raid on Ottarshall. She’s in my keeping, on the stone at Kettlebeach.” She did not look at Sifa. “There’s an Ykrul who came north looking for a mark on a door. There’s a cooper who speaks the old talk. We’re going north to find four people who were taken. We don’t know where they are. We don’t know who took them or why. We’ve got eight of the Bishop’s men as far as the boundary stone and after that we’ve got nobody.” She paused. “And I have a sword on my back that I’ve killed people with, and two knives, and a chisel that belonged to my grandmother, who I killed with my own hands this autumn.”

There was a silence. Behind her she heard one of the soldiers shift his feet.

Skeggi turned round in the doorway and looked at her. He looked at her for a long time, with his old eyes that had gone pale with snow-glare, and his ruined ear toward her as if he were listening with it.

“That’s the cargo,” he said.

“That’s all of it.”

“Good,” said Skeggi. “I’ll take you.”

He took them up the river road the next morning, ahead of the escort, with a staff in his hand and a pack on his back that he would not let anybody else carry. He walked as if the snow were a floor.

He talked, a little, at the halts. He did not talk about his ear. Riarhin did not ask. Eyvind had told her, on the voyage, that in the north you did not ask about a man’s scars unless he showed them to you on purpose, and Skeggi had pushed his hood back to look at them and then pulled it forward again, and that was not on purpose.

He talked about the road. He talked about the hills ahead, and the high moors beyond, and the old ways over them that nobody used any more because the people who had used them were gone. And on the first evening, by the fire in the lee of a rock, he talked about the old woman.

“There’s a fire,” he said, “up the side valley. A day off the road. Been there since the autumn. One fire.” He looked at Temür. “One of yours. An old woman. No horses. She came over the moors from the east before the first snow, on a horse with a young man, and the young man went on down toward the coast and the horse went with him and she stayed.” He poked at the fire. “I went up to her. Twice. Took her fish. She took it. She doesn’t talk. She sits.”

Temür was very still.

“Borte,” he said.

It took them most of the next day to reach it. Skeggi led them off the river road into a narrow side valley full of birch, where the snow lay deep and soft and the sleds bogged, and they left the sleds at the valley mouth with the soldiers and went on with frames. Riarhin’s leg did not like it. She went slowly at the back with her staff and Sifa beside her, and Sifa did not say anything and did not offer her arm, and did not go ahead either.

The fire was in a hollow under a cliff at the valley head, sheltered from the north.

It was a small fire. It was banked low, the embers raked together under a skin of ash, giving out a dull steady warmth and almost no light. Round it there was a little shelter of birch poles and felt, very neat, and a heap of firewood under a skin, and a pot, and a horse-hide spread on the snow. On the horse-hide, wrapped in felt and furs, an old woman was sitting cross-legged with her hands in her lap, looking at the fire.

She did not look up when they came. She looked at the fire.

Temür went down into the hollow ahead of the others. He went slowly. He stopped at the edge of the horse-hide, and stood, and said something in Ykrul that Riarhin did not quite catch, a form of greeting, old, the kind you used to the very old.

The old woman lifted her head.

She was tiny. She was the smallest grown person Riarhin had seen, a little dried bundle of a woman with a brown face folded in a thousand creases, and her hair was white and very long, in two braids that lay in her lap like ropes, threaded at the ends with small bones. Her eyes were black and bright.

She looked at Temür. She looked at him the way, Riarhin thought, he had described: as if she had not seen him before, or had seen something new in him.

Then she looked past him, up the slope, at the rest of them. At Orm and Bródd and Skeggi. At Riarhin. At Sifa.

She looked at Sifa a long time.

Then she took up a stick from beside her and raked the embers of the fire together, carefully, and drew the ash up over them, and laid two pieces of wood on top crosswise, and pressed them down, so that the fire burned lower still. She did it with great attention, as if it were the most important thing in the world.

Temür watched her do it. Riarhin saw his face. He looked as if he had watched her do it a hundred times, all his life, and had never once been glad.

“Sit,” said Borte, in Ykrul.

They sat. Not all of them. Skeggi stayed on the slope above, and Orm with him, and Bródd. Temür sat on the horse-hide in front of the old woman. Riarhin sat at the edge of it because Temür looked round at her once, briefly, and then away, and she understood that he wanted somebody there who could follow it and was not Borte. Sifa sat behind her in the snow.

They spoke in Ykrul. Riarhin could follow some of it. Not all. They spoke fast, at first, and in a way of speaking she did not know well, older and more crowded than the riders’ speech she had learned from Na’ar, full of words that meant one thing at a fire and another on a horse. She caught pieces. She caught enough.

Temür told her about the coast. He told her about Hvalgard and the burned hall and the four taken, and the mark. He told her that nobody had seen the mark. He told her about the woman in the net. He told it all quickly and plainly, the way a scout reports, and then he stopped, and said: Now tell me.

Borte looked at the fire.

I dreamed a winter, she said. In the autumn. The night of the first frost. A winter that did not end. The snow in it was hungry. It came out of the north on the wind, and the wind was following something, and the thing it was following was a voice. A voice saying names. She stopped. I could not see the voice. I heard the names. I heard it say them one by one, slowly, the way you say names over a grave, and at the end of each one it waited, as if for an answer.

What names?

Borte looked at him.

Ours, she said.

Temür did not move.

My mother’s, said Borte. My mother’s mother’s. Back. A long way back. Names I know from the songs. Names nobody has said aloud since before the crossing. She looked at the fire. And yours. At the end. Yours.

The fire ticked under its ash.

Not all the riders, said Borte. Not all the people. I want you to hear me. Not the whole of the crossing. One line. Ours. A few households, from before the crossing, and their children, and their children. Our line. She lifted her hands from her lap and let them fall. The voice said them. It said them as if it had a right to them. As if it had them written down somewhere, and had taken out the list, and was checking it.

And you woke up.

I woke up with my hands full of ice, said Borte. And my mouth tasting of iron. And you were asleep across the fire from me, and I looked at you. She was quiet. And then I sent you north.

Temür said nothing for a long time.

Then he said, very quietly, You knew.

Borte did not answer.

You knew it was me, said Temür. You knew it was our line. You heard my name in the dream. You heard something in the north say my name as if it owned it. His voice did not rise. And you put me on a horse and sent me north. Toward it. And you told me nothing. You said, go and look.

Yes.

Why?

Borte raked the fire again, though it did not need it.

Because if I had told you, she said, you would have come north looking for a thing that wanted you. And you would have found it. Things like that are found by the people who look for them. She looked at him. I wanted you to come north looking for a mark on a door. I wanted you to see what you saw with your own eyes, and not with mine. I wanted you to come back and tell me what was there before I told you what I was afraid of. She paused. Shapes travel. If I give you the shape of a thing, you will see that shape everywhere. I did not want you to see a shape. I wanted you to see the north.

And if I had died?

Then you would have died looking at the north, said Borte, and not at my dream.

Temür stood up.

He stood up very fast, all at once, and the horse-hide slid under him, and he took two steps back from the fire and stood in the snow with his fists closed at his sides. He said something. He said it so fast and so low that Riarhin lost it entirely, but she did not need the words. She heard the shape of it. She had heard people say that to their mothers.

Borte sat on the horse-hide and let him say it.

When he had finished he stood breathing in the snow. Then he turned his head and looked at Riarhin.

You heard, he said, in Ykrul. You heard her. Tell her.

Riarhin looked at him.

Tell her what? she said.

That it was wrong. You’re outside it. You can see. She sent me into a thing she knew wanted me and she told me nothing. His voice cracked. Tell her it was wrong.

Riarhin sat at the edge of the horse-hide.

She thought of a great many things. She thought of Farieth in the measured room, saying go home, granddaughter, you’ll have what you wanted. She thought of Vesket telling her almost nothing for a month on the road, for reasons that had seemed good to him. She had done it herself, more than once, to people she rode with. She had told herself it was kindness every time.

I can’t, she said.

Temür stared at her.

It’s not mine, said Riarhin. I’ve done what she did. I’ve kept a thing from people I was with because I thought knowing would hurt them more than not knowing. I thought I was right. Sometimes I was. She looked at him. I can’t tell you she was wrong. I can’t tell you she was right. If I say she meant well, that’s nothing. You know she meant well. That isn’t what you’re asking.

Temür looked at her a long moment. Then he looked at Borte.

Is there more? he said.

There was more, but not much.

Borte gave it to him slowly, sitting by the banked fire, with her hands in her lap. She gave him pieces of a song: the crossing song, the one the riders sang about the water, which Riarhin had heard Na’ar hum once on a deck and had not known the words of. Borte did not sing it. She said it, flatly, a few lines at a time, and stopped, and said other lines. The lines surprised her. They were about a cold place before the water. About a house with a mark on its door, and people taken out of it in the night, and a long walk on the ice in a line, roped together. About some of the line slipping the rope in a storm, and running.

That is not in the song we sing, said Temür.

No, said Borte. That is in the song my grandmother sang to me once when she thought I was asleep, and never again. She looked at the fire. I don’t know if it is true. I don’t know if it is ours. I know the dream said our names, and I know that song had a mark on a door in it, and I know the mark I gave you is the one my grandmother cut in the ash of the fire with her finger when she had finished singing, and then rubbed out.

Temür looked at the bone in his hand. He had taken it out without seeming to notice.

What else? he said.

Borte hesitated. It was the first time Riarhin had seen her hesitate.

The voice in the dream, she said. It said the names and waited. After every one. As if it were asking for an answer. She looked up at him. If you hear a name said to you in the north, your name, the way that voice said it, said as if it is owned, and then a silence after it, waiting—

What?

Don’t answer it, said Borte. I don’t know why. I don’t know what it is. I only know that in the dream I wanted to answer, every time, I wanted it so much that my mouth opened, and I knew that if I did, something would close. She looked at the fire. That is all I know. I don’t know what is in the north. I don’t know what wants us. I don’t know what it will cost you to find it. She lifted her eyes to his. I know you have chosen already.

Temür stood in the snow.

He was quiet for a very long time. The fire ticked. Up on the slope, Riarhin heard Orm say something to Skeggi, low, and Skeggi answer.

Yes, said Temür at last. I have.

He did not sit down again. He did not go to her. He stood where he was and looked at the old woman on the horse-hide, his grandmother’s sister, and then he turned and walked up the slope out of the hollow, past Riarhin, past Sifa, past Orm and Bródd and Skeggi, and went on down the valley toward the sleds alone.

Borte watched him go.

She did not call after him. She took up her stick and raked the embers together again, slowly, and drew the ash up over them, and sat looking at the fire.

Riarhin got up. Her leg had stiffened while she sat, and she got up badly, with the staff, and stood at the edge of the horse-hide.

Borte looked up at her.

You rode with one of us, she said. Once. I can hear it in how you talk.

Yes.

Is he dead?

Yes.

Borte nodded slowly, as if that were the usual end of riding with one of them.

Then go after my grandson, she said, and don’t tell him he reminds you of anybody.

Riarhin looked at her. Borte looked back with her bright black eyes, and then down at the fire again, and did not say anything else.

Chapter 15 — Where the copy stops

They took two days to come back from Borte’s fire, collecting the sleds where they had left them. The Bishop’s baggage came up the lake road to Dalsendi on the evening they reached it, and with it, sitting on a case in the back of the last wagon with a blanket over her knees, came Ilva.

Dalsendi was the last place on the lake. It was where Yrsa’s sister lived, and four other households, in a huddle of turf houses at the foot of the river road, and the column had come up to it in two days behind its escort and made it into a staging post. There were tents on the shore. There were horse lines. There was a long timber shed being put up by soldiers on the flat ground above the houses, with a great deal of shouting, for stores. Riarhin, coming down the river road at dusk with her leg on fire and Sifa beside her, saw the wagons come in along the shore in a line, lanterns swinging, and saw a small figure in a dark robe climb stiffly down from the last of them, and stand, and look round.

Temür had not spoken all the way down the valley. He had walked ahead of them, alone, a long way ahead, and at the halts he had sat apart. Nobody had gone to him. Riarhin had thought about it, and remembered Borte’s bright black eyes, and had not gone either.

Ilva found them in the stores shed an hour after dark. She came in out of the snow with a lantern and a leather case under her arm and looked round at them where they sat among the sacks: Orm and Bródd at the brazier, Sifa against the wall, Temür apart in the corner with his back to them, Riarhin with her leg up on a keg and Asta’s salve open beside her.

“I said I’d like to know what the old man counted,” said Ilva. “I have something to show you instead.”

She showed them on top of a barrel of salt fish, under the lantern, with the soldiers outside still shouting about the roof.

The leather case was old and worn soft and it opened with two brass hooks. Inside it, wrapped in linen, there was a roll of vellum, and Ilva took it out with both hands, the way Vesket had taken his rods out of their hollows, and laid it on the barrel-head and unrolled it a little way and weighted the edges with two stones from her pocket that she had plainly brought for the purpose.

The vellum was brown with age and very fine, and it was covered with writing. Two kinds. Down the left side of each panel there were marks Riarhin did not know: rows of short straight strokes, upright and slanting, crossing and branching, cut rather than written, as if somebody had copied them from wood or stone with a pen and tried to make the pen behave like a knife. Down the right side, in a neat brown southern hand, there was writing in the Temple’s tongue, and between the two, in red, in smaller letters, a third hand had written notes.

“The Long Dark Oath,” said Ilva. “Or what the Temple calls it. The Temple’s copy. Made at Thepyrgos four hundred years ago by a scribe who was sent north with an embassy and brought it back.” She touched the left-hand column. “That is the oath-script. The old northern script, the way it was cut on the staves. He copied it as well as he could. That,” she touched the right, “is his rendering into the Temple’s tongue. That,” the red, “is everybody since who has tried to understand it.”

“Including you,” said Riarhin.

“Including me.” Ilva did not smile. “I have read it more times than anybody living. I have read it every winter for twenty-two years.” She looked at the vellum. “There are ten staves. The oath was cut on ten staves, in a ring, at the place where it was sworn. Every account agrees on that. Every copy. The Temple’s own commentaries say ten. And the scribe, in his rendering, at the start, says: these are the ten staves of the oath sworn in the north, as I copied them.”

She unrolled the vellum further. It was long. The stones slid. Bródd, without being asked, came and held the end down with his big hands.

“One,” said Ilva. She pointed at a mark at the head of the first panel, a single upright stroke with a crossbar, set apart from the rest. “Each stave begins with its number. The count-mark. One.” She moved her finger. “Two. Three.” She went down the vellum, panel by panel, and under the edge of her sleeve Riarhin saw the fingers of her other hand moving, quickly, touching the thumb one after another as she counted. “Four. Five. Six. Seven. Eight.”

She stopped.

The eighth panel was long. It ran down the vellum for the length of a forearm, the strokes of the oath-script dense and close, the brown rendering beside them, the red notes crowding the margin. At the bottom of it the oath-script ended in a short line of strokes, and then, without any space at all, on the same line, close up against the last stroke of the eighth, there was another count-mark.

Riarhin looked at it.

It was not the mark for nine. She did not know the marks, but she had just watched Ilva count eight of them, and she could see how they went: a stroke for one, two strokes for two, the crossbars added and the strokes grouped. She could see that this one had too many strokes for nine.

“Ten,” said Ilva.

Nobody said anything.

“There is no nine,” said Ilva. “There never was. Not on this vellum.” She put her finger beside the place where the eighth ended and the tenth began, not touching it. “Look at it. Look at the vellum. If a panel had been cut out, there would be an edge. There would be stitching, where the two pieces were joined again. There’s none. If it had been scraped and written over, you could see it. Hold it to the lamp and the scraped place is thinner, it lets the light through. I’ve held it to the lamp. I’ve held it to the sun. It’s one skin. It was never anything else.” She looked up at them. “He wrote eight, and then he wrote ten. On the same line. Without lifting his pen, very nearly. And in his rendering, he does the same.” She moved her finger to the brown writing on the right. “The eighth stave, he writes, and then the eighth stave, and then: the tenth stave. He doesn’t say the ninth is lost. He doesn’t say the ninth is illegible. He doesn’t say anything at all.”

“He forgot,” said Orm.

Ilva looked at him.

“He was a very careful man,” she said. “I have twenty-two years of his hand. He corrects himself when he gets a letter wrong. He notes it in the margin when the stave he was copying was split, or weathered, or hard to read. He counts his lines. He says, at the start, ten staves.” She looked back at the vellum. “He didn’t forget.”

“What does it say?” said Riarhin. “The rest of it. Around the gap.”

Ilva read it to them. She read the end of the eighth stave, slowly, putting it into the northern speech from the brown southern rendering as she went, with her finger under the line.

“And her name is a word of this oath,” she read, “and it shall be spoken by the sworn and by none else, for whoso speaks it swears, and whoso swears what he may not keep is forsworn in the same breath, and the winter takes the forsworn.” She paused. “That’s the end of the eighth.” She moved her finger across the gap. “The tenth stave. This oath is cut in the ice before nine witnesses and the tenth is the ice itself, and what is cut here holds while the ice holds, and the ice remembers.” She stopped. “That’s the beginning of the tenth.”

Nobody spoke. The soldiers outside had stopped shouting. Somebody somewhere laughed.

Riarhin found she had looked at Sifa.

Sifa was sitting against the wall of the shed with her knees drawn up and her hands in her sleeves. She had not moved while Ilva read. She was looking at the floor. She lifted one hand from her sleeve, as Riarhin watched, and touched her jaw, under the ear, and along to the chin, slowly, and put it back.

“Her name,” said Riarhin. “Whose?”

Ilva did not answer at once.

“The scribe doesn’t write it,” she said. “In his rendering. Wherever it comes in the oath-script, he writes the Mother, or she, or the Hall. In the margin, at the first place, he writes: I have not set her name down, and I will not.” She looked at the vellum. “Nobody has. In four hundred years of notes. Not one.”

Bródd was looking at the end of the eighth stave, where his hands held the vellum flat. He had been looking at it for some time.

“That,” he said. He did not touch it. “Before the gap. The last line of the eighth. The way it ends.” He frowned. “Read it again. In the old script. Can you?”

Ilva could, roughly. She read the oath-script aloud, slowly, the sounds coming out stiff and strange in her southern mouth, a line of the throat-speech that Riarhin had heard from Sifa and Bródd and Temür and that sounded, from Ilva, like a careful child reading a prayer.

Bródd listened. His face changed.

“My grandmother said that,” he said. “Not that. Something like it. Near it.” He frowned at the vellum. “When she told the long one. The crossing. At the end, before the last part, she used to stop and say something like that. About the name. And then she’d say—” He stopped.

“What?”

“And when the count is full,” said Bródd slowly. “That’s what she said next. Every time. And when the count is full. And then she’d go on to the last part, about the ice remembering.” He looked at Ilva. “I never knew what count. I thought it was just a thing you said. Like and then.”

Ilva was very still.

“When the count is full,” she said. “And then what? What came after? Before the ice?”

Bródd shook his head.

“Nothing,” he said. “Nothing that I remember. She said and when the count is full, and stopped, like that, and drank, and then said the last part.” He looked unhappy. “I was small. I might have it wrong. I might be putting it there because you read me the rest.”

“Temür,” said Riarhin.

Temür had not moved from his corner. He turned his head.

“Borte’s song,” said Riarhin, in Ykrul. “The one she said, not sang. About the mark on the door, and the walk on the ice. Was there anything like this?”

Temür looked at her a long time. Then he got up, slowly, and came to the barrel and looked down at the vellum, at the two kinds of writing, at the place where the eighth stave met the tenth. He could not read any of it. He looked at it anyway.

“There was a line,” he said, in the northern speech, for the others. “Near the end. When some of them slipped the rope and ran.” He frowned. “They ran before the count was taken. That is what she said. Before the count.” He looked at Bródd. “Is it the same count?”

“I don’t know,” said Bródd.

“Nor do I,” said Temür.

They looked at each other across the barrel. Then both of them looked at Ilva.

Ilva looked at the gap in the vellum.

Riarhin saw her want it. She saw it plainly, in the way Ilva’s hand had lifted from the barrel-head toward the empty place between eight and ten and stopped a finger’s breadth above it. Twenty-two years. Ilva had a cooper’s half-remembered phrase from his dead grandmother and an Ykrul’s half-remembered line from an old woman’s song, and they both had count in them, and they both sat at the edge of the place where the ninth stave should have been, and Riarhin could see the shape Ilva could make out of them if she let herself. Something about a count. Something about it being full, and what happened then. It would fit. It would fit so well.

Ilva took her hand away.

“No,” she said.

They looked at her.

“I can’t,” said Ilva. “I don’t know what the ninth stave said. I know it isn’t here. I know a careful man left it out on purpose. I know two people who were not in the room with each other remember the word count near the end of two different tellings of something.” She rolled the vellum up, slowly, from the bottom. “That’s what I know. If I write anything else in the gap, I’ll have made it. Then the next person who reads this will find it in my hand and believe it, the way I believed his.”

“It might be true,” said Orm.

“It might,” said Ilva. “That’s why I won’t write it.”

Rhone came at midnight.

He came into the stores shed with two soldiers and a clerk carrying a writing case, and he did not read out a paper. He stood at the brazier and looked at Ilva, who was sitting on a sack beside the barrel with the leather case on her knees.

“The Bishop is sending a second detachment north when he comes up,” he said. “Forty men. To the boundary stone, to see the ground. Before they go, I need a statement. From you. In your hand. With your seal.” He nodded to the clerk, who opened the writing case. “That the Temple’s copy of the oath is the Temple’s copy, made at Thepyrgos and kept in the archive since. That it is the oath sworn in the north. That the Temple, as keeper of the oath’s record, has standing to read it and be heard on it. That is all.”

Ilva looked at him.

“That is not all,” she said.

“It is all I am asking.”

“You want me to say it is the oath.” She did not get up. “The whole oath. Ten staves. So that the Bishop can read it at the glacier and say this is what was sworn, and I hold it.”

Rhone was silent.

“It isn’t,” said Ilva. “It’s nine staves out of ten. I’ll write that it’s the Temple’s copy. I’ll write that it was made at Thepyrgos and has been in the archive since. I’ll write that it contains eight staves and a tenth, in the scribe’s hand, and that the ninth was not copied, and that I don’t know what it said.” She looked at the clerk’s open case. “I’ll seal that.”

“That is not what the Bishop needs.”

“It’s what I can establish.”

Rhone looked at her for a long moment. His clean narrow face did not change at all.

“Then write it,” he said.

She wrote it. She wrote it on the barrel-head, by lamplight, with the clerk’s pen, in a small precise hand, and Riarhin watched her write every word and saw that she did not write one word more than she had said. She signed it. She pressed her seal into a drop of wax at the foot of it. She gave it to Rhone.

Rhone read it. All of it, aloud, in his level voice, to the end. Then he folded it.

“The second detachment will go without you,” he said. “You will remain at Dalsendi with the baggage. The Bishop will decide, when he comes up, whether you go north at all.” He looked at the leather case on her knees. “The vellum remains in your keeping. It does not leave the column.”

Then he went out, and the soldiers with him.

Ilva sat on the sack with the case on her knees and looked at the door.

“You knew he’d do that,” said Riarhin.

“Yes.”

“You could have written what he wanted.”

“Yes.” Ilva opened the case. She took out of it a single sheet of paper, folded twice, that had been lying under the vellum in the linen. She unfolded it. It was covered in her own small precise hand: two columns, the oath-script on the left, copied stroke by stroke, and her own northern rendering on the right. The end of the eighth stave. The count-mark for ten. The beginning of the tenth. And between them, a space the width of three fingers, and nothing in it.

She folded it again and held it out to Riarhin.

“He said the vellum doesn’t leave the column,” said Ilva. “He didn’t say anything about my hand.”

Riarhin looked at the paper.

“Take it north,” said Ilva. “If you find anything up there that has a count in it. Or an ending. Or anybody who can read the old script on the staves themselves.” She paused. “Bring it back. Or send for me.” She looked at the door Rhone had gone out of. “I’ll find a reason to come.”

Chapter 16 — Eight shields to the marker

The order caught them on the third day above Dalsendi, a mile short of the boundary stone, in a wind that had been rising since dawn.

They had made good time. Skeggi had brought them up the river road out of the lake country into the hills, where the valley narrowed and the river went under thick grey ice and the road climbed beside it in long steps, from one cairn to the next. The escort marched behind the sleds. Eight of them, under Captain Doukas, a square silent man with a grey moustache and a southern face burned dark by northern weather, and they carried their shields on their backs on the march, round shields painted white, and on each one a golden candle. Eight shields in a line in the snow. Riarhin had counted them so often she had stopped needing to.

The rider came up the road behind them at noon, on a shaggy northern pony that was blowing hard, and Riarhin knew before he reached them what he was. She knew from the way Doukas turned and watched him come and did not call a halt and did not keep marching either.

The rider gave Doukas a paper. Doukas read it standing in the road in the wind, holding it in both gloved hands so that it would not tear. He read it twice. Then he folded it and put it inside his coat and came along the line of sleds to where Orm was hauling the lead one with Temür.

“Halt,” he said.

He read it to them at the cairn by the road, with the wind pulling at the paper. He read it in the southern tongue first, out of what Riarhin thought must be habit, and then again slowly in a heavy careful northern, with his finger under the line.

Rhone’s letter reported a complaint lodged with the Bishop’s Justiciar: an object of idolatrous worship concealed and removed from Hornvik’s circle by its keeper, aided by travellers under the Bishop’s safe passage. Until the hearing, the named travellers could not leave the Bishop’s column. The search was to return with its escort to Dalsendi and wait eight days, perhaps twelve, for the Bishop.

It named Riarhin. It named Temür. It named, as their keeper, Orm.

Doukas folded the paper again.

“That is the order,” he said.

Nobody answered for a moment. The wind came down the valley and threw snow along the road between them.

“Eight days,” said Orm. “Twelve. Back to Dalsendi.” His voice was very quiet. “Then up again. Six more days on this road. Near three weeks, if he comes when he says.” He looked at Doukas. “There are four people in the north. Somebody has them. It’s been two months.”

“I know,” said Doukas.

“Then why—”

“I didn’t write it.” Doukas did not raise his voice. “I carry it. It is my order. The Justiciar writes them, the Bishop signs them, I carry them. That is what the writ is.” He looked up the road. “And my writ runs to the stone. Not past it.”

Orm stared at him.

“You’ll take us back,” he said.

“I will take you back.”

“Or what? You’ll make us?” Orm’s face had gone dark red under the beard. “You’ve eight men. We’ve five and two sleds. If we walk on up that road to the stone and past it, what will you do? Put your spears in us? In the Bishop’s safe passage?” He laughed, short and hard. “The Bishop gave us you to keep us safe. And now you’ll keep us safe all the way back down the hill so a man who burns bells can ask us about a bell.” He spat in the snow. “That’s not an escort. That’s a fence. That’s a southern coward with a paper.”

Doukas did not move.

Behind him, in the road, the eight shields were drawn up in their line, and the men under them were watching. Riarhin looked at them. She had been looking at them for three days and had begun to know them: the two brothers from somewhere on the southern coast who argued about everything; the boy with the chilblains; the older man who sang under his breath on the march. They were standing very still. And they were not looking at Orm. They were looking at their captain.

Riarhin saw Orm see it.

She saw him stop. He had more to say and he did not say it. He stood in the road with his fists closed, breathing hard, and looked past Doukas at the eight men behind him, at their faces, at the way they were watching their captain to see what he would do.

He shut his mouth.

“The stone,” said Riarhin.

They looked at her. She had been standing a little apart, by the cairn, with her staff, and she came forward now, slowly, through the snow.

“Your writ runs to the stone,” she said to Doukas. “The order says return to Dalsendi. With the escort.” She looked at him. “Where are we now?”

Doukas looked at her. Something moved under the grey moustache.

“A mile short of the stone,” he said.

“Inside your writ.”

“Inside my writ.”

“So you can order us back. Here. Now.” She looked up the road, where it climbed beside the ice into the blowing snow. “If we were past the stone, you couldn’t. Your writ wouldn’t run. You’d have no order to carry. You could only report that we’d gone beyond it before it reached us.”

Doukas was silent.

“How long did it take him to find us?” said Riarhin. She nodded at the rider, who was standing by his blown pony at the back of the line. “From Dalsendi. Three days?”

“Not quite two.”

“And the Justiciar wrote it the morning after we left.”

“The evening.”

“So it was written when we were already on the road. Under your escort. Under the Bishop’s safe passage.” She looked at him. “Going north. As the Bishop agreed. With food for ten days.”

“Yes.”

“And the order says return to Dalsendi with the escort.” She waited. “It doesn’t say anything about what you’re to do if we won’t.”

Doukas looked at her for a long time. The wind blew snow between them.

“No,” he said. “It does not.”

“Then here’s what I’m asking.” Riarhin did not look at Orm. She did not look at anybody but Doukas. “You read us the order. Here. You did. We heard it. Write that we heard it. Write the cairn, and the hour.” She paused. “Then march us to the stone. It’s a mile. You’re going there anyway, your orders said to see us to it before they said to bring us back. March us to the stone in front of your men, the way you’ve marched us for three days. And at the stone, we go on. And you stop.”

“And then?”

“And then you go back to Dalsendi, with your escort, as ordered, and you tell the Justiciar that you read us his order a mile short of the stone and we refused it, and that your writ ran out before you could do anything about it.” She looked at him steadily. “Which will be true.”

Doukas looked at her.

“He’ll have my command,” he said.

“Will he?”

Doukas did not answer that. He looked back down the road, at his eight men drawn up behind him in the snow with their white shields. He looked at them for a long time. Then he looked at the rider by his blown pony, who had come a long way to find him and was cold and tired and did not, Riarhin thought, care in the least what happened next.

“He might,” said Doukas. “He might not. The Bishop is fair.” He took the folded order out of his coat again, and looked at it, and put it back. “And he will remember that I did not put a spear in anybody under the Bishop’s passage to bring them back to answer a complaint about a bell.” He turned. “Kostas. Paper.”

The older man who sang on the march came forward with a writing tablet. Doukas wrote on it, slowly, in the southern script, with the wind tugging the tablet, and showed it to Riarhin. She could not read it. She nodded anyway.

“The cairn,” he said. “The hour. That you heard it read. That you said you would not go back.” He handed the tablet to Kostas. “Form up. We march to the stone.”

They marched to the stone.

It was a mile, uphill, into the wind. The escort marched behind the sleds as they had marched for three days, eight white shields in a line, and nobody spoke. Orm hauled the lead sled with Temür. He did not look at Doukas. Once, when the road steepened and the sled slewed on the ice and Orm went down on one knee, it was the boy with the chilblains who stepped out of the line and caught the sled’s back and held it until Orm was up again, and then stepped back into his place.

The boundary stone stood on a shoulder of the hill where the valley opened out into a wide high moor, white and featureless and running away north into cloud. It was a single grey stone, taller than a man, leaning, with marks cut into it on the south face that Skeggi said were Linn and on the north face that Skeggi said were not, and beside it, on the snow, a cairn.

Skeggi stopped at the cairn.

He had said he would. He had said it on the first night: to the stone and not past it. I’ve never been past it. Nobody I know has. He stood beside it now with his staff in his hand and his ruined ear turned to the north wind and looked out at the moor, and Riarhin saw his face, and saw that he was afraid, and that he had been afraid for three days, the whole way up, and had not said so.

“That’s mine,” he said. He touched the stone with his staff. “Everything this side of it. I know every stone. That side—” He looked at the moor. “There used to be a road. My grandfather said. It went across to the far edge and on into the hills, and there were marks on it, cairns, but not like ours.” He shook his head. “Nobody uses it. The snow’s had it.
