You’ll want somebody who knows it.”

They looked at each other. Orm, and Bródd, and Temür, and Riarhin. Doukas stood with his men at a little distance, waiting.

Then they all looked at Sifa.

Sifa was standing at the back of the second sled with her hands in her sleeves. She had walked the whole way up from Dalsendi beside the sleds and had not spoken once. She was looking at the soldiers.

“Sifa,” said Riarhin.

Sifa did not look at her.

“If you want to go back,” said Riarhin, “you can.”

Orm turned his head sharply. Riarhin did not look at him.

“The order said all of us,” she said to Sifa. “Back to Dalsendi. Under the escort. If you go with them, you go back under the Bishop’s passage, and they can’t touch you; I’ll answer for you there as I would here. You can wait at Dalsendi till we come back. Or till the stone at Kettlebeach says something else.” She paused. “I won’t make you go north. I said I’d keep you. I didn’t say where.”

Sifa looked at her then.

She looked at her for a long time, with the flat yellow-brown eyes, and Riarhin looked back and did not know what Sifa was seeing.

Then Sifa looked at the soldiers. At the eight white shields with their golden candles. At Doukas with his grey moustache, and the boy with the chilblains, and the older man who sang. She looked at them for as long as she had looked at Riarhin.

Then she turned her back on them.

She walked past the stone. She walked twenty paces out onto the moor, into the wind, in the deep snow, and stopped, and stood looking north, and the wind blew her borrowed coat flat against her thin back. Then she crouched. She put her bare cracked hand flat on the snow, and moved it, slowly, from side to side, as if she were feeling for something under it. She moved forward a few paces and did it again.

She stood up. She looked back at them over her shoulder. She lifted one hand from her sleeve and touched her jaw, under the ear, and along.

“This way,” she said. “Come on.”

They went.

They went past the stone, one by one, the two sleds and the four of them hauling and Sifa ahead, out onto the high white moor into the wind. Riarhin went last. She went slowly, with the staff, her leg aching, and at the stone she stopped for a moment and put her hand on it, on the north face, on the marks that Skeggi said were not Linn. They were very worn. She could feel them under her glove, a row of short cut strokes, upright and slanting, like the strokes on Ilva’s vellum.

Then she went on.

She looked back once, a long way out, when the stone had become a small grey upright on the white shoulder of the hill. They were still there. Skeggi by the cairn, with his staff, very small. And beside him, in a line in the snow, eight white shields, with Doukas at the end of them. They had not turned to go back down. They were standing where they had stopped, watching.

She watched them for a moment, over her shoulder, from the moor. They grew smaller. The snow came between them, and thinned, and came again. The last time she saw them they were still standing, eight specks of white in a line on the grey hill, and one dark one at the end, and none of them had moved, and then the snow closed in and they were gone.

Part III — Those who are counted

Chapter 17 — The marked road

On the far side of the moor the land fell away into a country of frozen water, and Sifa found the road through it by touch.

It took them two days to cross the moor. What Riarhin kept of it was the wind and the white, and Sifa ahead of them in the snow, stopping every few hundred paces to crouch and lay her bare hand flat on the surface and move it from side to side. She was feeling for the road. Under the snow, somewhere, there were stones laid in a line, and Sifa’s hand could find them, or the hollow over them, or something about the way the snow lay along them that none of the rest of them could see.

At the halts she did not explain. She ate what she was given and sat with her back to the wind and her hands in her sleeves and looked north.

On the third morning they came down off the moor into the water country.

It was a wide low basin, ringed by hills, and it was full of ice. Lakes, Riarhin thought at first, a great many small lakes joined together; then she saw that they were a river, or many rivers, a braid of channels running between long low islands of rock and birch scrub, all of it frozen and white under snow, all of it running away north toward a range of dark hills on the far side of the basin. There was no road that she could see. There was ice, and islands, and more ice.

But there were marks.

Sifa showed them. On the first island, at the edge of the ice, there was a post. It was a short thick post of grey wood, weathered silver, set upright in a cairn of stones, and cut into it, near the top, there were strokes. Short straight cuts, upright and slanting. Riarhin had seen strokes like them on Ilva’s vellum and on the north face of the boundary stone.

Sifa put her hand on the cuts. She traced them with one finger, slowly, from top to bottom, the way Riarhin had seen people read with their fingers in the dark.

“Road,” she said. She pointed out across the ice to the next island, where Riarhin, squinting, could just make out another post. “That way. Not straight. There.” She pointed again, at a slight angle. “Water under. Fast. The ice is thin where it goes fast.” She touched the cuts again. “It says. How far. Where.”

“Who cut it?” said Orm.

Sifa looked at him.

“Us,” she said.

She spaced them out on the ice. She did it carefully, without hurry, before they set foot on it: Orm and Temür with the lead sled, then twenty paces, then Bródd and Riarhin with the second, then twenty paces, then herself at the back where she could see them all. She made each of them hold a line, tied from sled to sled, slack, running back along the ice between them. She made them walk exactly where she pointed, and no other place, even when the other place looked the same.

“If one goes in,” she said, “the others hold. Not pull. Hold. Lie down and hold.”

It went well. It went well for most of the morning. They went from post to post across the white basin, from island to island, not in a straight line but in long slow curves, bending east and west along routes that Riarhin could not see any reason for and did not question. The ice was hard under the snow. It made no sound. The sleds ran easily.

The other sled came up behind them an hour before noon.

They had seen it on the moor the day before, a long way back: a dark dot on the white with a smaller dot hauling it. It had been following their track. Sifa had looked at it for a long time and said nothing, and Riarhin had watched her look, and when they halted that night Sifa had gone back along their trail a little way in the dusk and stood, and come back, and said only: One man. Ours. Going home.

Now he was close. He came down off the moor onto the ice of the basin behind them, hauling a long low sled piled high with sacks and bundles under a lashed hide, and he was alone. He was a big man in furs, broad, stooping into the harness, and when he looked up and saw them ahead on the ice he stopped.

Sifa turned round.

She called to him across the ice. It was the throat-speech, the old talk, and Riarhin caught nothing of it. The big man called back. They went back and forth across the distance a while, short phrases, and Bródd, beside Riarhin, listened with his head bent.

“She says follow her marks,” said Bródd. “He says he knows the marks. She says follow her marks anyway, the water’s high this winter, it’s been moving. He says he’s crossed here every winter since he was a boy.” Bródd paused. “He says he’s Garrak of Brakka’s hearth and he’ll cross where he likes.” He paused again. “He says some other things about who she’s walking with.”

“Did he say that?” said Riarhin. “Or did he say something worse and you made it that?”

Bródd looked at her. Then he looked at Temür, ahead, who had turned and was listening too. Temür shrugged slightly: he had not followed.

“He called us something,” said Bródd. “I don’t know the northern for it. It means a thing you find on the beach after a storm that you shouldn’t eat.”

Riarhin very nearly laughed.

Garrak did not follow Sifa’s marks.

They watched him not do it. They had gone on from the post, and he came out onto the ice behind them, and at the second island, where Sifa had taken them east in a long curve round the end of a reach of open grey ice, he looked at the curve and at the reach, and Riarhin saw him decide. The reach was shorter. It went straight to the next post. It looked exactly like all the rest of the ice they had walked on all morning.

He took the reach.

Sifa stopped. She turned round and called to him, sharply, and he did not answer.

He was halfway across when the ice broke.

It did not break under him. He was walking in the harness ahead of the sled and the ice held him. It broke behind him, under the sled, where the weight was. There was a sound like a stick snapping, very loud in the silence of the basin, and then another, and the back of the sled went down.

It went down at an angle, tilting, the rear of it dropping through a black hole in the white that opened under it like a mouth, and the front of it reared up on the edge of the ice, and the sacks and bundles on it slid backward under their lashings and the whole weight of them went down into the water at the back and pulled. Garrak was in the harness. The harness was round his chest and shoulders. When the sled went down it took him with it, backward, off his feet, and dragged him along the ice on his back toward the hole, and he went in to his waist, and stopped, because the front of the sled had jammed across the edge of the hole and was holding.

He was hanging in the harness under the reared-up front of the sled, half in the water, with the current pulling at his legs and the weight of the sinking load pulling at his chest, and he could not get out of it.

They heard him shout.

Riarhin did not decide anything.

She remembered dropping the line she was holding. She remembered Sifa’s voice behind her, a single word, and Temür already moving, running back across the ice toward the second island with the slack line from the lead sled in his hands, and Orm shouting. She remembered going across the reach on her belly, because Sifa had said lie down, sliding on the snow with her elbows and her good knee, pushing, the stitched leg dragging, toward the black hole and the reared-up sled and the man hanging under it.

She remembered the cold of the ice coming up through her coat.

The front of the sled was jammed across the edge of the hole at a steep tilt, its two runners sticking up into the air like the horns of something, the near one closest to her. Garrak hung below them in the water with the harness straps cutting into his armpits and his face grey and his mouth open. The water was very black. It moved. She could see it moving, under the edge of the ice, fast, a dark sliding weight going north, and every time it moved it pulled at the load and the load pulled at Garrak and the sled shifted on the edge of the ice with a grinding scrape.

She could not reach him from the ice. The edge of the hole would not bear her. She could see it, cracked, grey, sagging under the weight of the sled.

She reached the near runner instead.

It was sticking up out of the hole at an angle, slick with ice, its iron shoe catching the light. She got her good hand on it and pulled herself onto it. She lay along it on her belly, the length of it, with the water under her, and got her knees over the cross-binding where the runner met the bed of the sled, hooked them, and held. The sled lurched. She felt it go down under her a hand’s breadth and stop.

Garrak was below her. She could see his face, a hand’s breadth from the water, looking up at her. His eyes were very wide. They were yellow-brown, like Sifa’s.

She let go of the runner with her good hand. She hung by her knees from the cross-binding with her body along the tilted runner and her head down toward the water, and she drew Toothpick.

The harness was leather, wet, thick as two fingers, and it went round his chest and over his shoulders and back to the sled. She could not reach the knots. She could reach the strap where it crossed his shoulder. She put Toothpick’s edge to it and sawed.

It was slow. The leather was frozen. The knife was sharp, but she was hanging upside down by her knees from a sinking sled over a black current, and her left hand was the only hand she had, and every time the current pulled the sled lurched and the strap moved under her blade. She sawed. She felt her stitched thigh, hooked over the cross-binding, begin to burn, and then begin to tear, a hot thin line down the inside of it. She sawed. Somewhere behind her, very far away, she heard Orm shouting, and Temür, and the sound of feet on the ice.

The strap parted.

Garrak’s right arm came free. He got it up. He got his hand on the edge of the ice and gripped, and the left strap was still round him, still pulling, and she reached down further, so far that her head was a hand’s breadth from the water and the cold came up off it into her face like a slap, and she found the left strap where it went under his arm, and cut that too.

He came out of the harness.

He came out of it all at once, and the water took his legs and swung him, and he clung to the edge of the ice with one hand, and the sled, freed of his weight on the front, shifted under Riarhin and began to slide backward into the hole.

Something hit her across the back.

It was a line. Temür had thrown it, from the ice behind her, and it fell across her and lay there, and she heard him shout in Ykrul, take it, take it, and she got her knife-hand round it, with Toothpick still in her fist, and held on.

The sled went down.

It went down under her, backward, into the black water, slowly and then all at once, and took her knees off the cross-binding, and she was on the ice on her face with the line round her hand and Temür and Sifa hauling on the other end of it, lying flat, the way Sifa had said, and the sled was gone. The runner she had been lying on went past her and down and was gone. The sacks and bundles went with it. There was a long black hole in the white, with the water moving in it.

Garrak was at the edge of it. Orm had him. Orm had come round the hole on his belly from the far side and had him by the collar and the wrist, and was lying flat on the ice and pulling, and Garrak came out of the water onto the ice like a seal, streaming, and lay on his face.

Nobody moved for a moment.

Then Sifa got up off the ice and walked across to where Garrak lay, and stood over him, and said something in the throat-speech that was very short.

Bródd, flat on the ice beside Riarhin with his face grey, began to laugh.

“What did she say?” said Riarhin.

“She said,” said Bródd, and stopped, and laughed again, helplessly, with his forehead on the ice. “She said follow the marks.”

She could not walk the last mile.

She tried. She got up off the ice with Temür’s hand under her arm and stood, and the stitched leg told her at once what it thought of her. She looked down. The inside of her trouser leg, from the hip to the knee, was wet, and it was not water. She could feel the line of the scar under it, hot, open along part of its length.

“Asta’s going to kill you,” said Orm.

He had come up beside her. He was soaked to the shoulders from pulling Garrak out and his hands were white and his lips were blue. He looked at her leg and then at her face.

“Asta’s not here,” said Riarhin.

“She’ll know,” said Orm. “She always knows.” He looked across the basin, north, to the far edge where the dark hills began and where, now that Riarhin looked, there was a line of smoke going up into the grey sky from somewhere behind a ridge. “There’s a hearth over there. His, I expect.” He looked at Garrak, who was sitting on the ice in his wet furs with Sifa standing over him, shaking. “We’ll need them to come and get us. Him and you.”

“They won’t see us.”

Orm looked at her a moment. Then he went to the lead sled and dug under the lashings and brought out a bundle of cloth. He shook it out. It was a long narrow banner of heavy blue wool, faded, with a white ship sewn on it and a fringe along the bottom edge. Ingrith had given it to him at the stone, folded, without a word, and he had carried it at the bottom of the sled since and Riarhin had forgotten it was there.

He tied it to the end of a spare sled-pole with two quick knots, and stood on the ice, and held it up.

The wind took it. It streamed out blue against the white, and Riarhin, sitting on the sled with her hand pressed against her thigh, looked at it and thought of a coat on a stick on the shingle under the forest.

Orm held it up until his arms shook. Then Temür took it from him and held it up.

On the far shore, on the ridge below the smoke, a figure appeared. Then another. They stood looking. Then one of them lifted an arm, and they came down off the ridge onto the ice, a long way off, in a line, with something long carried between them, a pole or a ladder, and began to cross.

Brakka’s hearth was a long low house of turf and stone dug into the south face of the ridge, and it was full of people, and all of them were looking at Riarhin.

They had carried her the last mile on a hurdle. She had not been able to stop them. Four of them, Grímhúð, big grey-skinned men and women in furs with the blue lines on their jaws, had come across the ice with the hurdle and Garrak’s brother and a coil of line and looked at the black hole and at Garrak and at her, and then they had picked her up and put her on the hurdle and carried her, and none of them had said a word to her the whole way.

Now she was sitting by the fire with her leg out on a bench and an old Grímhúð woman kneeling beside it with a bowl of something that smelled of pine and fat. The house was dark and warm and smoky and smelled of meat and wet fur and people. There were a great many people. Children. Old men. Dogs. They were all looking at her.

At the far end of the fire, Garrak was sitting wrapped in blankets with a cup in both hands, still shaking. Beside him a big grey-haired woman was standing with her arms folded, looking down at him. She had a stick in one hand, a short flat stick with notches cut in it, and as Riarhin watched, she cut another notch, with a knife, and said something to him, and he said something back, and she cut another.

“What’s she doing?” said Riarhin, low, to Bródd, who was sitting on the floor beside the bench.

Bródd listened.

“That’s Brakka,” he said. “It’s her hearth. It was her sled.” He listened again. “She’s asking him what was on it. Every sack. He tells her and she cuts a notch.” He winced. “Six sacks of meal. Two of dried fish. The seal fat. A bundle of hides. Rope. A box of—” He did not know the word. “Something for a wedding.”

Brakka cut another notch, and looked at the stick, and said something short and hard.

“She says,” said Bródd, “that’s the winter.” He paused. “And then she said a thing about Garrak’s mother that I won’t say.”

Across the fire, on the other side, Riarhin could hear another voice. It was a young Grímhúð man, one of the four who had carried the hurdle, standing in the middle of a ring of children and telling them something. He was telling it with his hands. He held out his arm, flat, tilted, and lay along it with the other hand, and hooked his fingers over it, and hung his head down, and made a sawing motion with his free hand, and the children watched with their mouths open. He made a sound like a strap parting. He made a sound like a sled going into water. The children shrieked.

He looked across the fire at Riarhin, and pointed at her, and said a word.

All the children looked at her.

“What did he say?” said Riarhin.

Bródd listened to the young man go on telling it. He was quiet for a while.

“He says you hung by your knees over the black water like a bat,” said Bródd. “With your head in the river. And cut Garrak out of his harness with a little knife.” He paused. “He says it was a very little knife.”

On the bench, with the old woman’s salve going hot into the torn scar on her thigh, Riarhin put her hand to her belt, where Toothpick was. It was there. She had put it back, at some point on the ice, and did not remember doing it.

Across the fire, Brakka cut another notch in her stick.

Chapter 18 — A place beside the sled

They kept her at Brakka’s hearth four days, because the leg would not let her go, and on the second day she was given a runner to mend.

It felt like a punishment. Brakka came and stood over her bench in the morning with her notched stick in her belt and looked down at her, and said something in the throat-speech, and went away, and two young men came and put a broken sled-runner across her knees and a bundle of rawhide thongs beside her and a bone awl, and went away too.

“She says you lost her a sled,” said Bródd. “She says you can help make the other two.”

“I didn’t lose her a sled. Garrak lost her a sled.”

“She says Garrak is her sister’s son and she can’t make him do anything useful, so you’ll have to.” Bródd looked at the runner. “I think it’s a kindness.”

“It doesn’t feel like one.”

“That’s how you know,” said Bródd, and went to help with the dogs.

There were two sleds being made ready in the long house, and the whole household was involved in both of them, and the whole household had an opinion.

Riarhin sat on her bench with the broken runner on her knees and the scarred leg up and learned the household by watching it. She had done this at Ottarshall and at Skarth and at Hornvik, and she did it here without deciding to. It was harder here. She did not have the language. She had Bródd when he was near, and Temür’s half when he was near, and Sifa’s silence, and the rest she had to take from faces and hands and who stood where.

Brakka ran it. That was plain from the first hour. She was a big old woman with iron-grey hair in a single thick braid and arms like a smith’s, and she went up and down the long house all day with her notched stick in her belt, and people got out of her way and then came after her to argue. Garrak was her sister’s son. Garrak’s brother was the young man who had told the children about the bat. There were Brakka’s own sons, two of them, grown, quiet men who did not argue with her, and their wives, who did, and a great many children who belonged to all of them, and two old men by the fire who did nothing but give advice. There were dogs, a dozen, big and grey and woolly, who lived in a pen at the end of the house and came in whenever anybody left the door open.

And there were the two sleds.

One was for a wedding. That was the one with the noise round it. It was a light sled, newly made, its runners shaved pale and its bed lashed with red-dyed thongs, and it was being loaded with things that Riarhin understood, after a while, were gifts: furs, a carved box, a pot, a bundle of something wrapped in a blue cloth that the women would not let the men touch. It was for Brakka’s granddaughter. Her name was Ytta and she was perhaps eighteen, tall and grey and fierce, with the blue lines on her jaw so new that the skin round them was still pink, and she was going over the hills to the north-east in five days to visit the hearth of the young man she meant to marry, and stay a month, and be looked at.

The other sled was for the due.

Nobody said so. Riarhin understood it anyway, by the end of the first day, from the way people moved round it. It was a heavy old sled, dark with use, and it stood at the far end of the long house near the door, and nobody laughed near it. The women who loaded it did it quietly. They brought things to it from the stores at the back of the house and laid them in its bed in a particular order, and Brakka came and looked at each one and cut a notch in her stick, or did not. They were good things. The best furs. Dried meat in birch-bark boxes. A stack of seal-fat cakes wrapped in gut. Meal.

There was a place in its bed that was empty.

Riarhin saw Brakka look at the empty place, on the second evening, after the women had finished loading. She stood by the sled with her stick in her hand and looked at the bare boards between the furs and the meat, the space where something had been going to go. She stood there a long time. Then she cut nothing on her stick, and turned away.

“That’s Garrak’s sled,” said Bródd, quietly, beside Riarhin. “What was on it. The meal and the fish. That was for there.”

Riarhin looked at the empty boards.

“What happens if it’s short?” she said.

Bródd did not answer for a while.

“I don’t know,” he said. “I asked one of the old men. He said—” He searched. “He said, then it is said back to us. I don’t know what that means. He didn’t want to tell me.”

The quarrel about the wedding sled went on for all four days, and Riarhin came to look forward to it.

It was between two young men, Durrik and Haskel. They were both about twenty, both cousins of Ytta’s in some way Riarhin never worked out, and they both wanted to go with her over the hills to the young man’s hearth. One of the household had to go, a man, to stand behind her at the door when she arrived and say who she was. It was a great honour. It meant a month at another hearth, where there would be a feast, and girls who had never seen either of them before.

Durrik was the taller and thought that settled it. Haskel was the better on a sled and thought that settled it. They argued it in the long house, at meals, at the dog pen, in the snow outside while they were splitting wood, and Riarhin, who could not understand more than one word in ten, followed it perfectly. Durrik’s chin went up whenever Haskel spoke. Haskel’s shoulders went back. Every time Ytta walked past, both stopped arguing and found something to do with their hands.

On the third evening Durrik combed his hair with a great deal of fat in it and appeared at the meal with it standing up in a crest. The whole long house looked at it. Nobody said anything for a long moment. Then one of the old men by the fire said a single word, and the house fell apart.

Riarhin did not know what the word was. She did not need to. She sat on her bench with the mended runner across her knees and laughed until her leg hurt, and Temür, beside her, laughed too, his head back, and across the fire Sifa put her hand over her mouth and her shoulders shook, and Durrik stood in the middle of it with his crest and his face going darker and darker grey, and then laughed himself, helplessly, and sat down.

Ytta, at the end of the fire, did not laugh. She looked at Durrik’s hair, and then at Haskel, who was laughing hardest of all, and then back at Durrik, and Riarhin, watching, saw her decide something about both of them.

The child found the carving on the last evening.

It was a little girl, four or five, called Nikka, who had spent four days staring at Riarhin from behind her mother and had at last, that afternoon, come close enough to touch the hilt of Remorse where it leaned against the bench, and been smacked for it, and come back. She came back again in the evening with something in her arms. It was nearly as big as she was. She put it down on the floor in front of Riarhin’s bench, in the firelight, and stood back.

It was a head. A carved wooden head, very old, black with smoke and grease, as long as Riarhin’s arm. It had been painted once; there were flakes of red left in the mouth and white on the teeth. It had a long snout. It had ears laid back flat along its skull and its jaws open in a snarl, full of carved teeth like pegs, and its tongue lolling out over them, and its eyes were two round holes with the wood carved in rings round them.

Riarhin had seen it before. Not this one. Its brother, over the door of the Old Hall in Hvalgard.

“From a boat,” said Bródd. He had come to sit by her, and he was looking at the head with an odd expression. “The front of a Linn boat. Where the post goes up.” He touched the bottom of it, where it had been broken off clean. “They put those on the prows, on the coast. In the old days. To frighten things. My grandmother said.” He looked at the snarling mouth. “It’s one of them. One of—” He did not say it.

The child Nikka was saying something. She was pointing at the head, and then at Sifa, and then at herself, and then at the head again, and her voice was very serious.

The long house had gone quiet to listen.

“She says,” said Bródd, “her grandmother’s grandmother’s brother took it off a boat on the coast. A long time ago. And brought it home.” He listened. “She says, the people on the coast. Do they think we look like that?”

Riarhin looked at the child.

“She says,” said Bródd, and hesitated, and Riarhin heard him begin to say something else, softer, a thing about how the coast-people did not know any better, and then Sifa laughed.

It was neither a kind laugh nor an unkind one. Sifa was sitting across the fire with her knees drawn up and she laughed, once, short, through her filed teeth, and said something to Bródd in the throat-speech, and Bródd stopped.

He looked at Sifa. Then at the floor.

“That isn’t what she said,” he said. “The child. I was making it—” He stopped again. “She said: Do the people on the coast really think we look like that? Because if they do, they must be very stupid, and they must have very bad eyes, and that would explain the boats.”

There was a silence.

Then the long house laughed. It laughed the way it had laughed at Durrik’s hair, all at once, the old men by the fire and the women at the wedding sled and Ytta and Haskel and Brakka with her stick, and the child Nikka stood in the middle of it, pleased, not entirely sure what she had done.

Riarhin did not laugh.

She sat on her bench and looked at the carved head on the floor. She thought about the shingle at Hvalgard, and the net, and the woman in it. She thought about what she had done in the first moment, before she had seen Sifa’s face properly. She had looked for the snout. She had not known she was doing it. She had looked at the woman in the net and her eyes had gone first to the mouth, to the jaw, to see whether it was like the thing over the door of the Old Hall, and only after that had she seen how tired Sifa was.

She did not know how to say it so that it would be true and not a speech. She did not want to apologise for the coast. She had not carved the head. She had not put it on a boat.

“Bródd,” she said. “Tell her this. Exactly.”

He looked at her.

“Tell her I don’t know what the coast thinks. I wasn’t born there. But when they brought Sifa down the beach in a net, I looked at her mouth first. Before anything else. To see if she looked like that.” She nodded at the carving. “I’d seen one over a door. And I looked to see. And she didn’t.” She stopped. “Tell her I’m sorry I looked.”

Bródd was quiet for a moment. Then he said it to the child, in the throat-speech, slowly. Riarhin watched Sifa while he said it. She watched Sifa listen to every word, and at the end, at I’m sorry I looked, she watched Sifa lift her head and look at Bródd, sharply, as if to check him, and then look at Riarhin, and then look away.

The child Nikka considered this. Then she said something short and went away to her mother, dragging the carved head behind her by its ear.

“What did she say?” said Riarhin.

“She said, that’s all right, everybody looks at the teeth first,” said Bródd.

Brakka would not guide them.

She said so plainly, on the fourth morning, standing by Riarhin’s bench with her stick in her belt. They had asked her the evening before, through Bródd, whether she would take them, or send somebody to take them, to the hearths further north, to whoever held the oath-stave for this country, to whoever would know where four people from the coast had been taken. And Brakka had listened to the question, and gone away, and thought about it all night, Bródd said, because he had heard her not sleeping.

“She says no,” said Bródd. “She says she doesn’t take strangers to another clan’s fire without that clan’s leave. Not even ones who cut her sister’s boy out of his harness.” He listened. “She says it isn’t hers to give. She says if she took us, and it went wrong, it would be her name on it.”

“That’s fair,” said Riarhin.

Bródd looked at her, surprised. Then he told Brakka, and Brakka looked surprised too, and then grunted.

“There’s a way,” said Bródd, after Brakka had said something else. “Maybe. Ytta’s young man. His hearth is under Hrogaz. The oath-warden. Hrogaz holds the stave for all the hearths over the hills.” He listened. “If we went with Ytta’s sled, as her people, as far as the young man’s hearth, then the young man’s people could ask Hrogaz. That would be leave. That would be the right way.”

Riarhin looked down the long house. Ytta was at the wedding sled, folding the blue cloth over the carved box with great care, and Durrik and Haskel were on either side of her, both helping, both getting in the way.

“Ask her,” said Riarhin.

Bródd went down the house and asked her.

Riarhin watched it. She watched Ytta listen to Bródd, with the blue cloth in her hands, and look up the house at the bench where Riarhin was sitting, and at Orm by the door, and Temür, and Sifa. She watched Ytta’s face. She watched Ytta put down the blue cloth, very carefully, on top of the carved box, and say something to Bródd, short and quiet, and turn her back on him, and pick the cloth up again.

Bródd came back.

“She says no,” he said.

“Why?”

“She says it’s her journey.” Bródd sat down on the floor by the bench. “She says she’s going to see the man she’s going to marry. She says she’s going with her people behind her, and gifts, and the red thongs on her sled, and she’s going to stand at his door and be looked at.” He paused. “She says she isn’t going to stand at his door with four strangers from the coast behind her and a woman under somebody else’s keeping, and have the first thing his mother says to her be who are these.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“She says,” said Bródd, “that it’s the only time in her life she’ll do it. And she’s not giving it to us.”

Down the house, Ytta was lashing the blue cloth over the box. She did it slowly, and properly, and did not look up the house again.

“She’s right,” said Riarhin.

“Yes,” said Bródd. “I know.”

Across the house, by the door, Riarhin saw Sifa get up.

She had been sitting against the wall, as she always sat, with her hands in her sleeves. She got up now and went to the door, and opened it, and stood in it with the cold coming in round her, looking out. Riarhin could not see what she was looking at. She saw Sifa’s face in profile against the grey light, and saw it change, and saw Sifa’s hand come out of her sleeve and go to her jaw and stay there.

Then Sifa turned back into the house, and came straight to Riarhin’s bench.

“There is a camp,” she said, in the northern speech, thick and slow. “Two days. North-west. Over the ridge.” Her jaw moved. “My mother’s sister’s hearth. My hearth.” She looked at Riarhin. “Hrogaz’s too. His people. They will know where he is.” She paused. “I take you.”

“You said you didn’t know where they went,” said Orm, from the door. “The four. At the stone.”

“I said I didn’t know,” said Sifa. “I didn’t say I didn’t know the way home.”

Chapter 19 — The fire left banked

Sifa’s hearth was empty, and it had not been left the way a place is left.

They came over the ridge in the afternoon of the second day, in a light snow, with Sifa ahead of them going faster than she had gone since Hvalgard, and saw it below them: a sheltered hollow under a crag, with a frozen stream at the bottom and a cluster of low turf houses along the stream, eight or nine of them, and a dog pen, and drying racks, and a long house at the head of the hollow larger than the rest.

There was no smoke.

Sifa stopped on the ridge and looked at it. She stood quite still. Then she went down.

Riarhin had seen empty places. She had seen villages that had been left ahead of soldiers, and villages that had been left after them, and they were different from each other and she knew the difference. A place that has been left ahead of trouble is packed. The doors are shut. The good things are gone and the things left behind are the things that could not be carried. A place that has been left after trouble is broken open.

This was neither.

The doors were shut, but not barred. The first house they came to, Temür pushed the door and it swung open on its leather hinges and showed the room inside: a hearth in the middle, swept, and the fire on it banked. Not out. Banked, carefully, the embers raked together under a skin of ash and two pieces of wood laid crosswise over them, the way Borte had banked hers. Riarhin crouched and put her hand over it. There was no warmth. It had been banked days ago and gone out under its ash on its own.

Round the hearth, everything was where it had been put down.

A pot on its hook, with something frozen in the bottom of it. Bowls on a shelf. Bedding on the sleeping platforms along the walls, folded back, as if people had got up in the morning meaning to come back to it at night. On a bench by the door, a strap of leather, half-stitched, with an awl stuck through it at the place where the stitching stopped and a hank of sinew thread wound round the handle of the awl.

Temür picked it up. He held it in his hands and looked at the stitching, and at the awl, and Riarhin saw his rider’s fingers find the place where it stopped.

“Somebody was mending this,” he said. “They put it down to go and see something. They meant to come back and finish it.” He turned it over. “It’s a halter strap. For a dog.”

He put it down where it had been, exactly.

It was the same in every house. They went from one to the next along the stream, the five of them, in the falling snow, and in every one the fire was banked and the beds were folded back and the work was lying where it had been put down. A half-scraped hide pegged out on a frame. A net with the needle still in it. A child’s boot with the sole half sewn on. In one house there was dough in a trough, frozen hard, with the marks of fingers still in it.

“They left in the morning,” said Riarhin. “All of them. At once. Without packing.”

“They meant to come back,” said Orm.

He was standing in the door of the dog pen. It was empty. Beside it, under a lean-to of hides, there was a stack of fuel: dried peat and birch logs and blocks of something dark that smelled of fat. It was a large stack. It came up to his chest.

“Look at it,” he said. “That’s a winter’s worth. More. You don’t leave that if you’re not coming back. You take it, or you burn it so nobody else has it.” He put his hand on it. “They thought they’d be back by dark.”

Sifa had not stopped in any of the houses.

She had gone along the stream ahead of them, fast, from door to door, looking in and going on, not going in. Riarhin had watched her. She had watched her look into each doorway and look at whatever was inside and turn away, and go on to the next. She went faster at each one. At the long house at the head of the hollow she did not look in at all. She went past it, round the side of it, to where a smaller house stood a little apart under the crag, with something carved on the lintel over its door.

She stopped in front of it.

Riarhin, coming up the hollow slowly with the staff, saw the carving on the lintel from twenty paces off. It was a mark. Strokes cut in the wood, and a shape like a bird’s foot. She did not know it.

Sifa knew it. Sifa stood in front of the door with her hand at her jaw and looked at the mark, and then she pushed the door open and went in, and Riarhin heard her calling inside. A name. She called it twice. Then a different name. Then she came out again, fast, and went round the side of the house toward the crag, calling, and her foot went through the snow.

It went through to the knee and then to the hip, and she went down sideways with her arms out, and Riarhin saw the snow round her crack and sag and begin to slide inward, into a hole, a dark hole opening under the white, and Sifa was going down into it.

Temür got her.

He had been nearer. He had seen Sifa go round the house and followed, and he threw himself flat on the snow and got her by the collar of her borrowed coat and held on, and the snow round them went on sliding into the dark, a long slow slither, and stopped. Sifa hung on the edge with her legs in the hole and her hands clawing at the snow and Temür’s fist in her collar.

Orm and Riarhin got there. They got her out between them, the three of them, hauling on her arms, and she came out of the hole and lay on the snow on her back and breathed.

Riarhin looked down into the hole.

It was a pit. A deep one, square, lined with stones, as deep as a man was tall. It had been covered with poles and hides and the hides had been covered with snow, and Sifa had stepped on the edge of the covering and it had given. At the bottom of the pit, in the dark, Riarhin could see the shapes of things. Frozen joints of meat. Fish. A great many of them. It was a cache. A winter store, dug into the frozen ground under the crag where the cold would keep it.

It was full.

“My mother’s sister’s,” said Sifa, from the snow. She did not sit up. She lay on her back with her eyes open, looking at the sky, at the snow coming down out of it onto her face. “Her pit. I helped dig it. When I was small.” Her jaw moved. “I forgot.”

Nobody said anything.

“I called her,” said Sifa. “She didn’t answer. I called my cousins.” She lay still. “Nobody is here.”

The ice was in the middle of the hollow, below the long house, where the stream widened into a frozen pool.

They had walked past it. Riarhin had seen it and thought it was the pool. It was only when the light began to go and Bródd went down to the stream for water that he stopped and stood very still on the bank and called them, and they came, and saw what it was.

It was a slab of ice. Clear ice, not the white of the stream, set upright in the middle of the frozen pool, as tall as a man and as wide as a door and as thick as Riarhin’s forearm, standing straight up out of the pool as if it had grown there. Its two faces were flat. Somebody had cut them flat, or they had been made flat, and the clear ice was so clean that Riarhin could see through it the dim shapes of the houses beyond, wavering.

And it was carved.

Both faces were covered from top to bottom in strokes. The same strokes. Short and straight, upright and slanting, crossing and branching, in long close lines. The oath-script. Riarhin had seen it on Ilva’s vellum and on the boundary stone and on Sifa’s road-posts, and here it was cut into clear ice, in lines so close and so deep that the whole slab caught the last of the light and glittered.

Sifa stood on the bank and looked at it.

“Can you read it?” said Riarhin.

Sifa did not answer at once.

“Some,” she said at last. “Not all. The road marks are easy. This is—” She stopped. “This is the old way. The old words.” She touched her jaw. “I can read it. I can’t say it all.”

“Then read it,” said Riarhin. “Bródd will say it after you. Temür will say if Bródd’s wrong.” She looked at Bródd. “Exactly.”

Bródd nodded. He did not look as if he wanted to.

It took them until full dark, and then by lantern, and then until the lantern had to be filled again.

Sifa read it from the top. She stood on the ice of the pool with her face close to the slab and her hand on it, tracing the strokes with her finger as she had traced the road-marks, and she read it aloud in the throat-speech, one line at a time, slowly, the thick broken sounds coming out of her damaged mouth with great effort. When there was a sound she could not make she stopped, and touched the stroke, and Bródd, beside her, said it for her, and she nodded or shook her head. Then Bródd said the line again in the northern speech. Then Temür, on her other side, said what he had understood of it, and where it was different, they stopped.

They stopped a great deal.

It began with a name. The name of a hearth: this hearth, Sifa said, the hearth of the people of the hollow under the crag, of the Hrafn-Grímr oath. Then a list of winters. Each winter had a number, a count-mark, and after each count-mark there was a line, and the line said what had been brought to the Hall in that winter by this hearth, and what had been owed, and how far short it fell.

“Short,” said Bródd, the first time, and stopped. “It doesn’t say short. It says—” He looked at Sifa. She said a word. “It says what was not brought.”

“Same thing,” said Orm.

“It isn’t,” said Temür.

They argued it. Riarhin let them. She stood behind them on the ice with the lantern and watched the strokes go by under Sifa’s finger, and listened to them argue, and the list of winters went on, line after line, down the slab.

It was a long list. In the old winters at the top the lines were short. Further down they grew longer. In the last winters, the ones at the bottom of the first face, the lines went on and on, and Sifa read them more and more slowly, and once she stopped in the middle of one and did not go on for a long time, and stood with her hand flat on the ice.

“What is it?” said Riarhin.

“Names,” said Sifa.

She went on.

They were not the names Riarhin had expected. She had expected, if she had expected anything, a list of the dead. They were names in a strange form, long and formal, three and four words each, and Sifa read them in a flat voice, and Bródd put them into the northern speech, and they made no sense.

“The one who is under the hand of the grandmother of the third door,” said Bródd. “The one who came after the one who was lost on the ice. The daughter of the daughter of the weaver of nets.” He stopped. “I don’t understand. These aren’t names. They’re—”

“They’re names,” said Sifa.

She took her hand from the slab and turned round.

“The one who is under the hand of the grandmother of the third door,” she said, slowly, in the northern speech. “That is Tukka. He is six. He lives in the third house. His mother died. My aunt’s mother keeps him.” She turned back and put her finger on the next. “The one who came after the one who was lost on the ice. That is Hessa. Her mother had a son before her who fell through the ice on the big river. Then Hessa.” She moved her finger. “The daughter of the daughter of the weaver of nets. That is my cousin’s girl. Ama. She is not walking yet.”

Nobody said anything.

“It is the old way of saying names,” said Sifa. “To the Hall. You don’t say Tukka. You say whose he is.” She looked at the slab. “These are children.”

Temür had gone very still beside her.

“Why are the children on it?” he said.

Sifa put her finger on a stroke above the list, a line Riarhin had seen her pass over quickly. She read it, and Bródd said it, slowly.

“These are counted under the acceptance of the elder of the hearth,” said Bródd, “who took the stave in their name and answered for them.” He looked up. “Your aunt. She answered for them. They’re counted under her.”

“She accepted the stave,” said Sifa. “For the hearth. When the old warden died. For all of us. Everybody under her roof.” She looked at the list. “I was under her roof.”

She did not read her own name. Riarhin did not ask whether it was there.

The four names were on the second face.

Sifa found them near the bottom, in a part of the slab that was set apart from the rest by a long double stroke, and she read the line above them first, and then stopped, and read it again, and then turned and looked at Bródd for a long time before she let him say it.

“Collected in this winter toward what was not brought,” said Bródd slowly. “Counted to this hearth, of the line given in the old time to the coast at the house of seals.” He stopped. “The house of seals.”

“Selhús,” said Riarhin.

Bródd looked at her.

“Selhús-Gunnvor,” said Riarhin. “That’s what they shouted through her door. Ragna said. The Seal-house. Her great-grandmother’s house.”

Sifa read the names.

She read them in the old way, long and formal, and Bródd said them, and they were not like the children’s names on the first face, because Sifa did not know these people and could not tell them who they were. The weaver who is of the house of seals by her mother’s mother. The boy who is of the house of seals by his father’s grandmother. The keeper of stones who is of the house of seals by his father. The daughter of the brother of the woman who holds the hall, who is of the house of seals by her mother.

Gunnvor. Kári. Ulf. Svala.

Riarhin stood on the ice with the lantern and listened to them said in the old way, and thought of Thora by the fire at Ottarshall with her bandaged hands, and the key in the ash, and Ingrith at the head of her table with four bowls in front of her.

“Given,” said Bródd. His voice had gone strange. “Of the line given in the old time. Given to the coast. By who?”

Sifa looked at him.

“By us,” she said.

Bródd did not move.

“A long time ago,” said Sifa. “When there was a peace. Before the songs. There was a peace with the coast, and you give people. To live there. To show you mean it.” She touched her jaw. “They lived at the house of seals. They married there. Their children were coast children.” She looked at the slab. “The Hall still counts them. It counts them to this hearth. They were ours when they were given. The Hall doesn’t forget who is whose.”

“So when the hearth was short,” said Riarhin.

“The Hall said who would make it up,” said Sifa. “It said their names. In the old way. To the warden. And the warden sent people to the coast with the names, to bring them.” She did not look at anybody. “I was on the hill. With the sleds. I didn’t go down.”

Bródd had turned away from the slab. He was standing on the ice of the pool with his back to them, looking at the dark houses along the stream, and his big shoulders were very stiff.

Riarhin knew what he was thinking. She could see it in the set of his back. A line given in the old time to the coast. His grandmother’s grandmother’s. A peace. A household set down among the Linn to show somebody meant it, and the songs, and the strange old words at the feasts, and eight years of making them gentle enough to eat by.

She did not go to him. She did not think he wanted anybody to.

At the bottom of the second face there was a last line, cut deeper than the rest.

Sifa read it alone. She did not ask Bródd to say it. She read it in the throat-speech, slowly, with her finger on each stroke, and at one place in the middle of the line she stopped, and her finger stayed on the stroke, and she did not say anything at all for the space of three breaths. Then she went on, past it, to the end.

“What was that?” said Temür. “The place where you stopped.”

“Her name,” said Sifa.

She took her hand off the ice.

“The rest of what is owed,” she said, in the northern speech, “will be collected in the dark of this winter, from this hearth and all under the hand of its elder, by the Hall, at the Hall’s own coming.” She looked at the slab. “That’s all. That’s what it says.”

“This winter,” said Orm.

“Now,” said Sifa. “The dark of the winter is now.”

The snow had stopped. Overhead, beyond the crag, the sky had cleared, and there were stars, very many of them, very cold. The lantern burned on the ice. Behind them, along the stream, the empty houses stood with their banked fires gone out and their work left where it lay.

“That’s why they went,” said Riarhin. “Your aunt. All of them. They read this, and they went.”

“They went to Hrogaz,” said Sifa.

She turned and walked away across the ice of the pool to the bank, and up past the long house, toward the head of the hollow, where the crag rose and the path went up beside it. Riarhin followed her with the lantern. At the foot of the path, beside a cairn, there was a post, grey and weathered, with strokes cut in it. Riarhin could see, even by lantern-light, that some of the strokes were new. Freshly cut. The wood inside them was pale.

Sifa traced them with her finger.

“Hrogaz,” she said. “The gathering. They cut it so the ones who were out on the ice would know where to follow.” She traced a stroke. “Three days. North and east.”

“And the four?” said Orm, behind them. “The coast people. Where would they be?”

Sifa did not answer at once.

“At the Hall,” she said. “If they were collected, they were taken to the Hall.” She looked north, where the hills went up black under the stars. “That way. Straight. Five days. Six.”

“And Hrogaz is three days the other way,” said Orm.

“Yes.”

Orm looked at her. Riarhin saw him think of the stone at Kettlebeach, and his hand flat on it, and I’m going north for the four. She saw him look north, where the Hall was, and the four.

“We go to Hrogaz,” said Sifa.

“Sifa—”

“My aunt went to Hrogaz,” said Sifa. “And Tukka. And Hessa. And Ama, who isn’t walking yet. They’re all on the ice. Under her hand.” She did not raise her voice. “The Hall comes for them in the dark of the winter. If you go straight to the Hall for the four from the coast, you’ll get there, and you’ll find them, maybe. And while you’re there, the Hall will come to Hrogaz’s gathering and take everybody under my aunt’s hand.” She looked at Orm. “And you won’t know. Because you won’t be there.”

Orm said nothing for a long time.

“Hrogaz holds the stave,” said Riarhin. “Brakka said. He’s the one who knows how it works. Who’s owed. Who says. If we go to the Hall without knowing that—”

“I know,” said Orm. He was still looking north. “I know.” He turned and looked at Sifa, and his face in the lantern-light was very tired. “Three days.”

“Three days,” said Sifa.

She touched the fresh-cut strokes on the post once more, lightly, with her fingertips. Then she went back down into the hollow, past the clear slab standing in the frozen pool with its lines of names glittering in the starlight, to the house with the bird’s-foot mark on the lintel, and went in, and they heard her in there in the dark, raking the dead fire, laying wood on it, getting it to burn.

Chapter 20 — The staff offered across

Hrogaz’s gathering lay in a long valley under a black ridge, and from the ridge it looked like a town that had been set down in the snow by somebody who did not expect to stay.

There were fires. A great many of them, more than Riarhin could count from the top of the ridge in the blowing dusk, scattered down the length of the valley floor in clusters, each cluster with its shelters round it: hide tents and brush lean-tos and sleds tipped on their sides and roofed over with skins. Dogs. Smoke. People moving between the fires, dark against the snow. At the head of the valley, against the foot of the ridge, there was a single larger shelter, long and low, of hides over a frame of whalebone, with a fire burning in front of it bigger than all the rest.

“How many?” said Orm.

“Every hearth from the four valleys,” said Sifa. “That are under Hrogaz.” She was looking down at the fires. “Not every hearth. Some.”

She went down first.

They came down into the gathering in the last of the light, and it was not like coming into Brakka’s hearth. At Brakka’s they had been carried in on a hurdle after a rescue, and the whole house had seen what had happened on the ice. Here nobody had seen anything. They walked down into the valley among the fires, four strangers from the coast and a woman who had been gone since the autumn, and the people at the fires stood up as they passed, and stopped talking, and watched.

Riarhin felt them watching. She had felt it at Hvalgard on the quay, the eyes going to her ears and her face and the sword on her back. This was different. This was the look of people who had been frightened for a long time and had just seen something new come down the hill, and were deciding what it meant.

Then a woman cried out.

It came from a fire on the left, near the stream: a short sharp cry, and a woman standing up from it so fast that the bowl in her lap went flying. She was old, small, grey, with blue lines on her jaw faded almost to nothing. She stood staring at Sifa.

Sifa stopped walking.

They looked at each other across the snow, the two of them, for perhaps three breaths. Then the old woman came. She did not run. She walked, quickly, with her hands out a little from her sides, the way Brother Halle had come up the ramp at Hvalgard, and she reached Sifa and stopped, and lifted her hands, and put them on either side of Sifa’s face.

She held it. She turned it, a little, toward the firelight, as if to see it properly, and her thumbs moved along the blue lines on Sifa’s jaw from the ear to the chin, slowly, the way Sifa’s own fingers did when she touched them.

Sifa stood and let her.

Riarhin looked away. She saw the others look away too, Orm and Temür and Bródd, all at once, as if they had agreed it.

When she looked back, the old woman had let go of Sifa’s face and was holding her by both wrists instead, and talking, fast and low, in the throat-speech, and Sifa was answering, short, and behind the old woman, at the fire by the stream, children had stood up. Four of them. Five. A small boy with a dog-halter in his hand.

“Her aunt,” said Bródd, very quietly. “Orra.”

Hrogaz received them that night, in the long whalebone shelter at the head of the valley, and he did it in front of everybody.

The shelter was full. Riarhin had expected that by now. There were elders along both sides of it on benches of piled hides, old men and old women, a great many, with the blue lines on their jaws and their hands folded and their faces closed. There were younger people standing behind them. There was a fire down the middle in a long trench, and at the far end of it, on a heap of furs, a man sitting with a staff across his knees.

He was not old. That surprised her. He was perhaps fifty, broad and heavy and grey, with a wide flat face and small deep eyes and a beard cut short and squared. He wore furs like everybody else. At his belt there hung a row of knots: small hard knots of braided cord, each a different colour, each no larger than a thumb-joint, tied to a thong. Riarhin counted them without meaning to. Nine.

The staff across his knees was black.

It was as long as he was tall, and as thick as her wrist, and it was black all over, darkened with age and handling and smoke, and the whole length of it was cut with strokes. The oath-script. Line after line of it, spiralling round the wood from the foot to the head, so close that there was hardly a finger’s breadth of plain wood anywhere.

As they came in, Hrogaz looked up. He looked at them, one after another, and his eyes stopped longest on Sifa, and then on Riarhin.

Then he stood up, and laid the staff down on the furs where he had been sitting, and stepped away from it.

He did it carefully. He did not throw it down. He laid it along the furs with both hands, and straightened, and took one step back from it, and stood with his hands at his sides. The staff lay a pace behind his heels. He did not look at it. Riarhin saw that he did not need to; he knew exactly where it was.

He spoke.

Bródd said it after him, slowly. Temür listened beside Bródd, and twice he shook his head, and Bródd stopped and said it again differently, and Temür nodded. Sifa stood by the fire with her aunt’s hand on her arm and said nothing at all.

Hrogaz said that he was the warden of the Hrafn-Grímr oath for the four valleys, and that he had held the stave eleven winters. He said he knew who they were. He said the coast had sent people north before, a long time ago, and they had not come back, and he had not expected the coast to send people north again. He said he knew about the four from the coast. He said their names in the old way, as the slab in the hollow had said them, and the elders along the benches did not move.

“He says,” said Bródd, “that they were collected. He says—” Bródd stopped. Hrogaz had said something more. Bródd listened to the end of it, and his face changed, and he said it without softening it: “He says he sent the people who collected them. He chose who went. He gave them the names.”

Riarhin looked at Hrogaz. Hrogaz looked back at her.

“Ask him why,” she said.

“He says because the Hall said the names,” said Bródd, when Hrogaz had answered. “And when the Hall says names, the warden sends. Or the Hall comes itself.” He listened. “He says when the Hall comes itself, it doesn’t take four.”

He offered it to her an hour later, when the talking had gone round the benches and come back.

Riarhin did not understand at first what he was offering. Bródd said it, and she heard the words, and they made no sense, and she made him say them again.

“The stave,” said Bródd. “He says carry it. For one season. This winter. Till the ice goes out of the rivers.” He listened. “He says he has thought about it since the slab was cut in the hollow under the crag. He says the Hall collects from the hearths that are under the stave. It counts them under the warden’s hand, the way it counted the children under Orra’s. It knows the stave. It knows whose hand is on it.” Bródd paused. “He says nobody has ever put the stave in a hand that wasn’t sworn. Not a hand from outside. He says he doesn’t know what the Hall would do if it came to collect, and found the stave in a hand it couldn’t count.”

“He doesn’t know,” said Riarhin.

“He says perhaps it would stop. Perhaps it would go away, and come back in the spring, when the stave is back in his hand.” Bródd listened. “Perhaps it would take one winter off. One winter, for the four valleys. That’s what he’s asking.”

Hrogaz was watching her. He was standing where he had stood all evening, a pace in front of the staff where it lay on the furs, with his hands at his sides.

“And me,” said Riarhin. “What would it do to me?”

Bródd asked it. Hrogaz answered. It was short.

“He says he doesn’t know,” said Bródd.

“Has anybody done it?”

Hrogaz’s face did not change. But Riarhin saw, along the benches, an old woman on the left side turn her head and look at the floor, and a young man standing behind her put his hand on her shoulder.

Hrogaz spoke for a long time. Bródd said it slowly.

“Once,” said Bródd. “Twenty winters ago. Not in his time. The warden before him. There was a man from the coast who married into a hearth in the eastern valley. Kolbein. A trader. Not sworn. He had a wife here, and children.” Bródd looked at the old woman on the bench. “The warden before Hrogaz gave him the stave to carry. For one season. To see.”

“And?”

“The Hall didn’t come for the eastern valley that winter,” said Bródd. “It came for the others. It didn’t come for the eastern valley.” He paused. “In the spring they found Kolbein sitting in the snow by the stream below his house. Frozen. With the stave across his knees. There wasn’t a mark on him.”

The old woman on the bench had not lifted her head.

“That’s his wife,” said Bródd, very low. “And his son. Behind her.”

“Was it the stave?” said Riarhin.

Hrogaz answered before Bródd could ask it. He had understood the question from her face.

“He says nobody knows,” said Bródd. “He says people freeze. He says it was a hard spring. He says perhaps Kolbein went out to the stream drunk and sat down.” Bródd hesitated. “He says he doesn’t believe that. He says he’s telling you because you asked.”

Riarhin looked at the old woman on the bench, and the young man with his hand on her shoulder.

“What would the others owe?” she said.

Bródd looked at her.

“If I took it,” said Riarhin. “Carried it. The stave counts the people under the warden’s hand. Orra answered for the children under her roof, and they’re on the slab under her name.” She did not look away from Hrogaz. “If I carry it, whose hand am I? Who’s under it?” She looked at Orm, and at Temür, and at Bródd. “Them? They came north with me. They’re under the stone’s word with me. If I put my hand on the stave, does the Hall count them under it? Do they owe what I owe?”

Bródd did not say it at once.

Riarhin saw him not say it. She saw him open his mouth, and think, and begin to make it into something else, something that could be asked in front of a hall of elders without making anybody ashamed.

Then Sifa spoke.

She said it in the throat-speech, across the fire, to Hrogaz, without asking anybody. She said it short and thick and plain, and Riarhin did not need it put into the northern speech to know that it had not been softened at all. She saw it land. She saw it go along the benches like a cold draught, the elders’ faces changing one after another, and she saw it reach Hrogaz.

He did not answer at once.

He stood in front of the staff with his hands at his sides and his heavy face slowly darkening, a deep dull grey-red under the skin.

When he answered, he did not look at Sifa. He looked at Riarhin.

“Yes,” said Bródd. His voice was very quiet. “He says yes. Probably. He says the stave counts the hand and whoever is behind the hand. He says he would have told you that.” He paused. “Before you took it.”

“Before?”

Hrogaz said something else. It was not quiet. It came out of him all at once, loud, harsh, and the elders on the benches went very still, and Bródd flinched.

“He says,” said Bródd, “that he has chosen nine times. Nine winters. Who goes on the sled to the Hall, when the stores aren’t enough. He says he’s chosen old men because they were old and children because they were small and his own daughter because nobody would believe him if he didn’t.” Bródd swallowed. “He says the Hall said the names of everybody under Orra’s hand, and it will come for them in the dark of this winter, and everybody under every other hand after. He says he is standing in front of a woman from the coast offering her the only thing he has left that he hasn’t tried, and she’s asking him who it will hurt.” Bródd looked at the floor. “He says, everybody. It hurts everybody. Everything I do. Do you think I have a thing to offer you that doesn’t?”

The shelter was silent.

Hrogaz stood in front of the staff, breathing hard. His hand had moved. Riarhin saw it. It had moved back, toward the furs behind him, toward the staff, and stopped, a hand’s breadth from it, and stayed there, open.

She refused it.

She did it as carefully as she knew how, standing by the long fire, with Bródd beside her saying every word after her and Temür beside Bródd listening to each one and Sifa across the fire with her aunt’s hand on her arm.

She said she would not take the stave. She said she would not take it because she did not know what it would do, and he did not either, and she would not put her hand on a thing that might count Orm and Temür and Bródd under it without their saying so, out loud, in front of everybody, knowing what it might cost. She said she had done that before, in other places, carried a thing for people who had not asked her to, without telling them, and they had lived with it afterward, and it had not been hers to decide for them.

She looked at Orm while she said that. He was standing by the door with his arms folded. He looked back at her and did not say anything.

“Tell him,” said Riarhin, “I can’t carry it. But we’re going to the Hall. We came north for the four, and they’re at the Hall, and we’re going there whatever happens.” She looked at Hrogaz. “If he sends people to the Hall. To speak. To ask for the hearths, for Orra’s, for anybody. People who are sworn, who can speak there, who have a right to. We’ll go with them.” She paused. “We can’t speak for them. We can walk beside them. We can keep the road. We can see they get there and see they get back.”

Bródd said it. Temür nodded at each line.

Hrogaz listened. When Bródd had finished he stood for a long while without saying anything. Then he turned round, and bent, and picked up the staff from the furs.

He held it across his body in both hands. He looked at it. Then he looked at her.

“He says,” said Bródd, “the staff stays with the one who bears it.”

“Yes.”

“He says he’ll send speakers. If the hearths will give him speakers. If they’ll agree to who.” Bródd listened. “He says that will take some days. He says the hearths don’t agree about much.”

“We’ll wait,” said Riarhin.

Behind her, she heard Orm let out a long breath.

Not all the hearths agreed.

Riarhin found that out before the night was over. When Hrogaz had sat down again on his furs with the staff across his knees, and the elders had begun to talk among themselves along the benches, low, in the throat-speech, an old woman on the right-hand side of the shelter stood up.

She was very old, older than Orra, bent nearly double, with a stick in each hand and white hair in a single thin braid down her back. She stood up slowly, with both sticks, and the talk along the benches stopped.

She spoke to Hrogaz. It was not long. Hrogaz answered, short. She spoke again, shorter.

Then she turned round on her two sticks and walked out of the shelter, slowly, into the dark, and five or six people who had been standing behind her bench got up and went with her.

“Gaurra,” said Sifa, beside Riarhin. She had come round the fire. Her aunt had let go of her arm. “From the high valley. Kel-dal.” She watched the door. “She says she won’t be under any speakers. She says if Hrogaz sends people to the Hall to ask for anything, the Hall will notice them. It will notice the hearths that sent them.” Sifa touched her jaw. “She says the Hall comes to people who make it notice them. She says she will not be noticed.” She was quiet a moment. “She says her hearth is not in it.”

Hrogaz was sitting on his furs with the staff across his knees, looking at the door where Gaurra had gone out. He did not try to call her back.

Chapter 21 — One hearth at a time

There was a watcher on the ridge.

Riarhin saw it on the second morning, from the door of the shelter Orra had given them, when the light came up grey over the black ridge above the gathering. It was a figure. It stood on the skyline at the top of the ridge, very small, very still, dark against the grey: a person in furs, upright, facing down into the valley. She looked at it for a long time. It did not move.

“How long has that been there?” she said.

Orra’s grandson, the small boy with the dog-halter, was squatting by the fire. He looked up where she was looking, and his face went closed, and he looked down again at the fire and said something in the throat-speech.

“He says it came the day after they got here,” said Bródd, from his blankets. “He says it doesn’t move. He says it doesn’t eat or sleep or sit down. It stands.” Bródd sat up. “He says his grandmother told him not to look at it, and not to point at it, and not to count it.”

“Count it?”

“He says it counts us,” said Bródd. “He says it stands up there and counts the fires, and the tents, and the people going between them. And the Hall knows what it counts.”

Riarhin looked at the figure on the ridge. It was too far to see a face. She thought she would not have wanted to.

“Somebody who fell behind,” said Sifa.

She had come to the door beside Riarhin. She did not look at the ridge. She looked at the fire.

“On the ice,” she said. “Long ago. Somebody who couldn’t keep up, when the due was going in. They sat down in the snow.” She touched her jaw. “The Hall keeps them. To stand.”

Gaurra’s hearth began to go that night.

Riarhin knew about it because Gaurra told her. The old woman with the two sticks came across the gathering in the dusk, slowly, through the snow, alone, to the fire outside Orra’s shelter where Riarhin was sitting with her leg up, and lowered herself onto a log beside it with great care, and looked at Riarhin for a while with her black eyes.

Then she spoke. Sifa said it after her.

“She says she heard you,” said Sifa. “In Hrogaz’s shelter. When you said you wouldn’t put your hand on the stave because the others hadn’t said they’d be under it.” Sifa listened. “She says that is the only sensible thing anybody has said in the four valleys this winter.”

Riarhin did not know what to answer.

“She says,” said Sifa, “that Hrogaz will send speakers to the Hall. She says she has known him since he was a boy and he will do it, because he has a thing he hasn’t tried, and he can’t bear to have a thing he hasn’t tried. She says when he sends them, the Hall will turn its head. To look at who sent them.” Sifa paused. “She says she is going before it turns.”

“Where?”

“The high valley. Kel-dal. Her own hearth. Up past the black ridge.” Sifa looked at Gaurra. “She says it’s small. Out of the way. She says if they’re there, and quiet, and don’t send anybody to say anything to anybody, perhaps the Hall will collect from the hearths that are making a noise, and go home, and forget the high valley this winter.”

“And the watcher?”

Gaurra had understood that. She turned her head, slowly, and looked up at the ridge, where the figure stood against the last of the light. She looked at it a long time. Then she said something, very low.

“She says it counts fires,” said Sifa. “She says it counts tents, and the people going in and out of them. She says if her hearth’s tents go dark, it will see. If it sees, the Hall knows. If the Hall knows, it will look for where they went.” Sifa paused. “She says her people will go tonight. In the dark. Out the bottom of the valley along the stream, under the bank, where the ridge can’t see. And the tents will stay. And the fires will stay lit.” She looked at Riarhin. “She says somebody has to keep them lit, and go in and out of them, so the watcher counts the same.”

Riarhin looked at the old woman.

“Why us?” she said.

Gaurra answered before Sifa could ask it.

“She says because you’re from the coast,” said Sifa, “and nobody here will think to count you.”

So they kept Gaurra’s fires.

There were six tents, at the high end of the gathering, under the black ridge, close together round a single fire. Gaurra’s people went out of the bottom of them that night, after full dark, one and two at a time, under the bank of the frozen stream, the way she had said, with their bundles and their sleds and their dogs muzzled with thongs so that they would not bark. Riarhin did not see them go. She saw the tents, from Orra’s fire, and watched the people go in and out of them in the dusk as people always did, and then she noticed that the people going out were not coming back.

In the morning she and Orm went up to the six tents and became Gaurra’s hearth.

It was simple, and it was very hard. They kept the fire in the middle of the six tents burning. They went in and out of the tents, at the hours people went in and out of tents: in the morning, carrying water; at midday, carrying wood; at dusk, carrying the children in to sleep. They did the last with blankets. Orm had thought of it. He took the cooking pots from the empty tents, Gaurra’s people’s pots, and wrapped them in blankets and tied them, so that each bundle had the size and the weight and the slump of a sleeping child, and at dusk he and Riarhin carried them in their arms from the fire into the tents, one at a time, the way a mother carries a child that has fallen asleep by the fire, with the head on the shoulder.

Riarhin carried hers in her left arm, against her shoulder, with her bad arm under it. It was heavy. The iron of the pot was cold through the blanket against her neck.

She did not look up at the ridge. She had been told not to look at it, and she did not. But she felt it. She felt it all the time, in the back of her neck, the way she had felt the trees at the edge of the shingle on the forest shore: something up there, facing down, very still, watching the fires and the tents and the people going in and out of them, and counting.

“Do you think it can tell?” said Orm, very low, on the second evening, when they were sitting by the fire with the bundles all carried in.

“I don’t know.”

“It’s a pot. It’s a pot in a blanket.” Orm looked at his hands. “If it can count people it can tell a pot from a person.”

“Then it’ll know,” said Riarhin. “And we’ll find out.”

Orm laughed, short.

“You say that like it doesn’t frighten you,” he said.

Riarhin looked at the fire.

“I didn’t say that,” she said.

He looked at her. Then he looked at his hands again. They were shaking, a little, as they had shaken on the shingle and at the stone. He did not hide them.

They sat by Gaurra’s fire in the dark under the ridge and did not look up.

The slow party was the problem.

Gaurra had not gone. She had sent her people, all but a few, and stayed. Riarhin found out why on the third morning, when she went into the largest of the six tents with a blanket of pots and found Gaurra sitting inside in the dimness on a heap of furs, with an old man lying beside her.

He was older than she was. He was very thin and very still, lying on his back under a fur with his eyes open, and his legs, under the fur, were wrong. Riarhin saw that at once. They lay at an angle that legs did not lie at, thin and twisted, and he did not move them.

“Asgrim,” said Sifa, behind her. “Her brother.”

“He can’t walk.”

“Not since he was a boy. A sled went over him.” Sifa looked at the old man. “They carry him. When they move. On a sled. Slowly.” She paused. “It takes four to pull it in the snow, up to the high valley. Four, and a whole day longer than the rest.”

“So she stayed.”

“She says she won’t leave him to come after on his own,” said Sifa. “She says she won’t send him first, because if the Hall comes on the road, he’ll be alone on it. She says she’ll go with him. When there are four to pull.” Sifa looked at Riarhin. “Her people who went are waiting at the head of the stream. Two of them will come back for him tonight. Two isn’t enough.”

Riarhin looked at the old man on the furs. He was looking at the roof of the tent. He did not seem to be listening.

“Hrogaz is going to the other hearths in the morning,” said Riarhin.

“Yes.”

He was. It had been settled the night before, in the whalebone shelter: Hrogaz would go down the valley at first light, to the lower gatherings where the eastern hearths were camped, to speak to them, to ask them for speakers. He had asked Orm and Riarhin to come with him. He had not asked it kindly. He had said it the way he said everything to them, short and hard, without looking at them. But he had asked. Come and stand where they can see you. Let them see what the coast sent.

Orm had said yes. Riarhin had said yes.

She stood in the dim tent and looked at Gaurra on her furs beside her brother. Gaurra looked back at her.

“We’ll pull,” said Riarhin. “Tonight. Orm and me. With her two.”

Sifa said it. Gaurra did not answer for a while.

Then she said one thing, short.

“She says Hrogaz goes at first light,” said Sifa. “If you’re on the road to the high valley all night, you won’t be back.”

“I know.”

“She says he’ll go without you.”

“I know.”

Gaurra looked at her a moment longer. Then she turned her head and looked at her brother, and put her hand on his forehead, and left it there.

They pulled Asgrim up to the high valley in the dark, Riarhin and Orm and two of Gaurra’s grandsons, with Gaurra walking behind on her two sticks, along the bottom of the frozen stream under the bank where the ridge could not see. It took all night. The snow was deep and soft and the sled bogged at every bend, and twice they had to unload the old man and carry him on a blanket between them and drag the empty sled after.
