Riarhin’s leg held. She did not know how. She pulled with her left hand on the trace and her bad arm hanging, and the thigh burned and burned and held.

They reached the head of the stream at grey dawn. Gaurra’s people were there, in a hollow among rocks, with small fires and the dogs. They came and took the sled and the old man without a word and put him by a fire.

Gaurra came up last, on her sticks, out of the dark, and stood among her people, and looked round at them. Then at Riarhin and Orm.

She did not thank them. Riarhin had not expected her to.

She said something to Sifa, who had come up the stream behind them. It was not long.

“She says,” said Sifa, “tell Hrogaz her hearth is in the high valley, and out of it. She says tell him she isn’t under his speakers, and isn’t under his asking, and if the Hall comes for anybody this winter, she hopes it’s for the ones who made a noise.” Sifa paused. “She says tell him she didn’t want to go. She says tell him it was his noise that sent her.”

Gaurra turned away and went to her brother by the fire.

Hrogaz had gone.

He had gone at first light, down the valley, to the eastern hearths, as he had said he would, without them. Riarhin and Orm came back into the gathering in the middle of the morning, grey with tiredness, and Orra told them, and Riarhin sat down by the fire outside Orra’s shelter and did not get up again for a long time.

Temür was there.

He had stayed, because Hrogaz had not asked him to come and because there was a singer at one of the western fires, an old man, who knew songs from before the crossing, and Temür had wanted to hear them. He was sitting by Orra’s fire with his knees drawn up and his face set, and Bródd was sitting beside him, and neither of them was talking.

“What happened?” said Riarhin.

Temür did not answer at once.

“He knows the song,” he said at last. “The old man. The one Borte said. About the cold place, and the mark on the door, and the walk on the ice roped together.” He looked at the fire. “He knows it. Not like Borte. Different. Longer. There are verses in his I never heard. About who did the roping. About the mark being cut on the doors in the night, before.”

“Then that’s good.”

“He doesn’t know the end.” Temür’s voice was flat. “Borte’s end. Where some of them slip the rope in the storm and run. He doesn’t have it.” He picked up a stick and put it in the fire. “I sang it to him. The end. Borte’s lines. So he would know it.” He watched the stick catch. “He said that is not how it ends.”

“How does it end?”

“In his,” said Temür, “they all go in. Roped. All of them. To the Hall. Nobody runs.” He looked at her. “He says that’s the end he had from his grandmother, and her grandmother, and that’s how it ends. He says my end is a story somebody made up after so they could live with it.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“And I say his end is a story somebody made up so they wouldn’t have to remember the ones who got away,” said Temür. “Because if some got away, then the rest could have.” He threw the stick into the fire. “We don’t know. Neither of us. His grandmother was there and Borte’s grandmother was there and they weren’t at the same place, or the same storm, or the same rope.” He was quiet. “There were a lot of ropes.”

Bródd had not moved. He sat beside Temür with his big hands hanging between his knees and looked at the fire.

“I wanted him to know,” he said suddenly. “The old man. I wanted him to know which one is true.”

They looked at him.

“If his is true,” said Bródd, “then they all went in, and the ones who were given to the coast at the house of seals were given after. From what was left. If Borte’s is true, some ran, and some were given, and—” He stopped. “I wanted to know which. I wanted somebody to tell me which. So I’d know where mine came from. Which rope.” He looked at his hands. “He won’t. He doesn’t know either.”

Sifa had come to the fire. She stood behind Bródd and looked down at him, and Riarhin saw her face, and for a moment she thought Sifa would say something hard.

Sifa touched his shoulder.

“Hessa’s mother has a place by her fire,” she said, in the northern speech. “Tonight. She asked me who the big one is from the coast with the old words. I said a cooper.” She took her hand away. “She has a sled that wants a new bed. She says if you mend it you can sleep by her fire as long as you’re here.”

Bródd looked up at her.

“That’s all,” said Sifa. “A fire. Tonight. I don’t know about the rope.”

Hrogaz came back two days later, from the eastern hearths, and he came back with speakers.

Not as many as he had wanted. Riarhin watched him come up the valley in the dusk with a string of sleds behind him and people walking beside the sleds, and counted: eleven hearths, out of the twenty-odd camped in the four valleys. Eleven had sent somebody. The rest had not. Some had said no. Some had said nothing.

He gathered them in the whalebone shelter that night. The eleven, and Orra for the hearth under the crag, and the elders who had come with them, and the company from the coast standing at the back.

It went on for a long time. Riarhin followed what she could, with Bródd and Temür beside her. She followed enough. She followed that the eleven had not agreed about what they were asking for. Some wanted the Hall to spare them this winter. Some wanted the four valleys left alone for good. Some, three or four of them, old and angry, wanted something Riarhin did not understand, a thing Bródd could not find the northern for and that Temür, when she asked, said was like a hearing, a counting back. They argued it round the long fire. They did not agree.

But they agreed on Hrogaz.

“Why him?” said Riarhin, very low, to Bródd, when it had been said and the elders had nodded one after another along the benches.

Bródd listened to the old man who was speaking.

“Because he’s owed,” he said slowly. “Like them. He sent his own daughter on the sled. Three winters ago. Gyrd. He’s got a claim like theirs. The same claim.” Bródd listened. “They say a speaker who isn’t owed will be easy to buy. They say if he has something the Hall took from him, he’ll keep asking for it, and while he’s asking for his, he’s asking for theirs.”

“And if the Hall offers him his daughter back?” said Riarhin.

Bródd asked it. He asked it aloud, in the throat-speech, before Riarhin could stop him.

The shelter went quiet. Along the benches the elders looked at each other. Hrogaz, on his furs with the staff across his knees, did not move.

An old woman answered. She answered slowly and plainly, and Bródd said it after her in the same way.

“She says then he can take her,” said Bródd. “For himself. That’s his. Nobody can stop him.” He listened. “But then he isn’t their speaker any more. Then he’s a man who settled. And the eleven hearths have to choose somebody else, there, wherever they are, in the middle of it.” He paused. “She says they don’t know who that would be. She says they’ve been arguing for two days about who it should be if it isn’t him, and they haven’t got anywhere, and they’ll be arguing about it in the Hall too, probably.” He listened again. “She says the Hall will like that.”

Nobody laughed.

Hrogaz, on his furs, put the staff down beside him. He laid it along the hides with both hands and took them away, and sat with his hands on his knees and the staff a hand’s breadth from his hip, and looked at the fire.

Chapter 22 — Vekk’s hands

Vekk could not read. Everybody in the gathering said so.

Riarhin heard it on her first day, and on her second, and every time anybody spoke of the old script. Old Vekk had it once, they said. His father had it. His father’s father was the last warden who could read the staves straight off the wood. Vekk had it as a boy. He lost it. His eyes went, or his memory, or it was never as much as people said. They said it fondly, the way people speak of an old man who had once been something.

He was very old. He sat all day at a fire at the western end of the gathering, among the hearths that had come up from the lower valleys, wrapped in a great many furs, with a pipe of bone that he did not light. He was small and bent and nearly bald, and his skin had gone the colour of old ash, and the blue lines on his jaw had faded to grey. His eyes were milky at the edges. Children brought him things to eat and he ate them. People came and sat by him and talked and he listened. When the talk was about the staves, or the Hall, or the old words, he looked at the fire and said he was an old man and had forgotten.

Riarhin did not think about him much, at first. She had other things to think about. Then, on the morning after Hrogaz came back with the speakers, a boy drove a tent-peg into the snow.

It was Orra’s grandson, the one with the dog-halter. He was helping his mother with the shelter, which had blown loose at one corner in the night, and he needed a peg, and he went looking for one, and he came back with a stick.

Riarhin was sitting by Orra’s fire with her leg out. She saw him come back. She saw the stick in his hand: a length of dark wood, old, as long as his arm, flattened on two sides. She saw that it was cut all over with strokes.

So did Vekk.

He had been brought to Orra’s fire that morning, because Orra’s fire was the warmest at the western end and the wind had changed, and he was sitting on a heap of hides on the other side of it with his bone pipe in his mouth. He was looking at the fire. Then he was not looking at the fire. Riarhin saw his head turn, very slightly, toward the boy.

The boy set the stick against the loose corner of the shelter and lifted a stone to drive it in. He set it upside down. Riarhin could see that it was upside down, because she had looked at enough of the oath-script by now to know that the strokes had a way up, the count-marks at the head and the long lines running down from them, and the boy had set it with the count-marks in the snow.

The stone came down. The stick went in a hand’s breadth.

Vekk was out of his furs.

He did not get up. He could not have got up that fast. He leaned, all at once, across the corner of the fire, further than Riarhin would have believed his old body could reach, and his hand closed on the stick above the boy’s, and stopped it.

The boy looked at him in astonishment.

Vekk did not look at the boy. He pulled the stick out of the snow. He turned it in his hands, end for end, so that the count-marks were at the top. And then, without seeming to know he was doing it, he ran his thumb down the first line of strokes, slowly, from top to bottom, the way Sifa had run her finger down the road-posts, and his lips moved.

Riarhin watched his lips.

They moved the whole length of the line. They shaped words, silently, one after another, in the right order, steady, the way lips move when somebody is reading to themselves and has done it all their life.

Then Vekk looked up, and saw her watching him.

His lips stopped.

They stopped in the middle of a word. He sat with the stick in his hands and looked at her across the fire, with his milky old eyes, and she looked back, and neither of them said anything. Around them the gathering went on: the boy staring, Orra calling from inside the shelter, a dog barking at the far end of the valley.

Vekk held out the stick to the boy.

He said something to him, short, gruff, and the boy took it and looked at it and looked at the old man and went off with it, back toward the western hearths, and came back after a while with a plain stick, which he drove into the snow at the corner of the shelter without anybody stopping him.

Vekk settled back into his furs. He put his bone pipe in his mouth. He looked at the fire.

Riarhin did not say anything.

She could have. She thought about it. She thought about Hrogaz in the whalebone shelter, saying he had nothing left to try, and about the clear slab in the hollow under the crag, and Sifa’s finger on the strokes, and the places where Sifa had stopped because she did not know the words. She thought of saying, in front of Orra and her grandson and whoever else was in earshot: he can read. She could see what would happen if she did. The whole gathering would know by dark. They would come to his fire, all of them, the eleven hearths and the speakers and Hrogaz with his staff, and stand round him in a ring and ask him, and ask him, and ask him.

She did not say anything.

She sat by the fire with her leg out and watched the old man look at the flames, and after a while he took the pipe out of his mouth and turned his head and looked at her again. It was a long look. It was the look of somebody taking a measurement.

He sent for her that night.

It was the boy who came, the one with the dog-halter, sent across the dark gathering to Orra’s fire with a message he had plainly been made to repeat until he had it. He said it to Sifa, carefully, in the throat-speech.

“He says come,” said Sifa. “The woman who watched. And the rider whose mark it is.” She paused. “And the one with the old words, to say it in the coast-talk. And me.” She looked at the boy. “Nobody else. He says if anybody else comes, he’s an old man and he’s forgotten.”

“The rider whose mark it is,” said Riarhin. “He said that?”

Sifa asked the boy. The boy nodded, solemn.

Temür had gone very still.

Vekk was not at his fire. He was in a small shelter of hides at the very end of the western hearths, on the edge of the gathering, alone, with a lamp of seal-fat burning in a stone dish and the flap pulled down. There was room for five people inside it if they sat close. They sat close: Riarhin, Temür, Bródd, Sifa, round the lamp, with their knees nearly touching. Vekk sat at the back on his furs.

Beside him on the hides there was a case. It was long and narrow, of sealskin, very old, the fur worn off it in patches. It was open. Inside it, laid side by side, there were staves. Riarhin counted them without meaning to. Eight. Dark wood, flattened on two sides, the length of a forearm, cut all over with strokes. One of them was the stick the boy had tried to drive into the snow.

Vekk looked at them, one after another, round the lamp. He looked longest at Temür. Then he spoke.

It was the throat-speech, but old, older than Sifa’s, slower, and Bródd had to lean close and ask him twice to say it again. Sifa helped. Temür listened with his hands on his knees and his face set.

“He says,” said Bródd, “that he has been able to read since he was six. He says his father taught him, and his father’s father taught his father. He says he could read every stave in the four valleys by the time he was twelve. He says when he was forty, the warden of that time came to him, and asked him to read something, and he read it.” Bródd listened. “And then he decided he would never read again. Not where anybody could see.”

“Why?” said Riarhin.

Bródd asked. Vekk answered for a long time.

“He says because of what was in it,” said Bródd. “He says because if nobody can read the staves, then nobody knows what they say. And if nobody knows what they say, nobody can be blamed for it.” He listened. “He says he thought, if the hearths can’t read their own oath, they can’t be held to have known. They’re only doing what was always done. They’re only paying. Like weather.” He paused. “He says he thought that was a mercy. He has thought it for forty years. He says he doesn’t know any more whether it was a mercy or whether he was afraid.”

Vekk said something else, short.

“He says he’s eighty-one,” said Bródd, “and he’s tired of carrying it by himself.”

“He’ll read it,” said Sifa. “He says he’ll read it. To us. Here. Now.”

“What does he want?” said Riarhin.

Bródd asked. Vekk looked at Riarhin across the lamp for a long moment before he answered.

“He says he wants somebody else to remember it,” said Bródd. “After. Not him. Somebody else. So it isn’t only in his head when he dies.” He listened. “He says he watched you watching him. At the fire. With the stick.” Bródd hesitated. “He says the coast-woman looks at things as if she is going to keep them. He says, is that true?”

Riarhin looked at the old man.

She thought of the ash at Ottarshall, and the key, and the rows of stumps at the woodlot, and the painted eye on the Hjalti’s bow. She thought of every face in every hall she had sat in since the Staðfast came in under the whale-bone gate. She had not forgotten any of them. She did not know how.

“Yes,” she said.

Vekk nodded slowly, as if he had known.

Then he took the first stave out of the case, and laid it across his knees with the count-marks toward him, and put his thumb on the first line, and began to read.

He read slowly. He read with his thumb, from top to bottom of each line, and his lips moved, and the words came out of him low and dry and very old, and he did not stop when he saw them watching his lips. Bródd said each line after him in the northern speech. Sifa, when Bródd stumbled, said a word. Temür sat with his hands on his knees and listened.

It was not the oath.

It was older than that, or beside it. It was a record. It said, in the first lines, in the long formal way that the slab in the hollow had used for names, that it was cut by the wardens of the Hrafn-Grímr oath in the third winter of the oath, so that it would be known what had been given to the Hall as the first giving, and from whom it had been taken.

From whom.

“In the third winter of the oath,” said Bródd, slowly, after Vekk, “the wardens of the hearth of the black crag, and of the hearth of the two streams, and of the hearth of the long ice—” He stopped. “He’s saying names. Hearths. Grímhúð hearths. Went south over the ice in the dark of the winter to the country of the riders, where the riders wintered by the frozen water. And they cut a mark on the doors of the riders’ houses in the night. And came again the next night, and took from the marked houses those that were in them.” Bródd stopped again. His voice had gone very quiet. “And roped them. And brought them north over the ice to be the first giving.”

Nobody moved.

Vekk went on. His thumb went down the next line, and the next. It was a list. Each line was a household: a mark, cut beside it, and a name in the old long way, and a number. The mark was the mark that had been cut on the door. The name was the name of whoever had held that house. The number was how many had been taken out of it.

He read them one by one.

They were not Grímhúð names. Riarhin heard that at once, even in the old throat-speech, even through Bródd’s careful northern. They were rider names, the long rolling names of the steppe, mangled by four hundred years of being said by people who did not speak the language. Bródd said them as best he could. Temür, beside him, listened to each one with his eyes shut.

At the eleventh line, Vekk’s thumb stopped.

He did not say the name. He looked down at the stave across his knees, at the mark cut beside the line, and then he lifted the stave and turned it, a little, toward the lamp, and toward Temür. He did not say anything.

Temür opened his eyes.

He looked at the mark. Riarhin, beside him, looked too. It was small and old and worn nearly smooth by four hundred years of thumbs: two short upright strokes, side by side, joined at the bottom by a loop.

The mark on the bone in Temür’s coat.

Temür did not take the bone out. He did not need to. He looked at the mark on the stave for a long time, in the lamplight, without moving, and Riarhin watched him and did not touch him.

“Say it,” said Temür.

Vekk said it. He said the name beside the mark, in the old way, long and formal and dreadful, the household of the mother of Alaq by the frozen water, from which were taken the mother and her two sons, and a daughter of her sister, and the old man who was her husband’s father—and there was more, and then a count-mark, and then a word Riarhin did not know, and then he stopped.

“Given,” said Bródd. “The last word. It says given. They were given. At the Hall.”

“The mother of Alaq,” said Temür.

He said it in Ykrul. Not to anybody. He said it to the lamp.

“Alaq is in the song,” he said, in the northern speech, after a moment, for the others. “Borte’s song. The old one. Alaq who rode the season with the Tögüs. She was a foster-daughter. She was sent away to ride a season with another clan when she was small. That’s how we do it. To make a bond.” He looked at the stave. “She wasn’t home. When they came. She was riding with the Tögüs.” He stopped. “She came back in the spring and the house was empty.”

Nobody said anything.

Vekk’s thumb moved to the next line. He read it. Another mark, another long name. The household of the brother of the woman of the red horse. Taken: four. And then a different word at the end.

“Slipped,” said Bródd. “It says—I think it says they slipped. The rope. In the storm on the ice. Slipped the rope and were not given.”

Temür turned his head and looked at Bródd.

“Borte’s end,” he said.

“One household,” said Bródd. “One. The next one—” He listened to Vekk. “The next one was given. And the next.”

“And the old man’s end,” said Temür. “At the western fire. They all went in.” He looked back at the stave. “Both. Some ran. Some didn’t. Different houses.” He laughed, once, not at anything. “Different ropes.”

There were twenty-two households on the stave. Vekk read them all. It took a long time. When he had finished the last one he laid the stave back in the case, beside the others, and sat with his hands in his lap and looked at the lamp.

“These aren’t all,” said Riarhin.

Bródd asked it. Vekk shook his head.

“He says no,” said Bródd. “He says the songs have more names than this. Riders’ songs. Other houses that were lost, other winters, other crossings. He says he doesn’t know them. He says they aren’t on his staves.” Bródd listened. “He says the Hrafn-Grímr did this. These hearths. These wardens. In that winter. He says other people may have done other things to other riders in other places and he doesn’t know and he won’t say he does.” He paused. “He says he hid this. That’s what he’s ashamed of. Not what the wardens did in the third winter. He wasn’t born. He says he’s ashamed that he could read it and he hid it for forty years.”

Sifa had not said anything for a long time. She was sitting with her knees drawn up and her hands in her sleeves, looking at the case of staves.

“The ones who did it,” she said, in the northern speech, slowly. “The hearth of the black crag. The two streams. The long ice.” Her jaw moved. “The black crag is my aunt’s hearth. The hollow under the crag. That’s the old name.”

Temür looked at her.

Sifa did not look away. She looked back at him across the lamp, flat, yellow-brown, and she did not touch her jaw.

“I didn’t rope anybody,” she said. “Four hundred years ago. I wasn’t there.” She paused. “I was on the hill with the sleds at Ottarshall. I didn’t go down. That’s mine. That one’s mine. Not that.” She nodded at the case. “I won’t say I’m sorry for that. I won’t say they were right either. I won’t say anything for them. They’re dead.”

Temür was quiet a long time.

“No,” he said at last. “I don’t want you to.”

Afterward, when the lamp was low and Vekk had closed the case and sat with his hand on it, Temür asked him for one more thing.

He asked it in Ykrul, first, to Riarhin, very low, so that she would put it into the northern for Bródd, and Bródd into the old speech for Vekk. He did not want to ask it himself in a language he would get wrong.

Ask him to say her name again, he said. Not all of it. Not the household and the taken and the given. Only her name. Alaq’s mother’s. The way it is cut. Only that.

Riarhin said it. Bródd said it.

Vekk looked at Temür for a long moment across the lamp. Then he opened the case again, and took out the stave, and found the line with his thumb without looking for it, and said the name.

He said it slowly. It was four words long, in the old way, and it was a woman’s name, and the name of her mother, and the name of the place by the frozen water where she had kept her house. He said it once and stopped.

Temür said it after him.

He said it badly, in his rider’s mouth, the sounds wrong, the way a man says a word in a language he has only heard old people speak. Vekk listened, and said it again, more slowly. Temür said it again. Vekk shook his head, very slightly, and said one part of it a third time, the middle part, the part that was her mother’s name. Temür said that part. And then the whole of it again.

They went on like that for a long time. Riarhin did not count how long. She sat in the small hide shelter with her knees against Sifa’s and the lamp guttering in its stone dish and listened to an old man who had not read aloud in forty years teach a young man from the steppe to say the name of a woman his family had been singing about for four hundred years without knowing it, a piece at a time, over and over, until he had it.

Chapter 23 — The family not on the stave

By noon the next day the whole gathering knew that Old Vekk could read, and that the rider from the steppe had a name on one of his staves.

Nobody had told them. Riarhin had not, and Bródd had not, and she did not believe Sifa or Temür had. Vekk himself had done it. He had come out of his shelter at first light with the sealskin case under his arm, and walked through the western hearths to Hrogaz’s shelter at the head of the valley, slowly, on a stick, with the case, and gone in, and the people at the fires he passed had watched him go, and by the time he came out again everybody knew.

They looked at Temür differently after that.

Riarhin saw it. She saw it at Orra’s fire, where the women stopped talking when he came to fill his bowl and then started again, too loudly. She saw it along the paths between the shelters, where people stood aside for him, which they had not done before. She did not know what it meant. Temür, she thought, did not either.

It was a young man who said it. In the afternoon, by the dog pens, where Temür had gone to help Orra’s grandson with the halter. A young Grímhúð of perhaps twenty, broad and earnest, with his jaw-lines new, who came up to Temür and stood in front of him and said something in the throat-speech, slowly, so that Temür would understand it, and then said it again with his hands, pointing at Temür and then round at the gathering, at all of it, the fires and the shelters and the people.

Temür did understand it. Riarhin, coming along the path with her staff, saw his face.

“What did he say?” she said to Sifa, who was beside her.

Sifa listened to the young man say it a third time.

“He says Vekk’s stave says the hearths took the rider’s people,” said Sifa. “He says that means the rider has a claim. Against the hearths. Against the black crag and the two streams and the long ice.” She paused. “He says that’s all of us. Everybody here is from one of them, or married into one. He says—” She stopped. “He says he’s sorry. He says the rider can name what he wants.”

The young man was standing in front of Temür with his hands open. He meant it. Riarhin could see that he meant it, with the whole of his earnest face.

Temür looked at him for a long moment.

Then he said, in the northern speech, slowly, so that Sifa could put it into the throat-speech word for word: “I don’t know you. I don’t know your mother. I don’t know your grandmother. Your grandmother didn’t take anybody. You didn’t.” He looked round at the gathering. “I don’t want anything from you. I don’t want anything from her.” He nodded at Orra, at her fire. “I don’t want anything from him.” Orra’s grandson, with the halter. “I don’t have a claim against a boy with a dog.”

Sifa said it. The young man listened. His face went through several things.

“Then what do you want?” he said, and Sifa said it after him.

Temür did not answer him. He turned and walked away from the dog pens, up through the gathering, toward the western hearths, toward Vekk’s shelter at the end of them.

Vekk was sitting outside it, on his furs, in the thin sun, with the sealskin case beside him. He looked up when Temür came, and when Riarhin came behind him, and Sifa, and then Bródd, who had seen them go and followed.

Temür stood in front of him.

“The stave,” he said. Bródd said it after him. “Alaq’s mother. Her household. It says given. Given at the Hall.” He did not raise his voice. “What happened after?”

Vekk looked at him.

“They were given,” said Temür. “To the Hall. The mother and two sons and the sister’s daughter and the old man. They went in. Into the Hall. Then what?” He took a breath. “Did they die? How? Did they live? How long? Where are they? Is there a stave that says?” His voice cracked very slightly and came back. “You read it. You said you can read every stave in the four valleys. Read me what happened after.”

Vekk did not answer for a long time.

Then he opened the case. He took out the stave with the twenty-two households on it and found the eleventh line with his thumb and turned the stave toward Temür in the sun.

He put his thumb at the end of the line. On the last stroke. On the word Bródd had said was given.

He did not move his thumb. He held it there. He looked at Temür.

“He says it stops,” said Bródd quietly. “He says that’s the end of the line. He says nobody cut what happened after. The wardens cut what they brought. Not what the Hall did with it.” He listened. “He says he doesn’t know. He says nobody in the four valleys knows. He says perhaps the Hall knows.”

Temür looked at the old man’s thumb on the last stroke.

He stood there a long time, in the thin sun. Then he turned round and walked away again, out of the western hearths, toward the stream.

Riarhin went after him.

She should not have. She knew it while she was doing it, going down the slope toward the stream on her staff, slowly, with the leg dragging. She went anyway.

He was standing on the bank of the stream, on the snow, looking at the ice.

“There are seven more staves,” she said. “In the case. He’s only read us one.” She stopped beside him. “There might be something. Another record. Something about what the Hall—” She stopped. “I could help you look. I could ask him to read them. All of them. I’d remember it.”

Temür did not look at her.

“Why?” he said.

She did not understand.

“Why would you help me look?” He said it in Ykrul, quietly. “Because I’m sad? Because I found my family on a stick and it stopped?” He turned his head. “You look at me like that. Since the fire at Hvalgard. Since my hands round the bowl. You look at me like I’m somebody you’re sorry for.”

“I’m not—”

“You are.” His face was very still. “You’re sorry for somebody. I don’t know if it’s me.”

Riarhin did not answer.

She thought of a cart going out of a granary gate in the dark. She thought of kneeling in mud with her hand on a man’s neck. She thought of getting up. She thought of a slow deep voice on a ship’s deck, at night, telling her nothing at all about a horse, for an hour, because she could not sleep, and she thought that Na’ar would have let her help. Na’ar would have let her ask the old man to read all eight staves, and sat beside her while she listened, and said something courteous about the weather at the end of it, and she would have felt better.

She did not say any of it. It would have been true, and it would have been a way of winning, and she did not want to win.

“I’m sorry,” she said instead. “I don’t want to be sorry at you.”

Temür looked at her for a long moment.

Then he looked back at the ice.

“Then don’t,” he said.

He came to Orra’s fire that night with a roll of birch-bark under his arm and a burnt stick for marking, and stood over her where she sat.

“Vekk says I can copy it,” he said. “The eleventh line. And the twelfth. The one that slipped the rope.” He held up the birch-bark. “The marks. So Borte has them. So the old man at the western fire has them. So they’re not only on a stick in a case in the four valleys.” He paused. “He says the shelter’s too dark. He says somebody has to hold the lamp close and not move it.” He looked down at her. “You keep things. You can keep a lamp still.”

Riarhin looked up at him.

“Yes,” she said.

So she held the lamp. She sat on the hides in Vekk’s shelter with the stone dish of seal-fat in both hands, the good one and the bad one, close over the stave where it lay across Temür’s knees, and kept it still, and Temür bent over the birch-bark with the burnt stick and copied the marks, stroke by stroke, very slowly, his rider’s fingers clumsy with the stick. Vekk watched him. Twice he reached out and put his old finger on a stroke on the bark that was wrong, and Temür looked, and scraped it with his nail, and did it again.

It took a long time. Her arms ached. The bad one shook. She did not move the lamp.

When he had finished he rolled the bark up and tied it with a thong and put it inside his coat, against his chest, where the bone with the mark was.

“Thank you,” he said, in Ykrul, not looking at her.

“It was a lamp.”

“Yes,” said Temür. “That’s what I wanted.”

The other staves were the oath.

Vekk read them two days later, in Hrogaz’s shelter, to the speakers of the eleven hearths. He chose that himself too. He had read the first stave, the record, to four people in a dark tent at the edge of the gathering; he read the rest to forty, in the whalebone shelter, by the long fire, with Hrogaz on his furs at the head of it and the staff across his knees.

It was the clan’s copy. The Hrafn-Grímr’s own. Seven staves of dark wood, the oath-script cut close on every face, kept in the sealskin case by Vekk’s father and his father’s father since before anybody could remember. And it was ruined.

Riarhin saw that when Vekk took them out and laid them along the furs. They had been kept somewhere wet, once, or frozen and thawed too often. The wood had split along the grain. Whole faces of some staves had flaked away, taking the strokes with them, and others had gone soft and grey and the strokes on them were blurred, sunk into the wood like footprints in old snow. Vekk ran his thumb along them and his lips moved and stopped and moved, and where the wood was gone he said so.

He read what was left.

It was not all the oath. Riarhin could tell that from what Ilva had shown her, even without being able to read a stroke. There were the beginnings of things Ilva’s vellum had in full, and gaps where the wood had gone, and long passages Vekk could only read a word in four of. But there was one stave that was nearly whole. The fifth in the case. It had been kept at the bottom, Vekk said, under the others, and the wet had not reached it.

He read it twice.

Bródd said it after him in the northern speech. Riarhin listened. She listened the way she had listened to Ilva in the stores shed at Dalsendi, with her whole attention, keeping every word.

It said that the oath bound both sides.

It said, in the long formal way, that the Hall had sworn as the hearths had sworn, before the same witnesses, in the same ice, and that what the Hall had sworn bound the Hall. It said that if the Hall took what the oath did not give it, or kept what the oath did not let it keep, that was a wrong done against the words. And it said that a wrong done against the words could be brought.

“Brought,” said Bródd. He stopped and asked Vekk. Vekk said it again. “Brought to the Hall. By one who is sworn. Before witnesses. The sworn may come to the Hall and stand in it and say the wrong aloud, before witnesses who are not the Hall’s, and ask that it be heard.” He listened. “And the Hall shall hear it. And if the words show the wrong, the Hall shall undo it. With her own hand. And not another’s.”

The shelter was very quiet.

“Her own hand,” said Hrogaz. He said it in the throat-speech, low, and Bródd said it after him. “Not another’s.”

“She may not lay the undoing on any who are not of the wrong,” said Bródd, as Vekk read on. “She may not take from one to give back to another. What she took wrongly, she gives back from what she holds. What she bound wrongly, she unbinds.” He listened. “He says that’s the old oath. That’s holding her to the words. That’s what it means to bring a wrong.”

An old woman on the left-hand bench spoke, sharply. Riarhin saw Orra, beside her, nod.

“She asks what if the Hall hides it,” said Bródd. “What it took. If it puts it somewhere nobody can see. In some room. Down some hole.” He turned to Vekk. Vekk’s thumb was already moving on the stave, further down, and his lips with it.

“What is to be given back is given back where it is,” said Bródd slowly, after him. “The witnesses do not hold it in their eyes. They see that it is done.” He listened to Vekk say something more, in his own voice. “He says hiding a thing doesn’t make it not owed. It makes it harder to see it given back. That’s all. The witnesses don’t have to stare at it the whole time like a dog at a door. They have to see it done.”

“And then?” said somebody along the benches.

Vekk read on.

There was more. There was a passage about settlement. It said that the sworn who brought a wrong might, instead of asking the Hall to undo it, agree to something new with the Hall: a new thing, sworn fresh, between the Hall and whoever brought the wrong. And that such a thing, freely sworn, would bind them as the old oath bound them. And that a hearth that settled its own wrong in this way settled only its own, and not any other hearth’s.

And then Vekk stopped reading, and looked up from the stave, and spoke to the shelter in his own voice, not reading.

Bródd said it slowly.

“He says listen to this part,” said Bródd. “He says, a new thing. It doesn’t say a good thing. It doesn’t say a fair thing. It says a thing you agree to. If the Hall offers you something, and you take it, you’ve taken it. It doesn’t matter who it hurts.” He listened. “He says the old oath has a shape. If you hold her to it, she has to give back what she took from you, from her own hand. She can’t make anybody else pay for it. That’s the old oath.” He paused. “But if you say, no, don’t give it back, give me this instead, and she gives you that instead, then that’s what you asked for. And if this is your neighbour’s valley—” He stopped.

“Then it’s your neighbour’s valley,” said Hrogaz.

Vekk nodded.

“He says the words don’t stop you choosing badly,” said Bródd. “He says the words only say what you chose.”

Riarhin asked about the rest.

She asked it late, when the shelter had begun to empty, of Vekk, through Bródd. She asked whether there was anything on the staves about an ending. About a count. About what happened when the count was full.

Vekk looked at her across the fire for a long time.

Then he picked up one of the ruined staves, the eighth in the case, the one most split and grey, and ran his thumb down a face of it where the strokes had sunk almost into nothing. He ran it slowly. His lips moved and stopped. He did it again.

“He says there’s something here,” said Bródd. “He says it might be a count. He says there’s a word that might be full. He says the rest of it’s gone.” He listened. “He says the stave this would have joined to is gone altogether. Not in the case. Never was, that he knows.” Bródd paused. “He says the whole oath is at the Hall. All ten. Cut in the ice where it was sworn. He says his grandfather said so. He says if there’s an ending, it’s there.”

Riarhin took Ilva’s folded paper out of her coat. She had carried it since Dalsendi against her ribs. She unfolded it and showed it to Vekk across the fire: the two columns in Ilva’s small hand, the oath-script stroke by stroke, the end of the eighth, the count-mark for ten, and the space between them the width of three fingers with nothing in it.

Vekk looked at it a long time. He put out his old finger and touched the empty space. He did not say anything.

At the head of the shelter, Hrogaz had been watching. He got up from his furs, and laid the staff down on them with both hands, and came down the long fire and looked at the paper too.

He looked at the empty space. He looked at the clan copy, the split and sunken wood, the word that might be full. He looked at Vekk.

“He says,” said Bródd, when Hrogaz had spoken, “that he doesn’t know what the ninth stave says. Nobody does. He says perhaps it says nothing that helps.” Bródd listened. “He says the fifth stave says the sworn can bring a wrong. He says the Hall took his daughter, and the four from the coast, and it’s going to take Orra’s children in the dark of this winter. He says he doesn’t know if any of that is a wrong against the words. He says he’ll have to stand in the Hall and say it, and find out.”

He looked round the shelter, at the speakers who were left, the old men and women of the eleven hearths along the benches.

“He says that’s what the speakers will carry,” said Bródd. “Not that they know. That they don’t. That they’re going to find out.” He paused. “He says nobody should go who thinks they know.”

Along the benches, one by one, slowly, the speakers nodded.

Riarhin folded Ilva’s paper along its creases and put it back against her ribs.

Chapter 24 — The courteous dead

He came on the evening of the fourteenth day at the gathering, out of the dark at the top of the valley, on foot, and the dogs did not bark.

That was the first thing. Every dog in the valley had barked at her when she came, and at Hrogaz’s sleds when they came back from the east, and at every stranger who had crossed the ridge in fourteen days. They did not bark at him. They went quiet. All of them, all at once, the length of the valley, as if somebody had shut a door on them.

She was in Hrogaz’s shelter. Most of the speakers were. They had been talking since noon about the road, and the sleds, and what they would carry to the Hall and who would carry it, and the long fire had burned down to embers and been built up again, and Hrogaz was on his furs at the head of it with the staff laid along the hides beside him.

The dogs went quiet.

Riarhin looked up. She saw Sifa, across the fire, look up too. She saw Sifa’s hand come out of her sleeve and go to her jaw and stay there.

Then the flap at the end of the shelter lifted, and a man came in out of the cold.

He was tall, and he was dressed for a hall. That was the second thing. Everybody in the gathering wore what they had: old furs, mended, patched, layered. He wore a long coat of white bear-fur, clean and combed, belted with a belt of plaited cord, and under it good felt, and boots of sealskin sewn with a close fine seam. His hair was grey and braided back. The blue lines on his jaw were very dark and very clear. He had a stave in his hand, a black one, like Hrogaz’s.

He stood just inside the flap, in the edge of the firelight, and he bowed.

It was a slow bow, from the waist, with the stave held upright beside him, and it went to the whole shelter, to the benches and the fire and the furs at the head of it. Then he straightened, and stood, and waited.

Nobody moved.

Riarhin looked at his face. It was a long grave face, the face of a man of sixty, with the lines of somebody who had spent his life listening to people and deciding things. It was still. The firelight was on it, and the firelight did not move on it the way firelight moves on skin. She could not have said what was wrong. She looked at it and felt the hair on her arms lift under her sleeves.

Along the benches, the speakers had gone grey.

Hrogaz had not moved either. He was sitting on his furs with his hands on his knees, looking at the man in the white coat, and his face was the colour of ash.

“Who is it?” said Riarhin. She said it very low, in Ykrul, to Temür, beside her. She did not know why she chose Ykrul. She thought that perhaps the man in the white coat would not have it.

Temür did not answer. Sifa did. She had come round the fire without anybody seeing her move, and she was standing at Riarhin’s other shoulder, and she said it in the northern speech, under her breath, without taking her eyes off the door.

“Ruuk,” she said. “The warden before Hrogaz.”

“I thought—”

“Eleven winters,” said Sifa. “He went on the sled to the Hall. The winter they were short. He put himself on it.” Her fingers moved on her jaw. “He doesn’t come back. He hasn’t come back. That isn’t him.” She paused. “That’s the one who brings the words.”

He waited.

He stood in the edge of the firelight and waited, and it went on, and nobody spoke. Riarhin felt the shelter feel it: the silence stretching, the speakers along the benches not looking at each other, not looking at him, looking at the fire. He did not seem to mind. He stood with his stave upright beside him and his long grave face turned toward the head of the shelter, and he waited, as if he would wait all night if he had to, as if there were no reason in the world why he should not.

Hrogaz spoke at last. It was one word, in the throat-speech, low.

The man in the white coat bowed again, a small bow, as if acknowledging something correctly done. Then he spoke.

He spoke in the old speech, the slow formal speech Vekk had used to read the staves, and his voice was low and clear and very courteous, and it filled the shelter without being raised. Bródd, at Riarhin’s knee, began to say it after him in the northern, in a whisper. His voice shook.

“He says the Hall has heard,” whispered Bródd. “He says the Hall has heard that the hearths under the stave of the Hrafn-Grímr have chosen speakers, and that the speakers will come to the Hall, to be heard.” Bródd swallowed. “He says the Hall will hear them. He says the Hall has always heard whoever came, in the old way.”

The man in the white coat stopped. He waited.

Nobody answered. He waited.

Hrogaz said the one word again. The man bowed, and went on.

He named the hearths.

He named them one by one, in the old long way, the eleven that had sent speakers, and Orra’s under the crag, and at each one he stopped, and waited, and Hrogaz, on his furs, said the word, and the man bowed and went on to the next. It was very slow. It was like listening to a list being read out in a court. Riarhin had heard lists like it read out in courts in the south, and she had never been afraid of one before.

Then he named another hearth. Bródd whispered it. The hearth of the high valley, of the woman of two sticks, under the stave.

He stopped. He waited.

Hrogaz did not say the word.

He sat on his furs with his hands on his knees, and the man in the white coat stood in the edge of the firelight and waited, and Riarhin saw Hrogaz’s face, and saw him decide.

He spoke. Not one word. A sentence, short, in the throat-speech, and Riarhin did not need Bródd to tell her what it was, because she had heard Gaurra’s name in it and the shape of a refusal.

“He says not that one,” whispered Bródd. “He says the high valley did not send. He says he isn’t speaking for the high valley.”

The man in the white coat looked at Hrogaz. He looked at him a long time with his still grave face. Then he said the name of the high valley again, exactly as before, in the old long way, and stopped, and waited.

Hrogaz said it again. The same sentence. Not that one. The high valley did not send.

The man waited.

Hrogaz said it a third time. His voice was steady and very low. Riarhin saw his hand, on his knee, close.

The man in the white coat bowed.

He went on to the next part.

He named them.

Riarhin did not understand, at first, what he was doing. He had turned, very slightly, so that his still face was toward the side of the fire where the company from the coast was sitting, and he was speaking, and Bródd had stopped whispering.

“Bródd,” she said.

“He’s saying us,” said Bródd. His voice had gone very thin. “He’s saying—he’s saying who walks with the speakers. He says the Hall has heard that the speakers will not walk alone. He says the Hall has heard that others have undertaken to walk beside them. On the road. To see them come and see them go.” Bródd’s breath caught. “He says that undertaking was given. Here. In front of the stave. Before witnesses.” He stopped. “He says the Hall notes it.”

The man in the white coat said a name.

It was Orm’s. Riarhin heard it, even in the old speech: Orm, and then a word that must have been son of Ketil, and then a long phrase she did not know. The man stopped. He waited.

Orm came up off the bench.

He came up all at once, fast, the way he had come up off the bench at the stone at Kettlebeach, and his hand went to his belt where his knife was, and his face was white and his lips were drawn back from his teeth, and he took a step toward the door.

Temür had him.

He did it without getting up. He reached out from where he sat and took Orm by the wrist, the knife wrist, and held it, and said in the northern speech, very low and very hard: “No.”

Orm stood with his wrist in Temür’s hand, shaking.

The man in the white coat had not moved. He stood at the door with his stave and looked at Orm with his long still face, and waited. He did not look afraid. He did not look anything. He looked as if a man coming at him with a knife were a thing that happened from time to time and had no bearing on the matter in hand.

“What does he want?” said Orm. His voice was cracking. “What does he want me to say?”

Riarhin got up.

She did it slowly, because she had to. She got up with the staff and stood beside Orm, close, and did not touch him.

“He wants an answer,” she said. She said it in the northern speech, steadily, for all of them. “To the undertaking. He said it was given. He wants to know if it was.”

“It was,” said Orm. “I said it. In front of Hrogaz. We all did.” His voice rose. “We’ll walk with them. We’ll walk with them into the Hall and out again, we’ll walk with all of them, all the hearths, we’ll walk with whoever—”

“Orm.”

He stopped.

Riarhin looked at him. She looked at him the way she had looked at him across the top of the stone at Kettlebeach, with his hand flat on it and his face grey.

“That’s not what we said,” she said quietly. “We said we’d walk beside the speakers. To the Hall and back. That’s all. Not all the hearths. Not whoever. The speakers.” She held his eyes. “Say what we said. Not more.”

Orm stared at her. His wrist was still in Temür’s hand.

Then something went out of him, and he nodded, very slightly.

“Bródd,” said Riarhin. “Say it for him. Exactly what he said in front of Hrogaz. Not more.”

Bródd said it. His voice shook, but he said it. He said it in the old speech, slowly, word for word, and Riarhin listened to Temür listen and saw Temür nod once. We undertook to walk beside the speakers to the Hall, and to see them come and see them go.

The man in the white coat listened. He bowed.

He said Temür’s name next, in the old way, of the household of the mother of Alaq, and waited, and Temür answered it himself, in his bad old speech, the same words, no more. He said Bródd’s, the cooper who carries the coast-tongue, and Bródd answered it in a whisper.

Then he said hers.

He did not say it in the old way. He said it plainly. He said Riarhin, in a low courteous voice, exactly as she said it herself, with the stress in the right place, and then he said two words more, in the northern speech, which nobody in the four valleys spoke.

“Of nowhere,” he said.

Riarhin stood with the staff in her hand.

She had said that once. To Rhone, in the long house at Hornvik, with the bell on the table between them. She had not said it since. She had not said it anywhere near here.

The man waited.

“I undertook to walk beside the speakers,” said Riarhin, in the northern speech, slowly. “To the Hall and back. To see them come and see them go.” She did not take her eyes off his still face. “That’s all.”

He bowed.

He said the time and the place last.

He said it to Hrogaz, and Bródd whispered it, and it was very simple. The dark of the next moon. The foot of the ice, where the due-road comes to the Hall. The speakers would be met there and brought in. He said it once, and waited, and Hrogaz said the word, and the man bowed.

Then he did a strange thing.

He turned to go. He turned toward the flap, with his stave, and as he turned the firelight caught his coat at the shoulder, where the white bear-fur had been torn once and mended. Riarhin saw it. A long tear, from the collar down across the shoulder-blade, sewn closed with a seam that ran not straight but in a little zigzag, back and forth, tight and close, in grey thread, like the track a bird makes in snow.

Sifa made a sound.

It was very small. Riarhin, beside her, heard it and nobody else did. A breath taken in through the teeth, and stopped.

The man in the white coat did not hear it, or if he did, he did not turn round. He lifted the flap and went out into the dark, and the cold came in where he had been, and the flap fell.

Out in the valley, a long way off, a dog began to bark. Then another. Then all of them.

Nobody spoke for a long time. Along the benches the speakers sat with their hands in their laps. Hrogaz, at the head of the fire, had put his hand on the staff beside him on the furs and was holding it, not lifting it, only holding it, the way a man holds the rail of a boat.

Orm had sat down. Temür had let go of his wrist. Orm sat on the bench with his hands between his knees and looked at them, and they were shaking so hard that Riarhin could hear his knuckles knock together.

She sat down beside him. She did not say anything. After a while she put her own good hand on the bench between them, not on his, beside his, and left it there, and after a while longer he moved his hand a little until the side of it touched hers, and they sat like that.

Sifa was standing where she had stood, by the fire, looking at the flap.

“The coat,” said Riarhin quietly. “The mend. You knew it.”

Sifa did not answer.

“Who sewed it?”

Sifa’s hand was at her jaw. She held it there a long time. Then she took it away, and put it back in her sleeve, and turned from the flap and looked at Riarhin with her flat yellow-brown eyes.

“Somebody who could sew,” she said.

She did not say anything else. She went back round the fire to where she had been sitting, and sat down, and drew her knees up, and looked at the embers. And Riarhin kept the zigzag seam, tight and close, like the track a bird makes in snow, and did not ask again.

Chapter 25 — Bread at the glacier

The due-road ran north from the gathering for nine days, and on the tenth they came round the shoulder of a hill and saw the ice, and the smoke of the Bishop’s fires in front of it.

Riarhin had been told about the ice. She had been told about it for nine days by the guides, Tokk and Yrra, two of Hrogaz’s people who had hauled the due-sleds up this road every winter of their lives and had never gone further than the foot of it. She had been told it was large. She had not understood.

It filled the north. It came down out of the sky between two black mountains, a river of ice, white and grey and blue, broken into great ribs and towers along its edges and smooth and vast in the middle, and it came down and down until it reached the head of the valley and stopped in a wall. The wall was a mile wide. It was higher than the cliffs at Hvalgard. It was blue in its deep places and white on its face and streaked with black where stones were frozen in it, and the cold came off it down the valley like a wind even when there was no wind. Riarhin stood on the shoulder of the hill and felt it on her face.

And it was making a sound. Very low, so low that she felt it in her teeth before she heard it: a long slow groaning, rising and falling, like a ship’s timbers in a swell. It went on and on.

“It does that,” said Yrra, beside her. She was a short broad woman of forty with a frost-scar across her nose. “Always. My father said it’s the ice moving.” She looked at the wall. “My grandmother said it’s breathing.”

At the foot of the wall, on the flat ground of the valley floor, there was a camp. Tents in rows. Horse lines. Fires. A long white banner on a pole, limp in the cold, with a golden candle on it.

“How?” said Orm.

He was standing on Riarhin’s other side. He had been very quiet for the last two days, since the due-road had begun to climb.

“There’s another road,” said Tokk. He was Yrra’s brother, younger, thinner, with a habit of whistling through his teeth. “From the lakes. Round the west side of the mountains. Longer, but flat. Wagons can do it.” He spat. “Somebody showed them.”

The Bishop was delighted to see them.

He said so. He came out of his tent to meet them as they came down the last of the slope into the valley, the company from the coast and Hrogaz with the staff and the speakers of the eleven hearths and their sleds and dogs, a long ragged line of them in the snow, and he walked out across the flat ground toward them with his white hair blowing up off his head and his hands held out, and he said: “You came. Oh, I am so glad. I prayed you would.”

Riarhin pulled her hood down and tried to return his smile.

The camp smelled of bread. Warm, yeasty air came from a long low tent at the end of the lines, where ovens of clay and turf stood in the snow with fires under them. The Bishop had brought ovens on wagons round the west side of the mountains.

It had fire. Not small fires of peat and fat like the gathering’s. Large fires of wood, cut wood, stacked in long ricks along the lines.

And it had a physician.

Old Hesk had been coughing since the fourth day on the due-road. She was a speaker for the hearth of the two streams, a tiny old woman with white hair and a voice that had gone thin and high, and she had ridden the last five days on a sled wrapped in every fur her hearth could spare, coughing, and on the ninth night she had stopped coughing and begun to breathe instead in a way that Riarhin had heard before, in the camps, in winter, and did not like.

The Bishop heard it too. He heard it when they carried her sled past his tent, and he stopped in the middle of what he was saying to Hrogaz and turned his head and listened, and then he called a name, and a man came out of a tent further down the line: a lean dark man in a grey robe with a leather bag on his shoulder.

“Kallias,” said the Bishop. “That woman.”

The physician went to the sled. He knelt in the snow beside it and put his ear to the old woman’s chest through the furs and listened, and his face did not change.

“Inside,” he said. “Now. By a fire. And steam.”

Hrogaz was beside the sled. He looked at the physician, and at the Bishop, and at the old woman on the sled, who was looking up at the sky and breathing in short wet pulls. He said something in the throat-speech.

“He says where?” said Bródd.

“My tent,” said the Bishop. “It has the best fire. Bring her.”

They brought her.

Riarhin saw the paper an hour later.

She was in the Bishop’s tent because Hrogaz had asked her to come, through Bródd, with his face set, and she had understood that he wanted somebody there from outside. Old Hesk was on a pallet by the brazier with a kettle steaming beside her and Kallias kneeling at her head, and her breathing was a little easier. The Bishop sat at a camp table with a lamp. Brother Anselm was beside him with his wax tablet. There was a sheet of paper on the table, and the Bishop was writing on it.

“It is only a record,” he said. He said it to Hrogaz, gently, in the northern speech, and Bródd put it into the old speech. “The Temple keeps a record of everyone it treats. Their name, their place, what was done for them. So that it is known.” He wrote a line. “Hesk, of the hearth of the two streams, in the four valleys, treated by the Temple’s physician at the foot of the ice.” He looked up. “Is that her name properly?”

Hrogaz said it was.

The Bishop wrote another line. He wrote it carefully, in the southern script, and then read it aloud in the northern, as Rhone read his papers, so that everybody would know.

“And the Temple undertakes her care, as it undertakes the care of all the northern peoples under its protection.”

Bródd put it into the old speech. He did it slowly. Riarhin, watching, saw him begin to make it gentler, and stop himself, and say it as it was.

Hrogaz looked at the paper.

He said something. It was short and it was not loud. Bródd said it after him.

“He says take out the end,” said Bródd. “Under its protection. All the northern peoples. He says the hearths of the four valleys are not under anybody’s protection. He says he doesn’t speak for all the northern peoples. He says he doesn’t speak for the four valleys, even. Only eleven hearths.” Bródd listened. “He says the Bishop can write that his physician helped an old woman who was dying. He says he’s grateful. He says write that.”

The Bishop looked at him.

“It is a form of words,” he said kindly. “It means nothing. It is what we write.”

“He says then it won’t matter if you take it out,” said Bródd.

The Bishop was quiet for a moment. Riarhin watched him. She saw him look at Hrogaz, and at the black staff in Hrogaz’s hand, and at the old woman on the pallet, and she saw him weigh it.

Then he smiled, and took the pen, and drew a line through the end of the sentence. A single neat line.

“There,” he said. “Treated by the Temple’s physician at the foot of the ice. And I will add,” and he wrote, and read it aloud, “at the Bishop Serapion’s own undertaking, freely given.” He looked up. “That is true. It is mine to give and I have given it. I would like it written.”

Hrogaz looked at the line through the words for a long time. Then he nodded.

The Bishop sanded the paper and gave it to Brother Anselm, and Brother Anselm put it away in his satchel, and Riarhin watched it go and thought that the line through the words was very neat, and that the words under it could still be read.

They gave her bread.

She had not asked for it. A young priest brought it to her at the fire outside the Bishop’s tent, where she was sitting with her leg out, a whole small round loaf, warm, the crust cracking, wrapped in a cloth. He put it in her hands and went away.

She held it. It was very warm. It smelled of summer, of the gold barley running out of the split sack on the shingle at Hvalgard. She had not eaten bread like it since the south.

She ate it. She ate all of it, sitting at the fire with the cold coming off the ice on the back of her neck, slowly, and it was the best thing she had eaten since Asta’s porridge in the hold.

Kallias came out of the tent while she was eating. He stood at the fire warming his hands and looked at her leg, stretched out, and at the way she was holding it.

“That’s been opened,” he said. “Since it was stitched.”

“Yes.”

“How long ago?”

“A month. More.”

He crouched without asking and put his hand on her knee, over the trousers, and pressed, and moved it up her thigh along the line of the scar, pressing, and watched her face. She did not stop him.

“It’s closed,” he said. “Badly. It’ll hold. Keep it warm. Don’t sit on cold stone with it. Don’t kneel on it on ice.” He stood up. “If it goes hot and red again, come to me.”

“Thank you,” said Riarhin.

“You’ll need it,” said Kallias, and glanced at the wall of the ice. “Where you’re going.” He paused. “The Bishop would be glad to see you there with his people. Under his banner. He’d send men with you. He has a great many men.”

“I know.”

“He’d ask very little.”

Riarhin looked at the warm crumbs in the cloth in her lap.

“Thank him for the bread,” she said. “And the advice.” She folded the cloth. “Tell him we go in with the guides from the four valleys, and the road they know, and a way back that we’ve agreed. Tell him that’s all.”

Kallias looked at her a long moment. Then he nodded, as if it were the answer he had expected and did not blame her for, and went back into the tent.

Ilva found them at dusk.

She came along the lines in her dark robe with her leather case under her arm, and she was not alone; Rhone was a pace behind her, with his clean narrow face, and two soldiers. Ilva did not look at Rhone. She came to the fire where Riarhin was sitting with Orm and Temür and Bródd, and stood, and said:

“I’m coming in with you.”

Riarhin looked past her at Rhone.

“The Bishop has decided,” said Ilva, before Rhone could speak. “The Temple’s copy of the oath is incomplete. The ninth stave was never copied. The Temple’s own record says the whole oath is cut in the ice at the place where it was sworn.” She did not raise her voice. “I am the keeper of the Temple’s copy. My office is to compare it with the original wherever the original can be found. The Bishop agrees that that is my office. He cannot send anyone else to do it, because nobody else can.” She looked at Rhone, at last, briefly. “The Justiciar has been very clear that he would prefer I stayed at Dalsendi.”

Rhone said nothing. His face did not change. His hand went to his coat, where the warrant was, and came away.

“Not in front,” said Riarhin. “Behind the guides. Behind us. Where we say.”

“Yes.”

“And when the guides say back, you go back.”

“Yes,” said Ilva.

Rhone turned and went away along the lines, and the soldiers with him.

Orm talked about the nave at supper.

He had not talked much for two days. At supper he talked. He sat by the fire with a bowl of the Bishop’s porridge and a piece of the Bishop’s bread and he talked about the ice, loudly, to Tokk and Yrra and anybody else at the fire, about what the guides said was in it, the long galleries and the great hall like a church with pillars of ice. He said he’d heard there was a hall in it so big a ship could sail in it. He said he’d like to see a dog-faced monster try to stop a Linn steersman walking down the middle of it. He said he’d taken a line ashore in a ground-sea at Brimness, and pulled a grown man out of the black water on the frozen river with his own hands, and if the Mother of the ice wanted to meet Orm Ketilsson she could come out and do it in daylight.

People laughed. Tokk laughed, whistling through his teeth. The soldiers at the next fire laughed.

Riarhin did not laugh. She watched his hands.

When he had finished eating he got up, still talking, and said he was going to see about the lines on the sled, and walked away from the fire into the dark between the tents.

She waited a while. Then she got up and went after him.

He was behind the last of the tents, where the lines ended and the snow ran out flat toward the foot of the ice. He was on his knees in the snow. He was being sick.

She stood a little way off and waited.

He was sick for some time. When he had finished he stayed on his knees with his hands in the snow and his head down, breathing, and she saw his back heave, and then go still. The ice groaned, very low, a long way off, in the dark.

She did not say anything. She did not go to him. She stood where she was, in the snow, where he could see her if he lifted his head, and waited.

He lifted his head.

He looked at her for a long moment. His face was white and wet and his beard was wet. He did not say anything either.

Then he scooped up a handful of clean snow and rubbed it over his face, hard, and over his mouth, and spat, and got up. He got up slowly, the way she got up, and stood.

“The lines on the sled,” he said.

“I’ll hold the lamp,” said Riarhin.

They went back between the tents together and saw to the lines on the sled, and Riarhin held the lamp, and Orm tied and untied the lashings three times until they were right, and neither of them said anything at all about anything else.

They went in at first light, the next morning: Riarhin and Orm, Temür and Sifa and Bródd, Tokk and Yrra with their lamps and their long poles, and Ilva at the back with her case. Hrogaz came with them to the foot of the ice, with the staff, and stood there while Tokk showed them the way in: a dark gap in the base of the wall where a stream had run in summer and was frozen now, high enough for a man to walk upright, going in.

“Two days,” said Tokk. “We go in two days and look and come out. Whatever we find. Back by this gap. If we’re not out by the third dark, Hrogaz sends nobody after us.”

“Nobody,” said Hrogaz, through Bródd.

“Nobody,” said Riarhin.

She looked back once, at the gap, at the camp on the flat ground behind them with its smoke going up and its white banner, and Hrogaz standing at the foot of the ice with the staff in his hand. Then she followed Tokk’s lamp into the blue dark.

Part IV — The song they wanted

Chapter 26 — The interrupted sentence

The way in went up.

That surprised her. She had thought, following Tokk’s lamp into the blue dark of the stream-gap, that they would go down, into the roots of the ice. They went up. The frozen stream-bed climbed under their feet in long ribbed steps, smooth as glass under a skin of grit, and the walls closed in on both sides, blue and black and grey, and the roof came down close overhead and went up again out of the lamplight, and the cold grew.

Tokk went first with a lamp and a long pole, sounding the floor ahead of him. Yrra went last with the other lamp. Between them the six of them went in single file, roped together at the waist with a light line, the way Sifa had roped them on the river ice. Nobody talked. The ice talked. It groaned and creaked round them, long low sounds that came out of the walls and went away along the passage, and once, far off, a crack like a door slamming, and a long rushing slither, and then silence.

After an hour the passage opened.

It opened all at once. One moment Riarhin had her left hand on the wall and the roof a hand’s breadth above Tokk’s pole; the next the wall was gone from under her hand and the roof was gone and the lamplight went out from them in every direction and did not reach anything. She stopped. Behind her Bródd walked into her and stopped too.

Tokk lifted his lamp.

It was a hall.

She could not see the end of it. She could not see the roof. She could see that they were standing at the edge of a great floor of ice, flat and smooth and grey-blue, going away from them into the dark. On either side of the floor, rising out of it at intervals, there were pillars: great ribs of ice as thick as the whale-bone posts at Hvalgard and three times as tall, curving up out of the lamplight and over toward each other, and between the pillars the walls were ridged and fluted like the folds of a hanging cloth, and the light went into the ice and came out of it blue.

The groaning was louder here. It came from everywhere.

“The nave,” said Ilva, very quietly, from the back of the line. “The Temple’s books call it the nave.”

“Why?” said Orm.

“Look at it,” said Ilva.

The Choir was a hundred paces in.

Tokk found it with his pole, sounding ahead across the floor of the nave toward a pale shape that grew out of the dark as they came closer, and then another beside it, and another. Figures. Standing on the ice in a curve, a half-ring, all facing inward, toward something at the centre of the ring that was too dark to see.

Tokk stopped ten paces short. He did not go closer. He held up his lamp and turned his face away.

Riarhin went closer.

They were people.

They were made of ice, clear ice, so clear that the lamplight went into them and came out the other side and lit the floor behind them blue. They were people, every one of them. She saw that at once and kept seeing it, and could not stop. A man in a fur hood with his hands lifted in front of him, palms out, as a man lifts his hands when he is explaining something. A woman bent forward a little at the waist with her mouth open. An old man with a staff. Another woman. Another man, younger, with one foot a little in front of the other as if he had been stepping forward when it happened. They were dressed, all of them, in furs and boots and hoods, and the furs were clear ice too, every hair of them, every stitch, and the ice was so clear that she could see inside them: the shape of the bones in the lifted hands, the dark lines of the throats.

Their mouths were open.

All of them. Every one. Some wide, some only a little, but all open, the lips parted, the jaws a little dropped, the way a mouth is in the middle of a word. Their eyes were open too. They were white. Every eye in the half-ring was white, solid, like the eye of a fish that has been cooked.

Riarhin counted them. She did it without meaning to, as she did everything. Nineteen.

At the end of the ring, nearest her, there was a girl.

She was perhaps eleven. She had a round face and a hood pushed back and her hair in two braids, and she was standing with her hands at her sides and her chin a little lifted, the way a child stands when she has been told to say something in front of people and has decided to say it well. Her mouth was open on a word. And in front of her mouth, a finger’s breadth from her lips, hanging in the air, there was a little cloud of ice. A breath. Her breath, the breath she had let out with the word, frozen in the air where it had come out of her, a small pale feathered shape, hanging there.

Riarhin stood and looked at it.

Behind her, nobody spoke. She heard Bródd breathing. She heard, very far away, the ice groan.

Sifa had gone past her.

Riarhin had not seen her move. She was suddenly there, at the far end of the half-ring, among the figures, walking slowly along the curve of them on the ice, looking at each one. She did not look at their faces. She looked at their coats. At their belts and hoods and the fastenings at their throats. She went from one to the next with her hands in her sleeves.

She stopped at the ninth.

It was a young man. He was standing a little bent, with his head down and one hand half-lifted toward his chest, as if he had been going to put it there. His mouth was open. His eyes were white. He wore a coat of grey fur, and at the throat the coat was fastened with a toggle of bone through a loop of cord, and the loop had been broken once and mended. Riarhin could see the mend from where she stood, through the clear ice: the cord stitched back to the coat with a little seam, back and forth, tight and close, in a zigzag, like the track a bird makes in snow.

Sifa stood in front of him.

She did not touch him. She did not touch her jaw. She stood with her hands in her sleeves and looked at the mended loop at his throat for a long time, and then at his white eyes, and then at his open mouth.

Then she turned away and came back along the curve of the ring, past the others, past the girl with her frozen breath, and stood beside Riarhin, and looked at the floor.

Riarhin did not ask her anything.

“There’s writing,” said Ilva.

She had come forward with Yrra’s lamp. She was standing at the centre of the half-ring, where the figures faced, and Riarhin saw what they faced: a slab of ice set upright in the floor, as tall as a man, clear and flat like the slab in the hollow under the crag, and cut all over with strokes.

“It’s the oath-script,” said Ilva. Her voice was thin and quick. “It’s the same hand as the vellum. The same forms. Look.” She held the lamp up to it. “This is the eighth stave. Part of it. The end. Her name is a word of this oath—” She put her finger out to the strokes, to the line below, and her lips began to move, shaping the sounds, the way Vekk’s had moved. “And this—this would be—”

Sifa’s hand came down on hers.

It came out of nowhere, fast, Sifa’s cracked black-tipped hand closing over Ilva’s on the face of the slab, pressing it flat against the ice, covering the strokes under it. Sifa had crossed the half-ring in two strides. She stood with her hand on Ilva’s and her face a hand’s breadth from Ilva’s face, and she said one word in the northern speech, thick, loud, so that it rang off the pillars.

“No.”

Ilva froze.

“Don’t say it,” said Sifa. She did not let go. Her voice was shaking. “Don’t read it. Not out loud. Not that line. Not here.” Her eyes went past Ilva to the half-ring of figures, to the ninth, with his head bent and his mouth open. “That’s what they were doing. All of them. Reading it. Saying it.” Her jaw moved. “Look at them.”

Ilva looked.

Riarhin saw her look. Saw her eyes go from the slab to the figures, to their open mouths, to their white eyes, to the girl at the end with her frozen breath, and saw her understand.

She took her hand away from the slab, slowly, from under Sifa’s. She held it against her breast.

“What happens?” she said. Her voice was very small.

Sifa did not answer at once. She was looking at the ninth figure.

“Three breaths,” she said. “The eyes. Then the rest.” She touched her jaw at last. “My brother was saying sorry. To somebody. In here. He said the name in the middle of it, because you say it, when you say sorry to the Hall. You have to. He was saying it, and he stopped.” She looked at the mended loop at the frozen throat. “That’s why I cut it. My tongue. So I can’t say it. Even if I forget. Even if I’m frightened. Even if I want to.”

Nobody spoke.

“But Vekk reads it,” said Riarhin slowly. “The staves. Hrogaz says it. The wardens.”

“The wardens can say it,” said Tokk, from beyond the ring. He had not come closer. His voice came out of the dark, hoarse. “The ones with the stave. The ones who swore. They say it and nothing happens.” He was quiet. “I don’t know why. Nobody knows why. My father said, it’s theirs to say. That’s all he said.”

Ilva was looking at the slab. At the line she had been about to read.

“Whoso speaks it swears,” she said, very quietly, to herself. “And whoso swears what he may not keep—” She stopped.

She did not finish it. She stood with her hand against her breast and looked at the slab, and Riarhin saw her want to finish it, the way she had wanted to fill the gap in the vellum at Dalsendi, and saw her not do it.

“I don’t know,” said Ilva. “I don’t know why. I can’t say why.” She looked at the figures. “I can see that it does.”

They went on along the nave because they had come to find four people and the four people were not in the Choir.

Tokk led. He did not want to. Riarhin saw him not want to, saw him look back at the gap they had come in by, a long way behind them now in the dark, and then look at Hrogaz’s staff in his mind, and at the four from the coast whose names he had heard read off a slab, and go on.

The nave went on a long way.

And it was alive. The ribs of ice that rose along the walls on either side, between the pillars, were channels: long grooves in the ice, as wide as her two hands, running up the walls and over the roof and down again on the other side, dozens of them, hundreds, close together like the ribs of a ship seen from inside. And in the channels, as she watched, something moved.

Frost.

It came along the channels in a slow pulse. She saw it come: a whitening, a feathering of frost that rose in the bottom of a channel and ran along it, up the wall, over into the dark of the roof, and down the other side, and faded, and behind it another pulse came, and another. Slowly. Regularly. Like breathing. Like something very large, somewhere, breathing, and every breath going out through the channels in the ice and coming back.

The groaning rose and fell with it.

“Where does it go?” said Orm. He was looking up at the roof. His voice was very quiet.

“In,” said Yrra. “Further in. Toward the middle.”

They went on toward the middle.

The floor of the nave began to slope down. The channels on the walls drew together, converging, all of them running toward a place ahead where the dark was deeper. Tokk went slower. He sounded every step with his pole. And then he stopped, and held up his lamp, and they all stopped behind him.

There was a gap in the floor.
