It ran across the nave from wall to wall: a shaft, a great round hole going down into the dark, wider than the hall at Ottarshall had been long, and from it came a sound of water, very far below, running. Cold air came up out of it. On the far side of it the floor of the nave went on, level, and the channels on the walls ran on over the shaft and on, converging.

And across the shaft there was a bridge.

It was ice, like everything. A single long arch of it, narrow, no wider than a cart, springing from the near edge of the shaft and curving over the dark and coming down on the far side. It had no rail. Its surface was smooth and grey and caught Tokk’s lamplight in a long pale line.

On the far side of the shaft, where the bridge came down, there was a wall. A screen. It was something darker than ice, set across the end of the nave from side to side, carved, pierced all over with holes in a pattern like lattice, like the screens Riarhin had seen in southern houses that let the women look out and nobody look in.

Behind it there was light.

Not much. Lamplight. Small and yellow and steady, coming through the holes in the screen in little points, a great many of them. Warm light. The first warm light she had seen since the Bishop’s fires.

And as Riarhin watched, something moved behind the screen. A shadow. It crossed the points of light, one after another, slowly, from left to right. It was the size of a person. It stopped. Then it went back the other way.

“Somebody’s there,” said Bródd, in a whisper.

Orm took a step toward the bridge.

The frost in the channels stopped.

It stopped all at once, all of it, in every channel on every wall. The pulse that had been running up the walls and over the roof and down again, slow and steady as breathing, stopped, and the whitening in the channels froze where it was, half-way up the walls, and stayed.

The groaning stopped too.

In the silence, from somewhere behind them, back along the nave the way they had come, there was a sound. It was a long slow scraping, like a keel being drawn up a shingle beach. It went on for a long time. Then it stopped. Then it came again, nearer.

Tokk had turned round with the lamp. His face was grey.

“The way out,” he said.

The scraping came again. It came from the dark between them and the Choir, and the stream-gap beyond the Choir, and the camp beyond that, and Hrogaz waiting at the foot of the ice with his staff.

Something was between them and the way out.

The frost began to run in the channels again. Faster now. Much faster. Whitening, feathering, racing up the walls and over the roof in pulse after pulse, and every pulse ran not toward the middle of the nave, where the bridge was, but back. Back along the walls. Back toward the dark where the scraping was. As if whatever was breathing had turned round.

Chapter 27 — A road through the nave

It came down the nave like weather.

Not like an animal. An animal comes at you; you can see where it is and where it is going. This came the way a squall comes down off a cliff onto the water, filling the space ahead of it, everywhere at once, with the frost racing in the channels on both walls and over the roof ahead of it like the white water ahead of a wave.

Tokk’s lamp found it.

A coil. That was what she saw first: a great pale curve of something, as thick as the mast of the Staðfast, sliding along the floor of the nave between the pillars, grey-white and wet, gleaming, with water running off it. The curve went on and on into the dark beyond the lamplight. It moved. It drew along the ice with the long slow scraping sound they had heard, the sound of a keel on shingle, and as it moved the curve of it rose, and turned, and she saw that there were many curves, coil on coil, a body longer than the nave was wide, folding and unfolding on itself between the pillars.

Then the head came up out of the coils into the light.

It was long. It was longer than a man was tall, and narrow, and it was the same pale wet grey as the rest, and it was scarred. The whole length of it was scarred. Old scars, deep, ridged, crossing and recrossing, like the strokes on the slabs, like writing. It had no eyes that she could see. Where the eyes should have been there were two hollows in the scarred grey, deep and dark, with ice in them. Its mouth was closed. It came up out of the coils and turned on its long neck toward the light of Tokk’s lamp, slowly, the way a blind thing turns toward warmth.

Then it opened its mouth.

It did not roar. It did not make any sound at all. It breathed out.

The cold hit her like a wall of water. It came down the nave in a long white rush, a cloud of it, glittering, and it went into her mouth and her nose and down into her chest and she felt it there, inside, a burning cold in the bottom of her lungs as if she had breathed in snow. Her eyes ran and the tears froze on her lashes. Beside her somebody cried out.

Tokk’s lamp went out.

Yrra’s went out.

It was dark.

The frost in the channels on the walls was glowing. Very faintly, blue-white, a pale cold light that came out of the ice itself where the frost ran in it, pulse after pulse, up the walls and over the roof. It was enough to see shapes by, and no more. The pillars, black against the faint glow. The floor of the nave, grey. And in the middle of the floor, between them and the Choir, the coils, pale, huge, moving, with the frost-light running along their wet backs.

“Down,” said Riarhin. “Everybody down. Against the pillars.”

She did not know whose voice it was for a moment. It was hers. It was the voice she had used in the Black Banners, on a field, at night, and it came out of her without her deciding, and she heard them obey it: feet on the ice, bodies going down, somebody sliding.

“Yrra. A light. Can you?”

“Under my coat. The little one.” Yrra’s voice was shaking. “If it breathes again it’ll go.”

“Keep it under your coat. Don’t light it till I say.” Riarhin was on her knees by a pillar. She did not remember getting there. “Where’s everybody? Say your name.”

They said their names, out of the dark. Orm, close, to her right. Temür, further, across the nave, by the far pillars. Bródd, behind her. Sifa. Ilva, very small. Yrra. Tokk.

The coils were moving. She could hear them, the long scraping, and see the pale shape of them in the frost-light shifting between them and the way out. The head was up. She could see it against the faint glow of the far wall, swinging slowly on its long neck, turning, the hollow eyes going along the nave from side to side.

It was looking for them. Or not looking. Feeling. Turning toward something.

“It can’t see,” said Riarhin, very low. “It hasn’t got eyes. It’s turning toward the warm.” She did not know how she knew. She looked at it swing. “It went for the lamps. The lamps were the warmest thing.”

“Then we’re next,” said Orm.

“Yes.”

She had been in narrow places before. She had been in a gallery under a hill with Farieth’s spear coming round, and in a granary yard in the dark, and on a hundred fields where the ground decided everything before anybody drew. She looked at the nave in the frost-light and saw the ground.

The pillars stood a dozen paces apart along both sides of the nave, with channels in the fluted wall between them. One turn of the coils would sweep the middle of the floor clean. She measured the distance to the nearest pillar, then the next.

The half-ring of the Choir stood between the coils and the way out. The coils bent round the ice figures without touching them.

The way out was the third thing. Beyond the Choir, a long way, in the dark at the end of the nave, the stream-gap. She could not see it. She knew where it was. She had counted the pillars coming in. Twenty-three on the left side, from the gap to the bridge.

“Listen,” she said.

They listened.

“We go along the wall. The left wall. Pillar to pillar. Never in the middle. Never two at the same pillar.” She took a breath of the cold that burned. “Orm. Line. The rope we came in on. Can you get it pillar to pillar along the wall? Tie off at each one? So anybody who falls can find it in the dark and pull themselves along it?”

A pause.

“Yes,” said Orm. His voice was very steady and very quiet. “Yes. I can do that.”

“Sifa. Yrra. The floor. It’s wet from the thing. Some of it’s soft. Find where it’s sound, along the wall, ahead of Orm, and tell him.”

“Yes,” said Sifa.

“Bródd, Ilva. Go with them. Behind Orm. On the line. Don’t let go of it.”

“Tokk,” said Riarhin. “Can you walk?”

“Yes.”

“Then go with them.”

“And you?” said Temür, out of the dark across the nave.

Riarhin looked at the coils. The head was turning. It had stopped turning, and was hanging, very still, on its long neck, toward the right-hand wall. Toward the far pillars. Toward Temür.

“It’s found you,” she said.

The head had come down. It had lowered itself on its long neck toward the far pillars where Temür was crouched, slowly, the hollow eyes turned toward him, and for a moment it hung there, low, close to the floor, a man’s height up, and the whole long scarred length of the neck was stretched out across the middle of the nave toward him.

It was open. She could see it. The underside of the neck, where it joined the head, pale and unscarred, stretched out flat across the floor in the frost-light, not two dozen paces from her pillar. If she went now, across the floor, fast, low, she could be under it before it turned, and Remorse would go in under the jaw where the scars stopped.

She went.

She came off the pillar with Remorse in her left hand and went across the ice, low, the bad leg screaming, her boots slipping on the meltwater, toward the long pale throat. She was halfway there when she saw it was wrong.

The neck was not stretched toward Temür. It was stretched past him. And the coils behind it, in the dark, were not still. They had been gathering. While the head hung low and turned, the body behind it had drawn itself together on the ice in a great tight spiral, and as she came off the pillar into the middle of the nave the spiral began to open.

She saw the first coil come round.

It came round the way a boom comes round when the wind changes, low and fast across the floor of the nave, a curve of grey wet weight as high as her waist, sweeping, and the head had never been the point. The head had been a lamp. The head had hung there to bring something out into the middle.

She stopped. She threw herself down. She went flat on the ice on her face with Remorse under her and the coil went over her.

It did not go over her clean. It caught her. It caught the frame on her back and the back of her head and drove her face into the ice, and the weight of it went across her, and she felt the frame break, and something in her side go, the old rib, the one Asta had bound, and she could not breathe. Then it was past. It went on across the nave, sweeping, toward the left wall.

Toward the line.

She heard Tokk scream.

She got up. She did not know how. She got up off the ice onto her knees with Remorse still in her hand and the broken frame hanging off her and the cold burning in her chest, and she looked toward the left wall, and in the frost-light she saw the coil come to rest against the pillars there, two pillars down from where she had sent them, and stop, and slide back.

There was somebody on the ice where it had been. Somebody lying between two pillars, half out in the open, not moving.

And beyond them, against the wall, the line. Orm’s line. It ran from pillar to pillar along the wall toward the Choir and the way out, and it was holding. She could see it in the frost-light, a dark thread tied off at each pillar, five pillars along, six. And at the sixth there were people. Shapes, pressed against the wall behind the pillar. Bródd. Ilva. Sifa.

And Orm, at the seventh, alone, on his knees at the foot of the pillar, with the line in both his hands, tying it off.

The coil was drawing back across the nave. The head was coming up again. Turning.

“Tokk,” said Yrra’s voice, out of the dark by the line, very high.

He was the one on the ice. Riarhin could see him now: lying on his side between the second and third pillars, out from the wall, where the coil had caught him and thrown him, with one leg bent wrong under him.

She had sent him there.

She went to him.

She went along the floor on her knees and one hand, with Remorse dragging, keeping low, and the head turned above her in the dark and did not find her. She reached him. He was alive. He was looking up at her with his teeth clenched and his face grey, and he was holding his leg.

“Can’t,” he said, through his teeth. “Can’t stand on it.”

“You don’t have to.”

She got her good arm under his shoulders and pulled. He was heavier than he looked. She pulled him across the ice a yard toward the wall, and another, and the rib in her side stabbed at every pull, and then somebody was beside her.

It was Ilva.

She had let go of the line. She had come out from behind the sixth pillar, small and quick in her dark robe, and she got down on the ice on Tokk’s other side and got her arm under him too, and between them they pulled. Ilva’s face in the frost-light was white and set and she did not say anything at all. They pulled him to the foot of the third pillar, behind it, against the wall, out of the open.

“The line,” said Riarhin. “Put his hand on the line.”

Ilva put his hand on the line.

“Now go along it. With him. Both of you. Pillar to pillar. Toward Orm.”

“He can’t walk.”

“Then you pull and he pulls himself along the line with his hands.” Riarhin looked at her. “Can you?”

Ilva looked back at her. She was shaking all over, a fine hard shaking that Riarhin could feel through Tokk’s body between them. She nodded.

“Go,” said Riarhin.

They went. Slowly. Ilva on her knees, pulling, and Tokk on his back with his hands on the line, hauling himself along it a hand-length at a time, his broken leg dragging. Toward the fourth pillar. Toward Orm.

Riarhin stayed at the third pillar.

She could not go with them. The head had come up and was turning, and she could see it in the frost-light swinging slowly along the nave, toward the left wall, toward the line, toward the warm bodies going along it. It would find them. It had found the lamps. It had found Temür. It would find a man dragging a broken leg along a rope.

She had to give it something warmer.

She looked across the nave. Temür was there. She could see him in the frost-light, still against his pillar on the far side, the right wall, where the head had first hung toward him. He had not moved. He was looking at her across the dark floor.

“Temür,” she said. She said it in Ykrul. She said it low, and it went across the nave anyway; the ice carried it. “Can you get to the bridge?”

A pause.

“Why?”

“Because if it’s looking at you on that side, it’s not looking at them on this one.” She watched the head turn. “Go along your wall. The other way. Toward the bridge. Not fast. Let it hear you.” She swallowed. The cold burned. “Make it turn round.”

Another pause. Longer.

“And you?”

“I’ll keep it from them,” said Riarhin. “From here. I’ll be between.”

“You can’t stand.”

“I can kneel,” said Riarhin.

She heard him breathe out, across the nave, a long breath. Then she heard him move.

He went along the right wall, toward the bridge, pillar to pillar, and he did not go quietly. She heard his boots on the ice. She heard him strike something against a pillar, his bow-case, a hollow knock, and then again. She watched the head.

It stopped turning toward the line.

It hung a moment. Then, slowly, on its long neck, it began to turn the other way. Toward the knocking. Toward the far wall, and the bridge, and Temür.

The coils followed it. They drew back off the left wall, away from the line, away from the pillars where Orm and Bródd and Sifa and Ilva and Tokk were pressed against the ice, and gathered themselves, and began to slide across the floor of the nave, slowly, scraping, after the head.

Riarhin knelt at the third pillar with Remorse across her knees and watched them go.

She was between. The coils were moving past her, a dozen paces off, toward the far side and the bridge; the line was behind her, along the wall toward the Choir and the way out. She could feel the cold coming off the body as it passed, burning on her face. She could hear Orm, along the wall behind her, tying off the line at the eighth pillar. She could hear Ilva breathing, pulling Tokk. She could hear, across the nave, Temür’s bow-case knocking against the pillars, going away from her toward the bridge.

The coils came between.

They came between her and Temür, across the middle of the nave, a long pale wall of them sliding across the floor, and she could not see him any more. She could see the coils, and the frost running along their backs, and beyond them, faintly, the far wall.

“Temür,” she said.

“Here.” His voice came through, over the coils, from the far side. It was far off, and steady.

“Orm,” she said.

“Here.” Behind her, along the wall. Further now. “Eighth pillar. Ninth. They’re on the line.”

Riarhin knelt at the third pillar with her rib grinding and the cold burning in her chest and listened to the two voices, one on each side of her, one going toward the way out and one going toward the bridge, with the great pale body of the thing sliding across the dark between them.

Orm had stopped. She heard him stop. She heard him at the ninth pillar, on his knees, and she heard him curse, very softly.

“What is it?” she said.

“Nothing.” A pause. “My glove. It’s wet. The line went through a puddle. It’s nothing.”

She heard him go on tying.

Across the nave, beyond the coils, Temür’s bow-case knocked against a pillar. Once. Twice. And then, after a long moment, a third time, further off, near the bridge. And the head of the thing, high up in the frost-light above the coils, swung after the sound, slowly, toward the shaft and the bridge and the screen with its small warm lights, and away from them.

Chapter 28 — The hand on the line

She waited for the light.

That was what they had agreed, in the few words there had been time for before the dark: when the people on the line reached the Choir, where the coils did not go, Yrra would take the little lamp out from under her coat and light it, and hold it up, once. Then they would be out of the sweep.

Riarhin knelt at the third pillar and watched the dark at the far end of the nave, past the line, where the Choir was, and waited for it.

It did not come.

Across the nave, beyond the coils, Temür’s bow-case knocked against a pillar, near the bridge. The head swung toward it. The coils slid after the head, scraping. They were nearly all across now, a long pale heap of them gathered on the far side of the nave between the last pillars and the edge of the shaft, and the head was up above them in the frost-light, swinging, slow, toward the knocking, away, back.

She could have gone then. She thought about it. She was behind the coils. The underside of the neck, where it came up out of the heap and rose toward the head, was turned away from her, toward Temür, toward the bridge. She could see it in the frost-light. Pale. Unscarred. Thirty paces. She could be across the open floor in the time it took the head to swing once.

And if she went now, and was wrong again, the sweep would come round again, and this time the line would not be at the ninth pillar. It would be somewhere between the ninth and the Choir, with Tokk on it dragging his leg and Ilva pulling him.

She stayed where she was.

The rib ground in her side when she breathed. The cold was in her chest. Her left hand was round Remorse’s grip on her knees, and the sword lay quiet in it, a long dark line across her lap, and she held it and did not move and watched the end of the nave for the light.

“Riarhin.”

Temür. Across the nave, beyond the coils. Low, in Ykrul.

“Here.”

“It’s closer. It’s very close.” A pause. His voice was perfectly steady. “I’m at the bridge. The last pillar. There’s nothing behind me but the shaft.” Another pause. “It’s coming down.”

She looked. The head had stopped swinging. It was coming down on its long neck, slowly, toward the foot of the bridge, toward the last pillar on the far side, the hollow eyes turned to something there she could not see.

“Can you go out on the bridge?” she said.

Silence.

“It’s narrow,” said Temür.

“I know.”

“There’s no rail.”

“I know. Go out on it. Not far. Three paces. Four.” She watched the head come down. “Make it follow you. Make it put its head out over the shaft to find you. If it comes down to the bridge, it has to bring the neck down too. Low. Over the edge.” She swallowed the cold. “Then it’ll be turned. Then I can see the throat.”

“And then what?”

“Then I come,” said Riarhin.

A long silence across the nave.

“I’ll go out,” said Temür. “Don’t come till I say.”

“Don’t say till the light.”

“What light?”

She told him.

The light came.

It came at the far end of the nave, past the line, small and yellow, very far away: Yrra’s little lamp, taken out from under her coat and held up, once, high, at the Choir. Riarhin saw it and her heart turned over. She saw it go up and stay up for the space of a breath, and come down, and go out.

“They’re out,” she said, in Ykrul, across the nave. “They’re at the Choir. Go.”

She heard Temür go out onto the bridge.

She heard his boots on it, a different sound from the floor of the nave, hollower, the sound of ice with nothing under it. One step. Two. She heard him strike the bow-case once on the surface of the bridge, a knock that went down into the shaft and came back up out of the dark below, echoing. Three steps.

The head followed.

It came down off its neck out over the edge of the shaft, slowly, turning, toward the knocking on the bridge. The coils behind it shifted, drawing it down. She watched the long neck stretch out over the dark, lower, lower, curving down toward the narrow pale arch of the bridge where she could not see Temür, and as it stretched it turned, the whole length of it turning on itself to bring the head round and down, and the underside came round toward her.

Pale. Unscarred. Low over the edge of the shaft, a man’s height above the floor of the nave, stretched out and turned and open.

It was not a lamp this time. She could see that. The coils behind it were not gathering. They were braced. They were holding the weight of the neck out over the shaft, and they could not hold it and sweep at the same time.

“Now,” said Temür, out of the dark on the bridge.

She went.

Most of it went past her. She remembered getting up. She remembered that her leg held, for once, all the way, and that the rib did not, and that she went across the floor of the nave with the cold burning in her chest and Remorse low in her left hand and her right arm hanging, and that the coils were on her right as she ran, a long pale wall of them, braced, trembling, and did not move.

She remembered Temür’s voice on the bridge, saying something in Ykrul that was not a word.

She remembered the head turning.

It had felt her. It had felt something warm coming across the floor behind it, fast, and it began to turn on its long neck, back, away from the bridge, toward her, and as it turned it began to rise, and she saw that it would be up and round and the mouth open and the cold coming before she reached it.

Then it jerked.

It jerked on its neck as if it had been struck, and the head snapped back and up, high, away from the bridge, away from her, and she heard Temür on the bridge shout, and she understood afterward what he had done: he had lain flat on the narrow ice with the head coming down over him and put an arrow up into the hollow of its eye from underneath, from a hand’s breadth away. She did not see it. She saw the head snap back. She saw the throat come up and round and open in front of her, pale, stretched, the head flung back away from it, the whole length of the underside bared over the edge of the shaft.

She was under it.

She put Remorse into it where the scars stopped.

It went in the way a blade goes in when it has found the place, as it had gone into Farieth: no resistance for a moment, and then all of it, the whole weight of her running body going in behind the point, up under the jaw, into the pale wet underside of the throat. The cold came out of the wound over her hand like water. She felt it. It went up her arm to the shoulder and into her chest, a cold so great it was a kind of silence, and her hand on the grip went white and then she could not feel it.

She held on.

The head came down.

It came down slowly, as a tree comes down, all of it, on its long neck, out of the dark above her, down past her, and she let go of Remorse and threw herself sideways and it came down on the edge of the shaft beside her with a sound like the sky falling, and the ice under her jumped.

And everything moved.

The coils were moving. All of them, all at once. Not sweeping. Thrashing. The great pale body behind the head heaved itself up off the floor of the nave in coil after coil, rising, falling, beating against the pillars and the walls and the floor, and the whole nave shook with it. Ice came down from the roof in sheets. A pillar cracked. Riarhin, on her face on the floor at the edge of the shaft with her left arm dead to the shoulder, heard it crack, a long splitting roar, and saw it lean and go, slowly, across the nave, and shatter on the floor in a thousand pieces.

The head was on the edge of the shaft beside her. Half on the floor of the nave and half over the edge. It was still moving. The jaws were opening and closing, slowly, with no sound. The hollow eyes, one of them with the shaft of Temür’s arrow standing out of it, were turned up toward the roof.

Remorse was in its throat. Up to the guard.

She could reach it. It was there, two arm’s lengths away. She could crawl to it and take hold of it and pull it out and put it in again, further back, behind the jaw, where the spine would be, and the thrashing would stop.

Behind her, back along the nave, very far off, she heard Orm.

He was shouting. She could hear him over the roar of the coils and the falling ice. She could not hear what he was shouting. She heard her own name in it, and Tokk’s.

She turned her head.

In the frost-light, which was flickering now, wild, racing in the channels in pulse after pulse, she could see the left wall. The line. Orm’s line, tied off pillar to pillar along it toward the Choir. And one of the coils had come across the nave in its thrashing and struck the wall there, between two pillars, near the Choir end, and come back, and in the place where it had struck there was a pillar down, and the line was gone from it. It hung loose from the next pillar along, trailing on the floor.

And on the floor at the end of it, between the fallen pillar and the Choir, in the open, there were two people. One lying. One kneeling.

The one kneeling was Orm. He had the line in his hands. He had the loose end of it, and he had it round his body, round his back and over his shoulder, and he was leaning back on it with his whole weight, braced, and at the other end of it, on the floor of the nave, out in the open where the coils were thrashing, was Tokk. The coil had knocked him off the wall when the pillar came down. He was on his back on the ice with his broken leg and his hands round the line, and Orm was holding him there, holding the line taut so that Tokk could pull himself along it, a hand-length at a time, toward the Choir, and the coils were thrashing across the floor a dozen paces from both of them.

The head beside her moved. The jaws opened.

She could go back for Remorse. She could finish it. If she finished it the coils would stop. That would take her two arm’s lengths, and a pull, and a stroke, and the time all that took.

Or she could go to Orm.

It was a hundred paces. She could not run it. She could not walk it. Her left arm was dead and her rib was broken and the floor of the nave was heaving under her and ice was coming down from the roof in sheets.

She looked at the head. She looked at the line.

Then she rolled over, and crawled to the head, and got her right hand, her bad hand, round Remorse’s grip, because her left would not close, and pulled.

It came out. She did not know how. It came out of the throat with a long cold sucking sound and her bad arm screamed from shoulder to elbow and she fell back with the sword across her body, and got her knees under her, and got the sword into her left hand by putting her right hand round her left and closing the fingers for it, and lifted it, and put it in again. Behind the jaw. Where the long neck joined the head. Down, with the whole of her weight on it, through the scarred grey into whatever was underneath.

The jaws stopped.

The coils stopped.

It was not all at once. They went on moving for a long moment, slower, slower, heaving, settling, like a sea going down after a gale. Then they lay still. A long pale heap of them on the floor of the nave, between the pillars, wet, gleaming, not moving.

The frost in the channels stopped.

It stopped in the middle of a pulse. Every channel, every wall, the whole length of the nave. The whitening froze where it was, halfway up the walls, and the faint blue light in it went out.

And the groaning stopped. The long low sound that had been in her teeth since she first saw the ice, rising and falling, rising and falling, like breathing. It stopped.

In the silence she heard the cold go.

She did not know how else to say it. It had been pushing. Ever since they came into the nave there had been something pushing against them, out, toward the way they had come in, a pressure like a wind that did not move anything but that she had felt on her face and in her ears and on the backs of her hands. It stopped pushing. It let go. She felt it let go, the way you feel a hand taken off your back.

Then she heard the ice in the roof, very far up, begin to crack.

Somebody had reached Orm.

She saw it when she looked up. She was on her knees beside the head with Remorse in her hands, and the frost-light was gone, and there was only one light in the whole of the nave: Yrra’s little lamp, lit again, at the Choir, held up. And in its light, far off, between the fallen pillar and the Choir, she saw Sifa kneeling beside Orm on the ice, and Tokk lying at the Choir’s edge where the line had brought him, and Ilva bending over Tokk.

Sifa had Orm’s hands.

Riarhin got up. She did not know how she got up. She went along the nave toward the light, slowly, with Remorse dragging from her hand, and the ice in the roof cracked over her and something came down a long way behind her and shattered. Temür was beside her. She did not know when he had come. He had come off the bridge and across the nave, and he got his shoulder under her good arm and took her weight, and they went toward the light together.

Behind them, there was a roar.

She turned her head. In the faint light from the Choir, a long way back, she could see the shaft, and the bridge across it, the long narrow arch of ice. The head of the thing lay on the near edge of the shaft. The neck went out over the edge. And the weight of the body, the long pale heap of it, lying still across the floor of the nave, had begun to slide. Slowly. Toward the shaft. Dragged by the head, by the weight of the neck hanging over the edge.

It went over. All of it. The neck, and then the first coils, and then the rest, sliding over the edge of the shaft and down into the dark, scraping, and as it went it took the bridge.

She saw the bridge go. The long arch of ice cracked at its near end where the body went over it, and sagged, and broke, and the whole span of it went down into the shaft after the body in pieces, turning over, catching the light, and was gone. She heard it hit, very far below, in the water. Then nothing.

The shaft was open. Clean across. A sheer black gap from wall to wall, and on the far side, beyond it, the screen.

The lamps behind the screen were still lit. She could see them: the little points of yellow light through the lattice, warm, steady, a great many of them, on the far side of the gap. Untouched. As if nothing had happened.

Something moved behind the screen. A shadow. It crossed the points of light, slowly. It stopped.

Orm was sitting on the ice at the edge of the Choir with his back against the leg of a frozen man.

He was holding his left hand in his right. Sifa was crouched in front of him. The glove on the left hand was black with water and stiff with frost, frozen into the shape of a fist round a line, and Sifa was trying to take it off.

It would not come.

She pulled at it, at the cuff, gently, and it did not come, and Orm made a sound. She stopped. She tried again. The leather was frozen to the hand under it, or the hand was frozen to the leather; Riarhin could not tell which. Sifa worked her black-tipped fingers in at the cuff and eased it, a little, and Orm’s face went the colour of the ice, and the glove came a finger’s breadth and stopped.

“Leave it,” said Orm. His voice was very calm. “Leave it. Asta can do it.”

“Asta is a long way,” said Sifa.

“Then the Bishop’s man can do it.”

Riarhin knelt beside him. She did it badly. She knelt on the ice, which Kallias had told her not to do, with the rib grinding, and looked at the glove on Orm’s left hand, frozen into the shape of the line.

“You held it,” she said.

Orm looked at her. His eyes were wet, from the cold or from something else.

“I said I would,” he said. “On the floor. To you. I can do that.” He looked at his hand. “So I did that.”

Riarhin did not say anything. She sat back on her heels on the ice and looked at the frozen glove, and at Sifa’s hands round it, and at Tokk lying beside the Choir with Ilva over him and his leg bent wrong, and at Yrra holding up her little lamp with her face streaked with tears, and Bródd beside her, white, holding her other arm. Behind her, Temür. She heard him breathing.

High up in the roof of the nave, the ice cracked again. A long splitting sound, going away along the roof into the dark. And then, somewhere back along the nave toward the way they had come in, a heavy rushing slither, and a crash, and the floor under them shook.

“The gallery,” said Yrra. Her voice was thin. “The way in. It’s coming down. The roof over the way in.” She looked at Riarhin. “If we don’t go now.”

Riarhin looked across the nave. At the black gap where the bridge had been. At the screen on the far side, with its small warm lights, and the shadow that had crossed them and stopped. Four people. Gunnvor that weaves. Kári, Aud’s boy. Old Ulf that kept the count-stones. Svala. Behind a screen, beyond a gap she could not cross, in a nave whose roof was falling in.

She looked at Orm’s hand. At Tokk’s leg.

“There’s another way,” said Yrra. She had come close. She was pointing, with the lamp, up. Up the wall of the nave beside the shaft, high, where the channels ran. Riarhin looked and in the lamplight saw, very high up, a long dark line running along the wall above the shaft and across it, toward the screen. A ledge. A gallery cut in the ice. “The old way. The hauling way. My grandfather said. Where they took the due across, before there was a bridge.” She swallowed. “You need ladders to get up to it. Timber. And men to haul. And the ice has to stop moving.” She looked at the roof. “Not now.”

“Not now,” said Riarhin.

She stood up.

“We go out,” she said. “All of us. Now. Tokk on the line, Orm on the line, everybody on the line. Back the way we came.” She looked at the screen across the gap, at the small warm lights. “Yrra, mark the way up. Something that’ll be seen. At the foot of the wall, by the shaft. Before we go.”

Yrra went. She went with her lamp to the edge of the shaft where the wall began, and Riarhin watched her take something from her belt, a strip of red cloth, and tie it to a fang of ice at the foot of the wall, and come back.

“We come back,” said Riarhin. She said it to the screen. Not loudly. “With ladders. And timber. And people to haul. We come back.”

Nobody behind the screen answered. The lights burned, small and steady. The shadow did not move.

They went out along the line, the eight of them, slowly, Tokk dragged on a cloak between Bródd and Temür, Orm with his frozen hand held against his chest, past the Choir with its nineteen open mouths, past the girl with her frozen breath, back along the dark nave toward the gap they had come in by, with the roof cracking over them and the lamplight going ahead.

Chapter 29 — The shore receives them

The dark of the moon came four nights after they came out of the ice, and nobody came to meet the speakers.

They waited for it. All of them. Hrogaz and the speakers of the eleven hearths went out from the Bishop’s camp at dusk on the appointed night to the place where the due-road reached the foot of the ice, and stood there in a line in the snow under the great blue wall, with the staff, as the man in the white coat had told them to. The company from the coast stood behind them. Riarhin stood with her rib bound and her right arm in a sling Kallias had made her and Temür’s shoulder under her left, because her leg had decided, the day after the nave, that it had done enough.

They stood all night.

Nobody came. The wall of the ice stood over them, blue-black in the starlight, and did not move, and did not make any sound. It had not made any sound since they came out. Riarhin had lain awake in the Bishop’s camp for three nights listening for it, the long low groaning she had felt in her teeth since the first day, and it had not come back. The ice was silent. It was only ice.

At grey dawn Hrogaz turned round and looked at the speakers, and at the company, and said something short.

“He says nobody came,” said Bródd. His voice was hoarse with cold. “He says they always come. When they say a time, they come.” He listened. “He says nobody came.”

The stream came the next day.

Riarhin did not see it start. Yrra came running into the camp at noon, shouting, and they went out to the foot of the ice and saw it: the gap they had gone in by, the frozen stream-bed at the bottom of the wall, and out of it, over the ice of the bed, water. Running. A thin dark sheet of it, sliding out from under the wall and spreading across the snow of the valley floor, steaming faintly in the cold air.

“It doesn’t,” said Tokk, from his litter. Kallias had splinted the leg and he was carried everywhere now on a hurdle, and he had made them carry him out to see. “Not in the winter. Never. It runs in the summer. In the winter it’s stone.” He looked at the water. “It’s running.”

It ran for four days, and went on running. They watched it. Hrogaz set watchers at the foot of the ice, his own people and some of the Bishop’s soldiers together, at a camp a little way up the valley in the lee of a rock, and the watchers watched the stream run and the wall of ice stand silent, and sent word every evening: running. Silent. Nobody.

On the third day Sifa asked to see the head.

It was gone. They told her so. It had gone into the shaft with the rest of the body and the bridge, a hundred feet down, into the water. Nobody was going to see it again.

“I saw it,” said Sifa. “On the edge. Before it went.” She was sitting by the fire in the Bishop’s camp with her knees drawn up. “In the little lamp. The scars.”

Riarhin waited.

Sifa did not say anything for a while. Then she got up and went out of the firelight to where the speakers’ sleds were drawn up, and came back with something under her arm. It was a shield. An old one, round, of hide on a wooden frame, from one of the hearths’ sleds. She laid it on the snow by the fire with its face up.

It was painted. Very old paint, faded, grey and white and black, and the paint made a shape. A long shape, coiled round on itself to fill the round of the shield, with a head at the top. A long narrow head, with two dark hollows for eyes. And all over the head and the coils, in black, thin lines, close together, crossing and recrossing.

Scars.

“Every hearth has one,” said Sifa. “On the shields. On the sleds. Over the doors. Since before anybody.” She touched the painted head. “That’s her. That’s what she looks like. Everybody knows. My grandmother knew. Her grandmother.” She looked at the shield. “Nobody has seen her. Not for a long time. Not since it was painted. But that’s her.”

“And the head,” said Riarhin. “On the edge of the shaft.”

Sifa looked at her across the fire.

“That’s her,” she said.

Riarhin believed it.

She believed it because of what she had seen. She went over it, lying awake in the Bishop’s camp, the way she went over everything, and kept coming to the same place. She had seen the frost run in the channels when the body moved. She had seen it run faster when the body turned, and turn when it turned. She had seen it stop in the middle of a pulse when the body stopped, every channel, every wall, at once. She had felt the cold go. She had felt it let go of her, the push that had been against her face since she came into the nave, and she had heard the groaning stop, and it had not come back.

She had seen a stream run in the winter that never ran in the winter.

She had seen a head with scars like writing on it, and a shield with the same head painted on it, older than anybody’s grandmother.

She did not know what the ninth stave said. She did not know why the Choir had died with their mouths open. She did not know what was behind the screen. She knew that something had been making the winter in that nave, and pushing it out through the channels in the ice, and that it had been the thing she put Remorse into, because when it died, the winter in the nave died with it.

She said so. To Hrogaz, and the speakers, in the Bishop’s camp, on the fifth night. She said what she had seen and what she thought it meant, and said which was which, as she always did. And she watched them hear it, and she watched them want it, and she wanted it too.

Hrogaz listened. When she had finished he sat for a long time with the staff across his knees.

Then he laid it down on the furs beside him, and took his hands away from it.

“He says,” said Bródd, “that the hearths under the stave will not send the due this winter.” He listened. “He says the sled is loaded. He says it will stay loaded. He says if the Hall wants it, the Hall can come.” Bródd paused. “He says he doesn’t think the Hall will come.”

Along the benches, nobody spoke. Then an old man said something, very low, and another, and Riarhin saw Orra, at the end of the bench, put her face in her hands.

The Bishop consecrated the ice the next morning.

He did it at the foot of the wall, by the gap where the stream was running, in front of his whole camp drawn up in rows and the speakers of the eleven hearths and the company from the coast. He did it in the southern tongue, with incense, and a great deal of singing, and at the end he took a golden candle from a priest and lit it and set it in a niche in the ice at the side of the gap, and stood back, and spread his hands.

He did not say that the ice was the Temple’s. Riarhin listened for it and he did not say it. He said that a great evil had been cast down here, by the courage of the faithful and the grace of the light, and that the place was cleansed. He said that the Temple would keep a light burning at it, so that it would be remembered. He said that his own soldiers had stood at the foot of this ice in the dark while the evil was cast down, and that he was proud of them.

He did not correct anybody.

Riarhin heard it go round the camp afterward. The Bishop’s men stood at the foot of the ice. The Bishop cast it down. The Temple keeps the light there now. She heard a soldier say it to another soldier, and a Grímhúð boy repeat it to his mother, and she heard the mother say nothing.

She went to the Bishop, that afternoon, at his tent.

“There are four people beyond the shaft,” she said. “Behind the screen. The four from the coast. They’re alive. We saw the lights.” She did not sit down. “There’s a way up, the guides say. A gallery. High on the wall over the shaft. It wants ladders. Timber. People to haul. And the ice to stop moving in the roof.”

“Then they will be brought out,” said the Bishop. He smiled at her. “It is only a matter of ladders now.”

“The coast will want to bring them out.”

The Bishop looked at her with his pale tired eyes.

“Of course,” he said. “They are the coast’s.”

They went south nine days after they came out of the ice: the company from the coast, and Sifa still under Riarhin’s keeping, with Orm on a sled.

They left Tokk with Kallias and Yrra. They left Ilva with the Bishop’s baggage, because Rhone had said so, and because Ilva, when Riarhin asked her, had looked at the silent wall of the ice for a long time and said: I didn’t see the ninth stave. I didn’t get to the Oath. I’m going to stay near it. They left Hrogaz and the speakers at the watchers’ camp with the due-sled loaded and standing, and the stream running, and the candle burning in its niche by the gap.

It took eighteen days to reach the coast. Riarhin kept it in pieces. She remembered the moor, and the boundary stone, and Skeggi coming up from his hut at the head of the lake with a sled when he saw them, and staring at Orm’s hand. She remembered Dalsendi, and Yrsa’s sister, and Hornvik, where Yrsa came out of her house and looked at them and said nothing and went in and came out again with bread. She remembered Skarth, and Bjarni’s shipyard, and Brother Halle’s green birch stacked in the snow. She remembered the valley road, and Brimness, where Leif’s boat was mended and floating, and Leif, who looked at Orm’s hand and then took them down the coast to Hvalgard himself, in two days, without being asked.

She remembered Orm on the sled, and then in the boat, with his left hand wrapped in a great bundle of the Bishop’s linen, held against his chest. He did not talk about it. When anybody asked him he said it was cold, and that Asta would see to it. He said it the same way every time.

The harbour at Hvalgard was full of ships.

She saw them as Leif’s boat came round the point. Not the boats of the town, drawn up on the shingle. Ships. Long dark Linn ships with their masts up, lying at anchor in the fjord in a crowd, a dozen of them, twenty, more; and more drawn up on the strand along the town; and on the shingle, fires, and tents, and a great many people. On the quays there were stacks of weapons under hides. On the whale-bone posts at the harbour mouth there were banners, one above another, all different colours, and at the top of the right-hand post, highest of all, a long blue banner with a white ship on it.

“Ingrith’s muster,” said Leif. He had stopped humming. “She’s called the bays.”

They came in through the whale-bone gate under oars, and people on the quays saw them, and stopped, and stared, and then began to shout.

It went along the quays and up the shingle and into the town, and people came down. Riarhin, in the bottom of the boat with her arm in its sling and her leg out straight, saw them come: men with spears and women with children and boys running ahead, along the quays and down the ramps to the edge of the water, all of them shouting, and the shout was a name. She did not know whose at first. Then she did. It was Orm’s.

Orm Ketilsson. Orm Ketilsson is back. Orm’s come back.

Orm, sitting on the thwart in front of her with his hand against his chest, did not move.

Sigrun was on the quay.

Riarhin saw her come through the crowd. She came the way she did everything, straight and without hurrying, and people got out of her way. She came to the edge of the quay where Leif was bringing the boat in, and she looked down into it.

She did not look at Riarhin. She did not look at Temür, or Bródd, or Sifa. She looked at Orm. She looked at his face, and then at the great bundle of linen held against his chest, and her face did not change at all. Then she got down into the boat.

She got down off the quay into the boat while it was still moving, a long step down onto the thwart, and the boat rocked, and she sat down beside Orm on the thwart and took his left forearm in both her hands, below the bundle, gently, and held it, and looked at the linen.

“Asta,” she said, without looking up. To anybody. To the quay. “Get Asta.”

Somebody ran.

Orm looked at her.

“It’s cold,” he said.

“I know it is,” said Sigrun.

Ingrith received them in Gudleif’s hall that night, in front of the muster.

The hall was full. It was fuller than Riarhin had ever seen a hall. Every jarl and hall-holder from every bay that had come to Ingrith’s call was there, on the benches and standing behind the benches and standing along the walls, and the doors were open at both ends and people stood outside them in the snow to hear. Riarhin had been carried up from the quay, again, on a hurdle, and set on a bench by the fire with her leg up. She sat and looked at the faces. She had never seen so many Linn in one place. They were all looking at her.

Ingrith sat at the high seat at the end of the hall with her braids tied in black. She had stood up when they came in. She had stood up, and come down from the seat, and walked down the length of the hall between the benches to the door, and stood in front of Orm, who was on his feet with his hand bound against his chest and Sigrun beside him, and she had looked at him.

“Nineteen,” she had said, very low. Not to him. To herself.

Then she had embraced him.

Now she sat at the high seat, and the hall was silent, and Ranveig stood beside her with her harp in its sealskin case, and they were waiting.

Orm told it.

He told it badly, Riarhin thought, and well. He told it as a steersman tells a passage, in order, plainly, without much in it about himself. The moor. The frozen river. Brakka’s hearth. The gathering. The due-road. The ice. The nave. He told about the Choir in three sentences and the hall was silent. He told about the thing coming down the nave like weather. He told about the line, and Tokk, and the dark. He told about the bridge, and Temür lying flat on it with his bow. He told about the coast-woman going across the open floor to the throat.

He did not tell about his hand.

When he had finished there was a silence in the hall that went on for a long time. Then somebody at the back began to beat his fist on the bench. And somebody else. And then the whole hall, every fist on every bench, and feet on the floor, and voices, rising, until the roof shook with it.

Ranveig had her harp out.

She did not play it. She came down the hall in the noise to the bench where Riarhin was sitting, and sat down on the end of it, and leaned close so that she could be heard.

“The Ykrul,” she said. “His name. All of it.”

Riarhin told her.

“The guides. The two from the north.”

Riarhin told her. Ranveig did not write them down. She said each one over once, under her breath, and Riarhin saw her keep it.

“And it was in the throat,” said Ranveig. “Orm says the throat. Hauk’s cousin’s boy, who had it from one of the Bishop’s soldiers on the road, says you put it through the eye.”

“The Ykrul put an arrow in the eye,” said Riarhin. “From the bridge. Lying down. I didn’t see it. I put the sword in the throat. Under the jaw. Then behind the jaw.” She thought. “It was twice. Not once.”

“Twice,” said Ranveig. She considered. “Once is better.”

“It was twice.”

Ranveig looked at her a long moment with her bird’s look.

“And it’s dead,” she said. “The Mother of the ice. The thing in the songs. Dead.”

Riarhin looked at the hall. At the fists on the benches, and the faces, and Ingrith at the high seat with her braids in black. At Sifa, sitting on the floor at the end of her bench with her knees drawn up, not beating anything, watching the fire.

She thought of the frost stopping in the channels. The groaning stopping. The cold letting go. The stream running in the winter. The shield on the snow with the painted head.

“I saw it die,” she said. “I saw the winter in the ice stop when it died. I saw the stream run.” She looked at Ranveig. “The four aren’t out yet. They’re behind a screen beyond a shaft. They’re alive. There’s a way up to them, the guides say, with ladders.” She paused. “But the thing that was making the winter is dead. I saw that.”

Ranveig nodded slowly.

Then she stood up, and went back up the hall to the high seat, and bent and spoke in Ingrith’s ear.

Ingrith did it that night.

Riarhin had not expected it to be so fast. She had thought there would be days of talk, as there had been at Kettlebeach, and at the gathering. There were not. Ingrith stood up from the high seat and the hall went quiet, and she said what was to happen, and it happened.

The muster would stand down. The bays could send their ships home. There would be no war-band north, because there was nothing north now to make war on: Orm Ketilsson and the coast-woman and the Ykrul had seen to that, under the stone’s word, with the guides of the four valleys, and Ranveig would make the verse.

But four of Ottarshall’s were still in the ice. Behind a screen. Alive.

So a company would go north again. Not a war-band. A hauling crew. Twenty men from the bays, picked, with sleds, and timber from the stores on the quay, and ladders, and line, and the two best ice-men on the coast to lead them, and they would go up the due-road to the foot of the ice, and wait at the watchers’ camp till the roof of the nave was still, and go in by the hauling way the guides had marked, and bring the four out.

“Tomorrow,” said Ingrith. “They go tomorrow. The timber is on the quay. The sleds are on the shingle.” She looked down the hall at Riarhin. “Will you tell them the way?”

Riarhin looked at her.

“Yes,” she said.

Ingrith nodded. Then she looked down the hall, at all of it, the benches and the faces and the open doors with the snow coming in, and for the first time since Riarhin had seen her at Ottarshall, at the edge of the burned hall with the meal boat gone to Selvík, her heavy reddened face did something that was not stillness.

“We’ll have a feast,” she said. “When they’re on the road. A feast for the ones who came back.” She paused. “Ottarshall has not had a feast since the autumn.”

The hall roared.

In the noise, Riarhin looked for Orm. He was by the door, with Sigrun beside him, and Asta. Asta had come up from the quay in the dark with her bag and her needles and she was standing in front of him, holding his bound left hand in both of hers, not unwrapping it, only holding it, and looking at his face. She was not looking at the hall. Orm was not looking at it either. He was looking at Asta.

Riarhin watched them through the roaring, across the length of the hall, and did not join in.

Chapter 30 — What Asta cannot mend

Asta unwrapped the hand in Jorunn’s hall in the morning, by the window, in the best light, with Orm sitting on a stool and Sigrun standing behind him and a man from the Temple ship standing beside her who had been a surgeon in the south before he took the grey robe. Riarhin was on the end of the women’s bench, where she had always been. Nobody had told her to go away.

It took a long time. The Bishop’s linen had been wound round and round and the inside of it had stuck. Asta soaked it in warm water from a kettle and peeled it back a layer at a time, and talked while she did it, about nothing, about Jorunn’s goats and Eyvind’s cough and the price of meal, in the same level voice she had used in the hold of the Staðfast with her needle in Riarhin’s thigh. Orm sat on the stool and looked at the wall.

Then the last of the linen came away, and Asta stopped talking.

Riarhin looked.

It was his left hand. The thumb and the first two fingers were red and swollen and blistered along their backs, the skin shining and tight, but they were the colour of a hand. The last two were not. The little finger and the one beside it, from the tips down past the second joint, had gone a dark dull purple-grey, mottled, the colour of a bruise that has been a long time healing, and the nails on them were black. They lay curled against the palm. They did not move when the others moved.

Asta looked at them for a long time. She did not touch them.

The surgeon from the Temple ship bent close and looked too. He took Orm’s wrist in his hand, very gently, and turned the hand a little in the light, and looked at the line where the purple-grey met the red, and pressed with his thumb above it, and below it, and watched.

Nobody said anything.

“Well,” said Orm, at last. He was still looking at the wall.

“Wait,” said Asta.

“For what?”

“For it to show me,” said Asta. “It doesn’t show you all at once. It shows you over days. What’s going to come back and what isn’t.” She looked at the hand. “Some of that may come back. Not all.”

“Which?”

“I don’t know yet,” said Asta.

“I do,” said Orm. He lifted the hand an inch off his knee and looked at it, at the two curled grey fingers, and put it down again. “I knew on the ice. I knew when Sifa was trying to get the glove off.” He looked at Asta. “Just say it.”

Asta looked back at him. She did not say it.

It was not said for six days.

Riarhin watched the six days. She did not know what else to do. She sat on the end of the women’s bench and watched Asta dress the hand twice a day, in warm water and goose-grease and clean linen, and watched the surgeon from the Temple ship come up from the broadshore every morning and look at it and go away without speaking, and watched Orm.

He did not like being watched. He liked less what they said over his head.

It happened on the second day. Asta and the surgeon at the window, with the hand between them on a cloth, talking low in a mixture of the northern speech and the southern, and Sigrun standing behind, and Hallbjorn in the door with his arms folded, and all of them looking at the hand, and the surgeon said something in the southern tongue that had the word for knife in it, and Asta answered him in the northern, not yet, not till the line shows, and Sigrun said, how long will that be, and Hallbjorn said, he can’t steer with that.

Orm stood up.

He stood up off the stool so fast that it fell over behind him, and his hand came off the cloth and he held it against his chest, and he looked round at them.

“I’m here,” he said.

They stopped.

“I’m here,” said Orm. His voice was shaking. “I’m standing in front of you. It’s on the end of my arm. If you want to talk about whether I can steer with it you can ask me.” He looked at Hallbjorn. “Ask me.”

Hallbjorn did not unfold his arms.

“Can you?” he said.

“I don’t know,” said Orm. “I don’t know. I haven’t tried.” He turned to the surgeon. “And you can say knife in the northern, I know the word, I’ve got one.”

The surgeon looked at him with a calm southern face.

“Knife,” he said, in the northern speech. “Probably. In some weeks. When the line shows. Not today.”

“Thank you,” said Orm. He bent, with his good hand, and picked up the stool, and set it on its legs, and sat down on it again, and put his bad hand back on the cloth.

“Now,” he said. “Go on.”

Riarhin tried to take his work.

She did not decide to. It began the way everything began with her that winter, before she had looked at it. On the third morning Sigrun was at the table with the freight lists for the spring, and there was a coil of new line on the floor by the hearth that Orm had been splicing before he went north, half-done, with the fid still stuck in it, and Riarhin saw it and took it onto her knees and began to finish it.

She could do that much. Her left arm had come back from the nave in two days, the feeling in it, though Kallias had shaken his head at her and said cold in the blood was a strange thing and she should keep it warm. Her right arm was no worse than before. She sat on the bench and worked the fid and finished the splice.

Then she did the next one.

Then she found the steering oar’s lashing, which Orm had been re-whipping, and re-whipped it. Then she went down to the quay with the staff, slowly, and found Hallbjorn on the Staðfast’s deck and asked him what else of Orm’s wanted doing, and Hallbjorn looked at her for a long time with his arms folded and said, nothing that’s yours, and she went back up to Jorunn’s and found the net needles in Orm’s bag and began to fill them with twine, because she knew how to do that now, from Hild.

On the fourth day Orm came along the hall and stood over her.

She was sitting on the bench with the bag of net needles beside her, filling them. He looked at the needles. He looked at the coil of line on the floor beside her, finished, flaked down neat, the splice tapered and rolled. He looked at the steering-oar lashing on the bench.

“That’s mine,” he said.

She looked up.

“The line,” said Orm. “I was doing the line. Before. I was halfway.” He looked at the splice. “You finished it.”

“I thought—”

“I know what you thought.” He did not raise his voice. He looked tired. “You thought, he can’t. You thought, I’ll do it for him so he doesn’t have to sit and look at it and know he can’t.” He crouched down beside the bench, slowly, on his heels, with his bound hand against his chest. “I sat and looked at it. Yesterday. I watched you do it. All of it.”

Riarhin put down the needle.

“I want the next one,” said Orm. “There’s another coil in the hold. Under the forepeak. Hallbjorn’ll tell you where. I want it.” He looked at her. “I want to do it. It’ll take me a day. It’ll take me two. It took me an hour before. I don’t care.” He looked at his bound hand. “I want one thing that’s mine that I do. Not everything. One.”

Riarhin looked at him for a long moment.

Then she picked up the bag of net needles, half filled, and the twine, and held them out.

“These too,” she said. “They’re yours. I took them out of your bag.”

Orm looked at them. Then he laughed, short, and took them in his good hand.

“The needles you can have,” he said. “I hate filling needles.” He put them back in her lap. “The line’s mine.”

Hallbjorn and Sigrun argued about it on the fifth night, at the table in Jorunn’s hall, in front of everybody, and Riarhin listened to every word.

It was about the ship. About the Staðfast, and the spring, and what Orm could do aboard her. Hallbjorn said a steersman needed two hands, and a man with three fingers on one of them would be a passenger in a gale whatever he said, and that it would be a kindness to say so now and let him find other work ashore before the spring. He did not say it unkindly. He said it the way he had said no on the shingle under the forest: because somebody had to.

Sigrun let him finish.

“No,” she said.

“Sigrun—”

“No. Not now.” She put her hands flat on the table. “Asta hasn’t said what’s coming off. The surgeon hasn’t said. Orm hasn’t tried. Nothing has healed. Nothing has closed.” She looked at Hallbjorn. “You want me to decide his whole life at this table before the wound has closed. I won’t.”

“Somebody has to.”

“In the spring,” said Sigrun. “When it’s closed. When he’s tried. Then somebody has to. Then it’ll be him and me.” She looked down the hall at Orm, who was sitting on the hearthstone with the new coil of line across his knees and the fid in his good hand, working at a splice very slowly, and had not looked up once. “Not you, Hallbjorn. Not tonight.”

Hallbjorn looked at her for a long time.

Then he got up and went out into the snow.

He came back an hour later with a thing he had made. Riarhin saw him put it down on the hearthstone beside Orm without a word and go to his bench. It was a palm-guard, a sailmaker’s leather palm, the kind Hallbjorn wore himself to push a needle through canvas. But it was made for a left hand, and it was made with three fingers and not five, and where the other two would have been the leather was cut back and stitched round, neatly, so that it would sit flat against the side of the hand and not catch.

Orm looked at it for a long time. He did not pick it up. He did not say anything.

He went back to the splice.

The recovery crew went north on the sixth day.

Riarhin went down to the shingle to see them go, because she had said she would, and because she wanted to. They had been loading since dawn: four long sleds on the strand above the boats, and on them timber, cut and squared, from the stores on Gudleif’s quay, and ladders, six of them, lashed in pairs, and coils of line, and iron spikes, and food. Twenty men from the bays, picked by Ingrith herself, in heavy coats, with their dogs. A great many people had come down to watch.

Their leader was an ice-man from Selvík called Gest. He was a short dry man with a white beard and pale eyes that had the snow-glare in them, like Skeggi’s, and he had spent two evenings in Jorunn’s hall with Riarhin and Sifa and a piece of slate, going over the way. Now he stood by the lead sled with the slate in his hand and went over it again.

“Brimness. The valley road. Skarth. Hornvik. The lake road to Dalsendi.” He tapped the slate. “Skeggi to the stone. Then the moor. Then the river country. The marks on the posts.” He looked at Sifa. “You’ve told me the marks.”

“I’ve told you,” said Sifa. “Follow them. Even when they look wrong.”

“Even when they look wrong.” Gest nodded. “Then the four valleys. Hrogaz’s watchers at the foot of the ice. Wait there for the roof to stop.”

“How will you know it’s stopped?” said Riarhin.

“Yrra will know,” said Gest. “The guide. You said.”

“Yrra will know.” Riarhin looked at the slate. “And the way in. The gap. The Choir.” She stopped. “Don’t let anybody read the slab. Not a line. Not out loud.”

“I won’t,” said Gest. He had heard about the Choir. Everybody had. “And the red cloth at the foot of the wall by the shaft. And the gallery. And the ladders.” He looked at her. “And when we’ve got them, or when we can’t—”

“A fire,” said Riarhin. “At the watchers’ camp. Two fires, side by side, if you’ve got them. One if you haven’t. Hrogaz’s people will send word south by the posts.” She paused. “And come back. Either way. Come back and say.”

“Either way,” said Gest.

He put the slate in his coat. Then he looked at her, and at her arm in its sling and her leg stiff on the shingle, and at Sifa beside her.

“You’re not coming,” he said.

“No.”

“Good,” said Gest. He said it without any unkindness at all. “You’ve done yours.” He nodded at the sleds. “This is ladders. This is hauling. This is twenty men and the ice being still. We can do this.”

He turned and called to the crew, and they took up the traces, and the sleds began to move.

Riarhin stood on the shingle and watched them go. Up the strand, past the boat-sheds, onto the track that went up out of the town toward the hills and the valley road to Brimness. Four sleds, with the timber and the ladders sticking up out of them against the snow, and twenty men, and the dogs, and the people of Hvalgard walking beside them as far as the last house and then stopping, and standing, and watching them go on.

She watched until they were over the first rise. Then she turned round.

The smell of roasting was coming down from the town.

It came from Gudleif’s hall, and from the long boat-shed below it, and from every house in Hvalgard that had an oven, and it came all afternoon. Meat. Mutton and seal and a whole ox that somebody had driven down from the hill farms for Ingrith’s muster and that would not now go north. Bread, from Brother Halle’s barley, in great round loaves. Fish. Smoke. Ale, in barrels, rolled down the ramps from the cellars.

Riarhin went up through it to Jorunn’s hall. She was hungry. She had not been hungry like this since the hold of the Staðfast.

Orm was on the hearthstone. He had the coil across his knees and Hallbjorn’s palm-guard on his left hand, three fingers through it and the leather stitched flat where the others would have been, and he was working at the splice with the fid in his right hand and the strand held down under the guard. It was slow. His face was set with it. He did not look up.

Asta was beside him on the bench, knitting.

“Gest’s gone,” said Riarhin.

“I heard,” said Orm, without looking up.

“They’re roasting an ox at Gudleif’s.”

“I can smell it.” He pushed the fid through. The strand caught on the edge of the guard and he swore and pulled it free. “Asta says I can go. If I don’t drink too much.”

“I said,” said Asta, knitting, “if you don’t drink more than you can hold with one hand.”

Orm laughed.

He laughed out loud, on the hearthstone, with the splice in his lap and the guard on his hand, and Asta’s needles did not stop, and after a moment Riarhin found she was laughing too. She did not know why. She stood in the door of Jorunn’s hall with the smell of the ox coming up the hill behind her and laughed until her rib hurt, and Orm looked up at her from the hearthstone, laughing, and his eyes were wet.

“Help me up,” he said. “I’m starving.”

She helped him up.

Chapter 31 — A verse everybody knows

They held it in the tide-shed, because nothing else in Hvalgard was big enough.

The tide-shed stood at the head of the long strand below the town, where the shingle was steep and the spring tides came up highest, and it was where the coast laid up its great ships in the winter: a long, high building of tarred timber open at the seaward end, with a roof like the upturned hull of a ship, and room under it for three longships side by side on their rollers. There were no longships in it tonight. They had been hauled out onto the strand in the afternoon, by a hundred people on the ropes, singing, and the floor of the shed had been swept, and fires had been lit down the middle of it in long pits, and tables brought down from every hall in the town.

Gudleif had lifted the watch.

Riarhin heard it said as she came down the hill on Temür’s arm in the dusk, and did not understand it, and asked. Every night of the winter, Temür said, who had asked too, there were men on the whale-bone posts at the harbour mouth with a horn, watching the boom, and men on the quays watching the ships, and men on the strand watching the tide-line, and it was the law of the harbour and it was never lifted. Gudleif had lifted it. For one night. He had stood on the quay at noon and said it aloud, in the old words, in front of witnesses, so that the sea would hear it: the boom was down and would stay down, and the watch was off, and every crew in the harbour could come up to the tide-shed and eat, and if the sea wanted anything tonight it could wait till morning.

“He said that?” said Riarhin.

“He said the sea could wait,” said Temür. “In front of everybody. Then he went and was sick behind his hall.” He looked down the hill at the tide-shed, at the firelight coming out of its open end onto the strand. “Then he came back and told them to roll out the ale.”

It was very loud.

That was the first thing. Riarhin had been in halls in the north for a whole winter and she had thought she knew what a Linn hall sounded like when it was full. She had not. She came in through the landward door of the tide-shed on Temür’s arm and the noise hit her like heat from an oven: hundreds of voices, all at once, shouting and laughing and singing three different songs, and somebody playing a pipe badly and somebody else playing a drum worse, and dogs, and children, and under it all, from the strand outside the open seaward end, the sea.

The second thing was Eyvind.

He hit her at the door. He came through the crowd at a run and stopped in front of her, a hand’s breadth off, and stood there, and his face went red and white and red again, and he looked at her sling and her leg and her face, and then he put his arms round her.

He did it badly, the way a boy of fourteen does it who has not decided to, with his elbow in her broken rib, and she made a sound and he jumped back.

“Sorry,” he said. “Sorry. Asta said your rib. I forgot. Sorry.”

“It’s all right.”

“I was on the mast,” said Eyvind. “Every day. I watched the valley road. Sigrun said I could. I saw Leif’s boat come round the point. I saw it first.” He looked at her. “Again.”

“I know,” said Riarhin. “You always do.”

He went redder still, and grinned, and then he saw the strap of her frame where it had broken in the nave and been tied up again with a bit of line, and his face changed.

“Who tied that?” he said.

They put her at the high table because Ingrith said so, between Orm and a jarl from the eastern bays she did not know, with Temür on Orm’s other side and Bródd beyond him, and food came.

It came for a long time. She had not seen so much food since the south. Mutton, and seal, and pieces of the ox, and bread, and fish in five different ways, and a sour pale cheese, and a dish of something sweet made with honey and the barley, and ale. The ale came round in great horns that went from hand to hand down the tables, and you drank, and passed it on, and somebody filled it.

Orm could not lift the horn.

She saw it happen. It came down the table to him, full, a great curved horn with a silver rim, held out to him by the jarl from the eastern bays, and Orm reached for it with his left hand, without thinking, the way he had reached for things all his life, and the bound hand with its three fingers came up and closed on the horn and could not hold it. It tipped. Ale slopped over the rim onto the table.

Orm’s face went still.

Hallbjorn, across the table, reached out and took the horn. He did not say anything. He did not look at Orm’s hand. He took the horn out of it by the rim and turned it in his own enormous fist and held it out again, the narrow end toward Orm, so that Orm could take it in his right.

Orm took it in his right. He drank. He passed it on.

Hallbjorn went back to his mutton.

The oar-master started it, at the end of the first remove, when the ale had been round twice.

His name was Kollsvein. He had been the Staðfast’s oar-master for twenty years, a bald round man with a voice like a bull, and on the voyage north he had set the stroke whenever they rowed, and Riarhin had lain in the hold and heard him calling it through the deck. Now he stood up on the bench at the Staðfast’s table, with a horn in his hand, and banged on the table with it until the end of the shed near him went quiet.

“The Fox Point stream,” he said. “I want to tell it.”

There was a groan from the Staðfast’s table, and a cheer from everybody else.

He told it. He told how they had put in for water at the Fox Point stream on the forest shore, the same as every year. How the boy had seen something on the slope and Hallbjorn had said it was a deer. How Orm had walked down the shingle. He told it very well, with his hands, and Riarhin sat at the high table and listened to herself being carried along the shingle again by a man with a voice like a bull, and the shed laughed in all the right places.

Then he came to the rowing.

“And we come off the beach,” said Kollsvein. “With her in the bottom of the boat. And the surf’s up at the point, it’s white, it’s coming over the bow, and I’m setting the stroke. One. Two. One. Two.” He beat on the table with the horn. “And who’s keeping it?”

“You,” shouted half the Staðfast’s table.

“Me,” said Kollsvein. “I am keeping it. And who is not keeping it?”

There was a pause.

“Hallbjorn,” said Kollsvein, and pointed.

The shed roared.

“I was keeping it,” said Hallbjorn. He had not looked up from his mutton.

“You were not keeping it. You were looking at the trees. You were rowing like a man looking at the trees. You were half a stroke behind the whole way to the ship, and the boat went round in a circle, and the boy had to—”

“The boy,” said Hallbjorn, “caught a crab.”

“I did not,” said Eyvind, from the far end of the table, outraged.

“You caught a crab and fell backward on Orm,” said Hallbjorn. “I saw it. I was looking at the trees and I saw it.”

“I was lifting her head out of the water!”

“With your backside,” said Hallbjorn.

The shed fell apart. Riarhin, at the high table, put her hand over her mouth. She remembered it. She remembered lying in the bottom of the boat in the seawater with her head on a coil of rope, and the boy kneeling beside her, and then the boat lurching and the boy coming down on top of her, all elbows, apologising. She had not known, at the time, that it was because he had caught a crab. She had thought it was the surf.

“And Orm,” said Kollsvein, over the noise. “Where was Orm? Orm was in the stern. Orm was steering. And Orm was—”

He stopped. He looked at Orm, at the high table, with the bound hand in his lap.

The shed went a little quieter.

“Orm was steering,” said Kollsvein, after a moment, in a different voice, “like Orm steers. Straight. All the way.” He lifted the horn. “Orm Ketilsson.”

The whole shed lifted its horns and its cups and its fists. Orm Ketilsson. Orm sat at the high table with his face gone dark red under his beard and did not look at anybody, and the jarl from the eastern bays clapped him on the right shoulder, carefully, and Orm took the next horn that came, in his right hand, and drank.

Riarhin laughed.

She found she was still laughing, about Eyvind and the crab, and Hallbjorn looking at the trees, and she did not stop. She sat at the high table in the noise and the heat and laughed, and nobody explained anything to her.

They danced after the second remove.

Riarhin could not. Her leg would not, and her rib would not, and Asta, before she left Jorunn’s, had looked at her and said if you dance I’ll know. She sat at the high table with her leg on a bench and watched.

The Linn danced in long lines, holding hands, stamping, round and round the fire-pits, faster and faster, singing. Temür was pulled into one by a girl from the eastern bays who would not let go of his hand. He did not know the steps. Riarhin watched him not know them. He went round the first fire-pit a beat behind everybody, stamping in the wrong places, and the girl on one side of him laughed at him and the man on the other side shouted at him, and he went redder and redder and stamped harder, and the line pulled him round.

Then the drum changed. Somebody from the north end of the shed, from the boats that had come down from the far bays, began to beat it differently, faster and harder and with a skip in it, and the line broke up, and some of the dancers went to the side, laughing, and some of them, the ones from the north end, began a different dance. A circle, not a line. Men only. Arms over each other’s shoulders, going round, and every few beats they all leapt, together, and came down, and went on round.

Temür watched it from the side of the fire-pit with the eastern girl still holding his hand.

Then he let go of her hand, and went in.

His arms went over the shoulders of the men on either side. Riarhin recognized a rider’s landing in his first leap. He went round the circle with the men from the far bays and he leapt when they leapt and higher, and came down light, and they looked at him, and grinned, and one of them shouted something, and the circle went faster.

The girl from the eastern bays watched him with her hands on her hips.

“He can’t dance,” she said to Riarhin, coming to the high table for her horn. She sounded aggrieved. “He couldn’t do the line at all. Look at him now.”

“He can do that one,” said Riarhin.

“That’s the north-bay one. Nobody can do that one but them.” The girl watched Temür leap. “He’s better than them.” She drank. “That isn’t fair.”

Bródd was wanted for the ale.

It was not what Riarhin expected.
