She had thought, if he was wanted for anything at the feast, it would be for words: somebody would want him to say the old talk, or tell the one about the crossing, the way they had at midwinter feasts since his grandmother died. Nobody asked him for words. A man from Selvík came to the high table with a cup in his hand and an argument going behind him, and stood in front of Bródd and said: “You’re the cooper. The cooper’s Bródd. Gudleif says the second barrel’s off. Is it the barrel or the ale?”

Bródd looked at him.

Then he got up and went down the shed with the man from Selvík, and Riarhin saw him at the barrels by the landward door, a great crowd round him, with a candle, putting his face to the bung of the second barrel and smelling, and then rapping the staves with his knuckles, one after another, all the way round, with his head bent and his ear close, listening. Then he said something. The crowd round him roared. Somebody put a cup in his hand. Somebody else pulled him to the third barrel.

He did not come back to the high table for an hour. When he did, he was flushed and his apron was wet and he had three cups in his hands and he gave her one.

“It was the barrel,” he said. “A stave had sprung. Not much. Enough to let the air at it.” He sat down. “The man who made it was from Kettlebeach.” He drank. “I said so. Loudly. In front of Kettlebeach.”

He looked, Riarhin thought, happier than she had ever seen him.

Ranveig sang at midnight.

The drums stopped for her. The pipes stopped. The whole shed stopped, the dancers at the fire-pits and the people at the tables and the children asleep in heaps on the cloaks by the walls, and outside the open seaward end the sea went on, and Ranveig stood up beside Ingrith at the high table with her harp against her hip.

She struck it once. Then she sang.

She sang it in the old way, the way Riarhin had heard her speak at the stone, in the old words and the old measures, and the cracked low voice went out over the shed and filled it. Riarhin could not follow it all. She followed enough.

It was the north. The moor and the frozen river and the long ice. It was nine of them going in. Nine, Ranveig sang, and Riarhin counted without meaning to, and could not make it come to nine, and let it go. It was the dark nave and the thing coming down it like weather. It was Orm Ketilsson with the line in his hands, holding, holding, and the cold eating his fingers, and he did not let go. It was the Ykrul on the bridge with his bow. It was the coast-woman going across the open floor to the throat with her sword.

Once. Ranveig sang once. One stroke, under the jaw, and the thing came down.

It was the cold letting go. It was the stream running in the winter. It was the four, alive, behind a screen, and twenty men gone north with ladders to bring them home.

It was very good. It was the best thing Riarhin had ever heard sung about anything she had done. The shed was silent while Ranveig sang it, and when she struck the last note and let it die there was a long moment when nobody moved, and then the noise came up from the tables like the sea coming up the strand.

Riarhin sat at the high table and listened to it.

It was twice, not once. There were eight going in, not nine, and two of them were guides from the four valleys whose names were not in the verse, and one was a woman from the Temple who had dragged a man with a broken leg along a rope on her knees, and one was a woman with a cut tongue who had stopped a hand on the face of a slab, and none of that was in the verse either. Tokk was not in it. Yrra was not in it. Ilva was not in it. Sifa was not in it at all.

She looked down the table. Orm, with his bound hand in his lap, was looking at Ranveig. His face was very still. Temür was looking at the fire. Bródd was looking at his cup.

She thought about standing up. She thought about it seriously, for the space of three breaths, with the noise of the shed going up round her: standing up at the high table, in front of Ingrith and the muster and the whole of Hvalgard, and saying twice, and eight, and Tokk and Yrra and Ilva and Sifa.

She looked at the shed.

It was full of people. It was full of people who had stood in the snow on the quay at midwinter with their lanterns up to watch five people go north in the dark, so that it was seen. People who had sat in the ash at Ottarshall with their hands bandaged and asked for a key. People who had set four places at a table. They were on their feet. They were shouting Orm’s name and Temür’s and hers, and the ale was going round, and the children had woken up on the cloaks by the walls and were shouting too without knowing what for, and the fires were roaring in their pits, and outside the open end of the shed the sea came up the strand in the dark and went back, and came up, and nobody was watching it.

She stayed in her seat.

She stayed in it, and let the verse stand, and when the horn came round again she took it in her good hand and drank, and passed it to Orm, holding it by the rim with the narrow end toward his right hand, the way Hallbjorn had, and he took it, and drank, and they did not look at each other.

It went on till dawn.

She stayed for all of it. She had not stayed for all of anything in a long time. She sat at the high table with her leg up while the dancers went round the fire-pits and the drums went on, and Temür came back from the north-bay circle sweating and sat down beside her and drank three cups of water one after another without speaking, and the girl from the eastern bays came and sat on his other side and argued with him about the steps, and he argued back, in bad northern, and lost.

At some point Jorunn sat down on Gudleif’s knee. Riarhin saw it. Nobody else seemed to think it was remarkable.

At some point Eyvind fell asleep with his head on the table among the bones.

At some point Orm, at her elbow, said something to her that she did not hear over the noise, and she leaned close, and he said it again.

“I was frightened,” he said. “In the nave. The whole time. Out of my wits.”

She looked at him.

“I know,” she said.

“I know you know,” said Orm. He was a little drunk. He was looking at the fire. “I wanted to say it. Here. Where you could hear it.” He drank. “Not on a deck.”

She did not answer. She put her good hand on the table beside his, the bound one, not on it, beside it, and after a while he moved it a little until the side of it touched hers.

When the light came grey into the open seaward end of the tide-shed, over the water, the fires were embers and half the town was asleep on the floor, and the drum was still going, slowly, somewhere at the north end, one beat and a long pause and another, and outside on the strand the longships lay on their rollers in a row with the snow on them, and the tide was going out.

Chapter 32 — Under the weir

The weir froze from underneath on a morning when the snow on the hills had begun, at last, to go grey at the edges.

Riarhin was there because Halla had asked her. That still surprised her. Halla had not spoken to her since the skerries, since the painted eye on the Hjalti’s bow and I didn’t see who owned it; she had walked past her on the quay all winter with her face set. Then, three days after the feast, she had come up to Jorunn’s hall and stood in the door with her youngest on her hip and said, without any greeting at all: You can sit and you can tie. The weir wants hands in the spring. Bárd’s brother says you can tie a knot. If you want work, it’s there.

Bárd’s brother had said it was Bárd’s boat, in Gudleif’s hall in the dark of the winter, with the paint-seller and the steersman’s mother beside him. Gudleif had given Halla the salvage. Riarhin had not been there. She had heard.

So in the long grey mornings, when the light came earlier now over the southern hills and stayed longer, she went down to the river mouth at the north end of the town and sat on the bank above Halla’s weir and tied.

Bárd’s grandfather had built the weir, Halla said, and his grandfather before him, on the same stones. Wattle between slanting stakes guided the eels into a basket of withies as tall as a man. Every spring four households mended it, then filled their barrels. Smoked and salted, the catch fed them and went up the coast for sale.

This year the catch was promised to Ottarshall. Ingrith had asked. Halla had said yes.

Riarhin tied wattle. She sat on the bank with a heap of green withies beside her and wove them into panels on a frame, the way Halla’s mother-in-law had shown her, over and under and round, and the old woman sat beside her and did the same, three times as fast, and corrected her.

“It should be going,” said the old woman, on a morning in the sixth week, looking at the river.

Riarhin looked too. The river at the weir was ice, as it had been all winter: grey, thick, with snow on it. Under the ice, down the middle, there should have been a darker line. She had been told about it. In the spring, at the river mouth, the ice went first down the middle where the current was, and the dark water showed, and then it spread, and then one morning the whole river was open and running and the eels came.

There was no dark line.

“Late,” said Halla, coming along the bank with an axe. “It’s late, that’s all. The hills are going grey. It’ll go.”

She went out onto the ice to the first of the stakes, with the axe, to see.

Riarhin watched her go. She watched her walk out onto the grey river ice to where the first stake stuck up out of it, a black post with a ring of ice round its foot, and stand, and lift the axe, and bring it down on the ice beside the stake.

It rang.

It did not crack. It rang, like iron, and the axe jumped back off it, and Halla stood and looked at the place where it had struck. Then she struck again. It rang again.

“Bring the bar,” she said.

They brought the bar. Two of Halla’s cousins, and the long iron bar with the chisel end that they used to break the weir out in the spring when it was slow, and they went out onto the ice beside Halla and set the chisel end against it and drove it down, both of them, with their whole weight, and it went in a finger’s breadth, and stopped.

“That’s not right,” said the old woman beside Riarhin. Very quietly. She had stopped weaving.

They worked at it all morning. Riarhin went out onto the ice with them after an hour, because she could not sit on the bank and watch, and because she could hold the bar steady even if she could not drive it. They broke a hole beside the first stake. It took them till noon. The ice was as thick as a man’s forearm, then as thick as his thigh, and it was not grey under the surface the way river ice was grey. It was clear. Clear and hard and blue, like the ice of the nave.

When they got through it, there was no water under it.

There was more ice. Below the clear ice there was a layer of something white and soft, like old snow, packed hard. And under that, when the cousins had dug it out with their hands and the bar, there was ice again, clear, grown up from the river-bed. Grown up round the foot of the stake. Round the wattle. Round the stones of the old weir. The whole river-bed, as far down as they could reach with the bar, was solid. Frozen from the bottom.

Halla knelt on the ice by the hole and looked down into it.

“The trap,” she said.

They went to the trap. It was at the downstream end of the weir, at the river mouth, where it opened to the sea. They had left it in the autumn, as they always did, half-sunk, its mouth turned upstream under the ice, so that it would be there when the river opened. Halla’s cousin cut through the ice above it. The ice was clear. Under the ice, in the clear blue, they could see the trap: the great basket of withies, every withy of it, sealed in ice from the bottom up, as clear as glass, its mouth open, upstream, waiting.

There was something in the mouth of it.

Halla’s cousin bent close to the ice and looked. Then he sat back on his heels.

“Eels,” he said.

They were there. A few. Six, eight, Riarhin counted, dark and thin, sealed in the clear ice at the mouth of the trap where they had been going in. They had come down early. A few always came early, Halla said, before the river opened, the bold ones, under the ice. They had come down the river under the ice in the dark, to the trap, and the ice had grown up round them from the bottom while they were going in, and they were there, in the clear blue, mid-swim.

Halla stood on the ice and looked down at them for a long time.

“We won’t get the trap out,” she said. “Not like that. Not till it goes.” She looked at the river. “And if it doesn’t go—”

She did not finish.

They tried to get the trap out anyway. They worked at it all afternoon, all of them, the cousins and Halla and Riarhin and two men who came down from the town when they heard, breaking the ice round it with the bar and the axe, trying to free the withies so that the trap could be lifted and mended and set again when the river opened. They broke the ice round it. They broke the trap. The ice had grown into the withies, between them, through them, and when the ice broke the withies broke with it, and the great basket came up out of the hole in pieces, splintered, each piece cased in clear ice, and lay on the river in the cold.

And the bar went down through the last of the ice where the trap had been, and the cousin who was driving it lost his footing, and the bar went out of his hands and down into the hole, into the soft white stuff, and did not come up.

Riarhin sat on the bank in the late afternoon with her hands raw from the bar and looked at the pieces of the trap on the ice.

She had thought, that morning, sitting there with the withies, that it was a late spring. People had been saying it for a week. Late. The hills are going grey. It’ll go. She had believed them. She had believed them because she wanted to, and because the hills were going grey, and because she knew nothing about springs on this coast and they did.

She did not believe it now.

She had seen ice like that before. Clear, and hard, and blue, and grown, not fallen. She had seen it in the nave. She had seen it in the slab in the hollow under the crag with the names cut in it, and in the half-ring of figures at the Choir with their mouths open, and on the walls where the channels ran. She had seen eels caught in it mid-swim, and a girl with her breath frozen in front of her mouth.

A late frost did not grow up from the bottom of a river.

She sat on the bank with her raw hands in her lap and felt something cold settle in her chest that had nothing to do with the river.

Gest came back that evening.

Riarhin was on the quay when they saw the sleds on the hill road. Eyvind saw them first, from the mast, and shouted, and the whole quay turned. Four sleds, coming down the hill road out of the valley in the dusk. Twenty men, walking. No ladders.

She knew before they reached the town. She knew from the way they walked.

Gest came to Jorunn’s hall. He came in out of the dark with frost in his white beard and his pale snow-glare eyes very tired, and he did not sit down. He stood by the hearth and looked at Riarhin, and at Orm, and at Sifa against the wall.

“We got to the watchers’ camp,” he said. “At the foot of the ice. Hrogaz’s people. Yrra.” He paused. “The stream was running. When we got there. It had been running ever since you left, they said. Every day.”

“And?”

“It stopped,” said Gest. “The second morning after we came. We woke up and it was stone. Frozen. Like it had never run.” He looked at his hands. “Yrra went in to look. She came out white. She said the roof was moving again. The ice in the nave. She said the channels on the walls were running.”

Riarhin did not move.

“The frost,” said Gest. “In the channels. Running. The way you said it ran when the thing was alive. Pulse and pulse.” He swallowed. “She said it was running toward the shaft. Toward the middle. Like it did before.”

“You went in,” said Orm.

“We went in.” Gest nodded. “We waited four days for the roof to stop, and it didn’t stop, and we went in anyway. We went in with the ladders. Past the Choir. I didn’t look at it.” He looked at Riarhin. “We found the red cloth at the foot of the wall by the shaft. We found the gallery, up the wall, where Yrra said. We got the ladders up. We spiked them. We got three men up onto the gallery, and they started along it toward the screen.” He stopped. “The lights were still lit. Behind the screen. They could see them.”

Nobody spoke.

“The ice closed over the spikes,” said Gest.

He said it plainly.

“The spikes we’d driven for the ladders. In the wall. It grew over them. While we watched. It came up out of the wall round the iron, clear, like water rising, and closed over the heads of the spikes, and the ladders were held to the wall by the ice and not by the iron, and then the ice round the top of the ladders began to grow out, along the rungs.” He looked at the fire. “We got the three men down. We got them down off the gallery and down the ladders with the ice growing on the rungs under their hands. One of them left the skin of his palms on a rung. We left the ladders. We couldn’t get them off the wall. They’re in the ice now.” He paused. “We came out. The roof was coming down behind us all the way to the Choir.”

The hall was silent.

“The lights are still lit,” said Gest. “Behind the screen. They’re alive in there. I think.” He looked at Riarhin. “We can’t get to them. Not that way. Not while the ice is doing that.”

Riarhin looked at the fire.

She heard it. She heard it all: the frost running again in the channels, pulse and pulse, toward the middle. The roof moving. The stream stopping. The ice growing over iron while men watched.

She had seen the frost run in the channels when the thing moved, and stop when it died. She had seen it. She had not been wrong about that.

And now the cold was moving again, and the thing was at the bottom of a shaft in a hundred feet of water.

So it had not been the only thing.

She had said, to Ranveig, I saw the winter in the ice stop when it died. That was true. She had said, the thing that was making the winter is dead. She had not seen that. She had seen the first thing and said the second, because they had looked like the same thing, and because she had wanted them to be.

“There’s more,” said Gest. He had taken something out of his coat. A strip of birch-bark, folded. “Yrra gave me this. From Hrogaz. For the woman in keeping.” He held it out to Sifa.

Sifa took it. She unfolded it, and looked at the strokes on it, and touched her jaw.

“He says the due was answered,” she said.

She said it in the northern speech, flat, to the hall.

“He says the sled stood loaded at the watchers’ camp all winter, and nobody came for it. He says the night the stream stopped, the hearths woke up and the meat in the pits was frozen to stone. All the pits. All the eleven hearths. He says in front of every hearth that sent a speaker, there was a slab of ice standing. In the morning. Clear.” She looked at the bark. “He says word came to him up the posts from the gathering. He says Vekk read the first one there. He says it’s the same as the one under the crag. With more on it.”

Ingrith came at midnight. She came up the hill from Gudleif’s hall in the dark with Ranveig behind her and stood in the door of Jorunn’s and looked at them.

“It’s true,” she said. Not a question.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

Ingrith came in. She did not sit. She stood by the hearth with her braids tied in black and her heavy face like a door.

“Selvík’s heard,” she said. “About the weir. About the river at Kettlebeach, which is the same. The eastern bays haven’t, yet. The eastern bays went home from the muster singing.” She looked at Ranveig. “They’re singing it in the eastern bays. Once, under the jaw. My niece’s name is in it. Twenty men gone north with ladders.” She looked at Riarhin. “I need to know what to tell them. Before they hear it from somebody else. Before Thorvald Hrafnsson’s boys take it down the coast and tell it their way.” She did not raise her voice. “I need you to tell me what happened. What you saw, and what you said, and what was true.”

Riarhin opened her mouth.

The dogs went quiet.

All of them. The whole town. Riarhin heard it before she understood it: the dogs of Hvalgard, who had been barking all evening at Gest’s sleds and his men and the comings and goings on the hill, going quiet, one after another, all down the slope from the top of the town to the quay, as if a door had been shut on them.

Sifa had stood up. She was standing against the wall with her hand at her jaw.

The door of Jorunn’s hall opened.

He stood in it. The white bear-fur coat, clean, combed. The black stave. The long grave still face with the firelight not moving on it. He did not come in. He stood just inside the door with the cold coming in round him and bowed, slowly, from the waist, to the whole hall, and straightened, and waited.

Nobody moved. Ingrith, at the hearth, had gone grey.

The man in the white coat waited.

Then he spoke. He spoke in the northern speech, which Riarhin had not heard him use before except for two words, and he spoke it perfectly, courteously, in a low clear voice that filled the hall.

“The Hall has noted,” he said, “that the hearths under the stave of the Hrafn-Grímr did not bring the due this winter.” He waited. Nobody answered. He went on. “The Hall has collected it. In kind. The Hall has noted that the speakers of eleven hearths, and those who undertook to walk beside them, came to the Hall, and did not wait to be heard, and went into the nave without being brought.” He waited. “The Hall has noted that while they were in the nave, they killed what was not theirs to kill.”

He turned his still face, very slightly, toward Riarhin.

“The Mother’s skin,” he said.

Riarhin heard it.

She heard the words, and they went into her and did not attach to anything for a moment. The Mother’s skin. She thought of the shield on the snow by the Bishop’s fire, with the painted head on it. She thought of the long scarred grey thing coming down the nave like weather, with no eyes, and no voice, and water running off it. She thought of Sifa, by the fire, saying that’s her. She thought of the frost in the channels running again toward the middle, pulse and pulse, and the ice closing over the heads of the spikes while men watched, and the eels in the clear blue at the mouth of the trap.

A skin. A thing that had been worn, and taken off, and kept.

“Which was not yours to kill,” said the man in the white coat. “There is a price on it. In blood. The Hall will say what, and to whom, in its own time.” He waited. Nobody answered. He bowed.

He turned to go. At the door he stopped, and turned back, and looked across the hall at Ranveig, who was standing beside Ingrith with her harp case on her back and her face like chalk.

“The Hall has heard the verse,” he said courteously. “It is very good.”

Then he went out, and the cold came in where he had been, and down the hill, one by one, the dogs began to bark.

Chapter 33 — What they said was finished

Ingrith called the halls to Gudleif’s three days later, and Riarhin stood up in front of them and said she had been wrong.

She had thought about how to say it for three days. She had lain awake on the end of the women’s bench in Jorunn’s hall and gone over it, and over it, the way she went over everything. She had thought of a great many ways. In the end she said it the only way she knew how to say anything, which was the way she had said it to Steinar at Skarth and the Bishop at Hornvik: what she had seen, and what she had said, and which was which.

She stood by the hearth in Gudleif’s hall with her rib bound and her arm out of its sling at last, and the hall full, and she said it.

She said that she had seen the frost run in the channels of the nave when the thing moved, and stop when it died. She said that was true. She said she had felt the cold let go, and heard the groaning stop, and seen the stream run in the winter. She said that was true. She said she had seen a head with scars on it, and a shield with the same head painted on it, and been told by somebody who had grown up under that shield that it was the Mother.

“And I said it was dead,” said Riarhin. “The thing that was making the winter. I said that to Ranveig, and to Ingrith, and to this hall. I didn’t see that. I saw something die and the winter in the ice stop with it, and I said the winter was dead.” She looked at them. “It wasn’t. I was wrong.”

The hall was silent.

Then it was not.

It came from everywhere at once. She had expected anger. She had not expected so many kinds of it.

A man at the back shouted that the coast-woman had lied. That she had come down out of the north with a story and Ranveig had made a song of it and the bays had stood down, every ship, every man, and now the weirs were frozen and the Dogmen’s mother was alive and the muster was scattered from here to Selvík and would take a month to call again. He said she had known. He said the Ykrul had known. He said she had come back with a story because she wanted a song.

A woman near him shouted back that the coast-woman had not lied, she had made a mistake, anybody could make a mistake, and the man said a mistake that stood down the muster, and the woman said and who stood it down? Did the coast-woman stand it down? Ingrith stood it down, and somebody near Ingrith shouted at her to be quiet, and she did not.

A jarl from the western bays stood up and said that it did not matter who had lied and who had made a mistake. He said the thing was alive, so the thing had to be killed. He said they had killed its skin with nine people, or eight, or whatever it was, and they could kill the rest of it with a hundred. He said call the muster again. He said go north with fire.

Some of the hall roared at that.

Riarhin stood by the hearth and listened to it. She did not try to speak over it. She had said what she had to say. She stood, and let them shout at her, and did not look away.

Then a woman’s voice came out of the noise, not loud, close to the front. It was cracked and old, and Riarhin knew it.

“Why are they still in there?”

Thora. She was sitting on the front bench, small and wrapped in shawls, with her bandaged hands in her lap. The bandages were cleaner now. Under them, Riarhin could see, the hands had healed badly, the fingers drawn in.

The hall quieted round her. Not all of it. Enough.

“Svala,” said Thora. “Gunnvor. Aud’s Kári. Ulf.” She looked at Riarhin. “You said they were alive. Behind a screen. With lamps. You said there was a way up to them with ladders.” Her voice did not rise. “There’s been a song. Since the feast. In every hall on the coast. About the coast-woman and Orm Ketilsson and the thing in the ice. I’ve heard it sung four times. They’re in it. The four, alive, behind a screen.” She looked down at her hands. “Why are they still in there, while they’re singing about it out here?”

The hall was silent now. All of it.

Riarhin did not answer at once.

She thought of a great many things she could say. That Gest had tried. That the ice had closed over the spikes. That nobody had known. That she had said, in this hall, the four aren’t out yet, and that it was true. They were all true. None of them was an answer to what Thora had asked.

“Because I said the thing that held them was dead,” said Riarhin. “And it wasn’t. And everybody believed me. And the people who went to get them went to get them out of a place that was finished, and it wasn’t finished.” She looked at Thora. “That’s mine. I said it. The song came out of what I said.”

Thora looked at her for a long time.

“Yes,” she said. “It did.”

She did not say anything else.

Ranveig came to Jorunn’s hall that night.

She came alone, with her harp in its sealskin case on her back, and sat down on the bench across the hearth from Riarhin and looked at her with her bird’s look for a long time before she said anything.

“I’ll unmake it,” she said.

Riarhin looked at her.

“The verse.” Ranveig’s cracked voice was very even. “I made it. It’s mine. It’s gone out along the coast with my name on it. If it’s wrong, I unmake it.” She looked at the fire. “That’s the law of it. My mother taught me. A skald who lets a wrong verse stand is worse than a skald who never made one.”

“How?”

“The same way I made it. In front of people. At a stone, or a feast, where it was heard.” Ranveig shrugged. “I stand up and say it again, right. And say where it was wrong.” She paused. “People won’t like it. Half of them won’t believe it. Half of them will remember the first one better, because it was better. The first one always is.” She looked at Riarhin. “And they’ll remember that Ranveig sang it wrong. Every time I sing anything, for the rest of my life, somebody at the back will say are you sure?”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“I want to know what to keep,” said Ranveig.

“Keep?”

“Out of the first one.” Ranveig leaned forward. “It wasn’t all wrong. The thing in the nave was real. You killed it. Orm held the line. The Ykrul lay on the bridge with his bow. Those happened.” She looked at Riarhin. “If I unmake the whole verse, people will hear it was all a lie. They’ll hear that nothing happened up there. That you didn’t go. That Orm didn’t lose his fingers.” Her voice hardened. “He lost his fingers. I’m not taking that out.”

“Then don’t.”

“Then what do I take out?”

Riarhin thought about it.

“Once,” she said. “It was twice.”

Ranveig made a face.

“And nine. It was eight.” Riarhin went on. “And the two guides from the four valleys. Tokk and Yrra. And the woman from the Temple who pulled Tokk along the line. And Sifa, who stopped a hand on the slab and kept us all alive.” She looked at Ranveig. “And that it wasn’t the Mother. That we thought it was. That I said it was. Put that in. Put my name on it.”

“That isn’t a correction,” said Ranveig. “That’s a different song.”

“Then sing a different song.”

Ranveig looked at her for a long time.

“You want me to make you smaller,” she said.

“I want you to make it right.”

“Those aren’t the same thing, coast-woman.” Ranveig stood up. She stood by the hearth with her harp case on her back and looked down at Riarhin. “I’ll put in the twice. I’ll put in the eight. I’ll put in the guides and the Temple woman and the woman in keeping. I’ll put in that you said it was the Mother and it wasn’t.” She paused. “I won’t make it sound as if you didn’t know what you were doing in that nave. You did. You got eight people out of it alive. If I sing you as a fool, they’ll hear that the next one who goes north is going with a fool, and nobody will go.” She turned to the door. “I’ll make it right. I won’t make it small. You don’t get to decide that. It isn’t yours.”

She went out.

Orm wanted to go.

He said so at Jorunn’s table two days later, in front of Sigrun and Asta and Hallbjorn and everybody, with his left hand on the table in front of him.

It was bound in clean linen. Under the linen there were three fingers now and not five. The surgeon from the Temple ship had taken the other two eight days ago, on this table, with Asta holding Orm’s arm and Hallbjorn holding Orm’s shoulders and Riarhin, because Orm had asked for her, holding a lamp. She had kept it still. She had watched the surgeon’s knife and the line where the grey met the red, and she had not looked away, and she had not let the lamp move.

“I said it on the stone,” said Orm. “I’m going north for the four. They’re still north.” He looked at Sigrun. “I’m going.”

“No,” said Sigrun.

“I said it.”

“You said it and you went.” Sigrun did not raise her voice. “You went north for the four and came back and said what you found. That’s what you said on the stone. That’s what you did. Ranveig can sing it.” She looked at his hand. “You’re not going north on that. Not now. Not with it eight days off the knife.”

“I can walk.”

“You can walk. You can’t hold a line.” Sigrun looked at him steadily. “You held one. In the nave. You held it till it froze your hand. I’m proud of you. I’ll be proud of you as long as I live.” She paused. “And you’re not going.”

Orm looked at her. His face worked.

“And I’m not sending anybody else,” said Sigrun.

The table went quiet.

“I’m not,” said Sigrun. She looked round at them. At Hallbjorn, and Eyvind, and Kollsvein the oar-master, and the rest of the Staðfast’s crew along the benches. “I brought a crew north in the autumn. I’ve got a ship mended and a spring freight to carry and the bays waiting for it, and if the river doesn’t open there’ll be no eels and they’ll want meal more than they ever did. I’ve got men here whose families eat if this ship sails.” She looked at Riarhin. “Ingrith sent twenty men north with ladders and they came back with the skin off their hands. If I send my crew north to do whatever it is you’re going to do up there, I don’t know what they’re going for. Neither do you.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“Do you?” said Sigrun.

“No,” said Riarhin.

“Then I won’t,” said Sigrun. “Not for a thing nobody can tell me the shape of.”

The shape came out of Ingrith’s hall, the next day, and it was smaller than anybody wanted.

Ingrith said it. She stood up in Gudleif’s hall in front of the jarls who were left, the ones who had not gone home in anger or gone home in fear, and said what the coast would send. Not a muster. Not a war-band. Not fire. A muster could not be called again before the summer, and by summer the eels would be gone and the coast would be hungry. A war-band could not fight a thing nobody could find. Fire would not burn ice.

What the coast would send was a handful. Again. Under the stone’s word, again. To go north to the four valleys and the Hall, and do two things only.

Bring the four out, if it could be done.

And hear what the Hall had to say. Not kill anything. Not claim anything. Hear it, and read it, and bring back what was said.

“The coast-woman goes,” said Ingrith, “because she has the woman in keeping, and the woman in keeping knows the road. And because she said the thing was dead, in this hall, and it wasn’t, and she said so in this hall.” She looked down the hall at Riarhin. “You go. You bring back what’s true this time.”

Riarhin looked back at her.

“Yes,” she said.

“The cooper goes, if he will. For the old talk.” Ingrith looked at Bródd, who was standing by the wall. “Will you?”

Bródd looked at her a moment. Then he nodded.

“And I’ll send somebody of mine,” said Ingrith. “To the coast’s edge. With my word. To see you onto the ice. After that—” She stopped. “After that I don’t know who goes. I don’t know who can.”

She did not look at Temür.

Riarhin found him on the herring stairs.

He had taken to going up there in the evenings. She had seen him, from Jorunn’s door, climbing the zigzag of steps among the racks in the dusk, alone, and sitting at the top by Hild’s fire long after the women had gone down. She had not followed him. Tonight she did.

It took her a long time. Her leg was better than it had been, and her rib was worse. She went up slowly with the staff among the racks of hanging cod, the dried fish rattling in the wind like bones, and rested on every landing, and at the top she came out onto the shelf of rock under the overhang and he was there, by the fire, alone, with his bow across his knees and the roll of birch-bark with Vekk’s marks on it in his hands.

He looked up when she came.

She sat down on Hild’s keg, across the fire from him. She did not say anything for a while. Neither did he. Below them, down the cliff, the harbour lay grey and still in the dusk, with the ice along its edges thicker than it had been in midwinter.

“Ingrith asked for the cooper,” she said at last. “She didn’t ask for you.”

“I heard.”

“She didn’t ask because she doesn’t think it’s hers to ask.” Riarhin looked at the fire. “It isn’t mine either.” She paused. “I wanted to know if you still choose it.”

Temür looked at her across the fire.

“You’re asking,” he said.

“Yes.”

“You didn’t think I’d go.”

“I didn’t think anything.” Riarhin met his eyes. “I didn’t want to think it for you. You went north the first time because Borte sent you and you didn’t know why. Then you found out why, at her fire. Then you found the mark on Vekk’s stave and it stopped at given. Then you lay on a bridge with no rail and put an arrow in a thing’s eye from underneath while it came down on you.” She looked at the birch-bark in his hands. “You’ve done what she sent you to do. You found where the mark is cut. You could go home and tell her.”

Temür turned the roll of bark in his hands.

“The Hall has a stave,” he said. “With Alaq’s mother on it. Not Vekk’s. Its own. Vekk said the wardens cut what they brought. The Hall keeps what it took.” He looked at the bark. “Borte dreamed a voice saying our names one by one, as if it had them written down somewhere, and was checking. It has them written down somewhere.” He paused. “It has mine.”

“You don’t know that.”

“No,” said Temür. “I don’t know it. I want to.” He looked at her. “And Svala is in there, and the boy, and the old man who counts, and the weaver. They’re on a stave too. Somewhere. Somebody cut their mark on a door in the night, the way they cut Alaq’s.” He rolled the bark tight and put it inside his coat. “I choose it.”

Riarhin nodded.

She did not say anything else. She did not say she was glad, or that she had hoped, though she was, and she had. She sat across the fire from him at the top of the herring stairs and he sat across from her, and below them the light went out of the harbour, and somewhere on one of the lower landings, in the dark among the racks, a woman going down late with a basket was singing the counting song under her breath, alone, six, seven, eight, and stopped.

Part V — The words she kept

Chapter 34 — No tide for the complaint

Ranveig’s correction reached the Black Ness two days before they did.

They found that out from the keeper’s face. The waystation stood on a low island at the foot of the Ness, the great dark headland that ran out into the sea north of Brimness and that every boat going up the coast had to round, and it was where the boats put in for water and shelter before they tried. There was a stone house on the island, and a boat-shed, and a well lined with old green copper that the keeper said had been there before her grandmother, and there was the keeper, Dagny, a tall grey woman with a face like a shut box. She came down to the landing as Leif’s boat came in, and she looked at Riarhin in the bow, and at Sifa behind her, and Riarhin saw her know who they were.

“You’re the coast-woman,” said Dagny. “From the verse.”

“Yes.”

“Which one?” said Dagny.

Riarhin looked at her.

“The first one,” said Dagny, “or the second.” She did not help with the line. “Ranveig sang the second at Kettlebeach eight days ago. The Selvík boats brought it up here the day before yesterday.” Her mouth moved. “Twice, not once. Eight, not nine. She said it was the Mother, and it wasn’t.” She looked at Riarhin. “I had the first one at the end of the winter. From the eastern bays. Off a boat going home from the muster.”

“I’m sorry,” said Riarhin.

“Are you,” said Dagny.

The store was nearly empty.

Riarhin saw it that evening. Dagny did not show it to her; she did not need to. The door of it stood open at the back of the stone house, and Riarhin, carrying her frame in, looked through it and saw the shelves. They were long shelves, stone, built into the wall, three tiers of them, and they had been made to hold a great deal: the waystation’s stores, the meal and the dried fish and the oil and the rope and the spare oars that a keeper at the Black Ness kept for the boats that came in battered from the headland and could not go on.

There were a few sacks on the bottom tier. Some rope. Nothing else.

“The muster,” said Dagny, behind her. She had seen her look. “When the verse came. The eastern boats stopped here on the way home. Thirty of them. Singing.” She looked at the shelves. “They said the Mother of the ice was dead. They said the spring would come early, and the eels would run, and the herring would come in to the Ness the way they used to in my grandmother’s time. They said the coast was done with winters like this one.” She folded her arms. “They were hungry. They’d been at the muster a month on short rations. So I opened the store. I gave them what I had. Because the spring was coming.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“And then the Selvík boats came,” said Dagny, “with the second verse.”

She turned away from the store door.

“That’s not all,” she said.

Her son was out.

He had gone round the Ness to the north four days ago, Ivar, with two other men, in the station’s second boat, to fetch meal from a hall on the far side of the headland whose people owed Dagny a favour from the autumn. He should have been back the day before yesterday. He was not back.

Dagny told it at the table that night, in the stone house, with the wind coming in off the sea and rattling the shutters. She told it to Leif, mostly, who was a boatman and would understand it, and Riarhin listened. She told it without any expression at all.

“The Ness has a race,” she said. “At the tail of it. Where the current comes round the point and meets the sea from the north. In a north wind it runs like a river. There’s reef under it, and the wrecks of three hundred years on the reef.” She looked at the shutters. “There’s a cut. Inside the race. Between the reef and the rocks under the cliff. You can get through it at the right water in a small boat if you know it. Ivar knows it.” She paused. “The wind’s been north for three days.”

“He’ll have waited,” said Leif. “On the far side. For it to drop.”

“He’ll have waited,” said Dagny. “If he could.”

Nobody said anything.

Riarhin looked at the table. She thought of the road ahead. Round the Ness, up the coast to the sea-landing below Skarth, and Skarth, and the lake road, and the moor, and the four valleys, and the ice. She had counted it on Gest’s slate in Jorunn’s hall. Thirty days if nothing went wrong. Every day the four were behind the screen. Every day the frost ran in the channels toward the middle, and the ice grew over iron.

“There’s a light,” said Dagny.

They looked at her.

“On the point,” she said. “At the tail of the Ness. Over the race. You can see it from the island at night when the weather’s thick. It moves.” She did not look at any of them. “Every keeper here has seen it. My mother saw it. They say it’s a lantern. They say it’s the drowned, on the reef, signalling the boats in. They say never follow it.” She looked at the shutters. “I saw it last night.”

Riarhin did not sleep.

She lay in the stone house on a bench by the wall with her frame beside her and listened to the wind. It was dropping. She could hear it drop, through the night, the long gusts coming further and further apart and the rattle of the shutters going quiet. Toward dawn she got up and went outside.

The sea was grey under a grey sky. The wind had gone round to the west and was light. The headland stood up black against the north, enormous, with the white water of the race at its tail, and the reef under the white water, and beyond it, she knew, the cut, and beyond the cut the far side, where a boat with three men in it had been waiting four days for the wind to drop.

She stood on the landing and looked at it.

Sifa came and stood beside her. Then Temür, and Bródd. Then Leif, with his boat-hook.

“If they come through the cut this morning,” said Leif, very low, “at the top of the tide, they’ll want somebody at the inside end of it. On the rocks. With a line. In case.”

“Dagny can’t go,” said Riarhin. “She’s the keeper. She has to be here if a boat comes in battered.”

“No.”

“And we can’t wait for the tide. We’ve thirty days to the ice.”

“No,” said Leif.

Riarhin looked at the headland.

“How long?” she said.

“To get round to the rocks inside the cut? By the cliff path? Half the morning.” Leif looked at her leg. “For you, more. Then the tide. Then whatever happens. Then back.” He shrugged. “A day. If they come. Two if they don’t and we wait.”

Riarhin thought of the screen with its small warm lights.

“A day,” she said.

They went round by the cliff path, the five of them and two of Dagny’s cousins with line, and it took most of the morning, and Riarhin’s leg did not like it. They came down at last onto the rocks under the cliff at the inside end of the cut, where the reef came close to the land and left a narrow channel of dark water between, and the race was roaring white on the far side of the reef, and the cut, inside it, was very nearly calm.

They waited.

The tide came up. Riarhin watched it come. She sat on a rock with a coil of line in her lap and watched the water in the cut rise, and the white water on the reef go down as it covered, and the wrecks show and vanish. Old ribs of boats, black, on the reef. A stem-post. A mast, broken off, sticking up out of the weed at an angle.

On the mast, near the top, there was something lashed. A lump of something, dark, the size of a man’s head.

“What’s that?” she said.

Dagny’s cousin looked.

“That’s the light,” he said.

They looked at him.

“That’s the drowned men’s lantern,” he said. He seemed embarrassed. “It’s a lantern. An old one. Brass. Somebody lashed it there, I don’t know when. My father’s time. It’s a wreck-light. You hang one on a wreck so boats can see it at night and keep off.” He looked at it. “Nobody’s ever been out to it to see. It’s on the reef. In the race. You don’t go out there.”

“And it’s lit,” said Riarhin. “At night.”

“It catches the light,” said the cousin. “The moon. Or the fires on the island. The brass catches it. And when the mast moves in the sea, it moves.” He shrugged. “It isn’t lit. It never has been. It looks lit.”

Riarhin looked at the lump of brass on the broken mast.

“Where does it look like it is?” she said. “From the far side. At night. If you’re in a boat, waiting, looking for the way in.”

The cousin did not answer for a moment.

Then he said, slowly: “It looks like it’s at the mouth of the cut.”

The boat came through at the top of the tide.

They saw it first as a dark speck on the far side of the reef, in the grey, coming round the point from the north under oars. It came slowly. It came toward the race, toward the white water, and then turned, and Riarhin, on her rock, felt her breath stop. It turned toward the reef. Toward the broken mast with the brass lump on it. Toward where the cut looked as if it was, from the far side, at night, if you had been taught all your life to come in on the light.

“No,” said Dagny’s cousin. He was on his feet. “No, Ivar, left, left—”

They could not hear him.

Riarhin was up. She did not remember getting up. She was on the edge of the rocks at the inside end of the cut with the coil of line in her left hand, and the boat was coming on toward the reef, and she could see the three men in it now, small, two at the oars and one in the stern, and the one in the stern was looking at the broken mast.

She did not shout. Shouting would not reach them over the race. She took off her coat.

It was the only thing she had. She took it off one-handed, the way she had taken it off on the shingle under the forest, and the wind took it and nearly had it, and she held on. She did not have a pole. She held it up in her good hand, high, and waved it. Slowly. From side to side. The way you wave a flag across a valley. Not at the mast. Here. At the inside end of the cut, on the rocks, where the dark water was calm.

The man in the stern saw it.

She saw him see it. She saw his head turn, from the mast on the reef to the rocks under the cliff, to the dark flapping thing on the end of somebody’s arm, and stay. She saw him stand up in the stern. She saw him look from the mast to the coat and back. And she saw the boat turn.

It came round, slowly, the oars working, away from the reef, away from the broken mast, and into the mouth of the cut.

It came through.

It came through the cut on the top of the tide between the reef and the cliff with the race roaring on the far side, three men pulling and the steersman standing, and the line went out from the rocks and they caught it, and Dagny’s cousins brought them in to the foot of the cliff, and the boat grounded on the weed, and the man in the stern climbed out of it onto the rocks and stood, and looked at Riarhin with her coat in her hand.

“That was you,” he said.

She nodded.

He looked back at the reef, at the broken mast with the brass on it, catching the grey light. He looked at it for a long time.

“I’d have gone for the light,” he said. “My mother said never follow it. I’d have gone anyway. I thought it was the cut.”

“It looks like the cut,” said Riarhin. “From out there.”

Ivar looked at her. Then he sat down on the rocks, suddenly, as if his legs had gone, and put his face in his hands.

Dagny did not thank her.

Riarhin had not expected her to. She came down to the landing at dusk when they brought Ivar round the Ness in his own boat with the meal from the far side, and she looked at her son, and took him by the shoulders, and held him, hard, for a long time, without saying anything. Then she let him go, and looked at Riarhin.

“Leif says you lost a day,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Thirty days to the ice and you lost one.”

“Yes.”

Dagny looked at her a while longer with her shut-box face.

“You’ll want the station boat,” she said. “To round the Ness. Leif’s won’t do it with a sea running, not loaded. Mine will.” She paused. “Ivar will take you. To the sea-landing below Skarth.” She looked at the empty store through the open door. “I can give you the boat. I can’t give you meal. I gave it away at the end of the winter because of a verse.”

“I know.”

“That doesn’t mean I believe you,” said Dagny. “About what’s in the ice. About what you’re going for.” She folded her arms. “It means my son came through the cut.”

“I know,” said Riarhin.

The pilot came to the landing in the morning. He was Dagny’s brother, a small dark weathered man with a ring in one ear, and he had taken boats round the Ness for forty years, and he stood on the landing and looked at the station boat with their frames and their sleds lashed into it, and at the five of them, and he did not get in.

“What are you carrying?” he said.

Riarhin looked at him. She thought of Skeggi in his doorway at the head of the lake.

“Food for thirty days,” she said. “Rope. Fuel. A woman in my keeping, under the stone, who knows the road north. An Ykrul with a roll of birch-bark with his family’s mark on it. A cooper who speaks the old talk.” She paused. “A copy of a thing that has a hole in it where the end should be. A sword I killed something with that I said was the Mother of the ice, and it wasn’t.” She looked at him. “And me. I said it was dead. I’m going back to see what’s true.”

The pilot looked at her for a long time.

“Ranveig sang that,” he said. “The second one.”

“Yes.”

“I liked the first one better,” said the pilot.

He got into the boat.

Chapter 35 — The people she can guide

Ingrith’s man was waiting at the sea-landing below Skarth with a pennon and a decision.

His name was Halldor. He was a grey square-shouldered man from Ottarshall who had been Ingrith’s steward before the hall burned, and he stood on the ice at the landing as Ivar brought the station boat in, with the long blue banner with the white ship on it furled on a pole beside him, and he did not wait for them to come ashore before he spoke.

“She won’t call the muster,” he said.

Riarhin looked at him from the bow.

“She sat up two nights with it,” said Halldor. “The jarls who stayed want it. The western bays want fire. Hauk wants it, and his cousins.” He leaned on the pole. “She says a muster would sit at the foot of the ice and eat the coast’s last meal and freeze, and the four would still be behind the screen. She says she won’t send two hundred men north to stand in the snow because they can’t stand to do nothing at home.” He looked at the boat. “She says she’ll do something else.”

“What?”

“Keep the road,” said Halldor. “Yours. Her men at Skarth. Her men at Dalsendi, with Yrsa’s sister. Food in both, what she can spare. Sleds. So that when you come back, if you come back, with four people who can’t walk, there’s somebody on the road with a fire.” He lifted the pole. “And me, with this, as far as the stone. So that anybody between here and the stone knows Ingrith of Ottarshall says you go through.”

Riarhin looked at the furled banner.

“And after the stone?” she said.

Halldor looked north, up the river, into the grey.

“After the stone, nobody knows her,” he said.

The fog came down on the second day out of Skarth, on the river road, and stayed.

It came up the valley from the sea in the night, and in the morning it was everywhere: thick and white, so close that Riarhin could not see the front of the sled she was hauling, so close that when Bródd walked ten paces ahead of her he became a grey shape and then nothing. Sound went strangely in it. A dog barked, behind them, and seemed to be in front. Somebody’s boots on the river ice seemed to be overhead.

Ingrith’s guide stopped them.

Her name was Sæunn, and Halldor had brought her from Skarth: a lean dark woman of fifty with a long face and very pale grey eyes, who had grown up on the river and run boats on it in summer and sleds on it in winter all her life, and who had been taking people up the lower road from the sea-landing to the lake in fog since she was a girl. She stopped them at the first bend above Skarth, when the fog came in, and made them rope together, as Sifa had on the frozen river in the north.

“I can take you in this,” she said. “To the lake. I know every bend. I don’t need to see it.” She looked at them with her pale eyes. “You need to do what I say, when I say it. Even if it doesn’t sound right.”

“We’ve done that before,” said Riarhin.

Sæunn looked at Sifa, roped behind her.

“I heard,” she said.

She took them up the river road in the fog for three days.

She did not look. She listened. She walked at the front of the line with her head a little on one side, the way Skeggi had walked with his ruined ear turned forward, and she stopped every few hundred paces and stood still with her eyes half-closed. Listening to the river under the ice, Riarhin thought. To the wind in the birch on the banks. To the sound their own sleds made on the ice and the way it came back. Then she would turn, and go on, and the river would bend, and she would bend with it, and they would follow the rope.

She said very little. At night, by the fire, she sat apart and mended things.

On the third afternoon, in the thickest of it, a horn sounded.

It came from ahead. Far ahead, up the river, in the fog: a long low note, rising, holding, falling away. Riarhin heard it and stopped, and the rope went taut ahead and behind her as the others stopped too.

It sounded again.

“That’s Dalsendi,” said Halldor. He was behind Riarhin, with the pennon. His voice was glad. “That’s the lake horn. They blow it in fog for the sleds coming up. Ingrith’s men. They’ve heard us.” He turned his head toward the sound. “That way. Straight up.”

Sæunn had stopped at the front of the line. She was standing very still, with her head on one side.

The horn sounded a third time. Long, rising, holding.

“It’s there,” said Halldor. “Straight. We could be there before dark.”

Sæunn turned toward it.

She took a step. Then another. The rope went taut, and the line began to move, following her, toward the horn, up the river, straight.

Sifa stopped.

She was at the back of the line, behind Bródd. She stopped dead and set her feet on the ice and held, and the rope came taut all the way along the line, from her to Sæunn, and Sæunn, at the front, was pulled up short.

“No,” said Sifa.

They turned. In the fog Riarhin could barely see her: a grey shape at the end of the rope with her hand at her jaw.

“That isn’t a lake horn,” said Sifa.

“It’s Dalsendi,” said Halldor.

Sifa did not answer him. She turned her head the way Sæunn turned hers, toward the sound, and stood, listening. The horn came again. Long. Rising. Holding. Falling.

“It’s coming over ice,” said Sifa. “Not down a valley. Listen to it. There’s nothing in it. No trees. No hills. It’s coming over flat ice, a long way.” She touched her jaw. “And it’s too long. A man can’t blow that long. Not three times.”

Nobody spoke.

“The due-roads,” said Sifa. “In the four valleys. Where the sleds go in. There’s a horn. You hear it in fog, on the moor, when you’re hauling, and it sounds like your own hearth calling you home. It’s the Hall.” Her voice was very flat. “You follow it, and you get home. Its home. The road turns under you. My mother said.”

“We’re not in the four valleys,” said Halldor. “We’re on the river road above Skarth.”

“Yes,” said Sifa. “So why can I hear it?”

Sæunn did not argue.

Sæunn stood at the front of the line in the fog and listened to Sifa, and then listened to the horn, a fourth time, long, rising, holding, and her pale eyes moved in her long face, and then she turned away from it.

“I took us toward it,” she said. “I heard Halldor say Dalsendi, and I thought Dalsendi, and I turned.” She looked at the fog. “The river bends left here. Hard left. Round a spit of gravel. I know it. I’ve known it since I was six.” She was quiet a moment. “That horn is straight ahead, over the spit. Over the gravel. There’s no ice on the gravel, it blows clear, and beyond it the river’s deep and the ice is thin where the spring comes up.” She paused. “If we’d gone straight we’d have gone through.”

“It sounded like Dalsendi,” said Halldor. His voice was not glad any more.

“It sounded like where you wanted to be,” said Sæunn.

She went left. Hard left, round the bend, in the fog, with the rope, and they followed her, and the horn sounded once more behind them, over the gravel spit, long, rising, and then not again.

They reached Dalsendi the next day at noon. The fog had lifted. Ingrith’s men were there, with Yrsa’s sister, and a fire, and they had not blown any horn. They had not heard one either.

Above Dalsendi the road followed the shore to the head of the lake and Skeggi’s knoll, then climbed beside the river toward the stone. Sæunn took them as far as the knoll that afternoon.

Skeggi was there. He came out of his hut as he had before and stood in his doorway with his ruined ear turned toward them, and looked at Sæunn, and nodded, as one road-keeper nods to another; and Sæunn stopped at the foot of the knoll and did not go up it.

“That’s mine,” she said. She looked back down the valley, toward the lake and the river and the sea. “Everything that side. I know it in fog. I know it in the dark. I know it with my eyes shut.” She turned and looked up the road, past the knoll, into the hills, where the cairns went up toward the stone and the moor beyond. “I don’t know that.”

“Skeggi knows it,” said Halldor. “To the stone.”

“Skeggi knows it to the stone,” said Sæunn. “And past the stone there’s nobody.” She looked at Sifa. “Except her.”

She took the rope off her own waist, and coiled it, and held it out. Not to Halldor. Not to Riarhin. To Sifa.

Sifa looked at it.

“I can’t take you further,” said Sæunn. “Past here, I’d be guessing. I’d hear a horn and think it was home.” She held the coil out. “I’m not going to pretend I know it. That’s how people go through the ice.”

Sifa took the rope.

Riarhin watched Sæunn give it, and Sifa take it, in front of Halldor with his pennon and Skeggi in his doorway. She had known people who would have gone on past the knoll, guessing, sooner than say I don’t know in front of anybody and turn round. She had been one of them.

“Thank you,” she said to Sæunn.

Sæunn looked at her with her pale eyes.

“For what?” she said. “I nearly took you through the ice at the gravel spit.”

“And then you didn’t,” said Riarhin.

Sæunn considered that for a moment. Then she nodded, once, and turned, and went back down the road toward the lake, alone, without hurrying.

Skeggi led them on from the knoll. They reached the stone on the third day above Dalsendi, with Halldor still carrying the pennon.

He walked beside Riarhin up the last mile of the river road with the pennon on its pole on his shoulder, as Doukas had marched beside them with his eight shields, and when they came to the shoulder of the hill and the stone stood up grey in front of them, leaning, with its cairn beside it, he stopped.

He unfurled the pennon. He did it without saying anything. He took the cord off it and shook it out and let the wind take it, and the long blue banner with the white ship streamed out from the pole over the stone, toward the moor.

Then he planted the pole in the cairn.

“She says it stays here,” he said. “Till you come back. So that anybody who comes up this road knows Ingrith of Ottarshall sent you past it.” He looked at the moor, white and empty and running away north into cloud. “It won’t mean anything out there. She knows that. It means something here.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“She said to tell you,” said Halldor, “that she’ll set the places.” He did not look at her. “At the table. For four. Like she did. Every night. Till you come back with them or come back without them.” He paused. “She said you’d know what that meant.”

Riarhin thought of the boat-shed at Ottarshall in the lamplight, and the four bowls on the clean boards, and nobody sitting at them.

“I know,” she said.

Halldor nodded. He stood by the stone with his hand on the pole, under the streaming blue banner, and did not come any further.

There was somebody on the moor.

Riarhin saw her when she turned from the stone: a figure, out on the white, a long way off, small and dark, standing still. Then another beside it. Then the first one lifted an arm.

“Yrra,” said Sifa.

She went past the stone with Sæunn’s rope in her hand, out onto the moor, toward the figures, and the others went after her, one by one, and Riarhin went last. At the stone she put her hand on it, on the north face, on the worn strokes that Skeggi said were not Linn. Then she went on.

She looked back once. The blue banner was still streaming over the stone. Halldor was still standing under it. Skeggi had stayed behind and was standing beside him. They did not wave. They stood and watched.

Then the snow came between, as it had before, and they were gone, and there was nothing behind her but the moor, and nothing in front of her but Sifa with the rope, and Yrra, coming across the white toward them with her lamp.

Chapter 36 — Those not carried north

The gathering had grown smaller, and colder, and there were slabs of ice standing among the fires.

Riarhin saw them from the ridge, coming down in the afternoon of the eighth day out from the stone. There were ten of them. They stood upright in the snow at the edges of the valley, clear, as tall as a man, one in front of each cluster of shelters, the way the slab had stood in the frozen pool in the hollow under the crag, and the low light went into them and came out blue. Nobody had touched them. Nobody walked near them. The paths between the fires went round them in long curves, the snow trodden grey, and at the edge of each slab the snow was clean.

There had been eleven hearths under Hrogaz’s speakers when she left. She counted the slabs twice. Ten.

“The two streams,” said Yrra, beside her, when she asked. Yrra’s face was thin. “Old Hesk’s hearth. They went home. Three days after the slabs came. Hesk said she’d been to the Hall once and nearly died of it and the Bishop’s man had saved her, and she wasn’t going to give the Hall a second chance at her by standing in its road with a speaker.” Yrra looked at the slabs. “Their slab went with them. In the morning it was gone from here, and they say it was standing at their hearth in the eastern valley when they got there.”

Hrogaz received them in the whalebone shelter, and he was not the man who had offered Riarhin the staff.

He had grown thin. The heavy grey face had fallen in at the cheeks, and the small deep eyes had sunk, and the hands that held the staff across his knees had a tremor in them that had not been there in the winter. He looked at the five of them come in out of the cold, and at Sifa longest, and he did not lay the staff down.

The shelter was half empty. The benches along the sides, which had been full of elders in the winter, had gaps in them. Riarhin counted the speakers. Fourteen, where there had been twenty. Orra was there, at the end of the left bench, with her grandson. Vekk was not.

Hrogaz spoke. Bródd said it after him.

He told them about the slabs. He told it plainly. The night the stream at the foot of the ice stopped running, the hearths had woken to find their pits frozen to stone and a slab standing in front of each hearth that had sent a speaker. Vekk had read the first. It was what the slab under the crag had been: the hearth named, its winters named, what was not brought named, the names of everybody under its elder’s hand, in the old long way. And at the bottom, the date of the collection. The next dark of the moon. Not the dark of the winter now. The winter was over; the Hall had collected the due in kind from the pits. The dark of the next moon, it said, the Hall would collect the rest. Everybody on every slab.

“Eleven days,” said Bródd. “That’s eleven days from now.”

Nobody spoke.

“He says,” said Bródd, as Hrogaz went on, “the hearths are not what they were. The two streams went home. Taking Hesk, and their speaker, and their claim. They’re out of it.” He listened. “He says the hearth of the long ice is gone too. Not home. Their shelter came down. The night the slabs came. The snow on the ridge over them slid and took their shelters down with everybody in them. They dug out. Three dead.” He stopped. “He says they came to him the next morning. Their elder. An old man. He says the old man stood in front of the staff and said, I don’t have anywhere to go now except north. He says put me on the road.”

Hrogaz said something else, short.

“He says the long ice is in it,” said Bródd. “With a new speaker. The old man. Haak. He wasn’t in it before. He is now.”

He stated the mandate.

He did it standing up. He laid the staff down on the furs and stood in front of it with his hands at his sides, the way he had stood the night he offered it to her, and said it to the whole shelter, the speakers along the benches and the company from the coast standing at the back, and he said it slowly so that Bródd could say it after him word for word.

He said that he spoke for ten hearths. He named them. Orra’s under the crag, and the black crag’s other hearths, and the long ice under its new speaker, and the rest. He said that each hearth had given him its word, through its speaker, in this shelter, before witnesses, to speak for it at the Hall. He said that he did not speak for the two streams, which had gone home. He did not speak for the high valley, which had never sent. He did not speak for any hearth that was not on his list, whatever the Hall said, whatever the Word-Bearer said, whatever happened at the Hall.

He said it twice. He named the ten twice.

Riarhin listened to it. She listened to every name, and kept them. And while she listened she counted days. Eleven to the dark of the moon. From here to the foot of the ice was nine, on the due-road, if nothing went wrong. Two days’ margin. Two days, to go in, and find the Oath, and find the four, and find whatever was behind the screen.

When Hrogaz had finished, she spoke.

“We should go tomorrow,” she said. “All of us. The speakers. At first light. If we go at first light we have two days at the foot of the ice before the dark of the moon.” She looked at Hrogaz. “Two days isn’t enough. But it’s two days.”

Bródd said it. Hrogaz looked at her.

Sifa spoke before he could answer.

She did not stand up. She was sitting at the end of the right-hand bench, beside Orra, with her hands in her sleeves, and she spoke in the throat-speech, short, to Hrogaz, and then she turned and looked at Riarhin and said it again in the northern speech.

“The two streams,” said Sifa. “Hesk’s hearth. You heard him. They went home.”

“I heard.”

“Their names are still on their slab. At their hearth.” Sifa did not look away. “The Hall will collect them too. At the dark of the moon. Everybody under Hesk.” She paused. “Hrogaz doesn’t speak for them. They went home. They took their word back.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“If we hurry,” said Sifa, “and go tomorrow, and get to the Hall, and Hrogaz stands up in front of it and says ten hearths, and he says and the two streams too, and the high valley, because they’ll die if I don’t—” She stopped. “He can’t. They didn’t give it. If he says it, he’s saying it for people who took it back. That’s what the wardens did. In the old winter. Spoke for people who weren’t there.” Her jaw moved. “I won’t walk beside that.”

“He just said he wouldn’t,” said Riarhin. “He named the ten.”

“He named the ten in here,” said Sifa. “Where it’s warm. Where there are two days.” She looked at Hrogaz. “In the Hall, he’ll be standing in front of her. With the two streams on a slab in the eastern valley. With the high valley up past the black ridge with the old man with the bad legs.” She looked back at Riarhin. “You want to hurry. So does he. When you hurry you say things for people.” She paused. “You did. He told me. He’s not refusing you.”

Riarhin did not answer. She remembered it: Temür by Jorunn’s fire with the bowl in his hands, and herself in the corner touching her throat twice, it’s a courtesy. Don’t answer for me.

“I know,” she said at last.

“Then know it in the Hall,” said Sifa.

They did not go at first light. They went at noon, after Vekk had read.

He read in his own shelter at the western end of the gathering, as before. But he did not read to four people this time. He read to eleven. He had asked for them by name, through the boy with the dog-halter: Hrogaz, and four of the speakers, and Orra, and Yrra who would guide them, and the company from the coast, Riarhin and Temür, and Bródd for the coast-talk, and Sifa. They sat round the lamp in the small hide shelter with their knees touching. There was not room. They made room.

He did not read the oath. He read the stave of the first giving, the twenty-two households, again, all of them, line by line. He read the fifth stave of the clan copy, the one that said the sworn could bring a wrong. He read the place on the eighth that might say full. And at the end of each, he stopped, and said in his own voice what he did not know. Bródd said it after him every time.

I don’t know what happened after given.

I don’t know if the Hall keeps what it took, or what it does with it.

I don’t know if this word is full. I think it is. I would not swear to it.

I don’t know what the ninth stave says. Nobody does. If anybody tells you they know, at the Hall, they are lying, or they have read it there.

He made them say it back. All eleven. Each in turn, round the lamp, in whatever speech they had: what the staves said, and what he did not know. He listened to each of them, with his milky eyes on their faces, and corrected two of them, and made one say it again.

Then he gave Temür the birch-bark.

It was a second roll, beside the one Temür had copied. Vekk had made it himself, in the days since they had gone south, by lamplight, in a cramped old hand: the eleventh line of the stave of the first giving, the mark and the name and given, copied stroke by stroke. And under it, in a different set of strokes that Riarhin had not seen before, more marks.

“He says that’s what he can’t establish,” said Bródd. “Written down. In the old way. So that it goes with the marks.” He listened. “This is the household of the mother of Alaq, as it was cut by the wardens in the third winter. The wardens cut what they brought. They did not cut what the Hall did after. I, Vekk, who read this, do not know what the Hall did after. Ask the Hall.” Bródd swallowed. “He says carry that. With the marks. So that nobody at the Hall can say the four valleys know more than they do. Or less.”

Temür took it. He looked at the old man for a long time.

“He’s not coming,” said Riarhin.

Bródd asked. Vekk shook his head, slowly, before Bródd had finished.

“He says he’s eighty-one,” said Bródd. “He says the due-road is nine days and the Hall is cold and he would die on the road or at the door, and then what he knows would die with him on the road. He says that’s why he read it to eleven.” Bródd looked at the eleven of them round the lamp. “He says now it isn’t only in his head. He says now he can stay here and die by his own fire like an old man, and it won’t matter.”

He said something else, and laughed, a dry old cough of a laugh, and Bródd did not translate it.

“What did he say?” said Riarhin.

“He said it’s the first winter in forty years he’s going to sleep,” said Bródd.

The speakers agreed what they would ask for on the road, the first night, at the fire. It took most of the night.

There were fourteen of them, and Hrogaz, for ten hearths. They sat round the fire in the lee of a rock on the due-road with the company from the coast a little apart, and argued, and Riarhin listened with Bródd and Temür beside her and followed what she could.

They agreed on the four from the coast. Release. Nobody argued that.

They agreed on the due. That it should stop. That if the words showed it was not owed, they would ask the Hall to stop taking it, and to say so, in the old way, before witnesses, so that it was cut. Nobody argued that either, though Haak, the new speaker for the long ice, said very quietly that he would believe it when he saw a winter pass without a slab.

Then Orra spoke.

She spoke slowly, by the fire, with her grandson asleep against her. She said that the Hall had taken people. Not only the four from the coast. Nine times under Hrogaz, and before that under Ruuk, and before that, back and back. When the stores were short, people went on the sled. Old men. Children. Hrogaz’s own daughter. She said the stave of the first giving said given and stopped, and nobody knew what came after. Perhaps they were behind the screen with the four.

She said: if the due was wrong, the Hall owed them back. All of them. Alive.

The fire was very quiet.

Riarhin watched Hrogaz. He was sitting with the staff across his knees, looking at the fire. His hands were still.

An old woman across the fire spoke. She spoke very low, and Bródd hesitated before he said it.

“She says no,” he said. “She says don’t ask for that.” He listened. “She says her husband went on the sled six winters ago. She says she has buried him. Not his body. Him. In her head. Every night for six winters. She says if they ask the Hall for the given back, alive, and the Hall says they are not alive, she will have to bury him again. And if the Hall says they are alive—” Bródd stopped. “She says she doesn’t know what she would do. She says she can’t. She says don’t ask for it in her name.”

Orra looked at her across the fire.

Nobody spoke for a long time.

Then Hrogaz said something, very low, without lifting his eyes from the fire.

“He says they’ll ask for the living,” said Bródd. “Whoever is living. The four from the coast and anybody else the Hall is holding alive. Released. And the due stopped.” He listened. “He says that’s what they ask for. He says if the Hall has the others, and they’re living, they’re living, and they’ll be released with the four. He says they won’t ask the Hall to say who’s dead.”

“That isn’t enough,” said Orra.

“He says he knows,” said Bródd. “He says it isn’t enough for anybody. He says it’s what they can ask for without breaking each other.” He paused. “He says Gyrd was his daughter. He says he isn’t going to ask the Hall about her by name. He says if he does, it will hear him.”

The old woman across the fire had put her face in her hands.

Orra looked at Hrogaz for a long time. Then she put her hand on her sleeping grandson’s head, and nodded, once, and did not say anything else.

Chapter 37 — The ninth place

The Bishop’s camp was still at the foot of the ice, smaller, colder, and Ilva was waiting at the edge of it with her leather case under her arm, as if she had not moved since the winter.

She had not, very nearly. Riarhin learned that in the first hour. The Bishop had gone back south to Dalsendi in the false spring with half his column, to hear cases and see to the chapel at Hvalgard, and had left the rest at the foot of the ice under Rhone, with the candle burning in its niche by the gap. Ilva had stayed with the baggage. She had stayed through the stream stopping, and the ice growing over Gest’s ladders, and the night the candle in the niche had frozen solid in its own flame. She had gone to the gap every morning and stood there.

“I wrote to the Bishop,” she said. “When the stream stopped. I wrote that the Temple’s copy was incomplete, and that the original was in the ice, and that whatever he had been told about what was dead was not established.” She looked at the wall. “He wrote back that he would come up himself, when the roads allowed. He wrote that I should do nothing until he came.” She did not look at Riarhin. “Rhone read me the letter. Aloud. All of it.”

“And?”

“And the speakers are here,” said Ilva. “And the dark of the moon is the day after tomorrow. And I am the keeper of the Temple’s copy, and the original is behind that wall, and nobody else here can compare them.” She turned her head. “He wrote that I should do nothing. Comparing a text is not nothing. It is my office.”

The Word-Bearer came for them at dusk on the day of the dark of the moon, at the foot of the ice, as he had said.

They were waiting. Hrogaz with the staff, and the fourteen speakers of the ten hearths, in a line in the snow under the great blue-black wall; and behind them the company from the coast, with Yrra and her lamp to show the way in; and Ilva, a little apart, with her case. Rhone had come out of the Bishop’s camp with a dozen soldiers to stand at a distance and watch, and had said nothing, and had not tried to stop Ilva standing where she stood.

The dogs in the Bishop’s camp went quiet.

He came out of the gap in the ice. The white coat, the black stave, the long grave still face. He stood at the mouth of the gap with the dark behind him and bowed, from the waist, to the line of them, and waited.

Hrogaz said the word.

The Word-Bearer spoke. Bródd said it after him in a whisper.

“He says the Hall will hear the speakers. He says come.” Bródd listened. “He says the Hall is not yet risen. He says it will rise when it rises. He says the speakers will wait for it in the Hall, in warmth, as guests.”

“As guests,” said Riarhin.

The Word-Bearer looked at her.

“As guests,” he said, in the northern speech.

Riarhin did not answer. She thought of Yrsa’s door at Hornvik, and Brakka’s hearth, and Hrogaz’s shelter, and every roof she had been under since the Staðfast came in, and of Vekk’s fifth stave, and the word brought. She thought of the Choir.

Hrogaz said something to the Word-Bearer. Short. Bródd said it.

“He says the speakers will come,” said Bródd. “He says they are not guests. He says they are sworn, and they come to be heard. He says the ones who walk beside them are walking beside them. He says nobody eats.”

The Word-Bearer looked at Hrogaz for a long moment with his still face.

Then he bowed, and turned, and went into the gap.

They followed.
