The nave was alive again.

Riarhin had known it would be. Gest had told her. She had not known what it would be like to walk into it again and feel it.

The groaning was back. It was in her teeth before they had gone a hundred paces up the stream-bed in the dark, the long low rising and falling, and it was louder than it had been the first time. The frost was running in the channels. When they came out into the nave, into the great dark under the ribs of ice, she saw it: the pale feathering racing up the walls in pulse after pulse and over the roof and down, toward the middle, faster than before, and the faint blue light of it lit the nave enough to see the pillars.

There was a gap in the left-hand pillars where one had fallen. There was a long scar across the floor where something had dragged itself toward the shaft. At the end of the nave, in the dark, there was the shaft itself, and no bridge.

And the Choir.

The Word-Bearer led them past it. Riarhin saw the speakers see it. She saw the old ones look away and the young ones look and not be able to stop, and she saw Orra, with her hand on her grandson’s shoulder, turn the boy’s face into her coat. The nineteen stood in their half-ring with their mouths open and their eyes white and the girl at the end with her breath hanging in front of her. Nothing had changed. Riarhin had not expected it to.

Sifa did not look at the ninth.

The Word-Bearer stopped at the far side of the Choir and turned.

He spoke. Bródd said it.

“He says the speakers will wait. Below. In warmth.” Bródd listened. “He says there is a way down. He says it will take them where the Hall keeps what it is keeping, and they can wait there till it rises.” He paused. “He says where it keeps what it is keeping.”

“The four,” said Temür.

“He says,” said Bródd, “the one who reads may compare.”

They looked at him.

“He says—” Bródd listened again. “He says the Temple’s reader asked to compare the oath. He says the Hall heard. He says the Hall does not refuse a reader the words. He says she may go to where the oath is cut and read it, and then come down to the others.” Bródd swallowed. “He says it’s there. Behind the slab. Through the Choir.”

Ilva had gone very white.

“With the warden,” said Riarhin. “Not alone. The warden goes with her. And me.” She looked at the Word-Bearer. “And the cooper, for the words.”

The Word-Bearer looked at her for a long moment. Then he bowed.

The Oath-Hall was a side chamber behind the slab at the centre of the Choir, and you went into it between the frozen figures.

There was no other way. Riarhin saw that when the Word-Bearer showed them: behind the upright slab with its strokes, in the wall of the nave, there was an opening, a low arch of ice, dark. To reach it you went through the half-ring, past the nineteen, between them, close enough to touch. The Word-Bearer did not go. He stood outside the ring and waited.

Hrogaz went first, with the staff. He went between the old man with his hands lifted and the woman bent forward with her mouth open, and did not look at either of them, and went round the slab, and stooped, and went in under the arch.

Ilva went second. Riarhin saw her stop at the edge of the ring. Saw her look at the figures, at their mouths, at the slab with the line on it she had been going to read. Then saw her press her lips together, hard, and go between them.

Riarhin went third. Bródd last.

It was a ring.

It was round. A round chamber, not large, its walls smooth and blue, its roof a dome of ice that caught the light of Hrogaz’s lamp and threw it back. And standing round the edge of the floor, in a circle, a little apart from each other, were ten slabs.

Unlike the slab in the Choir, or the slab in the hollow under the crag, they were cloudy: river-ice, grey and white and veined, as if they had been cut from a frozen river a very long time ago and carried here and stood up, and they were each as tall as a man and as wide as a door, and every one of them was covered from top to bottom on its inner face with strokes. Old strokes. Deep. Worn, at the edges, as if by four hundred years of the cold breathing on them.

Ilva stood in the middle of the ring and turned round slowly, looking at them.

Under her sleeve, Riarhin saw, her left hand was moving. The fingers touching the thumb. One. Two. Three.

“Ten,” said Ilva.

She did not say it loudly. She said it the way a person says a thing they have known for twenty-two years and never seen.

She read them with Hrogaz.

It took a long time. Riarhin did not know how long. She stood at the edge of the ring with Bródd, out of the way, and watched them go from slab to slab: Ilva with the leather case open on the ice at her feet and the vellum unrolled in it and Hrogaz’s lamp held up to the strokes, Hrogaz beside her with the staff, reading. He could read some of it. Not as Vekk could. He read slowly, with his finger, and his lips moved, and where he stopped Ilva put her finger on the same strokes on the vellum and read the scribe’s rendering, and they compared, and Bródd said it all into the northern speech, low, for Riarhin.

The first stave. The second. The third. They matched. Ilva said so at each one, in a whisper, the same, the same, with her finger on the vellum.

At the fifth stave Hrogaz stopped.

He put his finger on a word in the middle of a line and said it, and Ilva looked at the vellum, at the same place, and read the scribe’s rendering. And Hrogaz shook his head.

“He says that’s wrong,” said Bródd. “The scribe’s word. He says the scribe wrote winters. He says the word on the stave isn’t winters.” Bródd listened. “He says it’s the old word. It means the ones who are counted. Heads. When you count a hearth. Not the winters. The heads.” He paused. “He says the word changed. He says now we use it for winters, because we count winters, but in the old speech it was heads. Vekk told him. He says Vekk said the scribe would have had it from people who used it the new way.”

Ilva looked at the vellum for a long time.

Then she took a pen out of her case, and a small pot of ink, and in the margin of the vellum beside the scribe’s word, very small, she wrote something.

“What did you write?” said Riarhin.

“Heads,” said Ilva. “Per the warden of the Hrafn-Grímr, reading from the original, at the Hall.” She did not look up. “And the date.”

The eighth stave was the one with the name in it.

Riarhin knew it before Ilva said so. She saw Hrogaz reach it and stop, and stand in front of it with the lamp, and not read. She saw him look at a place in the middle of it. She saw Ilva look at the vellum and then at the same place on the slab, and then at Hrogaz.

“Her name is a word of this oath,” said Ilva, very quietly, from the vellum. “And it shall be spoken by the sworn and by none else, for whoso speaks it swears, and whoso swears what he may not keep is forsworn in the same breath, and the winter takes the forsworn.”

Hrogaz said something.

“He says it’s the same,” said Bródd.

Ilva looked at the stave for a long time. Then at Hrogaz.

“You can say it,” she said. “The name. The wardens can.”

Bródd asked. Hrogaz answered, short.

“He says yes,” said Bródd. “He says he’s said it every winter. When he sends the sled. He says you say it to the Hall when you send. He says he’s sworn. He holds the stave. He says it’s his to say.” Bródd paused. “He says the ones in the Choir weren’t sworn. They had no stave. They came to say sorry, and you say the name when you say sorry, and they said it.” He swallowed. “He says now he knows why.”

Ilva closed her eyes.

“Whoso speaks it swears,” she said. “It isn’t a curse. It’s the oath. To say the name is to swear the oath. If you’re already sworn, you’re only swearing what you’ve sworn. If you’re not—” She opened her eyes and looked at the slab. “You’ve sworn a thing you can’t keep, in front of the one witness that never lets a word go. And the winter takes the forsworn.” She was silent. “Nineteen people.”

Nobody said anything.

Then Hrogaz moved to the next slab.

It was there.

The ninth. Standing in its place in the ring, between the eighth and the tenth, grey river-ice like the rest, cut from top to bottom. Riarhin saw Ilva see it. She saw Ilva stand in front of it with the vellum in her hands and look at the place on the vellum where the eighth ended and the tenth began on the same line, and then up at the slab, and her face did something Riarhin had never seen it do. It was not quite weeping.

Hrogaz read it.

It was short. Shorter than the others. He read it slowly, with his finger, all the way down, and his lips moved, and his voice was low and rough, and when he had finished he stood in front of it with his hand flat on the ice and did not move.

Bródd had stopped saying it after him halfway through. He stood beside Riarhin with his mouth open.

“Bródd,” said Riarhin.

He shut his mouth. He swallowed. Then he said it.

“The ninth stave,” he said. “And when the count is full, this oath is ended. And the count is full when the sworn have given through nine of their generations, or when the heads of the sworn are fewer than four thousand, whichever falls first. And when the count is full, nothing more is owed, by any, to any; and what is taken after is taken against the words; and the wrong of it is the Hall’s.”

The chamber was very quiet. The groaning came through the walls, low, rising and falling.

“When the count is full,” said Bródd. His voice cracked. “My grandmother. That’s what she said. Every time.”

Ilva was writing. She had the vellum on her knee on the ice and she was writing in the space between the eighth stave and the tenth, the space that had been empty for four hundred years, in her small precise hand, very fast, with the pen shaking.

“Nine generations,” said Riarhin.

They looked at her.

“How long is that?” she said. “Here. For them. How long since the oath?”

Hrogaz answered. Bródd said it.

“He says the oath is older than the songs. He says the stave of the first giving says the third winter of the oath, and that was when they took the riders, and that was four hundred years ago, Vekk says. Nearly.” Bródd listened. “He says nine generations is—he doesn’t know. Two hundred and fifty winters. Three hundred. He says it doesn’t matter. He says it’s past. Long past.” He stopped. “He says the wardens never knew it was in there. He says nobody told them. He says the clan copy is ruined at the eighth and nobody had the ninth.”

“And the heads,” said Riarhin. “Fewer than four thousand.”

Hrogaz looked at her. Then he looked down at the staff in his hand.

He turned it. He turned it in his hands, the black staff with the strokes spiralling up it from foot to head, and lifted the head of it toward the lamp. Near the top, below the knob, there was a band where the strokes were different. Riarhin had seen it in the gathering and not understood it. Short groups of marks, one after another, round the staff, each set apart by a deep ring cut in the wood.

Hrogaz put his finger on the first group. He spoke.

“He says when a warden takes the stave,” said Bródd slowly, “he counts. Everybody. Every head, under every elder, in every hearth of the oath. All the sworn. He goes round them all in his first winter and counts them, and he cuts it here.” Bródd listened. “He says it’s the custom. He says nobody knows why. He says his father said you count them when you take it, so the Hall knows who it is owed. That’s all.”

Hrogaz’s finger moved round the staff. Group by group. Ring by ring. He said each one aloud, a number, and Bródd said it after him, and Ilva wrote them in the margin of the vellum as he said them, and Riarhin kept them.

His own. Eleven winters ago. Two thousand eight hundred and forty.

Ruuk’s. Before him. Three thousand one hundred and six.

Before Ruuk. Hrogaz’s finger stopped on it a long time. Three thousand nine hundred and sixty-two.

Before that. Four thousand and eighty-one.

“When?” said Ilva. Her voice was a thread. “The one under four thousand. The first one. When did that warden take the stave?”

Hrogaz counted backward on the staff, ring by ring, under his breath. He said a name. He said how long each had held the stave. He added them, slowly, with his lips moving.

“He says sixty-one winters,” said Bródd. “That warden took the stave sixty-one winters ago and went round every hearth of the oath in his first winter and counted three thousand nine hundred and sixty-two.” He listened. “He says the one before him counted four thousand and eighty-one, twenty-four winters before that.”

Ilva wrote it.

“Sixty-one winters,” she said. She looked up at the ninth slab. “Under four thousand. Cut on the staff. Sixty-one winters ago.” She looked at the numbers on the vellum. “The generations had run out long before that. But this—” She touched it. “This is the heads. Cut on the staff by the wardens themselves. Not by the Hall. By the people who were paying.”

Hrogaz was standing very still.

He spoke. Bródd did not say it at once.

“He says every one of those wardens counted,” said Bródd at last. “Every one. And cut it. And sent the sled. And none of them knew.” He stopped. “He says he’s sent the sled nine times since he cut his. He says two thousand eight hundred and forty, and he sent it nine times.” Bródd’s voice broke. “He says Gyrd.”

The ice cracked.

It came from outside the chamber. From the nave. A long splitting sound, and a slither, and a crash, and the floor of the Oath-Hall jumped under their feet.

Riarhin turned. Through the low arch she could see, beyond the slab of the Choir, the dark of the nave. The frost was racing in the channels. And down the nave, toward the way they had come in, toward the stream-gap and the foot of the ice and the Bishop’s camp, there was a sound she knew. A long slow grinding, like a door being dragged shut over stone.

“The way out,” said Bródd.

The Word-Bearer was standing outside the ring of the Choir, where he had been. He had not moved. He was looking at them through the arch with his still face.

“The others,” said Riarhin. “The speakers. Where are they?”

The Word-Bearer did not answer her. He turned his head and looked down the nave, not toward the way out, the other way. Toward the shaft.

Ilva was rolling up the vellum. Her hands were shaking so hard she could barely do it. She got it into the case and closed the hooks.

“We have it,” she said. “It’s here. It’s written. In my hand, from the original, with the warden.” She looked up at Riarhin. “But it’s evidence. It isn’t a claim. Vekk’s fifth stave says the sworn bring a wrong. Before witnesses. In the Hall. Out loud.” She looked at Hrogaz. “He has to say it. In front of it. With the speakers who sent him. Not in here.”

The grinding came again from the nave. Closer.

Hrogaz picked up the lamp from the ice. He looked once more at the ninth slab, at the strokes on it he had read, at the words nobody in four hundred years had seen. Then he turned and went out through the low arch into the Choir, between the nineteen, with the staff in his hand, toward where the Word-Bearer was waiting and the speakers had gone down, and the others followed him.

Chapter 38 — Warm enough to wait

The way down was a stair cut in the ice beside the shaft, and at the bottom of it was summer.

Riarhin felt it before she saw anything. The Word-Bearer had taken them out of the Choir and along the edge of the nave to the near lip of the shaft, where the bridge had been, and there in the wall, where she had never seen it in the dark the first time, there was a narrow opening and a stair going down. Steep. Spiralling. Cut in the clear ice, with every step worn hollow in the middle. They went down it in single file, Hrogaz first behind the white coat, with the lamp, and the stair turned and turned, and at every turn the air was warmer.

Not warm. At first only less cold. Then she noticed that her breath had stopped showing. Then that the ice of the walls was wet under her hand. Then that she was sweating under her furs.

At the bottom the stair came out into a passage, and the passage came out under an arch, and the arch was the far side of the screen.

She knew it at once. The lattice. She was standing on the other side of it now, looking back through the holes in the dark wood at the dim nave beyond, at the black gap of the shaft, at the red cloth on its fang of ice at the foot of the far wall where Yrra had tied it in the winter. From this side the holes in the screen were small and close and high, and the nave through them was very far away.

On this side there were lamps.

A great many. Small clay lamps, burning seal-fat, set in niches in the walls of a long low room, a hundred of them, more, and every one lit, and the light of them was yellow and soft and steady and the room was warm. Warm the way a room is warm in high summer with the shutters closed: a close, still, heavy warmth that smelled of fat and smoke and something sweet underneath. The walls were not ice here. They were stone, dark, smooth, and the floor was stone, and there were rugs on it.

And things.

Riarhin saw them in the lamplight as she came in under the arch, and could not at first make sense of them. A great brass thing on a table, a nest of rings one inside another, with little coloured balls on rods, very old, green with age. Shelves along one wall, and on the shelves stones: pale blue stones, as big as fists, rough-cut, a row of them, and as she passed them she heard them. A sound so faint it was hardly a sound. A thin high singing, many voices, very far off, as if people were weeping in another room. A jar on a shelf by itself, stoppered with wax, with nothing in it that she could see.

And at the far end of the room, on the rugs, by the largest of the lamps, four people.

Three of them stood up when the white coat came in under the arch. Riarhin saw them stand: quickly, together, the way prisoners stand when the door opens, before they know who it is. Then they saw the others behind him. The lamp. Hrogaz with the staff. The speakers coming in under the arch one by one. Temür. Bródd. Sifa. Riarhin.

Nobody moved.

There was a young woman. Seventeen, perhaps, tall and fair and very thin, with her hair in a single long braid and her face sharp and white. There was an older woman, forty, broad, with heavy hands and grey in her hair, standing with her arm out a little in front of the others, as if to keep them back. There was a boy. Nine, ten. Small and dark, with a pinched face and enormous eyes, half behind the older woman’s skirt.

And there was an old man. He had not stood up. He was sitting on the rugs with his back against the wall and his legs out in front of him, thin, wrong, and a pouch on a thong round his neck, and he was looking at them with very bright eyes.

Riarhin knew them. She had never seen them in her life. She knew them from a bench by a fire at Ottarshall in the snow, and a slab in a frozen pool, and four bowls on a table.

“Svala,” she said. “Gunnvor. Kári. Ulf.”

They stared at her.

“Who are you?” said the young woman.

She told them. She told them in the northern speech, slowly, standing on the rugs in the warm yellow light with the speakers of the ten hearths standing behind her and the Word-Bearer by the arch. She told them who she was and how she had come. She told them Ingrith had sent her, under the stone. She told them Ingrith set four places at her table every night.

The young woman, Svala, listened with her arms folded. When Riarhin had finished she looked past her at Hrogaz, and at the speakers, the blue lines on their jaws, and at Sifa, and her face did not soften at all.

“You came with them,” she said.

“Yes.”

“They took us.” Svala’s voice was very hard. “Out of my father’s house. In the night. They came in through the door and took me out of my bed and roped me.” She looked at Hrogaz. “Him. His people. And you came here with them.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

She did not explain.

“Can we go?” said Svala.

“Not yet.”

“Why not?”

Riarhin looked at the arch. At the Word-Bearer standing in it. At the stair beyond, spiralling up into the cold.

“Because if we try to walk out of here,” she said, “I don’t think it will let us.”

It would not.

She went to look. She went while Svala stood with her arms folded and Gunnvor watched her and the boy watched her from behind Gunnvor’s skirt; she went round the room, along the walls, past the lamps and the singing stones and the brass rings, looking for the way out that was not the stair.

There was one. At the far end of the room, behind the four, low in the wall, there was a door. Stone, square, with an iron ring in it. Beyond it, when she put her ear to it, she could hear water. Running. A long way down.

“That goes to the drain,” said Gunnvor. She had come up behind Riarhin. She spoke low, so that the others would not hear. “Where the meltwater goes. Under the shaft. Ulf says. It goes out under the ice to the valley. In the summer.” She looked at the door. “Svala tried it. The second week. She pulled the ring and it wouldn’t move. Then she pulled it again and it moved, a little, and there was ice in the gap, and the ice grew while she watched, and closed it.” She looked at Riarhin. “She tried again every day for a month. It closes. Every time.”

Riarhin put her hand on the iron ring. It was warm, from the room. She did not pull it.

“And the stair?”

“We can go up the stair,” said Gunnvor. “As far as the screen. We’ve been. We stood behind the screen and looked at the nave.” She paused. “We saw you. In the winter. Your lamps. Coming down the nave. We saw the thing come down after you. We saw you fight it.” She looked at the floor. “We saw the bridge go. We stood behind the screen and watched it go.” She was quiet. “And then the lamps went away up the nave, and we stood there till they were gone.”

“I’m sorry,” said Riarhin.

Gunnvor looked at her.

“You couldn’t cross,” she said. “I saw. There was nothing to cross on.” She looked back down the room at the old man sitting against the wall. “Svala says you should have stayed. Found a way. She says she’d have found a way.” She paused. “Svala’s seventeen.”

“I want to go now,” said Svala.

She said it to Hrogaz. She had walked down the room to where he stood with the staff, in the middle of the rugs, and stood in front of him with her arms folded and her chin up, and said it in the northern speech, loudly, so that everybody heard.

“You’re the one who sent them,” she said. “To my father’s house. You’re the warden. Bródd says.” She did not look at Bródd. “Tell it to let us go. You can talk to it. Tell it.”

Hrogaz looked at her.

Bródd said it into the old speech. Hrogaz answered.

“He says he can’t,” said Bródd. “Not yet. He says the Hall hasn’t risen. He says when it rises he’ll stand in front of it and say what’s owed, and one of the things he’ll say is you.” Bródd hesitated. “He says he’s sorry he sent them.”

“I don’t care what he’s sorry for,” said Svala. “I want to go now. Up the stair. Up the nave. Out.” She turned to Riarhin. “You came in. You can go out. Take me.”

“And Ulf?” said Gunnvor.

Svala stopped.

Gunnvor had not moved from where she stood by the old man against the wall. She stood with her heavy arms folded, the same way Svala’s were folded, and looked at the girl.

“He can’t walk,” said Gunnvor. “He can’t go up the stair. Somebody has to carry him. Up a stair cut in ice, and along a nave where the roof comes down, and past that thing with its mouth open.” She looked at Riarhin. “Can you carry him?”

Riarhin looked at the old man’s legs.

“Not up that stair,” she said. “Not alone. Not with my leg.”

“Then you take Svala,” said Gunnvor. “And Kári. And go. And I’ll stay here with him.” She did not raise her voice. “I’m not leaving him in this room by himself. I’ve been in this room five months with him. He’s told me every story he knows twice. He taught Kári to count to a thousand on his stones.” She looked down at the old man. “I’m not leaving him for it to keep.”

“Gunnvor,” said Svala. Her voice had changed.

“Go if you want to,” said Gunnvor. “I won’t think less of you. You’re seventeen. Go.”

Svala stood in the middle of the room with her arms folded. She looked at Gunnvor, and at Ulf, and at the stair beyond the arch, and at Riarhin. Her face was white and set and her eyes were very bright.

She did not go.

She did not say anything either. She turned round and went back to the rugs by the old man and sat down beside him with her knees drawn up, hard, and looked at the wall.

Temür found the stave on the last shelf.

Riarhin did not see him find it. She was at the drain door with her hand on the ring. She heard him make a sound. She turned and saw him at the shelf at the end of the room past the singing stones, with the lamp from a niche held up in one hand, looking at something on the shelf.

She went to him.

It was a case. Bone, long and narrow, yellowed, the size of a quiver, lying on the shelf by itself. Its lid was off. Inside it, on a bed of something soft and grey that might have been felt or might have been ash, there was a stave. Dark wood, flattened on two sides, cut all over with strokes, like Vekk’s. Smaller. Older. The strokes were worn almost to nothing.

At the head of it there was a mark.

Two short upright strokes, side by side, joined at the bottom by a loop.

Temür did not touch it. He stood with the lamp held up and looked at it.

“Bródd,” said Riarhin.

Bródd came. He looked at the stave, and at the mark, and at Temür’s face, and did not say anything. He bent close with the lamp and looked at the strokes, and his lips moved, slowly, the way Vekk’s had.

“I can’t read most of it,” he said. “It’s old. It’s older than Vekk’s.” He put his finger beside a line, not touching it. “That’s a name. The long way. The household of—” He stopped. “It’s the same name. Vekk’s eleventh line. The household of the mother of Alaq by the frozen water.” He moved his finger. “And then names. A lot of names. Long ones. I think—” He stopped again. “I think they go on. Down. After the first ones. I think it’s not only the ones who were taken. I think it’s—” He looked at Temür. “I think it’s the line. After. The ones who came after.”

Temür set the lamp down on the shelf.

He took the bone case in both hands, and lifted it off the shelf, and held it. He looked down at the stave lying in it on its grey bed. He looked at it for a long time. Then he looked at Riarhin.

“It’s ours,” he said. In Ykrul. Very quietly. “All of us. Alaq. Her children. Theirs. Borte’s. Mine.” He looked at the stave. “Borte heard it reading this. In the dream. Name by name. Checking.”

He put his hand flat on the stave.

“I want to break it,” he said.

Ilva stopped him.

She had come down the room without anybody seeing her, and she stood beside Temür and looked at the stave in its case, at the mark, and she did not touch it either.

“Don’t,” she said.

Temür looked at her.

“Not yet.” Ilva’s voice was very low and very steady. “If you break it now, here, before it’s been heard, you’ll have destroyed the only thing that says what it claims. And it will still claim it.” She looked at the stave. “It doesn’t need the wood. It has it in the ice. It has it in itself.” She looked at Temür. “When Hrogaz stands up in front of it, it will say I have the household of the mother of Alaq, and all their line, by the first giving. And you’ll want to say no. And you’ll need to know exactly what it says it has. What words. What names.” She looked at the case. “You can’t argue with a thing you’ve burned.”

Temür said nothing for a long time.

“And after?” he said.

“After,” said Ilva. She hesitated. “After, I’d like to copy it. For the—”

“No,” said Temür.

Ilva stopped.

“Not for your archive,” said Temür. “Not in a case in Thepyrgos with a candle on it. No.” He closed the lid of the bone case, carefully, over the stave. “I’ll carry it. Into the Hall. As what it is. So that when it says I have them, I can hold it up and say this is what you have. A stick in a box.” He held the case against his chest. “And after, I’ll decide.”

Ilva looked at him for a long moment. Then she nodded.

Sifa had gone to the four.

Riarhin saw her from the shelf. She saw Sifa walk down the room in the warm lamplight to where they sat on the rugs, Ulf against the wall and Svala beside him and Gunnvor standing and the boy behind Gunnvor, and stop a little way off, and stand there with her hands in her sleeves.

She spoke to them. In the northern speech, thick, slow. Riarhin could not hear what. She saw Gunnvor listen, and answer, short. She saw Ulf say something, and Sifa answer him.

The boy came out from behind Gunnvor’s skirt.

He came out a step, and looked up at Sifa, at her face, at the blue lines on her jaw and the filed teeth and the grey-brown skin, and Riarhin saw his face change. Saw it go from a child’s curiosity into something else, slowly, and then all at once.

He said something. High and clear, in the northern speech, and in the warm still room everybody heard it.

“You were on the hill.”

Sifa stood still.

“With the sleds,” said Kári. “When they carried me past. I looked back. You were on the hill with the sleds and you looked at me.” His voice shook. “You looked at me and you didn’t do anything.”

Sifa did not answer.

Riarhin watched her not answer. She watched Sifa stand in front of the boy on the rugs, with her hands in her sleeves, and not lift one to her jaw, and not look away, and not say I was on the hill with the sleds, I didn’t go down. She had said it at the stone, and at the slab, and at Vekk’s fire. She did not say it now. She stood in the lamplight and let the boy look at her, and said nothing at all.

The boy looked at her a moment longer. Then he went back behind Gunnvor.

Sifa turned and walked back up the room, past Riarhin, past the shelf with the stones singing on it, to the arch, and stood there with her back to the room, looking up the stair.

Riarhin did not go to her.

Behind her, by the wall, the old man had taken the pouch from round his neck. He had it open in his lap and was taking stones out of it, one by one, small pale pebbles from a beach, and laying them on the rug in a row, and his lips were moving.

Ilva was looking at him.

“Ask him,” said Riarhin quietly. “What he counts.”

Ilva went and knelt by the old man on the rugs, in her dark robe, and asked him.

He looked up at her with his bright eyes. He told her. Riarhin, from the shelf, heard it.

“The Seal-house,” said Ulf. “The old Seal-house’s people. All of us that came down from it. A stone for every one that’s born. Out of the pouch when one dies.” He laid another stone on the rug. “My grandfather’s grandfather started it. He said keep it. He said one day somebody will ask. He said have the number ready when they do.” He looked at Ilva. “Thirty-one. With Kári. With Gunnvor and Svala. With me.” He put his hand on the row. “Nobody ever asked.”

Ilva knelt on the rug and looked at the row of beach stones for a long time.

“I’m asking,” she said.

She waited while he put them back. Then she asked how old he was.

“Seventy-three.”

“Sixty-one winters ago, then. You were twelve. Were you counting with your father?”

“Before that.” His fingers closed round the pouch. “He let me put them in. Never take them out.”

“How many were there that winter?”

“I don’t know which died that year.” He frowned. “I won’t tell you I do.”

“The most, then. In all the years you helped him.”

“Thirty-four. Never more. We had thirty-four before my little sister died.”

Ilva took out her tablet. She wrote down his age first.

Chapter 39 — The mouth that hears

The Hall rose in the night, and the first they knew of it was that the lamps leaned.

Riarhin was awake. She had not slept. She was sitting on the rugs with her back against the warm stone wall between Temür and Bródd, with the speakers lying round the room in their furs, not sleeping either, and the four at the far end by the drain door, and the Word-Bearer standing in the arch as he had stood all night without moving. She was watching the lamps. There was nothing else to watch.

All at once, every flame in every niche leaned the same way.

They leaned toward the end wall of the room. The wall behind the four, beyond the drain door, the one wall of the Warm Room that was not stone but ice: a great smooth sheet of it, grey-blue, dark, filling the end of the room from floor to roof. A hundred little flames in their clay lamps leaned toward it together, as if a door had opened somewhere and drawn the air, and stayed leaning.

The air was moving. Riarhin felt it on her face. Warm air, going past her, toward the ice wall. Going out of the room.

The ice wall cracked.

It cracked from the top to the bottom, down the middle, with a sound like the sky breaking, and the two halves of it moved. They slid apart. Slowly. Grinding. Away from each other, into the walls on either side, and as they opened the warm air poured out between them in a great sigh, and the lamps all leaned after it, and the cold came in.

Beyond the wall there was dark. A great dark. And in the dark, a long way off, something very large moved, and the ice under the floor of the Warm Room groaned.

The Word-Bearer, in the arch, bowed.

It was a hall. Riarhin had stopped being surprised by halls. It was larger than the nave, and rounder, and its roof was so high that Hrogaz’s lamp, when he lifted it, did not find it. Its floor was ice, smooth and dark. Its walls were ridged with channels, as the nave’s were, hundreds of them, and the frost was running in them, but slowly here, very slowly, like the breathing of something asleep, or nearly.

And at the far side of the hall, against the far wall, there was a shape.

She did not see it at first as a shape. She saw it as the wall. A great pale mass rising up out of the floor, piled, folded, white and grey and blue, as high as the masts of three ships, filling the whole far side of the hall. Then the frost ran in the channels and the mass moved with it, very slowly, and she saw that it was coiled. Coil on coil on coil, round something in the middle, round a great heap of something pale and jagged that the coils were wrapped round the way a snowdrift is wrapped round whatever it has buried. She looked at the jagged heap and saw that it was teeth. Ice teeth. Fangs of ice as long as a man, as long as two men, standing up out of the heap at every angle, and the coils wrapped round them and through them and over them, and at the top of the heap, resting on the coils, there was a head.

She knew it. She had seen it painted on a shield in the snow. She had seen it come down the nave like weather, with its hollow eyes and its long scarred face, and put Remorse into its throat.

It was not that.

It was the same shape. The same long narrow head, the same coils. And it was scarred. Every inch of it was scarred, crossed and recrossed with old deep lines like writing. But the skin she had put her sword into in the nave had been grey and wet and blind. This was white. Clean white, like new snow under a moon, and the scars on it were blue, and where the hollow eyes had been on the thing in the nave there were eyes.

They opened.

They were very pale. They were the pale grey-blue of deep ice, and they were enormous, each one as large as a shield, and they opened slowly, and looked out across the dark hall at the little huddle of people standing in the opening of the Warm Room with their lamps.

The voice came from everywhere.

“Oh,” it said. “Visitors.”

It was a low voice. A woman’s voice, deep, warm, amused. It did not echo. It came out of the walls and the floor and the roof all at once, as the groaning had, and it was not loud, and Riarhin felt it in her chest.

“And so many,” it said. “All at once. After so long.” The great head lifted, very slightly, off the coils. “Come in. Come in out of the doorway, little ones, you’re letting the warm out. Come and stand where I can see you.”

Nobody moved.

The voice laughed. Somewhere very far up in the dark of the roof, the ice cracked.

“Hrogaz,” it said. “Come. You know the way. You’ve sent it often enough.”

Hrogaz walked out into the hall.

He went slowly, with the staff in his hand and the lamp in the other, out across the dark ice toward the coils, and stopped a long way short of them, and stood. The speakers came after him. All fourteen, in a line, the old and the young, Orra with her hand on her grandson’s shoulder, Haak of the long ice. Then the company. Riarhin went out with them onto the ice, with Temür on one side of her with the bone case in his arms and Bródd on the other, and Sifa behind. Ilva came last with her leather case.

Behind them, in the Warm Room, the four stayed by the drain door.

“There,” said the voice. “That’s better.” The pale eyes moved over them. Slowly. One face after another. “Now. Who speaks for whom?”

Hrogaz spoke.

He spoke in the old speech, and his voice was rough and low and it shook, and Bródd said it after him, very quietly, for Riarhin and the company. He said he was the warden of the Hrafn-Grímr oath. He said he spoke for ten hearths, and he named them, all ten, in the old long way. He said he did not speak for the two streams, nor the high valley, nor any hearth not named. He said he came to the Hall sworn, with the stave, to say a wrong.

“A wrong,” said the voice. It sounded delighted. “How long it has been since anybody came to say a wrong. Go on.”

Hrogaz went on. He began, as Vekk had told him to, at the beginning: with the oath, and the first giving. He said that in the third winter of the oath the wardens of the Hrafn-Grímr had brought to the Hall households of the riders from the frozen water, as the first giving, and that—

Sifa spoke.

She did not ask. She stepped out of the line, past Riarhin, and spoke across Hrogaz, in the throat-speech, thick and broken and loud, and Hrogaz stopped.

Bródd said it.

“She says brought is wrong,” said Bródd. “She says you said brought, as if they were carried, as if they were goods. She says say it as Vekk read it.” He swallowed. “Took. Cut a mark on their doors in the night and took them and roped them. She says say it. She says if you say brought to her, she’ll hear given. She’ll hear that they were ours to give.” Bródd’s voice shook. “She says they weren’t ours to give.”

The great pale eyes had moved to Sifa.

“Oh,” said the voice softly. “The one who cut her tongue.”

Sifa stood very still.

“I remember you,” said the voice. “You were here. With your brother. He was saying sorry so beautifully.” The eyes did not move from her. “You cut it so you couldn’t say my name. Even by accident. Even if you wanted to.” It laughed, low. “Do you want to?”

Sifa did not answer.

Hrogaz said it again. He said it the way Sifa had said it. Took. Cut a mark on their doors in the night and took them and roped them. He said it slowly, every word, in the old speech, to the great head on the coils.

“Yes,” said the voice. “They did. And brought them. And gave them. And I took them.” The eyes moved back to Hrogaz. “All of that. Every word. Go on.”

She did not let him go on.

She let him say perhaps three more lines, about the oath, and the due, and the winters. Then the pale eyes moved. They left Hrogaz and travelled along the line of people standing on the dark ice, slowly, from face to face, and stopped on Riarhin.

“You,” said the voice.

Riarhin looked back at it.

“You’re not from here,” said the voice. “You’re not from anywhere. That’s what you told the man who reads his papers aloud. Of nowhere.” It sounded amused. “That’s not true, you know.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“Half,” said the voice. “You told the steersman on the shingle. Half. He took the half that wasn’t.” The eyes were very close now, though the head had not moved; Riarhin could not have said how. “I know the half that was. I know whose it was. Your grandmother’s, that you put a sword into under a hill. And before her. And before.” A pause. “And I know the other half, little candle. The half you don’t. I know whose.”

The hall was very quiet.

“I could tell you,” said the voice. “I’d like to. It’s a long telling. You’d want to sit down for it.” The eyes did not blink. “It would cost you hardly anything.”

Riarhin heard her own heart.

She thought of a great many things, in the space of a breath. She thought of things she did not know about herself and had not let herself want to know for a long time. She thought of a face she had never seen.

“What would it cost?” she said.

“Hardly anything,” said the voice. “A little warmth. You have so much.”

“How much?”

The voice laughed. The ice cracked in the roof.

“You’d have to sit with me,” it said, “while I told it.”

“How long?”

“As long as it takes to tell.”

Riarhin looked at the pale eyes.

“No,” she said.

The voice did not seem surprised.

“No?” it said gently.

“You haven’t said what it costs,” said Riarhin. “You’ve said hardly anything and a little and as long as it takes.” She did not look away. “I don’t take a thing when I don’t know what I’m paying for it. Not from you.” She paused. “I came to walk beside the speakers. That’s all I came for. That’s all I undertook.”

The eyes looked at her a moment longer.

“Pity,” said the voice. “It really is worth hearing.”

The eyes moved away.

They moved to Hrogaz.

“Warden,” said the voice. “Come here. Closer. I want to show you something.”

Hrogaz did not move.

“Oh, come,” said the voice. “It won’t hurt you. You’re sworn. You know that. You’ve said my name nine times sending the sled, and you’re still standing.” The coils shifted, very slightly. “Look. Look there.”

The frost ran in the channels. And on the far side of the hall, to the left of the coils, the dark changed.

It was a wall of ice, like the one that had opened from the Warm Room. Grey-blue, smooth. As Riarhin watched, it began to clear. It went from grey to blue to pale, like a window that has been frosted and is breathed on, clearing from the middle outward, until it was as clear as the ice of the Choir, and she could see through it.

There was a room beyond it. Small. Lit. Warm, like the Warm Room, with rugs on the floor and a lamp.

And in the room, sitting on the rugs with her knees drawn up and her hands in her lap, there was a woman.

She was young. Twenty, perhaps. Broad and grey, with the blue lines on her jaw, and her hair in a braid. She was looking at the clear wall. She could see them. Riarhin saw her see them: saw her face change, and her hands come up off her lap, and her mouth open on a word that nobody could hear through the ice.

Hrogaz made a sound.

It was not a word. Riarhin had never heard a man make it. It came out of him from somewhere very low, and his hand opened, and the staff fell out of it onto the ice with a sound like a bone breaking, and he did not pick it up.

“Gyrd,” said the voice. Softly. Kindly. “She’s well. She’s been well all this time. Three winters. Fed and warm. She’s wanted for nothing.” The eyes watched him. “Except you.”

Hrogaz stood on the ice with his hands open and looked at his daughter through the clear wall.

“You could have her,” said the voice. “Now. Tonight. I’d open the wall. She’d walk out of that room and across this floor to you, and you could take her home.” A pause. “Not only her. Your hearth. Your own. Everybody under your own hand. Safe passage home, and no slab at your door, and no sled from your hearth again, ever. I’d swear it. Now. Here. In front of all these. You know I keep what I swear.”

Hrogaz did not move.

“All you have to do,” said the voice, “is take it. For yourself. Settle your own wrong. The fifth stave lets you. You know it does. A hearth may settle its own.” The eyes did not leave him. “Let the others say theirs. They can. I won’t stop them.”

Bródd had stopped translating. He stood beside Riarhin with his mouth open. Riarhin did not need it translated. She had heard Vekk read the fifth stave. She had heard the old woman in the shelter in the four valleys say then he isn’t their speaker any more.

Along the line, the speakers had gone very still.

Haak, the new speaker for the long ice, took half a step forward and stopped. Orra had her hand over her mouth.

Riarhin watched Hrogaz’s face.

She saw him want it. She saw it the way she had seen Ilva want to fill the gap in the vellum, the way she had seen herself want the shape of a dead thing in the nave to be the shape of the end of the winter. It was in his open hands and his eyes on the girl behind the ice. Nine sleds. Three winters. He wanted it so much that his body had begun to move without him, a step, toward the clear wall, his hand lifting.

And if he took it he would walk out of here with Gyrd and his own hearth, safe, sworn, and the ten hearths behind him would have no speaker. Fourteen people on the ice of the Hall, in front of that, arguing about who should speak next. Haak, who had been in it only since the slabs came. Orra, who wanted the dead back. The old woman who could not bear to bury her husband twice. They had argued half a night on the due-road over less than this. They would argue it here.

And the Hall would listen. And laugh. And the ice would crack in the roof.

Sifa spoke.

She did not speak to the Hall. She spoke to Hrogaz. She came out of the line again and stood in front of him, between him and the clear wall, and she spoke in the throat-speech, low, thick, fast, with her filed teeth bared, and her voice cracked and broke and went on.

Bródd said it. He said it in a whisper. He did not soften any of it.

“She says take her,” said Bródd. “She says take her, and go home, and I’ll hate you for it for the rest of my life, and I won’t blame you. She says my brother is in the Choir with his mouth open and I’d take him back if she offered, I’d take him and let the rest of you rot.” Bródd’s voice shook. “She says I’m telling you that so you know I’m not better than you. I’m not asking you because I’m better. She says I’m asking you because you said ten hearths in the shelter where it was warm, and you said them twice, and Orra’s children are on a slab.” He stopped. He swallowed. “She says look at Gyrd. Then look at Tukka. Then decide.”

Hrogaz looked at Sifa.

Then he looked past her, at the clear wall, at his daughter on the rugs with her hands against the ice.

Then he turned his head and looked along the line of speakers to Orra, and the small boy beside her with the dog-halter in his fist.

He stood on the dark ice a long time.

Then he bent, slowly, like an old man, and picked up the staff from where it had fallen.

He held it across his body in both hands. He did not look at the clear wall again. He looked at the great pale eyes on the coils, and he spoke, in the old speech, low and rough, and Bródd said it.

“He says no,” said Bródd.

The voice did not say anything.

“He says he speaks for ten hearths,” said Bródd. “He says his is one of them. He says Gyrd is under his hand, and under the hand of the ten, and he’ll ask for her with the rest, or not at all.” Bródd listened. “He says he’s going to say the wrong now. For all of them. In front of witnesses.”

On the coils, the great white head did not move. The pale eyes looked at Hrogaz. Then, very slowly, they closed, and opened again.

“Oh,” said the voice. “Well. Go on, then.”

The clear wall clouded over.

It went from pale to blue to grey, from the edges inward, the way breath clears from a cold window, and the room behind it and the girl in the room went with it, and it was a wall of ice again.

Hrogaz did not look at it. He began.

Riarhin heard the first words. She heard him say the oath’s name, and his own, and the ten hearths, and the word wrong. And she heard something else, behind her.

Feet.

She turned her head. Behind her, at the opening of the Warm Room, where the ice wall had slid back, the lamps were burning, and the four were by the drain door. They were not alone.

There were people with them. Figures. Four, five. Riarhin had not seen them come. They were tall, in old furs, very old, stiff and grey with frost, with their hoods up, and they moved slowly and did not make any sound except their feet on the stone. One of them had Ulf. It had lifted the old man off the rugs, the way you lift a child, and was holding him. Another had Kári by the arm. Gunnvor was pulling at it, at its furs, and it did not seem to notice her. Svala was backed against the wall with her hands up.

The drain door was open.

It stood open in the wall behind the four, the stone door with the iron ring, that had closed with ice every time Svala tried it. It was open now. Riarhin could hear the water beyond it, loud, rushing, far down. And the figures in the old furs were taking the four toward it.

She looked back at Hrogaz. He had not seen. He was standing on the dark ice with the staff, speaking, his whole body turned toward the great pale head on the coils, and the speakers were behind him in a line, and they had not seen either. The voice was listening to him. The pale eyes were on him. And behind them all, quietly, the four were being taken out of the room by another door.

Riarhin turned and went back toward the Warm Room.

Chapter 40 — The claim spoken

The drain passage went down in a long slope of wet stone, and at the bottom of the slope it split.

Riarhin saw it from the door. She came through the Warm Room past the lamps and the singing stones, as fast as her leg would take her, with Remorse on her back and her hand on Toothpick’s grip without remembering putting it there, and she came to the drain door and stopped in it and looked down.

The passage was lit. Faintly, grey, from somewhere below. It went down from the door in a slope of black stone, wet and shining, and twenty paces down it turned, and at the turning there were two ways. To the left the passage went on down, steeply, into the dark, and from the dark came the sound of water, loud, roaring, much louder than it had been through the door in the night. To the right it went level, a short way, to another door.

The right-hand door was open. It was ice. A slab of clear ice in a frame of stone, swung back. And through it came the grey light. Daylight. Real daylight, thin and cold and pale, and a breath of air that smelled of snow and open sky.

The figures in the old furs were carrying the four toward it.

They went slowly. They did not hurry. The one with Ulf in its arms was at the turning already, walking toward the grey light with the old man held against its chest like a sleeping child. The one with Kári had him by the arm, and Kári was pulling back with his whole weight and his heels skidding on the wet stone and it did not seem to notice. Gunnvor had hold of Kári’s other arm. Svala was behind them, coming down the slope, with her fists in the furs of the third figure, pulling, shouting, and it did not notice her either.

Through the open door at the right, in the grey, Riarhin could see snow. A slope of snow. Sky.

It looked like the way out.

“Svala,” she said.

The girl turned her head.

“Don’t go through it,” said Riarhin. “Any of you. Don’t go through that door.”

“It’s open,” said Svala. Her voice was high and wild. “It’s open, it’s out, look at it—”

“I know. Don’t.”

She went down the slope.

She did not remember thinking it through. She had known, the way she had known on the river ice at the far side of the moor that Garrak was going to take the reach. She had seen a road turn under a man’s feet in the fog on the river below Skarth, toward a horn that sounded like home. She had seen a brass lump on a broken mast catch the light at the mouth of a cut. She had stood in the warm room and listened to Gunnvor say the ice grew while she watched, and closed it. Every day for a month. And now, while the Hall listened to Hrogaz, it was open.

She went down the wet stone past Svala and past the figure with Kári and Gunnvor pulling at him, and she reached the turning ahead of the one carrying Ulf, and she did not try to take the old man from it. She went past it to the right-hand door.

There was a bench.

It was lying across the passage just inside the door, broken. A wooden bench, old, black with damp, like the ones on the rugs in the Warm Room, and one of its legs had snapped and it lay on its side on the stone where somebody had knocked it going through. Riarhin did not stop to think who. She took hold of the open door of ice with her good hand. It was very cold. It was heavy. She pulled.

It moved. It swung toward her on its stone frame, slowly, grinding, and the grey light narrowed, and the breath of snow and sky narrowed with it, and the figure carrying Ulf came round the turning toward the door and stopped, and stood, holding the old man, looking at the closing gap with its frosted face.

She got the door shut. She got the broken bench up off the floor with her left hand and her bad right and her knee, and jammed it between the edge of the door and the stone of the passage wall, wedged, the broken leg against the frame and the seat against the ice, and threw her weight on it.

The door held.

The figure with Ulf stood in front of it. It did not try the door. It stood, with the old man in its arms, and looked at the shut ice, and then turned its frosted face, slowly, toward Riarhin.

She did not draw.

She stood with her shoulder against the wedged bench and looked back at it. It was a person. Had been. She could see the face under the hood now, grey-white, crusted with frost, a man’s face, middle-aged, ordinary. His eyes were white.

“Give him to me,” said Riarhin.

The figure did not move.

“The door’s shut,” said Riarhin. “You can’t take him through it. That was what you were told. Through the door.” She held out her bad arm, her right, open. “Give him to me.”

It stood. Then, slowly, as if it had been waiting for somebody to say exactly that, it held out the old man.

She took him. He was very light. She took him against her chest with her right arm, and the arm screamed from shoulder to elbow, and she held on, and Ulf looked up at her from the crook of it with his bright eyes.

“Thirty-one,” he said, quite clearly.

The water came up the left-hand passage while she was holding him.

She heard it first. The roar from the dark below changed. It got louder, and nearer, and deeper, and the stone under her feet began to tremble. Then she saw it: a black edge of water, foaming, coming up out of the dark of the left-hand way onto the wet stone of the turning, and spreading. It was warm. She felt it on her boots. Warm, like a bath, steaming in the cold air of the passage. It came up over the toes of her boots and over the ankles and kept coming.

“The drain,” said Gunnvor. She had Kári round the waist now; the figure holding his arm had stopped, and stood, and let go. “It’s melting. All the ice down there. It’s melting all at once.”

It was rising. Fast. It was at Riarhin’s shins. She could feel it pull, a current running back down the left-hand way and then surging up again, like the backwash at the jetty at Brimness.

The wedged door shook.

It shook against the bench. The water on the other side of it, on the side of the grey light and the open snow, was rising too, and pushing, and the bench creaked and slid an inch on the wet stone and stopped.

“Up,” said Riarhin. “Gunnvor. Svala. Take Kári. Up the slope. Back to the room.” She shifted Ulf against her chest. “Take him too.”

Gunnvor came and took Ulf. She took him the way the figure had held him, against her chest, as if she had done it a hundred times, and went up the slope with the water foaming round her knees and Kári’s hand in hers. Svala went after her. At the door into the Warm Room she stopped and turned and looked back down the slope at Riarhin with the water at her thighs, braced against a wedged bench against a door of ice with the daylight behind it.

“Come on,” said Svala.

“I can’t,” said Riarhin. “If I leave it, it opens.”

The bench slid another inch.

Then there was somebody beside her, in the water, with his shoulder against the bench, and it stopped sliding.

“You said you’d be between,” said Temür. In Ykrul. He was breathing hard. He had the bone case slung on his back by a thong. “In the nave. I came to see if you were.”

They held it. She did not know how long. The water came up to her waist and stayed there, warm, steaming, pulling at her, and the bench shook against the door, and they leaned on it, the two of them, with their shoulders and their whole weight. The figures in the old furs stood in the water round them and did not move. They stood with their hands at their sides and their frosted faces turned toward the shut door, and waited, as if they would wait in the water until it closed over their heads, and it did not matter to them either way.

And through the drain door, up the slope, through the Warm Room and out the other side into the great dark hall, Riarhin could hear Hrogaz.

He was speaking the ninth stave.

She heard it in pieces, over the roar of the water and the creak of the bench. She heard his voice, low and rough, in the old speech, slow, saying the words he had read off the grey slab in the ring with his finger. She heard Bródd’s voice under his, very faint, saying it in the northern speech. And when the count is full, this oath is ended. She heard the numbers. She heard Hrogaz say them one by one, off his staff, the wardens’ counts, ring by ring. Four thousand and eighty-one. Three thousand nine hundred and sixty-two. Sixty-one winters.

She heard the voice of the Hall laugh.

It came down the slope through the door and through the water and she felt it in the bench under her shoulder: low, warm, amused, enormous. Somewhere above them, very far, ice cracked.

“Three thousand nine hundred and sixty-two,” said the voice. “Counted by whom?”

Hrogaz answered. The warden. In his first winter. Every hearth of the oath.

“Every hearth?” said the voice. It was delighted. “Every head? Every one under every hand?” A pause. “And the house of seals?”

Silence, up there in the hall.

“The line given to the coast,” said the voice. “At the house of seals. In the old time, for the peace. Counted to the hearth of the black crag. Mine. Sworn. Did your warden walk down to the coast in his first winter and count them too?” The voice laughed. “He didn’t, did he. He didn’t know they were there. Nobody in the four valleys has counted them for two hundred years. And they’re sworn.” A pause. “Your count is short, warden.”

Riarhin, in the water, with her shoulder against the bench, closed her eyes.

Then she heard Ilva.

Ilva’s voice was small and thin and it carried. It came down the slope through the Warm Room and the drain door, clear over the roar of the water, the way a single high note carries over a crowd, and it did not shake at all.

“No more than thirty-four,” said Ilva.

Silence.

“The keeper of stones at Ottarshall,” said Ilva, “is of the house of seals by his father. His family has kept its count since his grandfather’s grandfather. A stone for every one born. Out of the pouch when one dies.” Her voice did not rise. “He is seventy-three. When that warden took the staff, he was twelve and already helping his father count. He cannot give you the exact number for that winter. He can swear it never exceeded thirty-four.” A pause. “Use the largest number. Three thousand nine hundred and sixty-two, and thirty-four. Three thousand nine hundred and ninety-six.”

Nobody spoke.

“Which is fewer than four thousand,” said Ilva.

Riarhin, in the warm water, against the bench, against the door, found she was laughing. She did not make any sound. Her whole body shook with it against the wet wood. Beside her, Temür looked at her as if she had lost her mind.

Up in the hall, the voice did not laugh.

“Yes,” it said, after a while. “It is.”

Then it said the other thing.

It said it lightly. Riarhin heard it come down the slope, and she heard Temür go still beside her, his shoulder rigid against the bench.

“And the riders,” said the voice. “The household of the mother of Alaq by the frozen water. And all their line. Taken in the third winter, and given, and mine.” A pause. “The oath has an end. Very well. I have heard it. But what I took under the oath before the end is mine, warden. That was owed. That was given. The line of the mother of Alaq was given to me in the third winter, when nothing was full, and everything that has come of it since is mine.” The voice was very gentle. “I have the stave.”

Temür took his shoulder off the bench.

Riarhin felt it go. She felt the bench slide, and she threw her own weight against it and held it alone, and the door shook, and she looked at him.

He had the bone case off his back. He was holding it up out of the water in both hands, high, and he turned, and he shouted up the slope, through the drain door, through the Warm Room, toward the great dark hall and the thing on the coils. He shouted it in the northern speech, because that was the speech Bródd would say into the old one, and he shouted it so loud that it rang off the stone.

“You have a stick in a box!”

Silence.

“I have it,” shouted Temür. “Here. In the water. Your stave. The household of the mother of Alaq. I have it in my hands.” He shook the case. “It says given. Vekk read it. The wardens cut what they brought. They cut given. They didn’t give anything that was theirs. They went to the frozen water and cut a mark on the doors in the night and took people out of their beds and roped them.” His voice cracked and came back. “Taken isn’t sworn. Nobody of the mother of Alaq swore anything to you. Nobody asked them. The wardens gave you what they stole.” He held the case higher. “And Vekk says they don’t know what you did after. And I don’t either. And I’m not going to ask you, because you’ll tell me, and I’ll have to hear it.”

He was breathing hard.

“That’s what you have,” he shouted. “A stick that says some men stole Alaq’s mother and gave her to you. That’s all it says. It doesn’t say I’m yours. It doesn’t say Borte is. It doesn’t say anybody born since is.” He lowered the case, a little. “It says you were given stolen goods. I’ll carry it home and show it to everyone. A stick in a box.”

Up in the hall, Bródd’s voice, faint, saying it into the old speech. Every word. Riarhin heard him not soften any of them.

The bench slid. Riarhin held it. The water was at her ribs.

Then Temür put his shoulder back against the wood beside hers, and it stopped.

The voice said nothing for a long time.

When it spoke again it did not speak to Temür. It spoke to Hrogaz. And it was very calm.

“The four from the coast,” it said, “are gone already, warden. I let them go. While you were reading. They went out by the summer door into the snow. You can see they aren’t in my warm room.” A pause. “The wrong is undone. You may go home.”

Riarhin, in the water, against the door, went cold to the bone.

She heard Hrogaz hesitate. She heard it in the silence, up there: a man who had just read the ninth stave and the wardens’ counts and heard thirty-one, and had been told the four were free, and could not see them, and did not know.

Ilva’s voice came down the slope again.

“What is to be given back is given back where it is,” said Ilva. She was reading. Riarhin could hear the rustle of the vellum. “The witnesses do not hold it in their eyes. They see that it is done.” A pause. “The fifth stave of the clan copy. Read by Vekk. Witnessed by eleven.” Another pause. “We haven’t seen it done.”

Riarhin opened her mouth and shouted.

She had not shouted since the nave. It came out of her cracked and hoarse and half-drowned by the water and it carried anyway, up the slope and through the drain door and the Warm Room and out into the hall.

“They’re here!”

She heard the speakers turn.

“The four,” she shouted. “They’re here. In the warm room. Gunnvor has the old man. Svala has the boy. The summer door’s shut. I’m holding it.” She swallowed water. “Nobody went out of it. Nobody’s going out of it. They’re here.”

Silence.

Then Hrogaz’s voice, in the hall. Low, rough, and very steady. Bródd under it, faint.

“He says the claim is spoken,” said Bródd. “He says the oath was already full sixty-one winters ago by the wardens’ own count with the house of seals, and every due taken since was taken against the words. He says the four from the coast are wrongly held, and every other living person the Hall holds from the ten hearths. He says the due is ended. He says the wrong is the Hall’s.” A pause. “He says undo it. With your own hand. And not another’s.”

The water in the passage stopped rising.

Riarhin felt it stop. It stood round her ribs, warm, steaming, and did not come up any further.

It did not go down either.

Chapter 41 — The bishop at the threshold

The figures in the old furs turned round in the water and walked back up the slope into the Warm Room, and stood along the wall among the lamps, and did not move again.

Riarhin watched them go. She did not trust it. She stood with her shoulder against the wedged bench and the warm water round her ribs and watched them go up past Temür, past the drain door, into the yellow light, and take their places along the wall like servants at a feast who have been told to wait, and stand there with their frosted faces turned toward nothing.

“Leave the door,” said Ilva’s voice, from up in the hall. “It’s been seen. They’re there. In the room. All of us in the line have seen them through the arch.” A pause. “Come up. Bring them where we can all see them. That’s what the witnesses are for.”

Riarhin looked at the door. At the bench jammed against it. At the thin grey line of daylight at its edge.

She took her shoulder away.

The door did not move.

She came up out of the drain passage with Temür, soaked to the ribs, the warm water running out of her clothes and turning cold as it ran, and she went through the Warm Room past the lamps and the four, and out under the great slid-back wall of ice into the hall.

It was colder. Much colder. The frost in the channels on the walls was running faster than it had been, and the pale coils on the far side of the hall were moving. Very slowly. Shifting, one over another, round the heap of ice teeth, the way a snake shifts when it has been lying still a long time and is deciding.

The great white head lay on the coils. The pale eyes were open. They were looking at the far side of the hall, to the left, where the stair came down from the nave.

There were lights on the stair.

Torches. A great many of them, coming down, and the sound of feet, a great many feet, in step, and armour. And the white banner with the golden candle, lit from below, coming down the last turn of the stair and out onto the ice of the hall.

“Oh,” said the voice. It sounded pleased. “The candle people.”

The Bishop came first.

He came down the stair onto the dark ice with his white hair standing up from his head in the cold and a staff of his own in his hand, a plain wooden one, and he walked out a little way into the hall and stopped, and looked up at the coils, and at the white head, and at the pale eyes.

Riarhin watched his face.

He was afraid. She saw that at once. He was more afraid than she had ever seen anybody, more than Orm on his knees in the snow behind the tents, more than Hrogaz with his hands open in front of his daughter. His hand on the staff was shaking and his lips were moving. Praying, she thought.

He did not go back up the stair.

Behind him the soldiers came down. Forty, fifty, with torches and spears and white shields, filling the foot of the stair. Rhone among them, at the front, with his clean narrow face. And at the end of the first rank, square and silent, with his grey moustache, Captain Doukas.

“Bishop,” said the voice. “How good of you to come so far. In such weather.”

The Bishop did not answer at once. He was looking at the coils.

“I have come,” he said, in the northern speech, and his voice shook and steadied, “with the Temple’s copy of the oath sworn in the north. I am the Bishop of the Temple. The Temple keeps the record of this oath. It has kept it for four hundred years.” He drew breath. “I have come to hear what is owed under it. And to whom.”

The voice laughed. The ice cracked in the roof.

“You’ve come to hold my leash,” it said. “Haven’t you. Little candle. Somebody told you that if you held the words, you held me.” The pale eyes looked down at him, very kindly. “Who told you that, I wonder.”

The Bishop’s hand tightened on his staff.

“Never mind,” said the voice. “I’ll tell you what I’ll give you. Since you’ve come so far.”

It offered him everything.

It did it gently, conversationally, the way it had offered Riarhin her lineage and Hrogaz his daughter. It did not raise its voice. And as it spoke the hall changed round them.

It offered him the road.

Behind the Bishop, at the foot of the stair, the ice of the wall began to move. It drew back. It slid away from itself, grinding, the way the wall of the Warm Room had, and behind it there was a passage. Wide. High enough for a wagon. Its floor was not ice. It was stone, dry, and it went away from the hall, level, toward a pale grey light a long way off. Daylight. And the air that came down it was not cold. It smelled of earth. Of wet earth in spring.

“A thawed road,” said the voice. “From the foot of my ice to the coast. Every winter. Dry. Your column can walk it. Your wagons can roll on it. Your bell can go north on it to every hearth in the four valleys, and nobody will freeze on the way.”

The soldiers at the foot of the stair had turned. Riarhin saw them turn. She saw them look down the dry passage at the grey light, and smell the earth, and she saw their faces.

“And the four from the coast,” said the voice. “There they are, in my warm room. I’ll give them to you. You can carry them out on your dry road and give them back to their jarl yourself.”

The Bishop looked at the Warm Room. At Svala in the opening, with Kári, and Gunnvor holding Ulf.

“And a chapel,” said the voice. “Here. At the foot of my ice. Where your candle burns. Stone, and a bell, and nobody ever to burn it or take it down. I’ll swear to that. Protected. Forever.” A pause. “All you have to do is take the winter-due.”

The Bishop went very still.

“Not as it was,” said the voice. “Gently. As you would. The hearths will come to your chapel, every winter, and give what they give. To you. As penance. Freely. You understand penance. And you’ll bring it to me.” The pale eyes did not blink. “It will be so much kinder than the old way. Nobody will be roped. Nobody will be taken from their beds. They’ll walk up your dry road to your chapel and give, because you tell them it is good for their souls, and you’ll believe it.” A pause. “And the north will be yours. All of it. And the winter will be kind to you.”

The hall was silent.

Riarhin looked at the soldiers. At the dry road behind them. At their faces.

She looked at the Bishop.

Ilva walked out across the ice.

She went past the speakers. Past Hrogaz with his staff. Past the company, past Riarhin, soaked, with the water still running out of her clothes. She went across the dark floor of the hall in her ink-stained robe, small, with the leather case in her arms, toward the Bishop, and the soldiers at the foot of the stair watched her come, and Rhone took a step forward.

She stopped in front of the Bishop. She opened the case. She took out the vellum.

She unrolled it, there, on her own arm, and held it up so that he could see it. Not all of it. The end of the eighth. The space that had been empty for four hundred years. Her own small precise hand in it, very dense, very fast, shaking. And beside it, in the margin, the numbers.

“The ninth stave,” said Ilva. “Copied from the original. Through the Choir. In the ring. With the warden of the Hrafn-Grímr reading it beside me, before witnesses.” Her voice did not shake. “And when the count is full, this oath is ended.” She held it closer. “The count was already full sixty-one winters ago, by the wardens’ own reckoning, cut on their own staff. And the scribe at Thepyrgos left it out.” She looked up at him. “On purpose. On the same line. Without lifting his pen. I have twenty-two years of his hand, Bishop. He didn’t forget.”

The Bishop looked at the vellum.

“That is not the Temple’s copy,” said Rhone.

He had come forward from the stair. He stood at the Bishop’s shoulder and looked at the vellum with his clean narrow face.

“That is the Temple’s copy,” said Rhone, “with something written into it by a woman who was ordered to do nothing until the Bishop came.” He did not raise his voice. “She has falsified it. In front of the thing it binds. She has given that thing a reason to say the oath is ended, and the Temple’s claim with it.” He looked at the Bishop. “Bishop. She has handed it the north.”

“Go and read it,” said Ilva.

Rhone looked at her.

“The original,” said Ilva. “It’s through the Choir. Behind the slab. Ten staves in a ring. The ninth is there. Go and read it.” She held his eyes. “You can’t read the oath-script. Take the warden. He’ll read it to you. I’ll stand beside you while he does. Then come back and tell the Bishop I made it up.”

Rhone did not move.

“Bishop,” he said quietly. Not to Ilva. “Listen to me. It is offering you everything you came north for. A road. A chapel that will never burn. Every hearth in the four valleys walking up to your door. The north saved and held, without a drop of blood.” He did not look at the coils. “And all you have to do is let the old paper stand as the Temple has always had it. Ten staves. Without the ninth.” He paused. “Without a phrase from four hundred years ago that nobody alive has ever heard.”

The Bishop did not answer.

“Nobody here can read the script but the warden and the old man in the valleys,” said Rhone. “Who will know?”

The voice on the coils laughed, softly.

“I will,” it said.

The Bishop looked at the vellum for a long time.

Riarhin watched him. She watched the clear pale tired eyes go over Ilva’s small dense hand in the space between the eighth stave and the tenth, line by line. She watched them go to the margin. Heads. Per the warden of the Hrafn-Grímr, reading from the original, at the Hall. She watched them go to the numbers.

His lips moved. She thought he was reading the numbers. She thought perhaps he was praying.

Then he put out his hand and took the vellum from Ilva, and held it, and turned round, and looked up at the white head on the coils.

“No,” he said.

The voice did not say anything.

“I came north to save the north,” said the Bishop. His voice was shaking. “I believed you were a demon. I believed you ate children. I believed the Temple held a word over you, because it held the record of your oath, and that I could use it to make you stop.” He looked at the vellum. “I was told that. I believed it. And the men who told me took out the words that would have let the north make you stop by themselves, four hundred years ago, and kept them out, so that the Temple would always be needed here.” He swallowed. “I will not take your road. I will not take your chapel. I will not take the winter-due, not as penance, not as anything.” His voice cracked. “I will not carry the leash those men made for me.”

The coils shifted on the far side of the hall.

“Pity,” said the voice softly. “You’d have been so good at it.”

The Bishop turned to his soldiers.

“The road,” he said. He pointed with his plain staff down the dry passage at the grey light. “Hold it. Hold it open. For the four from the coast and anybody else the Hall gives back.” He looked at the soldiers. “Not as the Temple’s. Not as wards. Not under my protection. They are not the Temple’s to protect. They are going home. Hold the road so they can.”

The soldiers did not move.

Riarhin saw it. She saw them stand at the foot of the stair in their ranks with their torches and their white shields and look at the Bishop, and then at Rhone, and then at each other. She saw them hesitate. They had seen the road and smelled the spring coming down it.

Rhone said nothing. He stood at the Bishop’s shoulder with his face like a blade and said nothing at all.

Then Captain Doukas stepped out of the first rank.

He did not say anything either. He walked out of the line, square and silent, with his grey moustache and his white shield on his arm, and crossed the ice to the mouth of the dry passage, and turned round, and stood in it with his back to the grey light and his shield up.

After a moment the boy with the chilblains came out of the line after him. Then Kostas, who sang on the march. Then the two brothers from the southern coast, arguing under their breath. Then others. Not all. Riarhin counted them as they went. Twenty. Twenty-two. They went and stood with Doukas in the mouth of the passage in two ranks with their shields up, facing the hall.

The rest stayed at the foot of the stair with Rhone.

Riarhin moved.

She did not decide to. She saw the dry passage held, and the ice of the hall between the Warm Room and the passage, and she saw the floor of the hall shake under her, a long slow tremor as the coils shifted, and she saw the old man in Gunnvor’s arms at the opening of the Warm Room, and she went.

She went back across the ice to the Warm Room. To Gunnvor. She did not ask.

“Give him to me,” she said.

Gunnvor looked at her. Then at the dry passage across the hall, held by Doukas and twenty-two shields.

“It’s a hundred paces,” said Gunnvor. “Across that.” She looked at the coils. “Your leg.”

“My leg’s held all night,” said Riarhin. “It can hold a hundred paces.” She held out her arms. “He’s the one who can’t walk. If it comes apart in here, he’s the one who doesn’t get out. Give him to me and I’ll put him at the mouth of the road behind the shields and come back for the boy.”

Gunnvor gave him to her.

Riarhin took Ulf against her chest. He was so light. She turned and went out across the dark ice of the hall toward the dry passage, slowly, not running, with her leg holding and her rib grinding and the old man in her arms, and the frost raced in the channels on the walls and the coils shifted on the far side and the floor trembled under her, and she did not look at the white head. She looked at Doukas.

He saw her come. He did not move from the mouth of the passage. When she reached the shields he spoke one word, and the front rank opened, two shields swinging apart, and she went through between them and set Ulf down on the dry stone floor of the passage, in the smell of wet earth, behind the shields, with his back against the wall.

He looked up at her.

“Is this the way out?” he said.

“It’s the way that’s held,” said Riarhin.

She went back for the boy.

When she came back across the hall with Kári by the hand, Temür was standing with the speakers. He had the bone case in his arms. He had not put it down. He stood beside Hrogaz in the line of the sworn with the case held against his chest, and when the Bishop’s soldiers at the foot of the stair looked at him he looked back.

Sifa was there too. At the end of the line, beside Orra. She had not moved since Hrogaz spoke. She stood with the speakers with her hands in her sleeves and her face turned toward the coils, and she did not look at the dry road at all.

The Bishop stood alone in the middle of the ice, between his two sets of soldiers, with Ilva’s vellum in his hand.

He looked old. Riarhin saw it as she went past him with the boy. He looked very old and very small, a thin stooped man in a grey robe in a hall of ice with half his column at his back refusing to move and the other half holding a road he had just told them he would not take. Rhone kept his eyes on the Bishop.
