The Bishop did not look at Rhone. He looked at the coils, and at the pale eyes, with the vellum held against his chest, and his lips moved.

He was praying. Riarhin was sure of it now.

She took the boy through the shields to Ulf, and went back for Svala and Gunnvor.

On the far side of the hall, the white head lifted itself off the coils, slowly, for the first time, and the voice said, very pleasantly, to all of them:

“Well. Shall we talk about something else?”

Chapter 42 — What she owes

“A new thing,” said the voice. “Since the old one is so tiresome.”

The white head had lifted off the coils. It hung above them now on its long neck, high, in the dark under the roof, and the pale eyes looked down at the line of the sworn on the ice, at Hrogaz with the staff and the fourteen speakers behind him, and they were warm. Riarhin did not know how ice could look warm. It did.

“The fifth stave,” said the voice. “Your old man read it to you. In his little tent. The sworn who bring a wrong may, instead of asking the Hall to undo it, agree to something new.” A pause. “Something new. Freely sworn. I’m offering it.”

Hrogaz did not answer.

“Listen,” said the voice. “Before you say no. You’re so quick to say no tonight, all of you.” The eyes moved along the line. “The four from the coast go home. Now. Down that dry road the candle-man’s soldiers are holding so bravely. And your daughter, warden. And the old man from the long ice who went on the sled last winter, who is also in a warm room of mine, and also well.” A pause. “And every hearth of the ten never sends a sled again. I’ll swear it. The due is ended. You won’t have to argue with me about counts and heads and houses of seals ever again.”

Along the line, nobody moved.

“And grazing,” said the voice. “The four valleys are hard. You know they are. You know what you eat in a bad year.” The voice was very gentle. “There’s a valley to the south of the black ridge. High. Sheltered. Good grass under the snow, the best in the north, and a river that never freezes all the way. Every hearth of the ten could winter its herds there and never go hungry again.”

Riarhin went cold.

“All you have to do,” said the voice, “is let me keep what I have. Don’t ask me to give back what I took past the end. Don’t ask me to undo anything with my own hand. Let it stand. Waive it.” A pause. “And let my winter go somewhere else. It has to go somewhere. If it doesn’t press on you, it has to press on somebody.” The pale eyes did not blink. “Let it press on the high valley.”

Riarhin knew the high valley.

She had been there. She had pulled a sled up the bottom of a frozen stream to it in the dark with an old man on the sled whose legs were wrong. She had stood in a hollow among rocks at grey dawn with her leg burning and watched Gaurra’s people take the sled from her and put the old man by a fire. She had heard Gaurra say if the Hall comes for anybody this winter, I hope it’s for the ones who made a noise. She had heard her say it was his noise that sent her.

Kel-dal. The hearth that did not send. The hearth that went up past the black ridge and kept quiet so that nothing would notice it.

“They aren’t ours,” said a voice along the line.

It was Haak. The new speaker for the long ice, the old man who had lost three under the snow-slide and come to Hrogaz with nowhere to go except north. He had stepped half out of the line. He spoke in the throat-speech, and Bródd, beside Riarhin, said it in a whisper.

“He says Kel-dal didn’t send,” said Bródd. “He says they went off by themselves and left the rest of us to make the noise. He says they aren’t under the stave. He says they aren’t in the claim.” Bródd’s voice shook. “He says his hearth went under a slab of snow the night the slabs came. He says three of his are dead. He says the best grass in the north.” Bródd stopped. “He says he knows who’s in Kel-dal. He says he knows Gaurra. He says he’s known her since they were children.”

Haak was looking at Hrogaz. His old face was terrible.

“He says take it,” said Bródd.

Riarhin looked at the dry road.

Across the hall, at the mouth of the passage, Doukas stood with his shields. Behind the shields, on the dry stone, in the smell of wet earth, she could see them: Ulf against the wall, and Kári beside him, and Gunnvor, and Svala. She had carried them there. All four. Through the shields to the stone. A hundred paces from the coils.

And the floor of the passage was cracking.

She saw it from where she stood. At the mouth of the passage, where the dry stone met the ice of the hall, there was a sill: a long lip of ice, a step up from the floor of the hall onto the stone of the road. She had carried Ulf over it. It had been solid. Now there was a line across it. A dark crack, running from one side of the passage mouth to the other, under Doukas’s feet, and as she watched, it widened. A hand’s breadth. Water welled up in it, black.

The wall of the passage, at the mouth, moved. Very slightly. Grinding. Inward.

The road was closing.

“Take it,” said Riarhin.

She heard herself say it. She did not say it loudly. She said it to herself, under her breath, looking at the cracking sill and the four on the far side of it. Take it. Take it, Hrogaz, and they walk down that road tonight, all of them, and Gyrd, and nobody else goes on a sled, and the best grass in the north, and it’s Gaurra’s valley and Gaurra didn’t send and Gaurra said she hoped it came for the ones who made a noise.

She wanted him to take it. She wanted the four out of the hall and down the dry road before the sill went, and she wanted it more than she had wanted anything since she came out of the forest.

Sifa was standing in front of her.

Riarhin had not seen her move. Sifa had come out of the end of the line of the sworn and crossed the ice and was standing in front of her, close, between her and the dry road, with her hands out of her sleeves.

“Say it again,” said Sifa.

Riarhin looked at her.

“You said take it,” said Sifa. In the northern speech. Thick. Every word got exactly right. “I heard you. Say it again. To me.” Her yellow-brown eyes did not move. “Say take it and put the winter on Kel-dal.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“Asgrim,” said Sifa. “Gaurra’s brother. The legs. You pulled him up the stream on a sled all night so Gaurra wouldn’t have to leave him.” Her jaw moved. “He’s there. In the high valley. By a fire. Because you pulled him.” She did not look away. “Say take it, and let the winter press on the old man I pulled up the stream.”

Riarhin stood on the ice.

She looked past Sifa at the dry road. At the crack across the sill, widening. At the four behind the shields.

She did not say it.

Hrogaz answered the voice.

He did it standing in front of the line, with the staff held across his body in both hands, and Haak beside him with his terrible face, and the speakers behind. He did not look at Haak. He did not look at the dry road. He looked up at the white head in the dark.

He spoke in the old speech. Bródd said it.

“He says no,” said Bródd.

Haak made a sound.

“He says the high valley isn’t under the stave,” said Bródd. “He says that’s right. He says Kel-dal didn’t send, and isn’t in the claim, and he doesn’t speak for it.” He listened. “He says that means he can’t give it away either. He says he can’t speak for Kel-dal to say spare it and he can’t speak for Kel-dal to say take it.” Bródd’s voice cracked. “He says the old wardens did that. Gave people who weren’t theirs to give. He says he’s not going to stand in front of you on the night the oath is read and do it again.”

The voice did not say anything.

“He says he asks for the old remedy,” said Bródd. “The fifth stave. The wrong undone. With your own hand. And not another’s.” He paused. “He says you can’t lay it on Kel-dal. The stave says. She may not lay the undoing on any who are not of the wrong.”

“I can,” said the voice softly, “if you agree.”

“He says he doesn’t,” said Bródd.

Haak had turned. He was looking at the line of speakers behind him. At the old woman who could not bear to bury her husband twice. At Orra with her grandson. At the others. He said something to them, low, urgent, in the throat-speech.

Riarhin watched them. She watched Haak say it, and she watched them hear it, and she watched them look at each other, and at Hrogaz, and at the dry road with the crack across its sill.

The old woman who could not bear to bury her husband twice stepped back from Haak.

Then another. Then Orra, with her hand on the boy’s head, looked at Haak a long time and shook her head, once.

Haak stood alone.

He stood a moment longer. Then he turned round, slowly, and looked up at the white head, and spoke one sentence in the throat-speech, and Bródd did not translate it, and nobody needed him to. Then he stepped back into the line, beside Hrogaz, and stood there, with his face in his hands.

“Very well,” said the voice.

It did not sound angry. It sounded the way it had sounded all night: amused, warm, enormous, a little bored. But under it, for the first time, there was something else. Riarhin heard it. She felt it in her chest, the way she had felt the groaning in her teeth. Something tightening.

“Very well,” said the voice again. “The old way. With my own hand.” The white head hung in the dark. “You’ve all been so very clever.”

Then it began.

The frost in the channels stopped.

It stopped all at once, as it had in the nave when the thing she had killed died: every channel on every wall of the great dark hall, the pale feathering frozen in place halfway up the walls. And then it began to move again. The other way.

It ran backward. Down the walls. Out of the roof. Back along the channels toward the coils, toward the white head and the heap of ice teeth, pulse after pulse, faster and faster, and as it came the walls of the hall darkened behind it, the ice going from pale to grey to clear, and the cold went with it. Riarhin felt it go. She felt it drawn past her, out of the air of the hall, out of her own wet clothes, toward the coils, the way water is drawn down a drain.

Across the hall, where the clear wall had shown Gyrd in her small warm room, the ice cracked down the middle and fell open.

She was there. On the rugs. Standing up. She stared out through the gap at the hall.

Hrogaz dropped the staff.

He dropped it for the second time that night, and did not pick it up, and he did not go to her either. He stood. And Gyrd came out of the gap in the ice, slowly, and across the floor of the hall toward him, and stopped in front of him, and they looked at each other.

Riarhin looked away.

Behind Gyrd, out of the same gap, an old man came, stooping, blinking. The speakers of the long ice made a sound. Haak lifted his face out of his hands.

The coils were changing.

Riarhin saw it when she looked back. The frost running back down the channels into the coils did not vanish into them. It stayed, and came up over them. Over the great pale piled body, coil on coil, white frost growing and thickening into ice, grey ice, clear ice, the way the ice had grown over Gest’s spikes and the eels in the trap and the withies of Halla’s weir. It grew up over the folded body. Over the heap of ice teeth. Over the long neck.

The white head was coming down.

It came down slowly, as the head of the thing in the nave had come down, out of the dark under the roof, onto the coils, onto the heap of teeth. And as it came the ice came up over it. Over the scarred white skin. Over the blue scars. Clear ice, thickening, sealing.

The pale eyes were still open.

They looked out at the hall through the thickening ice. At the line of the sworn. At Hrogaz and Gyrd. At the Bishop with the vellum against his chest. At Temür with the bone case. At Riarhin.

“You are so warm,” said the voice. It was fainter. It came through the ice muffled, very far away. “All of you. Little candles.” A pause. “I’ll be here. When you’ve forgotten. When the next one comes to say a wrong, and can’t read.” The voice laughed, very softly, and somewhere far above in the roof the ice did not crack. “I’ll be here.”

The ice closed over the eyes. Not all the way. A seam. A narrow seam of clear ice, over the pale grey-blue, and behind it the eyes, still open, watching.

The glacier broke.

It started in the roof. A sound Riarhin had never heard and never wanted to hear again: a long tearing roar, high up in the dark, that went along the roof of the hall from one end to the other and on, out, along the channels, the way the frost had run, into the walls, into the nave, out into the ice of the whole glacier.

The floor jumped.

Slabs came down. Out of the dark of the roof, great slabs of grey ice, falling past the walls, and where they struck the floor of the hall they shattered and the pieces went skidding across the ice, and some of them went past the coils to the far side of the hall where Riarhin could not see, and she heard them go down. Into the shaft. Into the water. She heard the water. It was roaring. Under the floor, under everything, water running, everywhere, rushing, as if the whole glacier had turned to water underneath and was pouring out.

“The road,” said Doukas.

He was shouting. It was the first time she had heard him raise his voice. He was in the mouth of the dry passage with his shields, and the crack across the sill had opened into a gap. Black water in it, rushing, a forearm wide and widening, between the floor of the hall and the stone of the road. And the walls of the passage mouth were grinding inward.

The four were on the far side of it.

Riarhin ran.

She did not know she could. She ran across the ice of the hall with the slabs coming down and the floor jumping under her, and her leg held, and her rib did not, and she did not care. Temür was beside her. She did not know when he had come. He had the bone case slung on his back again and he ran beside her.

At the sill she stopped.

The gap was too wide for a step from the firm edge. Black water, rushing, a long stride wide. On the far side, on the dry stone, behind the shields, Gunnvor had Kári by the hand and Svala had her back against the passage wall, and Ulf was on the floor against the stone where Riarhin had put him, and the stone under him was wet. Water was coming up through the floor of the road.

“Over,” said Riarhin to Doukas. “Get them over. To us. Not down the road. It’s closing.”

Doukas looked at the passage behind him, at the grey light at the end of it, and the walls grinding in, and he understood.

He picked up Kári and threw him.

He threw him the way you throw a sack across a gap, under the arms, hard, and Temür caught him on the hall side, and they both went down on the ice. Svala jumped. She made it. Gunnvor jumped, heavy, and her foot went into the water on the far edge and Riarhin caught her wrist and pulled and she came over onto the ice on her knees.

Ulf.

He was on the floor of the road. He could not jump. He could not stand. He was sitting with his back to the grinding wall with the water coming up round him, looking at the gap with his bright eyes.

Riarhin went over.

She stepped onto the sill. It moved under her, a lip of ice over rushing water, cracking, and she stepped across the gap onto the far side, onto the wet stone of the road, and her leg went into the water to the knee and she pulled it out. She got to Ulf. She got her arms under him, the bad one and the good one, and lifted. He was so light.

Temür was on the sill behind her. On the cracking lip of ice, with his feet wide apart, the way a rider stands on ground that might move. He held out his arms.

She gave Ulf to him across the gap. Then she stepped back across herself, onto the sill, and it broke under her.

She felt it go. The lip of ice went out from under her heel and dropped into the black water, and her foot went down with it, and Temür had Ulf in his arms and could not reach her.

Somebody caught her by the collar.

It was Doukas. He had come back across the sill behind her, the last of his men, the last of anybody, and he had her by the coat at the back of the neck the way Temür had caught Sifa at the edge of the pit, and he threw her forward onto the ice of the hall, and came after her, and behind them the sill went down into the water all at once, the whole lip of it, and the walls of the passage mouth ground together, and met, and the grey light at the end of the road went out.

She lay on the ice of the hall.

Round her, the slabs had stopped falling. The roar in the roof had gone away along the glacier and was very far off now, going out. The water under the floor was still rushing. It would be rushing, she thought, for a long time.

She turned her head.

On the far side of the hall, the coils lay under their ice. Grey, clear, still. A great frozen heap round a great frozen heap of teeth, and on top of it, under the clear ice, the white head, resting. And in the white head, behind a narrow seam of clear ice, the eyes. Open. Pale. Watching.

They were watching her.

She looked back at them for a long time. They did not move. Nothing moved. There was no frost in the channels on the walls. There was no groaning. There was no voice.

Beside her on the ice, Temür was sitting with Ulf in his lap. The old man was looking up at the roof. He had his pouch in his hand, and he was holding it shut.

Across the hall, Hrogaz stood with his daughter. Neither of them had moved since she came out of the gap. They stood with a pace of ice between them and looked at each other, and the speakers of the ten hearths stood round them in a ring, and nobody spoke.

The Bishop was on his knees on the ice with the vellum held against his chest.

Ilva was standing beside him. She had her hand on his shoulder.

And Sifa was standing a little way off from all of them, alone, with her hands in her sleeves, looking at the frozen coils on the far side of the hall. At the eyes behind the seam. She looked at them for a long time.

Then she turned away, and came across the ice to where Riarhin was lying, and crouched down beside her, and held out her hand.

Riarhin took it. Sifa pulled her up.

Chapter 43 — The road out

The way out was not the way in.

They found that at the top of the stair. Riarhin went up it first, because somebody had to and her leg had decided, after the sill, that it would go on holding as long as she did not ask it to stop. The stair spiralled up through the ice beside the shaft, worn hollow, and at the top of it there should have been the nave: the great dark under the ribs of ice, the pillars, the Choir, and the long floor going back toward the stream-gap and the valley.

There was light.

She came out of the top of the stair into it and stopped. Grey light. Daylight. Coming down from above, through the roof of the nave, in long pale shafts, where the roof had broken. The great ribs of ice that had arched over the nave were split along their length, along the lines where the channels had run, and through the splits she could see sky. Grey sky, low, moving. Snow coming down through the cracks in slow drifting curtains and lying on the floor of the nave in long white lines.

And the floor was water.

Not all of it. In channels. The floor of the nave had cracked along the same lines as the roof, and the cracks were full of water, running, black and fast, out from the middle of the hall toward the walls and the way in, and the ice between the channels stood up in long uneven ridges like the furrows of a field, some of them level, some of them tilted, some of them already sliding.

The Choir was there.

She saw it at the far end, past the water. The half-ring of nineteen, standing in the grey light from the broken roof with the snow coming down on them. Nothing had touched them. Nothing had fallen on them. They stood with their mouths open and their eyes white and the girl at the end with her breath frozen in front of her, and the snow settled on their shoulders.

“We can’t go that way,” said Yrra.

She had come up the stair behind Riarhin. She stood at the top of it and looked down the nave at the stream-gap, at the far end, where they had come in. It was a torrent. Riarhin could hear it from here: a roaring white rush of water pouring out of the end of the nave into the gap and down it, filling it from wall to wall.

“There,” said Temür.

He had come up too, with Ulf on his back now, the old man’s thin arms round his neck. He was pointing. Not down the nave. Up. At the wall of the nave beside the shaft, the left wall, where the gallery had been, the hauling way Yrra’s grandfather had told her of, where Gest’s ladders had been frozen into the ice.

The wall had split. It had split from the floor of the nave to the broken roof along the line of the gallery, and the split was wide, wide enough for a sled, and the gallery had become the floor of a passage. A long rough slope of broken ice going up through the split in the wall, steep, uneven, in steps and ridges, toward the grey light. Toward the outside.

Halfway up it, frozen into the ice of the passage wall, Riarhin could see the end of a ladder.

“That goes out,” said Yrra. Her voice was strange. “That goes up onto the top. The top of the ice. My grandfather said there was a road. Before. Over the top of the ice to the north side, and down. A winter road. The old one. Before the oath.” She looked at the split. “He said it was shut. He said the Hall shut it.”

“It’s open,” said Temür.

It was open the way a wound is open.

They brought them up the stair from the hall one by one and stood them at the top in the grey light from the broken roof: the four from the coast, and Gyrd, and the old man from the long ice; the fourteen speakers and Hrogaz with his staff; the Bishop, walking now, with Ilva at his elbow; Doukas and his twenty-two; and behind them, last, Rhone and the soldiers who had stayed at the foot of the stair with him. Riarhin counted them as they came. She could not stop.

Then they went up the split.

It took the rest of the day. It was broken ice, piled and tilted and sliding, with water running under it and through it in a hundred channels, and every step had to be tried before it was trusted. Yrra went first, with her pole, sounding. Sifa went beside her. Behind them the rest came up in a line, slowly, helping each other.

The litters went in the middle.

There were three. Ulf, who could not walk. The old man from the long ice, who could barely stand. And one of the speakers from the black crag, who had slipped on the stair and broken his ankle. Doukas made the litters out of spear-shafts and cloaks in the nave, at the top of the stair, quickly, without being asked.

He chose who carried them.

Riarhin watched him choose. She watched him look along the soldiers at the top of the stair, at his own twenty-two and at Rhone’s men behind them, and she watched him put his own on the litters. Kostas. The boy with the chilblains. The two brothers from the southern coast. He did not ask Rhone’s men. He did not look at them. One of them, a big man with a broken nose, came forward and put his hand on the pole of Ulf’s litter, and Doukas looked at him, and the man took his hand away again and went back to the others.

Nobody said anything about it.

Temür carried the bone case.

He carried it on his back all the way up the split, slung by its thong, with Ulf on his back for the first part of the climb and then the case alone when Ulf went on the litter. He did not put it down. When the ice tilted and he went to one knee he went down with the case still on him and got up with it. Riarhin, behind him, watched it ride on his back, yellowed bone in the grey light, all the way up.

At the top, the split came out onto the surface of the glacier.

It was very wide and very quiet. The snow was coming down out of a grey sky onto a great broken plain of ice that went away north and south and east as far as Riarhin could see, cracked across and across in long lines, and in the cracks there was water, running. She could hear it everywhere. Under her feet. All round. The whole glacier, running underneath.

And there was a road.

It ran away from the top of the split across the broken ice toward the north. It was hardly a road: a line of old cairns, half buried, every few hundred paces, going away into the snow, and between the cairns the ice was smoother than the rest, flatter, as if something had once kept it so. It was broken in places. Cracked across, with water in the cracks, and some of the cairns had fallen. But it went on. North. Toward the four valleys.

Yrra stood at the first cairn with her hand on it and wept.

Temür stopped at the top of the split, by the first cairn, and turned round.

He took the bone case off his back.

Riarhin saw him do it, and went to him. So did Ilva. So did Hrogaz, with his daughter beside him. Nobody had asked them. They came and stood round him in the falling snow at the top of the broken ice, with the plain of the glacier on one side and the split going down into the broken nave on the other.

“It’s done,” said Temür. In the northern speech. “It’s been seen. Outside.” He looked at Ilva. “You saw.”

“I saw,” said Ilva. She had a sheet of paper in her hand, already written, held flat against the leather case against the wind. “I wrote it. The four, released, brought out by the hauling way, alive. Gyrd of the hearth of the warden, released, alive. Arrok of the long ice, released, alive. The due ended. The Hall—” She stopped. “Sealed. I wrote that. Sealed in its own cold, and watching.” She looked at Temür. “And you.”

Temür opened the bone case.

He took out the stave. It was smaller than Riarhin remembered in the lamplight of the warm room: dark wood, flattened on two sides, cut all over with strokes worn almost to nothing, and the mark at its head. It was bound. She had not seen that in the warm room. It was made of several slats of dark wood laid together and bound at either end with a winding of old dark sinew, so that they made one stave.

Temür put his thumb under the binding at the head.

“Bródd,” he said. “Which one has her name? Alaq’s mother’s.”

Bródd came. He looked at the strokes. He put his finger beside one of the slats, the top one, near the mark.

“That one,” he said.

“And the ones after?”

Bródd looked. He touched another slat. And another. “The line,” he said. “After. I think. The names go on down these.”

Temür drew the long knife at his belt. He put the edge under the sinew at the head of the stave, and cut it. Then at the foot. The binding fell away in the snow, old and brittle, in pieces. The stave came apart in his hands. Five slats. Separate.

He held them.

“It said I have them,” he said. “When it was bound. One thing. I have the household of the mother of Alaq and all their line.” He held up the slats. “Now it doesn’t say anything. Now it’s five pieces of wood with names on them.” He looked at Ilva. “I’m keeping them. All five. I’m taking them south. To Borte. To the old man at the western fire with his end of the song. To anybody who wants to see what the Hall had.” He looked at the slats. “They’re names. They’re ours. They were never hers.”

“I won’t ask for them,” said Ilva.

“No,” said Temür.

“I’d like to write down that you have them,” said Ilva. “That they exist. That they were taken from the Hall at the release, in your keeping. So it’s known.” She paused. “Not what’s on them. That they are.”

Temür looked at her a long moment.

“Write that,” he said.

He put the five slats back in the bone case, side by side, loose, and closed the lid, and slung it on his back again. He threw the cut sinew into the crack at his feet, where the water was running, and watched it go.

Sifa stood a little way off, by the second cairn, looking north up the road.

Riarhin went to her.

She did not say anything. She stood beside her in the falling snow and looked where Sifa was looking: up the old road, cairn after cairn, toward the four valleys.

“Orra’s children,” said Sifa, after a while. In the northern speech. “Tukka. Hessa. Ama who isn’t walking. They’re off the slab.” She touched her jaw. “I saw it go. In the hall. When the ice came up over her. I felt it go.”

“Yes.”

“The slabs at every hearth,” said Sifa. “They’ll be water. By the time we get there.” She was quiet. “I want to see.”

“You will.”

Sifa turned her head and looked at her.

“I’m in your keeping,” she said. “On the stone. You have to bring me back to the coast. To Ottarshall. To the man whose hand I bit.” Her yellow-brown eyes did not move. “Nineteen in the hall. I was on the hill with the sleds.” She paused. “That boy saw me. Kári. He said it in front of everybody. You looked at me and you didn’t do anything.” She touched her jaw again. “That’s still mine. The Hall being sealed doesn’t make it not mine. That isn’t between me and her. It’s between me and them.”

Riarhin looked at her for a long time.

“I know,” she said.

“Good,” said Sifa. “Then don’t let anybody on the coast tell me it’s finished. Not even you.”

She turned and went back to the litters.

Rhone read his paper at the foot of the glacier, on the third day, in the snow, in front of everybody.

They had come down the old road across the top of the ice and off its north edge, and round, slowly, by the broken ways, to the valley floor and the Bishop’s camp at the foot of the wall. The wall was not a wall any more. Riarhin saw it from the slope as they came down: great slabs of it had come away and fallen into the valley, and lay there in a long ruin of blue and white, and the stream-gap was a river, running brown and white out over the snow, and the candle in its niche was gone, niche and all.

Rhone stood in front of the ruin. He took a paper out of his coat. He unfolded it. He read it aloud.

He read all of it. In the southern tongue first, and then in the northern, in his level voice, to the end. It said that on this day, at the Hall in the ice, the Bishop Serapion had refused, before witnesses, a settlement offered to the Temple that would have secured the north for the faith; that he had accepted, in place of the Temple’s copy of the oath, a text written into that copy by its keeper in contravention of his written order; that he had released to the northern peoples, without condition, the claim the Temple had held for four hundred years; and that the Justiciar Rhone, who had witnessed it, would carry this report to the synod at Thepyrgos himself.

He folded it. He put it back in his coat.

“I read it,” he said, to the Bishop. “Before I act on it. Always. So that nobody can say afterward they did not know.”

The Bishop looked at him.

He did not say anything. He was very old in the snow, and very tired, and he had the vellum still in his hands, rolled, against his chest. He looked at Rhone for a long time with his pale clear eyes.

Then he nodded.

“Yes,” he said. “Go and tell them.”

Rhone went south that afternoon, with the men who had stayed at the foot of the stair with him, and the Bishop stood at the edge of the camp and watched him go, and did not call him back.

Doukas stayed. So did the twenty-two.

That night, in the Bishop’s camp, in the warm, with the stream roaring past outside, Riarhin sat by a fire with Ulf.

He was in a blanket on a pallet, with his legs out in front of him and his pouch in his lap. He had not opened it since the hall. He was holding it shut.

“Thirty-one,” he said, when he saw her looking.

“Yes.”

“The Temple woman counted them.” He turned the pouch over in his hands. “Out loud. In front of it. My grandfather’s grandfather said one day somebody would ask, and have the number ready.” He looked at her with his bright eyes. “I had it ready.”

“You did,” said Riarhin.

He nodded slowly, as if that settled something. Then he put the pouch back round his neck on its thong, and lay back on the pallet, and closed his eyes, and after a while he slept.

Part VI — What goes south

Chapter 44 — The four returned

The thaw came behind them all the way down.

It came the day after they left the foot of the ice. Riarhin woke in the Bishop’s camp to the sound of water, everywhere, dripping off the tents and running in the snow, and went out and found the valley floor turned to slush and the sky blue for the first time since the autumn. It followed them south. Over the moor, where the snow went grey and then thin and then gave way in patches to black heather, and the posts with the strokes on them stood up out of bare ground. Past the stone, where Ingrith’s blue banner hung limp and sodden on its pole in the cairn, and Riarhin took it down and folded it and carried it. Down the river road, where the ice went out of the river in a single night with a noise like the end of the world, and in the morning the water was running brown and high and full of broken floes.

At Hornvik the lake was open. At Skarth, Bjarni’s boat was off the trestles and in the river, and Bjarni was standing on its deck with his arms folded, watching them come down the valley road, and when he saw the litters he came ashore and walked beside them as far as the last house without saying anything.

At the sea-landing below Skarth there were three boats waiting, and Halldor, and Leif.

Ottarshall had a roof.

Not on the hall. The hall’s walls stood as they had stood in the winter, black, round nothing. But the byre had been mended, and the boat-shed, and there were two new houses along the stream where the burned ones had been, raw timber, the turf on their roofs still brown. And there were people on the shingle. A great many. More than had been at Ottarshall in the winter. They had come from the other bays, Riarhin learned later, and from Hvalgard, and from Kettlebeach, because the word had come down the coast ahead of the boats from post to post and hall to hall: the four are coming home.

They stood on the shingle in the spring wind and watched the boats come in.

Riarhin was in the first boat with Svala and Kári. She had not planned it. She had been put there. She sat in the bow with her leg out and watched the shingle come closer, and the faces on it, and she saw Ingrith.

Ingrith was standing at the water’s edge. In front of everybody. In her blue coat with the scorched hem, with her braids tied in black. She was standing very still, with her hands at her sides, and she did not move as the boat came in. She did not wade out. She stood at the edge of the water and waited.

The boat grounded.

Svala got out.

She did not wait for anybody to help her. She climbed over the gunwale into the shallows, into water to her knees, and waded ashore, and walked up the shingle to Ingrith, and stopped in front of her.

They looked at each other.

Riarhin, in the bow, saw Ingrith’s face. She saw it do what it had done in Gudleif’s hall the night they came back from the ice: break, all at once, the heavy reddened stillness going out of it like water out of a broken weir. Ingrith put out her arms.

Svala stepped back.

It was only a step. Half a step. But she took it, and Ingrith’s arms stopped in the air, and the shingle went quiet.

“Svala,” said Ingrith.

“I know,” said Svala. Her voice was high and tight. “I know. I’m sorry. I can’t.” She was shaking. “Not yet. Not in front of everybody. I can’t.”

Ingrith lowered her arms. She lowered them slowly. She stood on the shingle with her hands at her sides and looked at her brother’s daughter.

“All right,” she said.

“I want to go in,” said Svala. “Somewhere. Somewhere with a door.”

“All right,” said Ingrith again. She turned. “The new house. By the stream. It’s yours. It was going to be yours.” She did not reach for her. She walked beside her, a pace apart, up the shingle toward the houses, and the crowd parted for them, and Svala walked with her head down and her arms folded, and did not look at anybody.

At the door of the new house she stopped. She looked back down the shingle, once, at the boat. At Riarhin in the bow.

Then she went in, and Ingrith went in after her, and shut the door.

Kári ran.

He came out of the boat behind Svala and stood in the shallows, small, with his enormous eyes, and looked up the shingle at the crowd, and a woman in the crowd screamed his name. He ran. Up the shingle, through the water, through the people, and the woman came down to meet him and they met halfway and she went down on her knees on the stones and held him and did not stop screaming, and he held on to her neck and did not say anything at all.

Aud. His mother. Riarhin heard people say it.

She held him a long time. Then she held him away from her and looked at his face, and touched it, and held him again.

Later, in the evening, Riarhin saw them by the fire outside the boat-shed. Aud had Kári on her lap, though he was too big for it, wrapped in a blanket, and she was trying to feed him porridge from a bowl, and he would not eat it. He kept turning his face away. He was not crying. He sat in his mother’s lap and turned his face from the spoon, and Aud kept trying, with her face wet.

“He won’t eat anything warm,” said Gunnvor, beside Riarhin. She had come to stand by her at the edge of the firelight. “Not since the room. Everything in the room was warm. He’ll eat it cold. Bread. Fish. Cold.” She watched him. “She doesn’t know yet. I’ll tell her in the morning.”

Gunnvor had nobody on the shingle.

Riarhin had not known that. She learned it watching her come out of the second boat with Ulf on his litter between two of Doukas’s men, and walk up the shingle beside the litter, and nobody came to meet her. People touched her as she passed. They touched her arm, her shoulder, said her name. Thora was there, small in her shawls, and put out her bad hand and Gunnvor took it and held it a moment. But nobody ran.

“My husband went down off the skerries twenty years ago,” said Gunnvor, when Riarhin asked. They were standing by the boat-shed in the dusk. “No children.” She looked at the shingle. “I had my loom. In the house in the row. It burned.” She was quiet. “I’ll weave again. When there’s a loom. When my hands stop doing this.”

She held them up. They were shaking. A fine steady tremor, the way Orm’s had shaken on the stone at Kettlebeach.

“They’ve done that since the room,” said Gunnvor. “Since the first week. I can’t make them stop.” She lowered them. “I don’t think I can thread a shuttle like this.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“I’ll learn,” said Gunnvor. “I learned the first time. I was six.”

Hauk found Sifa on the second morning.

Riarhin had known he would. She had seen him on the shingle when the boats came in, at the edge of the crowd, with his bitten hand healed into a white scar across the back, and she had seen him see Sifa come out of the last boat and stand on the shingle with her hands in her sleeves. He had not moved then. He had watched.

He came in the morning, when Sifa was sitting by the boat-shed fire with Riarhin and Temür and Bródd. He came with four of his cousins behind him, and he stopped in front of the fire, and looked at Sifa, and then at Riarhin.

“The custody,” he said. “On the stone. If she runs, you answer.” He looked at Sifa. “She didn’t run. You brought her back.” He did not raise his voice. “Ottarshall keeps its claim. Ingrith said. On the stone. When we know what she did, we’ll know what’s owed.” He looked at Riarhin. “Do we know what she did?”

Riarhin looked at him.

“Yes,” said Sifa.

They turned.

She had not stood up. She sat by the fire with her knees drawn up and her hands in her sleeves, and she looked at Hauk, and she touched her jaw, once, and spoke in the northern speech. Thick. Slow. Every word got right.

“I was on the hill,” she said. “With the sleds. The night of the hall. I didn’t go down. I didn’t burn it. I didn’t kill your brother.” She did not look away from him. “I held the sleds while the others went down. I saw the hall burn from the hill. I saw them bring the four up past me. I looked at the boy, and I didn’t do anything.” Her jaw moved. “That’s what I did.”

Hauk stood very still.

“I want it said at the stone,” said Sifa. “All of it. By me. Not by the coast-woman. Not by Bródd. By me.” She paused. “And the ones who went down. I know their names. I’ll say them at the stone too. If they’re asked for.”

Behind Hauk, his cousins looked at each other.

“And then?” said Hauk.

“And then the stone says,” said Sifa. “Not me.”

It was not settled that day. It was not settled for many days.

What was settled, in the end, at the stone at Kettlebeach, at low water, with Ranveig and Ingrith and Hauk and a great many others on the holm, and Grim’s bronze ring still lying where Hauk had put it in the winter, was not a verdict. Riarhin had thought it might be. She had seen Linn law at the stone and she had thought they would decide, there, at once, the way they had decided the search. They did not. Ranveig would not let them.

“There are nineteen in the hall,” said Ranveig, by the stone. “And four taken. And now four back. And a woman who held the sleds, and others who went down, who are in the four valleys, and have names. And a hearth that sent them, and a warden who sent them because the Hall said, and a Hall that said because of an oath with an end in it that nobody had read.” She looked round the ring. “Who do you want to hang?”

Nobody answered.

“Nineteen,” said Ingrith, under her breath.

“Nineteen,” said Ranveig. “Yes.” She struck her harp once. “So it will be heard. All of it. Properly. Not in a day.”

So what the stone agreed was a way. Sifa would stay on the coast, under a keeping that was not Riarhin’s now but Ingrith’s, with Hrogaz’s word sent from the north that she was his to answer for too, until the hearing was done. The hearths of the four valleys would send speakers to the stone at midsummer, Hrogaz among them, and the names of the ones who had gone down to the hall. Ottarshall would say what it had lost. The four valleys would say what they had been made to do and by whom. Ranveig would hear it, and Steinar from the upper valley, who had a meltwater cup, and a speaker from the four valleys whom both sides would agree. And what was owed for nineteen would be said then, by the people who had lost them and the people who had taken them, face to face, and not before.

“And if they don’t come?” said Hauk. “The four valleys. At midsummer.”

“Then that will be heard too,” said Ranveig.

Riarhin gave her evidence on the first day. They asked her to, and she did.

She stood by the stone and said what she had seen, from the quay at Hvalgard in the autumn to the top of the broken ice at the end. She said which was seen and which was heard. She said what she had said in the winter that was wrong. She said it all in the northern speech, slowly, and Ranveig did not interrupt her once.

At the end Hauk asked her one thing.

“The woman in keeping,” he said. “You had her all winter. You walked beside her. Do you want her forgiven?”

Riarhin looked at him. She looked at Sifa, standing a little apart by the edge of the holm with her hands in her sleeves, looking at the sea.

“It isn’t mine to want,” she said. “I didn’t lose anybody in the hall.” She looked at Hauk. “I brought her back. I said I would. That’s what I can do.”

Hauk looked at her a long time. Then he nodded, once, and stepped back into the ring.

The four were asked to speak.

Ranveig asked them at the end of the first day, at the stone, gently, one at a time. Whether they wanted to say anything. Today, or another day, or never.

Ulf said yes. He said it from his litter, with his pouch in his lap, and he told the stone about the room, and the lamps, and the stones on the shelf that sang, and the thirty-one, and the Temple woman counting them out loud. He told it well. People laughed once, when he described Svala trying the drain door for the thirtieth time and swearing at it. He did not seem to mind them laughing.

Gunnvor said not today.

Kári did not answer at all. He stood by his mother with his face against her side, and Ranveig looked at him a long moment and said another day, then, and he nodded without looking up.

Svala came last.

She had not come to the stone in the morning. She came at noon, walking down the causeway alone with her arms folded, and stood at the edge of the ring and listened to Ulf, and to Riarhin, and to everything. When Ranveig asked her, she stepped forward.

“They’re saying I waited,” she said.

The ring went quiet.

“In the song,” said Svala. “The second one. Ranveig’s. The four behind the screen, waiting, never doubting. They sang it at Hvalgard. They sang it at Selvík. A man sang it at me on the boat.” Her voice was hard and high. “I didn’t wait.”

Ranveig did not say anything.

“I tried the door,” said Svala. “The drain door. Every day. For a month. I pulled the ring and the ice closed and I pulled it again. I stood behind the screen and watched you fight that thing and watched the bridge go and watched your lamps go away up the nave, and I hated you.” She looked at Riarhin. “I hated you for going. I hated Ingrith for not coming herself. I hated the coast for singing.” She stopped. “I didn’t wait. I wasn’t brave. I was angry every day for five months, and in the last month I stopped trying the door, and that was worse.” She looked at Ranveig. “Don’t sing that I waited. I didn’t.”

The ring was silent.

Ranveig looked at her for a long time. Then she struck the harp once.

“I won’t,” she said.

Svala nodded, sharply, once. Then she turned round and walked back along the causeway to the shore, alone, with her arms folded, and Ingrith, at the stone, watched her go and did not follow, and Riarhin saw her mouth move.

Four, Ingrith said. Under her breath. Not nineteen. Four.

Chapter 45 — The roof still missing

Orm said it on the quay at Hvalgard, the morning after the stone, and Riarhin heard him say it.

“Hornvik,” he said. “Yrsa’s store. The house under the post.” He was standing at the foot of the Staðfast’s ramp with his left hand in Hallbjorn’s palm-guard, three fingers through it and the leather stitched flat where the other two had been, and he was talking to Sigrun. “I said it to her. On the point. In front of the Temple’s men. We’ll see it put right before we’re done.” He looked at his hand. “We’re not done.”

Sigrun looked at him.

“The freight sails in twelve days,” she said.

“Hornvik’s five there and five back by the valley road,” said Orm. “Less, now the river’s open. Leif’ll take us to the sea-landing.” He did not look away from her. “I said it.”

Sigrun was quiet a moment.

“Then go and do it,” she said. “And be back in twelve days, or I sail without you and you can explain to Ranveig why.”

Brother Halle came with them. He had the materials.

He had been at Hvalgard all the false spring, building the chapel by the whale-bone gate. Not on the net-drying ground. Riarhin had seen it on her way down to the boat that morning: a small square stone footing at the top of the shingle, beside the boat-sheds, on a piece of rough ground nobody used, with three courses of wall on it and no roof. Old Hild was sitting in the lee of the whale-bone post with a net across her knees, among the drying posts, singing under her breath, and the nets were hanging between the posts in the spring wind.

“It wasn’t right,” Brother Halle said, when Riarhin looked at it. He went red. “The other place. You were right. I asked Hild. She said the nets had dried there since her grandmother, and she said a great many other things, some of which I did not entirely follow.” He looked at the footing. “This is worse ground. It floods in the spring tides. I will have to build it higher.” He paused. “I don’t mind.”

He had the birch. The green birch from the western fork at Skarth that he had felled himself in the winter with blistered hands, stacked and seasoned all winter on bearers in Bjarni’s shed. Bjarni had sent word that it was dry enough. It was meant for the chapel roof.

“Some of it,” said Brother Halle. “Some of it for the chapel roof. Some of it can go to Hornvik.” He looked at Riarhin. “The Bishop has written. From Dalsendi. He says the Temple is to restore what it took at Hornvik, and at every place the Justiciar cleared, and not to ask anything for it.” He hesitated. “Not to ask anything. He wrote that twice. He underlined it.” He looked at the boat. “And he sent the bell.”

He had it. In a bundle of sheepskin in the bottom of his pack. He showed her. The little black iron bell from the ring of stones on the point, with the crack up its side, and the clapper wrapped separately in a rag.

“Not melted,” said Brother Halle. “He kept it safe. He said he would.” He wrapped it up again. “He said give it to the keeper. He said tell her it is hers. Not the Temple’s, lent. Hers.”

Yrsa met them at the edge of the point, as she had in the winter.

She came down the track with her skirts held out of the mud, quickly, square and grey and red-faced, and she looked at them: at Orm, at his hand, at Riarhin, at Brother Halle in his grey robe with his pack, at Temür and Bródd and the two men from Skarth Bjarni had sent with the sled. Her face did not change.

“You came back,” she said.

“I said,” said Orm.

Yrsa looked at him. Then she looked up the point at the ring of standing stones on the high ground above the houses, nine of them, man-high, and inside them the black heap, under a skin of old snow going grey.

“Nobody’s touched it,” she said. “All winter. The Bishop’s men went away. The officers gave the houses back. I got the store back. Most of it.” She looked at Brother Halle. “Some of the meal they took went to the column and didn’t come back. The paper says it will. In the spring. At Hvalgard.”

“It will,” said Brother Halle. “I have it. In the store by the chapel. Measure for measure, and a measure over. It’s yours whenever you send for it.”

Yrsa looked at him for a long moment.

“And the stones?” she said.

Brother Halle took the bundle out of his pack, and unwrapped it, and held out the bell.

They began on the third day, when the snow in the ring had gone and the black heap lay bare in the thin spring sun.

It was not Riarhin’s work. She did not know what anything was.

The heap was the remains of the house under the post. A small house, Yrsa said, no larger than a byre-stall, of turf on a frame of timber, built against the foot of the post in the middle of the ring where the bell had hung. Under its floor, in a stone-lined pit, the bones. Old ones. The keepers’ bones, and others, from before anybody remembered, brought there and laid there, each in its place. The Justiciar’s men had burned the house and the post and pulled the cloths out of the pit and burned those, and left the bones, because, Yrsa said, even the Justiciar’s men had not wanted to touch them.

Ásgeir knew how it had been built. His head had mended. He stood in the ring with a stick and drew it in the mud for the men from Skarth: where the post went, how deep, which way the frame was jointed, how the turf went on. He drew it slowly, and twice he stopped and rubbed it out and drew it again, and the Skarth men watched and said nothing, and when he had finished they looked at the drawing and at Brother Halle’s birch on the sled, and began.

Riarhin tried to help.

She did what she could do. She could not dig, not with her rib. She could not lift a post. She sat on a stone at the edge of the ring and shaved bark off the birch poles with the Last Blade, whose dressed-down point was blunter than it had been and better for this than for anything else, slowly, the way she had seen Bjarni do it at Skarth, and passed them to the Skarth men when they were done, and that was useful, and she was glad of it.

Then, on the fourth day, she saw a joint going wrong.

At least she thought it was going wrong. It was the joint where the first rafter met the post, and one of the Skarth men was cutting it, and Riarhin, watching him from her stone, saw that he was cutting it at an angle that would make the rafter sit low on the post, lower than the drawing in the mud, and she got up and went over and said so.

The Skarth man looked at her.

“It sits low,” he said. “It’s meant to.”

“The drawing—”

“The drawing’s where the old one sat. The old one was wrong.” He did not stop cutting. “It rotted at the joint. Ásgeir said. That’s why it burned so quick. Water got in at the joint and it was rotten through. This one sits low so the water runs off.” He looked at her. “Do you know about joints?”

“No,” said Riarhin.

“Then go and shave poles,” said the Skarth man. Not unkindly.

She went and shaved poles.

She felt her face go hot, sitting on her stone at the edge of the ring with the birch across her knees and the Last Blade in her hand, and she went on shaving, and did not get up again that day to tell anybody how anything should be done.

Yrsa, passing with a basket of turves, looked at her, and almost smiled.

Tokk came on the fifth day.

Riarhin did not expect him. She saw him come along the lake road from the north in the afternoon, limping, on a stick, with his leg in a splint of birch bark that Kallias had made him at the foot of the ice, and Yrra beside him, and three others behind them in furs, with the blue lines on their jaws. They were going south, Yrra said. To Kettlebeach. To the stone at midsummer. Speakers from the four valleys, sent ahead by Hrogaz to see the road and the coast and the stone before the rest came down.

They stopped at Hornvik because there was a fire.

And because Tokk, coming up the point on his stick, had seen the ring of stones, and the house going up in it, and had stopped dead at the edge of the ring and stood there staring.

Riarhin, on her stone, followed his eyes.

The Skarth men had set the post that morning. The new post, in the middle of the ring, birch, pale and fresh, taller than a man, with the house going up against its foot. And at the top of the post, where the bell would hang, Ásgeir had fixed the head.

It had come out of the ashes. It was the only thing that had. A carved wooden head, black with age and fire, scorched down one side, as long as Riarhin’s arm. It had a long snout. It had ears laid back flat along its skull and its jaws open in a snarl, full of carved teeth like pegs, and its eyes were two round holes with the wood carved in rings round them.

Riarhin had seen it before. Its brothers. Over the door of the Old Hall in Hvalgard. On the floor of Brakka’s hearth, dragged by the ear by a child.

Tokk said something.

He said it in the throat-speech, low, and his voice shook. Then he said it again, louder. He was looking at Yrsa, who had come out of the half-built house with turves in her arms.

Yrsa looked at Bródd.

Bródd had been carrying birch from the sled. He put it down. He looked at Tokk, and at the head on the post, and at Yrsa, and Riarhin saw him think. She saw him begin to make it into something else, gentler, something you could say at a feast. She saw him stop.

“He says take it down,” said Bródd.

Yrsa did not move.

“He says,” said Bródd, “that’s what you think we look like. He says it’s on your boats and over your doors and now you’re putting it on top of your holy place, over your dead, where your bell rings.” Bródd’s voice did not soften. “He says if you put it up there, every time one of us comes down this road to the coast, we’ll walk past it. He says every time your bell rings for a boat, it’ll ring under that.” He paused. “He says he’s been to the ice. He’s seen the thing that took the four. He says it didn’t look like that either.” He paused again. “He says you’re ugly people with bad eyes and he wants it down.”

The ring was silent.

The Skarth men had stopped work. Ásgeir was standing by the post with his stick. Yrsa stood with the turves in her arms, and her face had gone dark red, and Riarhin saw her open her mouth to say something.

Then she shut it.

She looked at the head on the post for a long time. At its snout, and its pegged teeth, and its round ringed eyes. Ásgeir’s grandmother’s grandmother had brought the bell from the coast. Somebody, before that, had carved the head and put it on the post over the bones, and it had been there as long as anybody remembered.

“It was my grandmother’s,” she said.

Bródd said it into the old speech.

Tokk looked at her. He said something short.

“He says his grandmother’s was the one under it,” said Bródd.

Nobody said anything.

Yrsa put down the turves.

She went to the post. She did not ask anybody. She went to the foot of the post and looked up at the head on top of it, and then she turned and said something to Ásgeir, low, and Ásgeir looked at her for a long moment and then put down his stick and went for the ladder.

They took it down.

Ásgeir did it. He went up the ladder against the new post and worked the head free from the pegs he had fixed it with that morning, and brought it down, and stood at the foot of the ladder with it in his arms, looking at it. Then he gave it to Yrsa.

She held it.

She did not give it to Tokk. Riarhin had half thought she might. She did not. She held it a while, in her arms, the scorched black thing with its open jaws. Then she carried it down out of the ring and along the point to her house and went in with it, and came out without it.

“It’s mine,” she said, to nobody. “It was my grandmother’s. I’ll keep it.” She looked at the post. “It won’t go back up.”

Tokk looked at her for a long time. Then he nodded, once, and limped to the fire, and sat down.

The Skarth men went back to work.

Riarhin, on her stone, shaving bark off a birch pole with the Last Blade, watched the house go up against the foot of the post in the afternoon sun, rafters set low at the joint so the water would run off, and the turf going on, and Tokk at the fire with his leg out, and Yrra beside him eating Yrsa’s bread.

The bell went up on the last day.

Ásgeir hung it. He climbed the ladder against the new post with the bell on its cord over his shoulder and the clapper in his teeth, and fixed it to the peg at the top, where the head had been, and hung the clapper in it, and came down.

There was no head on the post. There was the bell, small and black and cracked, swinging a little in the wind.

Yrsa pulled the cord.

It rang. It was a bad sound, cracked and flat, like a pot struck with a stone, and it went out over the point and the lake and the hills, and came back.

On the lake, a long way out, there was a boat. The first boat of the spring, Ásgeir said, going out to the nets at the head of the lake.

Yrsa rang it again. For the boat.

Then she let go of the cord and turned round and looked at Orm, standing at the edge of the ring with his three-fingered hand, and at Riarhin on her stone, and at Brother Halle in his grey robe with his empty pack.

“There,” she said. “It’s right.”

She did not thank them. She went back into the house under the post to see to the bones.

Chapter 46 — Orm’s share

The Staðfast lay at her berth with the boom down and the spring freight stacked on the quay beside her, and Orm stood in front of Sigrun with his hand in Hallbjorn’s palm-guard and said he would not be carried.

“I’m not a passenger,” he said. “I won’t sit in the hold on the sacks where she sat with Asta bringing me apples.” He did not look at Riarhin. “I’ve been aboard this ship twenty-two years. I’m not going south in her as cargo.”

“Nobody said cargo,” said Sigrun.

“Hallbjorn said.”

“Hallbjorn said you can’t steer in a gale with three fingers,” said Sigrun. “Hallbjorn’s right.”

Orm’s face went dark under his beard. He did not answer that. He stood on the quay with his three-fingered hand at his side and looked at the steering oar on the Staðfast’s stern quarter, the long dark blade of it lashed up out of the water, and Riarhin, sitting on her bench by the post where she had sorted rope in the autumn, saw what was in his face, and looked away.

“Then I’ll do something else,” said Orm.

“What?”

“I don’t know,” said Orm. “I’ll think of it.” He turned round. “Give me till tomorrow.”

He thought of it on the herring stairs.

Riarhin knew that because she was there. She had gone up, in the evening, because Hild had sent word by Eyvind that there were nets wanting mending before the spring fishing and the woman from the forest shore was the only one who had learned to pull a knot even and then gone off into the ice and stopped coming. So she went up. Slowly, with the staff, though she hardly needed it now. The racks on the landings were nearly empty. The winter’s fish had gone down to the boats.

Orm was at the top. Sitting by Hild’s fire, with Hild, and a net across his knees.

He was mending it. Slowly. With the palm-guard on his left hand, the three fingers holding the mesh open, the netting needle in his right, under and round and through. Hild was watching him.

“Too tight,” said Hild.

Orm loosened it.

“Even,” said Hild. “You’re not tying up a thief.”

Riarhin stood at the top of the stair and watched them. Orm did not look up. He did the next knot, and the next, and Hild watched, and grunted, and looked away.

Riarhin sat down on the other keg and took the net Hild handed her.

They mended for a long time. The light went red and then grey over the harbour below. The women going down the stairs with their baskets sang the counting song, and stopped at eight.

“Kollsvein’s getting old,” said Orm, at last. Not looking up.

Riarhin looked at him.

“He sets the stroke,” said Orm. “He’s set it twenty years. His voice is going. He told me in the winter. He said another season, two, and he’d not be heard over a gale.” He did the next knot. “You don’t need two hands to set the stroke. You need a voice, and you need to know the oars, and you need to know when to change it.” He pulled the knot even. “I know the oars. I’ve steered her twenty-two years. I know when she wants a long stroke and when she wants a short one before she knows it.” He looked at the net. “And I’ve got a voice. Ask Ranveig. Ask anybody I’ve ever shouted at.”

“Ask Kollsvein,” said Riarhin.

“I asked him,” said Orm. “This afternoon.” He did another knot. “He said if I set it a beat behind he’d throw me over the side with his own hands, fingers or no fingers.” Orm almost smiled. “That means yes.”

Hallbjorn tested it.

He did it the next morning, on the fjord, in the Staðfast, with the whole crew at the oars and Sigrun at the steering oar and Orm standing in the waist where Kollsvein usually stood. Riarhin watched from the quay. Half of Hvalgard watched from the quay. They had heard.

It was not a kind test. Riarhin saw that from the first stroke. Hallbjorn had taken the stroke oar, the one nearest the stern, the one the others followed, and he rowed it wrong: a little long, then a little short, then late, then early, the way a tired man rows in a long pull, the way a crew comes apart in a sea when the man at the stroke oar is frightened or sick or hurt. Riarhin had seen crews come apart. She watched the oars behind Hallbjorn’s begin to follow him, and stumble, and the Staðfast begin to yaw.

Orm saw it.

He did not shout at Hallbjorn. He shouted at the crew. He shouted the stroke, one, two, in a voice that came across the water to the quay like a door slamming. He called a little ahead of Hallbjorn’s stroke. The oars behind Hallbjorn followed the voice. One. Two. He held it there. Steady. Long. The oars came back together. Hallbjorn rowed wrong for another twenty strokes, and the crew did not follow him, and the Staðfast went straight down the fjord on Orm’s voice.

Then Hallbjorn rowed right.

On the quay, Kollsvein, who had been watching with his arms folded, spat in the water.

“A beat ahead,” he said, to nobody. “I always said he’d be a beat ahead.”

When the Staðfast came back to her berth, Hallbjorn shipped his oar and came up the ramp first, and walked past Orm without saying anything, and stopped, and turned round.

“You’ll do,” he said.

He went up the quay.

Orm stood at the top of the ramp and looked after him. Then he looked down at his hand in the palm-guard.

“That’s the most he’s said to me since the forest shore,” he said.

Riarhin went to Sigrun that night.

Sigrun was at the table in Jorunn’s hall with the slate and the lists, where she had been the night Riarhin first asked about a ship south. She did not look up.

“I want to work my passage,” said Riarhin.

“You can’t row,” said Sigrun. “Not with that rib. Not with that arm.”

“I can sort rope. I can splice. I can mend nets. I can keep a watch. I can keep a lamp still.” Riarhin sat down across the table. “I can cook badly.”

Sigrun looked up.

“I don’t want to go south as somebody you carried,” said Riarhin. “Again. I’ve been carried since the forest shore. Asta. Orm. Hallbjorn over the side. The whole coast. On hurdles.” She looked at the slate. “I don’t want to be owed. I don’t want to owe you either. Not for the rest of my life because you came round the Fox Point and Eyvind saw a coat on a stick.” She paused. “Give me work. A share of it. For this passage. As far as you’re going. And then we’re done.”

Sigrun looked at her for a long time with her pale eyes.

“The lines,” she said at last. “All of them. Every lashing and every sheet and every line aboard, sorted and spliced and coiled, every morning, before the watch changes. And the night watch, the middle one, the one nobody wants, every third night. And you keep out of Kolli’s galley.” She made a mark on the slate. “That’s your passage. To the first harbour. That’s all it is.”

“That’s all it is,” said Riarhin.

“And when you get off,” said Sigrun, “you get off. You don’t write to me telling me what you owe me. You don’t send me a gift. You don’t come back in ten years with a sword and a song and stand on my quay.” She looked at Riarhin. “You were aboard. You worked. You got off. That’s what it was.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

Sigrun nodded, and went back to the slate.

“The Ykrul came this afternoon,” she said, without looking up. “He wants a berth. He offered to hunt for the pot. I said there’s nothing to hunt on the sea. He said he’d think of something.” She made a mark. “He’s going to stand the morning watch and haul when he’s told and not complain about the fish, and I’m going to put him off where you get off, and after that he can go where he likes.” She did not look up. “He made me write it down. Separately. Not with yours.”

They could not sail.

Riarhin learned why on the fifth day, when the spring freight was all aboard and the Staðfast was ready at her berth, and Sigrun stood on the quay and looked up the hill at Jorunn’s hall and did not give the order.

“The byre,” said Asta, beside Riarhin.

Riarhin looked at her.

“The Ottarshall people,” said Asta. “In Jorunn’s byre. Since the autumn. Sigrun said, on her name, across the water, the day we came in, that they could have the byre till the spring. On her name.” She looked at the hill. “Spring’s come. They want to go home. Ottarshall’s got two new houses and a mended boat-shed and Svala in one of the houses, and they want to go home and plant.” She looked at the Staðfast. “Sigrun said she’d see them home. Not in so many words. She said on my name, and that’s what it means. Eleven people. Their things. Their winter. And Halle’s barley for their seed, from the chapel store.” She folded her arms. “Ottarshall’s north. A day and a half. And a day and a half back.”

“Three days,” said Riarhin.

“Four, with the loading.”

Riarhin looked at the Staðfast. At the open fjord beyond the whale-bone gate. At the south.

She had Coryd’s letter in her coat. She had read it so often the folds were wearing through. Come when you can.

“Four days,” she said.

“You could go with Thorvald Hrafnsson,” said Asta. “The Kráka’s going south tomorrow. She leaks like a basket. She’ll get you to the first harbour if she doesn’t sink.” She did not look at Riarhin. “You don’t have to wait.”

Riarhin looked at the Kráka, down the quay, low in the water, with Thorvald’s two sons on her deck bailing.

“I’ll wait,” she said.

They took the Ottarshall people home in the Staðfast, all eleven of them, with their bundles and their winter’s goods and forty sacks of Brother Halle’s barley, up the coast in a day and a half of fair wind, and put them ashore at Ottarshall on the shingle under the black walls of the old hall.

Riarhin worked the lines. Every morning, before the watch changed. She sorted them and spliced them and coiled them on the deck, and Hallbjorn came along behind her and looked at every coil and said nothing, which she had learned meant they were right.

Orm set the stroke. Coming into Ottarshall against the tide, in the narrows below the point, he set it long and slow and then short and hard, a beat ahead, and the Staðfast came in on his voice and grounded on the shingle exactly where Sigrun wanted her, and the eleven people from the byre stood up in the waist and looked at the black walls and the two new houses and the smoke, and one of the women began to cry.

Ingrith was on the shingle.

She did not come to the boat. She stood at the top of the strand with her hands at her sides. Beside her, a pace apart, with her arms folded, stood Svala.

On the way back down the coast, the second evening, in the dusk, with the sea flat and grey and the Staðfast running south under sail alone, Orm came and sat beside Riarhin on the hatch cover where she was coiling the last of the day’s lines.

He had a lashing in his hands. A short piece, for the sea-chest by the mast. He was trying to tie it off. The knot wanted two hands, a turn held down with one while the other took the end through, and he held it with the palm-guard and the three fingers and it slipped. He tried again. It slipped.

Riarhin did not reach for it. She went on coiling.

He tried a third time. It slipped. He sat with the lashing in his lap and looked at it.

“Hold that,” he said.

She held it. She put her finger on the turn where he showed her, and held it down, and he took the end through with his right hand and drew it tight, and it held.

“Thank you,” said Orm.

She went back to her coil.

Behind them, at the stern, Sigrun was at the steering oar with the sunset on her face. Ahead, past the bow, the coast ran away south toward the whale-bone gate, headland after headland, and beyond the last of them the sea opened out, grey and flat and enormous, going away to a horizon lost in haze, with nothing on it at all.

Chapter 47 — The part not sung

Ranveig sang the third verse in the tide-shed on the night before the Staðfast sailed, and half of Hvalgard came to hear whether she would get it wrong again.

She had sung the second at Kettlebeach in the false spring, and at Selvík, and up the coast as far as the boats would take her. The second had been short. Riarhin had heard it at Kettlebeach: twice, not once; eight, not nine; she said it was the Mother, and it wasn’t. It had been a correction, and it had sounded like one, and people had listened to it with their arms folded.

This was the third. Ranveig had made it in the days since the four came home, and nobody had heard it yet.

The tide-shed was not as full as it had been for the feast. The longships were back in it, on their rollers, with the spring tide lapping at their keels, and the tables were set up between them, and there were perhaps a hundred people, sitting on the benches and on the gunwales of the ships and on the shingle at the open seaward end. Riarhin sat at the end of a bench near the landward door, with Temür on one side and Bródd on the other. Orm was across the shed with Sigrun and the Staðfast’s crew. Sifa was not there; she was at Ottarshall, in Ingrith’s keeping, till midsummer.

Ranveig stood up at the end of the shed with her harp against her hip, and struck it once, and the shed went quiet.

It was longer than the first.

It began where the first had begun, with the burned hall and the four taken. It went north. The moor, the frozen river, the hearths of the four valleys. And it was different almost at once, because it had names in it. Brakka, who cut notches in a stick for every sack lost on the ice. Garrak, who did not follow the marks. Hrogaz Nine-Winters, who had chosen nine times. Old Vekk, who could read and said he could not. Ranveig sang them all in the old measures, in her cracked low voice, and the shed listened, and Riarhin watched faces she knew from the feast hear names they had never heard.

It went to the ice. Eight, Ranveig sang. She named them. Tokk and Yrra of the four valleys, who knew the way in. Ilva of the Temple, who would not read the line. Sifa Cut-Tongue, who stopped the hand. It went down the nave. The thing coming down like weather. The line along the wall, and the woman of the Temple on her knees pulling a man with a broken leg along it. The Ykrul on the bridge with no rail, lying flat, the arrow going up into the eye. Orm Ketilsson with the line, holding, holding.

Twice, Ranveig sang. Under the jaw, and behind it.

And then it went where the first verse had not gone.

And she came down off the ice and said it was the Mother, and it was not. And the halls believed her, and the bays went home, and the song went out along the coast, and it was not true. Ranveig did not lower her voice for it. She sang it the way she had sung the rest, steadily, in the same measures. And the river froze from beneath, and the weir broke, and the ladders froze to the wall, and the four were still behind the screen while the coast was singing.

The shed was very quiet.

Riarhin sat on her bench and heard her own mistake sung in front of a hundred people, in the old words, by the best skald on the coast, and did not look away from Ranveig, and did not look at the floor.

Then the verse went north again. The second time. The waystation under the Ness, and the light on the broken mast, and the coat on the rocks. The horn in the fog that sounded like home. The ring of ten staves through the Choir, and the ninth, and the count cut on the warden’s staff. Thirty-one beach stones on a rug in a warm room. The door wedged with a broken bench in the warm water. The warden on the dark ice with his daughter behind a clear wall, saying no. The candle-bishop on his knees with the vellum. The cold drawing back down the channels, and the ice closing over the pale eyes, and the eyes still open. Watching.

She is there yet, Ranveig sang. Under the ice, and waiting. For the year the north forgets how to read.

And the four, walking down off the broken glacier, alive.

She struck the last note and let it go.

Nobody moved for a long moment.

Then somebody at the back of the shed said, quite loudly: “I liked the first one better.”

There was a laugh. Not much of one. Riarhin turned her head and saw who had said it: a big man from the eastern bays, one of the ones who had gone home from the muster in the winter singing. He was looking at Ranveig with his arms folded, unsmiling.

“The first one was a song,” he said. “That one’s a—” He did not have the word. “That one’s a Thing. It’s a hearing. It’s got all the names in it. You can’t sing that at a feast. People would be there till dawn trying to remember who Garrak is.”

“Then they’ll be there till dawn,” said Ranveig.

“And the end,” said the man. “She is there yet. That isn’t an end. That’s a—” He shook his head. “The first one ended with the thing dead. You knew where you were.”

“You knew where you were,” said Ranveig, “and you were wrong.”

The man looked at her a long time. Then he unfolded his arms, and got up, and went out of the tide-shed into the night. A few people went with him. Not many.

Most of them stayed.

Temür went to Ranveig afterward, with Bródd. Riarhin went with them, because Temür looked round at her once at the door and then away, and she had learned what that meant.

“The Ykrul line,” said Temür. In the northern speech, slowly. He was much better at it now. “In your verse. The riders taken from the frozen water.” He looked at Ranveig. “Say it again. The words. So Bródd can hear if they’re right.”

Ranveig said it again. Spoken, not sung. In the third winter, by the frozen water, the wardens cut the mark on the riders’ doors in the night, and took them, and roped them, and gave them to the Hall. And of all their households, one slipped the rope, and one was never home, and the rest went in.

Bródd listened. He nodded.

“That’s Vekk’s stave,” he said. “That’s what it says. As near as anything.”

“Not all their households,” said Temür.

Ranveig looked at him.

“Twenty-two,” said Temür. “On Vekk’s stave. Twenty-two households. From the frozen water. In one winter.” He held up the bone case, on its thong. “That’s what it says. Not the riders. Not all the riders. Twenty-two houses by the frozen water in the third winter.” He looked at her. “My people crossed the water. The Great Wake. Thousands. Hundreds of thousands. From a hundred places, in a hundred years, for a hundred reasons. Borte’s song is about one rope. The old man at the western fire’s song is about another. Vekk’s stave is twenty-two houses.” He lowered the case. “If you sing the riders, somebody at the back will go home and say that’s why the Ykrul crossed the water. Because of the Dogmen and the dragon. And it isn’t. It’s why twenty-two houses did.” He paused. “Maybe.”

Ranveig was quiet for a moment.

“Twenty-two houses,” she said. “By the frozen water.” She tried it in the measure, under her breath. It did not fit. She frowned, and tried again, and changed a word, and it fitted.

“Yes,” said Temür.

“It’s worse,” said Ranveig. “As a line.”

“It’s right,” said Temür.

Ranveig looked at him with her bird’s look. Then, unexpectedly, she smiled.

“The coast-woman said that to me in the winter,” she said. “Word for word.”

There was one thing in the verse Riarhin did correct.

She did it at the end, when the shed had begun to empty, and Ranveig was putting her harp back into its sealskin case. Riarhin went to her and stood.

“The door,” she said. “In the warm water. The bench.”

“Yes?”

“You sang she held the door alone against the water till the claim was spoken.” Riarhin looked at her. “I didn’t. Temür came. He held it with me. Most of the time he held it more than I did. My leg was going.” She paused. “And when he let go to shout about the stick in the box, I held it alone for a while. That much is true. But not alone till the claim.”

Ranveig looked at her.

“Alone is better,” she said.

“It was two,” said Riarhin.

Ranveig sighed. She took the harp out of its case again, and struck a note, and tried something under her breath, and changed it, and tried it again.

“And the rider came down the slope into the water and set his shoulder by hers,” she said. “That do?”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“And the bit where your sword shone blue in the dark of the nave when you put it in the throat,” said Ranveig. “Is that true?”

Riarhin thought about it. She remembered the frost-light in the channels, the faint blue glow coming out of the ice. She remembered Remorse going in under the jaw. She did not remember what it had looked like. She had not been looking at it. She had been looking at the throat.

“I don’t know,” she said. “I wasn’t looking.”

“Then I’ll leave it,” said Ranveig. “Somebody always asks about the sword.”

Riarhin let it stand.

The letter came on the last trader before the Staðfast sailed, which was also the first trader of the spring.

He was a small dark man from the south with a boat full of iron and salt and a box of letters, and he came into Hvalgard on the afternoon tide and tied up at the far quay, and Gudleif went down to him with the box of letters for the south that he had been keeping all winter.
