Riarhin’s was in it. The one she had written in Jorunn’s hall with Brother Anselm’s pen in the dark of the year. Gudleif gave it to the trader. The trader gave Gudleif another box.

There was a letter in it with her name on.

This time the seal was a plain blob of red, and wrapped in oilcloth, and tied with a string with a wooden tag, and the tag had three names burned into it, three harbours, one after another, the last of them Hvalgard. Inside the oilcloth the paper was Coryd’s. The square letters.

She read it sitting on the end of the quay with her legs over the water.

Coryd had had her first letter. The one from the harbour on the way north, written in the hold with a pen she could barely hold. You’re alive, Coryd wrote, at the top, alone, on a line by itself. Then nothing for a space. Then the rest.

There was news. Sivelle, and the house, and the stair that did not creak. Harissa. And then the other thing.

The cousins wrote again in the winter. It hasn’t stopped. It’s worse. They say there are more of them now on the shore road at dusk, and that they don’t only walk toward her house any more, they walk away from it, along the coast, as if they’d been sent somewhere. They say she’s rarely seen. They say she’s been at the book for years, since she was a child, since she found it in the ruin above the village, and that now she’s nearly grown she’s stopped hiding what she reads in it.

They asked in the village whether anybody had come for the book. Anybody of the Lethai. To take it back, or to ask about it. Nobody has. The priest says whoever wrote it has been gone for centuries, long before the trees went wrong up there. He says it’s older than anything on that coast. He says that like it’s a comfort.

The cousins say they have a man who will meet a stranger off a boat at the harbour and not tell anybody. His name is Hesvar. He keeps the chandlery by the steps. Ask for rope and say my name.

Come when you can. Please. I’m not expecting you on the first any more. I’m expecting you when you get here.

And at the bottom, the stupid lines. Bea’s.

Riarhin sat on the end of the quay with the letter in her lap and the tide coming in under her feet.

Sigrun was on the Staðfast’s deck, checking the lashings on the freight.

“The first harbour,” said Riarhin. She stood on the quay at the foot of the ramp. “Where Asta told me about the letter-man. Where do the ships go from there? South.”

Sigrun did not look up.

“Some go south and west,” she said. “Along the coast. To the Valewood shore and past it. Some go across.”

“To the Mistlands?”

Sigrun looked up then.

“Some,” she said. “In the summer. Not many. Nobody goes there who doesn’t have to.” She looked at Riarhin. “Why?”

“Because I said I would,” said Riarhin. “In the winter. To my sister. In a letter.”

Sigrun looked at her for a long moment with her pale eyes.

“There’s a man at the first harbour called Arngrim,” she said at last. “He runs salt across to the Mistland coast twice a summer. He’ll take a passenger if she pays and doesn’t ask what else is in the hold.” She went back to the lashing. “I’ll put you ashore at his quay. I won’t wait.”

“I won’t ask you to.”

Riarhin went back up the quay to the trader, who was still there, sitting on his boxes eating bread. She gave him a letter. She had written it on the end of the quay in the time it took the tide to come up two steps, on the back of Coryd’s, with Brother Halle’s ink. It was short. I’m coming. On the salt boat from the first harbour, with a man called Arngrim, as soon as he sails. Tell Hesvar. Tell the cousins not to do anything until I get there. And the stupid lines.

The trader took it, and put it in his box, and went on eating.

She went down to the strand that night, alone, after everybody was asleep.

The tide-shed was dark. The longships lay in it on their rollers with the water lapping at their keels. Outside, on the shingle, the tide was going out under a thin moon, and the sea was very quiet.

She stood at the edge of the water.

She did not know why she had come. She had not planned to. She had lain on the end of the women’s bench in Jorunn’s hall for the last time and not slept, and got up, and come down here.

She said his name.

She said it quietly, to the water. Not to anybody. There was nobody there. She said it once, and then she said the rest, things she had not said to anybody, to the sea going out under the moon. That there was an Ykrul who did not cook, who held a bowl the way he had, and said it was how everybody held a bowl. That she had looked at his hands, and he had asked her why, and she had told him the truth. That he had lain on a bridge with no rail and put an arrow in a thing’s eye from underneath, and come down a slope into warm water to stand beside her at a door. That he did not remind her of anybody. That she was trying.

She said that she had left him by the gatepost. She said she was sorry.

She said Ailsa’s name, after, and Bea’s. And then, after a long time, her grandmother’s.

The sea went out. Nobody answered. Nobody had been meant to.

When she turned round to go back up the strand, there was nobody on the shingle behind her, and nobody in the dark of the tide-shed.

Chapter 48 — South of the last beacon

They sailed on the morning tide, in daylight, with the boom down and the whole quay come to see them go.

There were no lanterns this time. It was spring, and the sun was up over the southern hills by the time the freight was lashed and the last of the crew aboard, and the fjord was blue and the snow on the slopes above the town was going in long grey streaks. People stood on the quay in the sun with their hands in their pockets, as they had stood in the snow in the dark of the year. Gudleif in his yellow bear-fur, though it was too warm for it. Jorunn beside him, not quite touching his arm. Brother Halle in his grey robe with his borrowed mittens, which he did not need, held in his hands. Old Hild, who had come down the herring stairs for the first time that anybody could remember, on a stick, with her belt of knots. Thirty, she had told Riarhin. Thirty-one, now. She had tied one for the winter.

Ranveig, at the end of the quay, with her harp in its sealskin case. She did not play it. She stood and watched.

“For Orm,” said Asta, at Riarhin’s elbow, at the rail. “Again. So it’s seen.” She looked along the quay. “And for you, I expect. Some of them.”

Riarhin looked at the faces.

“And for the Ykrul,” said Asta. “Some.”

Sifa was not on the quay.

She was at Ottarshall, in Ingrith’s keeping, till midsummer. Riarhin had gone up the coast to see her the day before the tide-shed, in Leif’s boat, because she wanted to, and because it had been her name on the stone.

Sifa had been sitting on the shingle under the black walls of the old hall, with Gunnvor. They had a loom between them. A small one, a frame loom, new wood, that one of the Ottarshall men had made in the false spring from a pattern Gunnvor drew in the sand, and Gunnvor was threading it, slowly, with her shaking hands, and dropping the shuttle, and picking it up, and Sifa was holding the warp straight for her.

They did not stop when Riarhin came.

“The four valleys are sending speakers,” said Sifa, when the shuttle had gone through. “At midsummer. Hrogaz. Orra. Two from the black crag who know who went down to the hall.” She held the warp. “And somebody for me. To stand beside me at the stone. Hrogaz sent word. He said I’m his to answer for, under the stave. He said he’ll send the best they have.” She touched her jaw. “I don’t know who that is.”

“Ingrith’s keeping you,” said Riarhin.

“Ingrith’s keeping me.” Sifa looked up at the black walls. “Hauk comes and looks at me every few days. He doesn’t say anything. He looks.” She went back to the warp. “Thora brought me a blanket.”

Riarhin did not know what to say to that.

“It isn’t finished,” said Sifa. “Midsummer. The stone. Nineteen.” She did not look up. “You said you wouldn’t let anybody tell me it was.”

“I won’t.”

“You won’t be here,” said Sifa. “You’re going south.” The shuttle went through. Gunnvor caught it. “So I’ll have to do it myself.” She looked up, at last, with her flat yellow-brown eyes. “I will.”

When Riarhin left, at the top of the shingle, she looked back once. Sifa was holding the warp straight. Gunnvor was threading the shuttle, and dropping it, and picking it up.

The word from the north had come with Tokk and Yrra, who had gone on to Kettlebeach to see the stone, and come back to Hvalgard to see the sea.

Hrogaz’s hearths were moving. All ten. And the two streams, Yrra said, and the long ice, and some of the others who had not sent. Over the old road across the top of the broken ice, the one that had opened at the release, with the old cairns half-buried along it, north, to the far side and down. To grass. There was grass on the north side, Yrra said, in the summers, that nobody living had seen, because the road to it had been shut for four hundred years.

“They’re going to mend it,” said Tokk. He was sitting on the quay with his splinted leg out, eating Jorunn’s bread. “The road. Hrogaz said. Every summer. The cairns, and the cracks, and the bad places. Every hearth sends people.” He chewed. “He says it’s ours now. He says the Hall kept it shut and it’s open and if we don’t mend it, it’ll shut itself.”

“And the high valley?” said Riarhin.

Yrra looked at her.

“Gaurra sent somebody,” she said. “To Hrogaz. After. A grandson.” She looked at the sea. “To say they’d heard. About the Hall, and the offer, and the valley. And what Hrogaz said.” She paused. “The grandson said Gaurra said to tell him she still wasn’t under his stave. And that she’d send two people to mend the road in the summer.” She shrugged. “That’s all.”

They cast off.

The lines came in. Hallbjorn coiled them as they came, and Riarhin, beside him, flaked them down on the deck so that they would run clear, and he looked at every coil and said nothing. Eyvind was up the mast. The oars went out, twelve a side, and Kollsvein stood in the waist with his arms folded, and Orm stood beside him, and Orm set the stroke.

One. Two.

His voice went across the water to the quay like a door slamming, and on the quay somebody laughed, and somebody cheered, and the Staðfast came off the berth and turned on the oars toward the whale-bone gate.

Riarhin looked back once at the quay. At the faces. At Ranveig at the end of it with her harp case, not playing. At the bench by the post where she had sorted rope in the autumn, empty.

Then the whale-bone posts went by, one on either side, grey-white, curving in toward each other overhead, with the iron rings in them and the boom lying on the bottom between. She looked up at them as they passed. Then they were behind.

Temür was on the hatch cover.

Riarhin found him there when the sail was up and the oars were in and the Staðfast was running down the fjord under the wind alone. He was sitting in the sun on the hatch cover with his back against the mast and the bone case beside him and his bow across his knees, and he had his eyes shut.

“That’s Kollsvein’s place,” said Riarhin.

He opened one eye.

“Kollsvein isn’t in it,” he said.

“He will be. He sits there after the stroke. In the sun. Every day. Eyvind told me.” She looked down at him. “He’ll want it.”

“Then he can ask,” said Temür, and shut the eye.

Kollsvein came along the deck a little later and stood over the hatch cover and looked down at Temür in his place in the sun. Temür did not open his eyes. Kollsvein looked at him for a long time. Then he said something to Orm, who was going past with a coil of line, that made Orm laugh out loud, and went and sat on the sea-chest by the mast, in the shade, grumbling.

“He’ll have it back tomorrow,” said Orm, to Riarhin, still laughing. “He’ll be there at dawn.” He looked at Temür. “And the Ykrul will be there before him. I’ll lay you a cup on it.”

Riarhin did not take the bet.

Temür sat up when the fjord opened out.

He did not say anything. He took the bone case on his knees, and held it, and looked south, past the bow, where the coast ran away in headland after headland toward the haze.

“The first harbour,” said Riarhin. She sat down beside him on the hatch cover. “Sigrun puts me ashore at a man called Arngrim’s quay. He runs salt across to the Mistlands.” She paused. “She puts you ashore there too.”

“I know.”

“After that you can go where you like.”

“I know,” said Temür. He was looking south. “Borte’s fire is east. Over the moors from the coast road. Five days. Six.” He turned the case on his knees. “She sent me north to find where the mark was cut. I found it. On a stave in a case in the four valleys, and on a stave in a box in the Hall.” He touched the lid. “I said I’d come back and tell her what I saw.”

“Yes.”

“And then she’d tell me what she dreamed.” He almost smiled. “She’s told me already. At her fire. So I suppose I go and tell her the rest.” He looked at the case. “And show her this. Five pieces of wood.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“The Mistlands,” said Temür, after a while. “What’s there?”

“A young woman with an old book,” said Riarhin. “And the dead walking on a shore road at dusk. And my sister’s husband’s cousins, who are frightened.” She looked south. “I don’t know what else.”

“That’s not much.”

“No.”

He was quiet a long time, looking at the haze.

“I said the first harbour,” he said. “To Sigrun. I made her write it down. Not with yours.” He did not look at her. “That’s all I’ve said.”

“I know,” said Riarhin.

He nodded. He did not say anything else about it, and she did not ask.

Asta found her at the rail in the afternoon, with Eyvind’s slate.

It was the same slate. Riarhin knew it at once: the column of names in a careful round hand, Sigrun, Orm, Asta, Hallbjorn, and the rest, and at the bottom, smaller, Eyvind. Below that there were two more. One of them was Temür, spelled right, in a different hand, a rider’s hand, the strokes too heavy.

The other was hers.

It was spelled right. She looked at it. In the autumn, in the hold, the boy had sounded it out under his breath and written it, and spelled it wrong, and she had not corrected him. It had been wrong on the slate all winter.

It was right now.

“He asked Ranveig,” said Asta. “In the false spring. How it was spelled. She didn’t know either. So he asked the Temple clerk, Brother Anselm, who had lent her the pen for her letter, and he wrote it out for him on a bit of bark.” Asta looked at the slate. “He rubbed the old one out. He said he didn’t want it wrong on the slate if anything happened. So somebody could say it right.”

Riarhin looked at it for a long time.

Then she gave the slate back to Asta, and Asta took it below.

The last beacon was on the headland at the southern end of the Hvalgard coast, a stone tower on the high bluff over the sea with an iron basket on top, and they passed it at dusk.

Eyvind saw it first, from the mast, and called it. The whole ship looked. It stood up black against the yellow sky on its bluff, very high, very old, and in the iron basket on top a fire was burning. Somebody kept it. Somebody climbed the tower every evening and lit it, so that the ships going south would know where the coast ended.

Riarhin stood at the rail and watched it go by.

Orm came and stood beside her. He leaned his forearms on the rail, the right one and the left one with the palm-guard on it, and looked at the beacon.

“I made a verse,” he said.

She looked at him.

“About you,” said Orm. “While I was setting the stroke this morning. Coming out of the gate.” He did not look at her. “There was a woman on the shingle with a coat on a stick, and I went and got her, and she contradicted me in front of Ranveig.” He paused. “That’s as far as I’ve got.”

“It doesn’t scan,” said Riarhin.

“It doesn’t have to scan. It’s true.”

“It isn’t,” said Riarhin. “You said nothing to tell. I said you were afraid. That isn’t contradicting. That’s correcting.”

Orm turned his head and looked at her.

“There,” he said. “You see? You’re doing it again.”

She opened her mouth. She shut it. He was grinning at her over the rail, with the beacon going by behind him on its bluff, and she found that she was annoyed, properly annoyed, the way she had been annoyed with Coryd on the stairs at home when they were children and Coryd had won. She looked at him and could not think of anything to say that would not prove him right.

“Go and set something,” she said.

Orm laughed, and went.

The beacon went astern. It dropped behind them into the dusk, a point of fire on a black bluff, smaller, and smaller, and then the headland came between, and it was gone.

The sea opened out. Grey, and flat, and going away south into the dark.

Riarhin went forward along the deck. It was nearly the change of the watch. The lines wanted coiling before it changed; Sigrun had said every morning, but there had been a sheet let run in the afternoon when the wind came round, and it lay in a tangle by the mast, and Hallbjorn would look at it in the morning and say nothing, and she would know.

She knelt by it. Her leg let her. She took up the end and began to work the tangle out, a turn at a time, and when it was clear she coiled it, sunwise, in long even loops, and flaked it down on the deck so that it would run clear if anybody needed it in the night.

Behind her Kollsvein said something to Temür about the hatch cover, and Temür said something back, and Orm laughed. Up the mast Eyvind was singing. Aft, at the steering oar, Sigrun called the watch.

Riarhin laid the last loop down, and stood, and went aft to take hers.

Reader’s Companion

A selective reference to the people and terms in this volume, without recounting the ending.

Dramatis Personae

Asta — A member of Sigrun’s crew, attentive to Riarhin’s recovery.

Bródd — A local interpreter whose inherited speech gives him access others lack.

Eyvind — A young member of the ship’s crew.

Gunnvor, Kári, Ulf and Svala — The four people taken from the coast.

Hrogaz — A representative of the northern households.

Riarhin — A half-Lethai traveller recovering aboard the Linn ship.

Sifa — The captive whose arrival brings the northern dispute to the coast.

Sigrun — Skipper of the Staðfast, responsible for its crew and freight.

Temür — Riarhin’s Ykrul companion, whose family history bears upon the journey.

Vekk — A reader of the old record.

Places and Terms

The Hall / the Mother — Names used for the power to which the northern households owe their contested obligations.

Linn — The northern people among whom Riarhin travels.

“Dogmen” — A Linn legendary name for hobgoblins, based on an inherited depiction rather than reliable eyewitness knowledge.

The northern households — The particular clans involved in this dispute; they do not represent all hobgoblins or the whole history of Ykrul migration.

Remorse — Riarhin’s double-edged elven sword.

Toothpick and the Last Blade — Riarhin’s two distinct Ykrul knives; the Last Blade has been shortened.

Staðfast — Sigrun’s ship.
